**325**

**THE FORTY-EIGHT HOURS WEEK.**

Considerable attention is still being given to the question of the forty-eight
hours week and the possibility or otherwise of it becoming law. At the Seventh
Session of the League of Nations International Labour Office the question was
discussed at length, and we regret to report that the attitude of the Government,
through its representatives, is one of disappointment. Many members of
the League have already ratified the Convention of 1919, but will not put it
into operation until the British Government has done so. We trust that our
members will do what is possible to keep this matter before their local repre-
sentatives, and thus create such an opinion as to show the Government that
the Convention must be ratified, and that at an early date.

The Seventh Session of the I.L.O. also passed conventions in favour of
the abolition of Night Baking, Equality of Treatment of Workmen and
Foreigners' Accidents, and Compensation for accidents and industrial
diseases.

It is, however, very regrettable to have to record the fact that the attitude
of the British Government, instead of leading in industrial matters, as we
contend they should do, they either remain neutral or are reactionary. This
has very considerable effect upon other nations, and undoubtedly reduces
the influence of Great Britain upon other nations' representatives.

This is additional evidence of the necessity to as far as possible, and as
early as possible, change the representation of the country so as to ensure the
Labour Party not only being in office, but in real power, and thus give an
incentive to progress and peace amongst and between the nations of the
earth.

We wish to congratulate the General Secretary upon his re-election as the
British Workers' Representative upon the Governing Body of the International
Labour Office. Mr. Poulton was elected at the top of the poll, and was also
one of the acting chairmen of the Conference, which is indeed an honour for
the Union.

**COUNCIL MEMBER FOR No. 9 DISTRICT.**

The list of names of members nominated to fill the vacancy created by the
lamented death of Mr. C. Bleakley, will be found on page 357. Voting must
take place at the monthly meeting nights, and the votes counted on the 13th
prox., and posted to reach the Central Office by the FIRST POST on the
15th July, otherwise they will be disqualified.

**POLITICAL MATTERS.**

Since writing our last Address, the Labour Party has made a further
attempt to ascertain the attitude of the Government on Unemployment.
Reference was made to the fact that "163,000 more persons are unemployed
than twelve months ago." Mr. J. R. Clynes, speaking to the resolution, called
attention to the fact that in the King's Speech reference was made that the

when an attempt should be made to find a solution, or frankly declare that
there is nothing which the Government can do. The reply on behalf of the
Government was indeed vague, and they only expressed the hope that within
have again reached something like what they were before, when we shall share
"the not too distant future, when the consuming powers of our customers
that the Government have no real policy to meet the grave subject of unem-
in the prosperity which will come to them as to us." It is therefore evident

and when they have an opportunity to record their vote-be it early or late-
we trust they will so use it as to secure a majority of Labour Members in the
next House of Commons.
WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION.
We are pleased to report that W.J.B., of Bristol, who met with an accident
in October, 1921, has recently secured a lump sum of £200, the costs being
paid by the Insurance Company.
We beg to repeat, however, that lump sums are not obtainable by law,
but only by agreement, and members desiring legal assistance must report
immediately to the local officials, who will then communicate with the Central
Office.
NEW IRISH BRANCH.
We are pleased to report that as a result of negotiations with the Central
Office and with the assistance of Mr. G. White, of Belfast, a Branch has been
formed at Dundalk, 29 members joining at the first meeting. The officers
have been appointed, and we trust that not only will the branch in Dundalk
tand loyally by the Union, but that they will be enabled to enjoy the full
Ivantages of their fellow members throughout the United Kingdom.
JUBILEE HISTORY OF THE UNION.
Members who desire to secure a copy of this most interesting and valuable
record of the work of the Union for its first fifty years should place their order
immediately with the Branch Secretary, as the stock is constantly being reduced,
and we do not intend to have a reprint. They are only 6d. per copy, and
should be ordered at once.
THE "DAILY HERALD."
We trust that all our members will take every available opportunity to
push the Herald, the only daily paper owned and controlled by the movement.
By purchasing the Herald daily a full report of the political world as seen by
Labour can be read.
REMEMBER THE FIFTEENTH!
Once again we feel bound to call the serious attention of Branch Secretaries
to the fact that the FIFTEENTH day of each month is the latest day for

**328**
**GENERAL PRESIDENT'S REPORT.**

**FELLOW MEMBERS,**

COMPETITION.—The state of trade appears to be making some of our manufacturers very dissatisfied with what they describe as foreign competition, although many of them have been critics of their confrères in other towns, and not a few have hinted at the way that some of their nearest neighbours conduct their business; to all this I hope we shall always lend a willing ear, because it is just as much to the Union's advantage as well as to the operative, to note that scientific management does not culminate in lower wages. We have already responded to the appeals made to investigate conditions; we are satisfied that the workers' slogan, "equality of opportunity." is fast becoming an international demand amongst the organised workers. The unorganised have no conception, but just follow the organised, "picking up the crumbs." The attempt to interfere with the eight-hour day, which to this day has not been legalised in this country—(although we have had a Labour Government in power for some of the time)—it is passing strange that some other countries should be ahead of us in this respect, the eight-hour day becoming law or adopted by agreement:—Panama 1914, Uruguay 1915, Ecuador 1916, Mexico, Portugal, Russia, Finland 1917, Norway, Germany, Italy, Poland, Luxembourg, Austria, Czecho- Slovakia 1918, Jugo-Slavia, France, Denmark, Switzerland, Spain, Sweden, Washington, Bulgaria, Greece, Roumania and India 1919. Tory England, Ireland and Holland don't appear amongst the above list of enlightened countries, and up to the present all attempts have been defeated. Will our members remind their employers that the above statement that nineteen countries had adopted an eight-hour day before the Washington Convention and of these the most important countries from which we get shoes, viz.:—France, Germany, Austria, Switzerland, Czecho-Slovakia, and Sweden, had all adopted the eight- hour day, and in most other matters we find generally that conditions are not half so bad as our employers try to make us believe. If they would throw in their lot with those of us who believe in dealing with and buying only from those whose conditions of employment are equal to the best in the country, then they would be setting an example that they could honestly ask others to follow, but it is futile to complain of others doing exactly what you may be doing yourself. In this connection we have been very busy putting members of the Employers' Federation to complain of home or foreign competition whilst they place orders and encourage these people who studiously avoid paying the rates of wages governing the trade. It makes one think and wonder whether these pretenders are really acting privately as they would have the public believe.

MARKET HARBOROUGH has occupied more than a week of my time during the past month, chatting with the operatives daily during that time, and eventually calling a meeting and taking a number of names of operatives who were willing to join the Union. For conscience sake we called another meeting nearer the factory, but in the meantime the employer had asked all the operatives (more than one hundred) to stay behind for fifty minutes. This gentleman, an old shopmate of mine, worked with me at Messrs. Cooper & Sons, Redcross Street, Leicester, and was a loyal member of ours for years, and I am informed is a good living man—so tampered with the minds of these operatives that none beyond a handful dared to come to the meeting. I therefore sent him the following letter, and append his reply thereto :-

[COPY.]

MR. G. LOOMS,                                                                              15th May, 1925.

Bath Street, Market Harborough.

DEAR MR. LOOMS,

I am very sorry indeed to find that after several days I have been attending outside your factory for the main purpose of persuading your employees to join the Union. This, I think you will agree with me, is a very perfect legitimate and legal thing to do. I should regret, if I have at any time, during the many visits I have paid, said a thing which could be construed as of a detrimental character to yourself or the firm. I have one object in view, which you had when a young man, and a member of our Union, in doing your best in this short span of life for yourself without the least injury to others, and I have endeavoured, I hope, to carry out this principle, and I thought by taking on this organising of your factory myself, it would be in keeping with your own impression, viz.: that you did not get on very well with one of my colleagues who visited you some time ago, and I thought, therefore, I could assuredly depend upon your sympathy, if not your spirit: in fact some of the men have said (I don't know all the circumstances under which the statement was made), but they had got it at one of the meetings this week that you were rather favourable to the Trade Union. I think you have rather dispelled that idea.


---
We are bound in our humble way, to attend outside the factory of any firm of
boot and shoe manufacturers, or who are doing auxiliary work, work which undef
ordinary circumstances would be done by boot and shoe manufacturers, to endeavour
to get them to adopt our National Conditions, of which I enclose a copy. I think
you will admit that your conditions are below the conditions enclosed, and as these
are minimum conditions, we think at least that, speaking as man to man between our
two selves, and as an old shopmate, I was not aware that you wished to have an
advantage over your fellows in any respect. I believe you subscribe to a form of
faith in which that is its fundamental principle. I should be very pleased to receive
from you the slightest intimation that you had no intention of preventing or interfering
with the liberty of the subject, that you don't wish to say whether your operatives
shall go to Church or to Chapel, join the Salvation Army or the Boys' Brigade, and I
would like to respectfully suggest that you permit them to spend their wages in those
directions which are likely to give them the best results economically, socially, and
politically.
It would be a pleasure to still find that you are still in heart and spirit the same
man as an employer, as you were when a wage-earner.
Hoping that this appeal will have the desired effect in allowing and encouraging
everyone to keep up the standard of life and living to the highest possible point,
is the wish of
Yours sincerely,
T. F. RICHARDS,
CONTRACTORS TO H.M. GOVERNMENT.
General President.

London, W.C. 1.
MARKET HARBOROUGH.
9th June, 1925.
We desire to acknowledge receipt of your letter of the 15th ult., which we
regret has not been replied to earlier owing to the writer being away from business.
We have carefully taken note of the contents, but do not see there is anything
calls for a reply from us. The relations with our workpeople are pleasant, they have
perfect freedom, and you may rest assured that their welfare has always been our
constant aim. In fact, we think they know, the conditions of work and wages here
are better than generally prevailing in this industry.
Therefore, further correspondence on this subject would not serve any useful
purpose.
Yours sincerely,
For G. LOOMS.
W. LOOMS.
This cannot go on. What right has any man to interfere with the liberty of the
subject; this cannot be tolerated, and I am asking my colleagues to allow our Mr. L.
Smith and Mr. J. H. Baum to give the firm special attention. It is only fair to say that
this firm, being an auxiliary firm, is kept going by the Boot Manufacturers' Federated
members, and is not associated with any organisation. This is not all; we note that
this firm say they are Government Contractors. If they were, we could make them pay
in accord with the Fair Wages Contract Clause of the House of Commons, and to make
sure, I am approaching the Director of Army Contracts for information. We believe
this firm has already been removed from the Government list, but we will make sure.
I hope they are on the list, they will then be compelled to pay at once.
MANSFIELD. We have still another firm in the same auxiliary branch of the trade at
Mansfield, where we are trying to get the heads of the firm at Leicester to adopt the trade
conditions. The "heads" say they have no antipathy or opposition to us, but the local
people at Mansfield have had the girls in the office and lectured them on the "folly" of
belonging to a Trade Union and the dire consequences that would, or will follow. These
people who set up this business ought to have informed their principals what were the
conditions of the trade, and not have taken advantage of unorganised labour to make a
success of a business on non-union lines. Further, one would have thought that sooner


---
330
or later these gentlemen ought to have known that they could not be exempt from a set
of conditions wholly imposed by themselves on others. In this they must have been out
of date completely, but we still hope that they will conform to the conditions without
any friction.
LEICESTER.- We have been pressing three firms in this City who are Stiffening Makers,
in the Manufacturers' Association, we expect them to conform to the conditions covering
for compliance with the Holiday Scheme, and although they are not eligible for membership
the trade. Several of these firms who do this kind of work are already paying, but the
three firms of Mr. J. Peake, Messrs, Gardner & Pratt, and Messrs. Burrows Bros. cannot
agree, because of their competitors (and as the two firms at Mansfield and Market Har
borough are in a much larger way of business, one can easily sympathise), but we have
obtained, through the kindly offices of Mr. W. A. North, J.P., who has acted as mediator
between us (and at the employers' request, we were bound to accept the offer), and we have
at last obtained a definite date when these firms will commence to pay. Meantime there
will be no relaxation of our efforts to bring into line all firms engaged in the industry.
LONDON SEW-ROUND BRANCH held a general special meeting of members for the
purpose of getting the members' views on the question of pressing the employers to make
provision for them to work on premises provided by the employer in preference to home
employment. The usual arguments were advanced in favour, but yet a few were found
to vote against this humane and commonsense plan, and as we have already assisted
them before the National Joint Standing Committee to grant them the benefit of the
Holiday Scheme (Mr. W. Addington Willis decided that all workers were involved), and
that one farthing in the shilling up to 56s. per year was in keeping with the letter and spirit
working indoors will eradicate evils which have always attended home employment.
They asked me to preside on behalf of the Union, which I did, and we wish them success
is
being present, and some of their Executive. The next move will be to table this motion
at the Board of Arbitration.
The MANUFACTURERS' FEDERATION has had to be appealed to because either the hot
weather or bad trade appears to be affecting the kindly way in which some employers have
treated us recently. They now suggest that all things are all right, if we will let their
people alone. My answer is: No news is bad news; if we hear often all is generally
good, but when bad or wrong, we must ask all to toe the line irrespective of the size or
reputation of the firm. Wrong is no man's right.
PRODUCTIVE FACTORIES. We have been appealed to respecting one of these firms
whose generosity in making advances and giving advantages, has reduced the volume
of work, and they find that our old adage of a good statement and no work is not beneficial
to a business, and when a whole factory is involved someone has to take the lead and
point a way out. We are always willing to advise in these matters, and if necessary
address those involved. We are well aware that when the Co-operative Wholesale Society
appealed for loyalty from the Distributive Societies, and the fact that trade has been very
good with them this year up to the present, unless they cut off the private firms who supply
the movement - which they have not done, we knew that the fall in trade would find the
productive. In fact some societies won't allow their Managers to place orders outside
the C.W.S.; some don't allow them to place them outside the Movement. I agree with
this latter attitude, and have tried and shall still try, although I don't appear to get much
help from the operatives themselves, that the C.W.S. as such ought to give no orders for
shoes to any private firm. Let them utilise more or both of the "white elephant" factories
they have at Wellingborough and Norwich; neither building is half occupied, and yet
the C.W.S. are buying goods from two factories where we have only a minority of members,
ad the Directors refuse to do as the Government always do, i.e., publish the name of the
ospective contractors. This they have refused.
RAUNDS, ARMY CONTRACTS. In order that there may be no overlapping or different
ices paid in the various districts, I visited Raunds for this purpose, and found that the
Red Book was still the guide for Army Canvas. I was shown the stuff now being made
there, which is in appearance, whilst new, something entirely different. The canvas
bodies are about as thick as leather and thin leather blank cap and golosh cover them with
a leather through runner and a crepe sole and heel.
IRTHLINGBOROUGH, NENE VALLEY TANNING CO. - The Secretary of the Northants
thanks to the Chairman of Directors, Dr. Robb, Mr. Hawthorne, of Finedon, and others
Advisory Council (Mr. G. Chester) had arranged for the Council to visit the above works
and proved to us most interesting. Our party numbered 40 and a very instructive and
enjoyable afternoon was spent. We saw the process of making upper leather from the small
South African hides to the finished article of Box Calf the consecutive processes of Chrome
Tanning are as follows: Soaking, unhairing, neck, splitting, fleshing, deliming, machine
scudding, hand scudding, tanning in drums, neutralising, sammying, splitting, shaving
dyeing, setting out, drying, sawdusting, staking, tacking on, seasoning, glazing, boarding
plating, trimming, measuring. The rates paid men are 60s. per week, and about 20
---

are members. After the visit, which occupied about two hours, the Directors were kind
enough to provide us with a very nice substantial tea, of which we heartily partook and
warmly thanked the Directors for their kindness which was twofold. This visit has made
me think and feel again that the Leather Workers ought to be one unified body, but we
ought to get the Amalgamated and the Slipper Union of Rossendale to join up with us
first.
HOME SECRETARY.-With the General Council we waited on the Home Secretary
to hear his replies to several resolutions, including Compulsory Workshops put forth by
the Factories and the Shoe Makers, and he said these places were on the wane and he did
not feel that he ought to be hurried, in fact he was entirely unsympathetic. On the ques-
tion of bringing buildings under the Factory Inspectors, there is a move in that direction,
if and when he gets his amended Factory Bill through. He agrees that more Factory
Inspectors must be appointed. Like all his friends, they are perfectly satisfied with this
beautiful world; those who voted for them are getting their desserts, and have helped to
set back the clock of progress.
Fellow members, support the Daily Herald, your own paper, and encourage your
friends to do the same, and expose Shoddy Shoes wherever found.
Yours fraternally,
Richard

REPORT OF DELEGATES TO BRUSSELS.
As fraternal delegates to the Biennial Conference of the Belgian Leather and Skin
Workers' Union, which has some ten thousand shoe operatives, who by the way are about
75 per cent. of the whole of the shoe workers in Belgium, we beg to report that the Con-
ference was held on Saturday, Sunday and Monday of the Whitsuntide holidays. The
delegates, some eighty in number, assembled at the Maison du Peuple, or in English the
House of the People. We had a pleasant, but rough, passage from Dover to Calais, and
were struck by the policy of small holdings which are so prevalent in France and Belgium.
We boarded our train at Calais and went through St. Omer, Armentiers, Lille to Blandain,
the Belgian frontier. Here we were for the second time in the hands of the Customs.
Later we passed through Fingeens, Tournai, Barry Moude, to Loos, Englies, Halle Hall,
to Brussels, where Comrade Hellinck and Comrade Wartel met us. The first named is
the general secretary of the Union, and the latter our interpreter, who could speak a little
English, and was a tailor by trade employed at the Maison du Peuple, and a member of
the Management Committee of that place, who are trade unionists and co-operators and
socialists all rolled into one. The building is used for trade union meetings, and for the
sale of some articles, a restaurant where food and drink can be obtained, and where the
people from the provinces are sure of getting a meal at a reasonable cost. To us the cost
of living appeared to be quite 25 per cent. cheaper than in this country. Clothes and
some other articles cost much more, including furniture. Most of the labourers on the
land, those in the streets and gardens, wore sabots (or woodon shoes) with everlasting
wear, and only wear for a dash a pair of slippers or something easy for the feet. This
only applies to the very poor, and, as stated above, those who work on the land, which is
very moist in some parts, more so in France than Belgium. Women are in the majority
as field workers; oxen are used extensively by milkmen, rag and bone dealers and those
who pretend to push a hand cart have a dog underneath in harness who never appears
to be told where he has got to pull as his neck is in the collar as soon as the cart moves.
The Congress was attended by their three presidents-Charles Jorden, Jean Diericks
and Egene Deprex. Delegates were Citoyens Richards and Warner, and Madam Richards,
a visitor from Angleterre (England), Drett (France), Lex (Germany), Shinty (Luxembourg).
Interpreters were Wartel (for English), Shinty (for German), Vanhove (from French into
Flemish), and Luppens (Flemish into French). All understood French or Flemish who
belong to the Union, and they had to have every speech translated into French and Flemish.
The General Secretary was Alex Hellinck; the Secretaries were Leon Petry and Georges
Terrin. The basis of their organisation and benefits are similar in character to our own;

We tried to get permission to go rotind a shoe factory, but one had a fire during the
week prior to the holidays and the other the employer was not of the kind to help us,
says Comrade Hellinck. The nearest was about 100 miles away and permission was not
sare, so we abandoned the idea during this visit. Of one thing learned, is that Belgium
or Belgique or Eke as they call it, and especially Brussels (in their spelling Bruxelles,
no competitor of this country, and there is no outrage of the 48-hour week; they do not
believe that their Government nor the employers will ever be strong enough to abolish
the 48-hour week. The only regret is that, like ourselves, we both permit overtime.
This is, I hope, our next demand for it to be abolished.
We cannot close without thanking all the comrades in Brussels and Belgium who added
to our comfort and permitted us to spend a pleasant outing at Waterloo and other districts.
Statuory, music, painting and very fine and beautiful buildings were much in evidence.
When the same effort is put forth by the powers that be to utilise their wealth for the
benefit of making a sturdy and intellectual people, we shall be nearing the millenium.
12th June, 1925.

ORGANISERS' REPORTS.
FELLOW MEMBERS,
Many of the factories in the parts of Leicestershire where I have been during
the month have been on short time. It is bad for operatives who suffer in their earnings
because of bad trade, but it makes the work of an organiser unpleasant as well. Even
at shops that are weak in trade union organisation and where the National Agreements
are not observed, short time is the rule.
There are two factories at Derby, Maden & Ireland's and Lynch's, where we have
only a few members. Mr. W. R. Smith and I visited these shops. We had a fine meeting
at the first factory. The heads of departments seemed a bit alarmed over the operatives
staying out so long, and giving Mr. Smith a cheer at the close of his speech. The meeting
at Lynch's was good, but all did not turn up as at Maden & Ireland's. The conditions
at this shop are bad according to reports, and yet some were having half days off for want
of trade. The non-unionists working for less than trade union rates have no guarantee
of regular work any more than trade unionists have where the Agreements are observed,
and the anti-trade union employer has no real assurance year by year of increasing his
trade and banking account by employing non-union labour and paying below a fair standard.
Poor pay ultimately means poor work.
We have had several good meetings at Sileby, making special appeal to the females
to recognise how the Union has improved their conditions in recent years. At Messrs.
Newbold & Burton's, where about one hundred operatives are employed, nearly all returned
from dinner in time to give Mr. Smith and myself a good opportunity to state our case
for the Union. So at Willett's, and other shops in a fair way gave us a hearing. It was
only at one shop where the whole of the workers except one did a good sprint to get away
from us. I have a better opinion of these people probably than they would give me credit
for. I believe that they must have felt within themselves that they are an unworthy
set, and that they could not stand and find reasons for their position as non-unionists.
D. MCCARTHY.
23rd June, 1925.
FELLOW MEMBERS,
Since writing our report we have met the repairers working at Upsom's, and we
are very pleased to report that the whole of the repairers are now members of the Union.
We also met the operatives working at Pocock's. We are hopeful that further visitations
will eventually induce them to again rejoin. All the girls are receiving the recognised
scales, but up to the present we have no members amongst them, though they quite
readily admit that it was mainly due to the Union that they receive the present wages.
Met the girls working at the Carlton Shoe Company. This is a well-organised firm so far
as the men are concerned, but we have only a few women members. We canvassed the
whole of them with membership cards, and while a good number of them appeared to be
interested, there was a great deal of indifference. A number of youths working at Freeman,
Hardy & Willis's were not members. After meeting them on two occasions, all of them
promised to become members. Met the women working at De Larnge's, where all the
men are members, but we have only three women members. We are hopeful that as a
result of our visit here, some improvement will take place in our membership. We have
again visited the firm of Polikoff's. In the evening we met the clickers, who appear to
be the best organised, for the purpose of dealing with prices paid for the work cut. After
Mr. Mason had dealt with the grievance, we took the opportunity of pointing out to them
the position of the girls working at the firm, with the result that we are likely to get more
active support in this direction. Met the girls working at Turner's, another firm where
the men are all members. In conversation with them we discovered some time ago some
of them were members, but lapsed. Constant visits are required at this firm if an improve-
ment is to take place in our membership. We have again met the girls working at Good-
man's. This continued visitation should have some effect, but there appears to be a great
deal of hesitancy amongst them, and we have not been able to discover the reason why.
It is a good firm, and the chief objection may be due to the fact that when a fire took place
adequate damages were not forthcoming in the opinion of the girls to meet their losses.
On three occasions we have met the girls working at Britten & Bannister's. We
canvassed the whole of them with literature and membership cards, and when they entered
the factory simply gave the Shop President the cards with nothing on. We again met the
girls after this incident, but some of them seemed totally indifferent. The girls who are
members have approached the firm with reference to the attitude of the girls toward the
Union, but without any success, stating that they could not interfere. Met the girls
working at Lehany's. At all these firms that we have visited in connection with the organi-
sation of women, we estimate that upwards of 400 women are engaged, with a membership
of about 50, while the men are practically all organised, and if we are going to make any

and rather resented our appeal to them to become members. In some cases we are of the

conversations we have no other evidence. At all these firms all the men are members,

fully organised. The existence of such a large body of women unorganised at this class
of firm is deplorable, and owing to the attitude of our members who seem to have neglected
them so long, it makes the position much more difficult. We are hoping as a result of our

efforts that some improvement will take place, and we are fairly confident that if the efforts
results ought to follow.

between to Derby. The former place is a contrast to the East End of London, especially
at this time of year, which is perhaps the most pleasing of all the seasons.

The volume of trade in the county areas round Leicester gives ample scope for work,

335

The attendance and attention at the meetings at the factory gates is on the whole
very good. On several occasions one has had nearly twenty minutes in which to address
them, which gives the opportunity of presenting a more practical statement than is possible
in a shorter time.
The places visited are Syston, Anstey, Mountsorrel, Sileby, and as already stated,
Derby. In some cases the spirit of the operatives is very good, but in others it is of another
kind, and the task is greater in consequence. The conditions in some cases also add to
the difficulties of organising. The presence of other industries such as hosiery give the
women an economic independence which is of great value to them, and girls pass into the
trade and obtain our rates of wages without any real effort on their part, and many of them
appear to have no idea that the Union has done anything in fixing these rates.
At the meetings and in conversation the work of the Union in obtaining the National
Terms of Settlement and the need of a strong union to maintain same has been emphasised,
and we think the result will be some new members, though their minds move somewhat
slowly, and their actions may be similar. But as hope has to be a strong feature in the
make-up of a trade union organiser, we view the situation with confidence.
The effort, however, must be a sustained one in these areas if good results are to follow,
and the Union obtain the control that is essential if our conditions are to be permanently
secured. I believe it can be done, but new conditions require new methods, and it may be
that the Union will have to review the situation in this spirit.
The situation at Derby is perhaps not so hopeful from the point of view of member-
ship, though here again the meetings at the factories were very good, both from the point
of view of attendance and attention given to what was said. At all these meetings literature
has been distributed, in which our conditions are fully explained, and the obligation of
the operative to the Union duly emphasised.
The situation at Derby has not been helped by the action of the late Secretary, and
the members express themselves very strongly on the subject. A case like this makes
one doubt whether a generous attitude is on the whole the best for the interest of the Union.
In this case our members are firmly convinced it has been harmful to the branch.
There are other features that want consideration before we can proceed here with
any confidence, though one cannot help thinking that some of these places drift too far
before we deal with them.
20th June, 1925.
WALTER R. SMITH.

MR. LLOYD GEORGE ON UNEMPLOYMENT.

When the Unemployment Bill was introduced by the Labour Party on the 22nd
May, 1925, Mr. D. Lloyd George, M.P., made, amongst other statements, the following :-
There is one survey which I should like to see undertaken; it is a very difficult one
to make, and that is a survey of the extent to which we can hope in future to provide
employment for our people out of our export and foreign business. Are the Government
quite convinced that in future, looking at what is going to happen, that we can depend
to the same extent upon our foreign business as we have in the past. We have depended
upon foreign trade more than any other country in the world. There is no country in the
world that has, I will not say entirely, but to such an extent depended for the very life
of its population upon trading with countries across the seas. It is a very perilous position
to be in. That is one of the questions that ought to be examined the extent to which
in future we can hope to depend for 100 per cent. employment upon our foreign trade.
If we cannot, then we must look to our resources at home, and to our resources in the
Empire, as my hon. Friend very well stated in his speech. There is no country in Europe
with a soil like ours which has so small a proportion of people settled on the land. I am
not speaking of Russia and the countries which are not highly developed, but I am speaking
of Western Europe. There is no country in Western Europe which yields as little from
the soil. We are paying £375,000,000 every year for produce which this country is capable
of producing. In addition, we are paying £60,000,000 every year for timber which might
be produced here. I do not say that the whole of that produce, but a very large proportion
of it, could be produced here, if you would only examine what is done in other countries,
where the condition of the peasantry is very much better than it is in this country. Take
the question raised so effectively the other day, the question of afforestation. That is
a question of vast moment in regard to the produce of this country and the exchanges of
this country. We are spending £60,000,000 every year upon timber, a very considerable
proportion of which could have been produced here.
[It is remarkable what statements can be made when not in office. When the oppor-
tunity was within the power of the older parties, the carrying out of these reforms-which
would have been beneficial to the workers-were constantly shelved.-F.L.P., G.S.].

and no one was sorrier than he himself in not accomplishing more for their

Agreements or to take up some thorny question which had caused trouble
His transparent honesty prevented him from deluding his conscience or his

of trade, the distinctive factory, such as they exist in Scotland, called for treat

was ever anxious to have them rightly understood as a sphere of Union labour,
and we must ever feel grateful to him for his efforts. His last years were

member for whom he had a warm side, namely the Repairer. His work on
the Trade Board makes it easier for the one who follows, and although it was,
and will be for some time to come, a heartbreaking struggle, still he was always
optimistic of what might be achieved. Although so earnest in his work for the
Union, he was an ideal companion in his leisure. His sunny disposition made
it easy for him to blend with any company, and his presence was always accept-
able where happy recreation was desired. We regret his economic circumstances
caused him such great anxiety latterly, and the pity is that such worry may
have hastened the end. It was a sad shock to hear of his death, and all his
colleagues of this branch mourn the passing of a brave spirit and a sincere
friend to the rank and file of the trade. We are glad his home town branch
has already started a fund for his people, and we hope it will meet with great
success. While his worth is still vivid in the minds of all the members it should
be possible to raise a sum worthy of the object, and a great tribute to a champion
who has secured a good place in the history of the Union.

DUNDEE.-" He's gane; he's gane. He's frae us torn." The announce-
ment of the sudden and tragic death of our Council Member, Christopher
Bleakley, created a profound and deep feeling of sadness in many of the members
of this branch. 'Tis but of recent date we heard his cheery voice and ringing
laughter; and now how painfully sad the thought, these are stilled for ever.
Our friend, Chris., was, in a sense, a man of outstanding characteristics. None
could question his sincerity in the manner in which he espoused the cause of
labour. His determination of what he took in hand to successfully carry
through. A man of a lovable disposition. Rarely if ever offending. Keen to
resent an injury. A large and generous heart, with always an open hand when
occasion arose to assist. The members of the Scottish branches will miss him
very much, as he was most assiduous in looking after their interests, as a member
of our general executive. We shall miss him very much. His death has created
a vacancy it will be difficult to fill. "Now the labourer's task is o'er," and our
hearts go out to the widow and family.
EDINBURGH.- Our Repairing Section has been very busy during the
month. Mrs. Bleakley and family wish us to thank all the branches and
shopmates for their kind expressions of sympathy in their sad bereavement.
The Edinburgh Branch will certainly miss his counsel, he having been an active
member of the Branch for over thirty years. He was always trying his best
to better the working conditions of not only the shopmates, but the general
body of workers all over. His place will be difficult to fill in the Union. May
we all endeavour to follow his example. Whatever the consequences, "Right
was always might," however much the sacrifice.
GLASGOW.-Trade has somewhat slowed down here this month, a
considerable number of our members losing time, and the outlook is discouraging
owing to the lack of orders for Shipbuilders and Engineers in the Clyde Valley.
It is with sorrow we lament the great loss to the Scottish branches, by the
death of our esteemed Council Member, Chris. Bleakley. Its suddenness one
feels as a great shock as only a day or two previous we were engaged with him
in the interest of our Union, and in fact it must be said he " died in harness."
His place will be hard to fill. We wish to express our heartfelt sympathy to
his wife and family at this time of their great loss and bereavement.
GLASGOW No. 2. -Trade is very quiet at present, some of our members
having a considerable period of suspension. We regret the passing hence of
our Council Member, Chris. Bleakley, to whose memory the usual token of
respect was paid at our Branch Meeting. To those of us who came into personal
touch with him we feel the members have lost one whose interests were theirs
all the time. To Mrs. Bleakley and family we extend our deepest sympathy.
HIGHAM AND RUSHDEN.-Trade during the month has become
considerably worse. Rushden very poor. Wollaston more unemployed.
Finedon three firms closed, 120 or more out. Irthlingborough fair, but not so
good as last month. Irchester whole of clickers at one firm finished or trans-
ferred to Rushden firm. Bozeat more hopeful. At an interview with Mr. F.

would be starting in the middle of Whit-week or week after. On June 8th we
find most of the operatives have returned to work. Raunds is improved in
ordinary Army and Navy work. Stanwick number standing off. During the
month Messrs. John Cave & Sons, Rushden, provided and opened bowling greens
and tennis courts for employees of that firm. President attended as member
of Rushden U.D.C., and Secretary represented the Union. A rest-room has
also been provided for female employees. As Secretary remarked at opening
ceremony, " it is hoped that it will set an example to other employers," that
the workers must cease to be spoken of as "hands" but as human beings,

LIVERPOOL. During the past month the Boot Repairing trade has been
very good. To the stereotyped query "any complaints," the reply is in the
negative. We note in the May Report the General President's remarks re the
Trade Board. We can hardly realise from a brief report what the specific
trouble is, but we know what it is to be fed up and feel like throwing up the
sponge. We hope he does not contemplate sacrificing "bottom dog" on the
altar of prestige and dignity. Better to worship at the shrine of Bacchus and
acquire "that fellow feeling which makes all the world akin."
Man and His Shoes.
How much a man is like his shoes!
For instance both a sole may lose;
Both have been tanned; both are made tight
By cobblers; both get left and right;
Both need a mate to be complete,
And both are made to go with feet.
With shoes the last is first; with man
The first shall be the last; and when
The shoes wear out they're mended new;
When men wear out they're men dead, too.
They both need healing, oft are sold,
And both in time turn all to mould.
They both are trod upon and both
Will tread on others, nothing loth.
Both have their ties, and both incline
When polished, in the world to shine.
They both peg out. Now, would you choose
To be a man or be his shoes?
-Shoe Trades Journal, 29th, May 1925.
LONDON METRO. Trade here is still very bad and there does not
appear to be any prospect of it improving for some time to come. A joint
mass meeting of the London Metro. and London Sew-Round Branches was
held at the Lansdowne Club, on 21st May, when the General President (Mr.
T. F. Richards) presided for the purpose of putting before the members the
advantages of indoor working to both Louis heel and Sew-Round makers. It
was a poorly attended meeting, but a resolution that negotiations be opened
for the provision of indoor workshops for all Louis heel and Sew-Round makers
was carried with but four dissentients. At one time it was the foreign element
who were the sinners as regards to taking large quantities of work out and
employing several hands in their own homes, but now unfortunately our own
people are doing likewise. Mr. T. F. Richards, General President, in the May
Report, in his wisdom attacks the London Clickers' Statement. Well, when
the responsible employers themselves have agreed to a list of prices to be paid
for cutting, it seems a bad policy to tell them that they are paying too much.

the cheap shops, can dictate through their cut-throat policy the line any

LONDON SOUTH. We are very pleased to announce that we have had
very good results from the visit paid by Mr. Len. Smith, Organiser, who did

MAYBOLE. - Trade is much improved, nearly all our members having been
restarted. We are also pleased to report sickness light. It is with extreme
sorrow and regret that we heard of the sudden death of our colleague and friend,
Chris. Bleakley. We are sure this is a blow to all members of the Union,
especially the Scottish members. Mr. Bleakley was a staunch friend of all
workers, and especially the workers in the boot and shoe trade. We shall all
miss him, but none like Mrs. Bleakley and family, to whom we tender our
heartfelt sympathy in their great loss of one who was a devoted husband and
father. A man among men.

NOTTINGHAM. - There is no improvement in trade here. Quite a num-
ber of our members are only working on alternate days. We have to report
the death of H. Mais, one of our old members. He has been a member of the
Union almost since it started, and has been in this branch over 40 years. The
Secretary and some of his old shopmates attended the funeral as a mark of
respect.

PLYMOUTH.Trade has been bad during the month and our Out-of-Work
figures have not been so high since 1920-21. Some of our men, discontented
drawing unemployment pay and doing nothing, have commenced business on
their own account, and as a Branch we wish them good luck. We ask for a
little more consideration and forethought when discharges are contemplated,
for we do not see where employers are advantaged by forcing into the already
overcrowded market skilled men who will act as their competitors. This is
what invariably happens with an active intelligent worker; having led an active
life and formed the habit of industry, he begins to tire after a few weeks holiday.
He gets anxious for the future and looks about for a little shanty to open up
on his own account. This is not an easy task, and many a night finds him foot-
sore and despondent after a weary search, but with perseverance and a little
luck a place is found and with courage, hope, fear and uncertainty intermingled,
he pays his first week's rent, and buys a patching machine. After this every
nerve is strained and every sail is set to win the prize, "success." Sometimes
a cut is made on the prevailing Price List. Be this right or wrong the man has
to live under a system of competition and get a foothold as a tradesman, but
who is responsible ? We suggest a little more consideration and forethought.
Our men generally prefer to work short time rather than standing in the ranks
of the unemployed, but many employers argue that their men are members
of the Union, and with the additional State benefit no harm will be done by
discharging, but discharges in the boot repairing trade acts in the form of a
boomerang, and requires considerable dexterity in handling; sometimes the
thrower is hit, and a knock-out blow administered in this fashion does not
excite much sympathy. We hear with deep regret of the sudden death of Mr.
C. Bleakley, and join with the Council in their expressions of sorrow and appre-
ciation of his sterling value and worth.

STAFFORD. There is an improvement in this centre, and we are hoping
for a retention of same.
Our members have suffered a long period of depression.
WATERFORD. Trade continues good.
GREAT YARMOUTH. Sorry to still have to report trade very slow.

HOW GREAT BRITAIN HINDERS RATIFICATION
OF THE HOURS OF CONVENTION.
At the present session of the International Labour Office, the chair was taken by
MR. E. L. POULTON, J.P., O.B.E., General Secretary of the National Union of Boot and
Shoe Operatives, and the workers' representative on the I.L.O., when the question of
the Ratification of the Hours of Convention was under discussion.
MR. CULLEN, a representative of the Irish Free State, said :—
Perhaps the most important of the Conventions yet adopted is the Washington
Hours Convention, and, speaking from this tribune last year, my adviser ventured to
Our General Secretary, Mr. E. L. POULTON, J.P., presiding at the
League of Nations International Labour Office, Seventh Session,
Geneva, 30th May, 1925.
express the fear that the statement then made by the British Government Representative
could not be accepted as an indication of the early ratification of the Hours Convention
by Great Britain. That fear has been realised. The other day the distinguished Repre-
sentative of the French Government suggested that the workers of the world might begin
to think that the many delays and endless discussions about ratification were only a screen
facts? The position is that ratification by Great Britain would undoubtedly result in
for a determination not to ratify. What other opinion can the workers draw from the
and our workers are determined to do their best to secure further ratifications regardless
say that, although we have brought our list of ratifications up to a substantial number,
ratification by a large number of States. Certainly, speaking for my own country, I can
of what may be done by other countries, it would nevertheless be much easier to secure
ratification by Ireland if Great Britain were known to be prepared to ratify without any
room for doubt. At present there is grave reason for doubting the intentions of the
British Government.


---
Two difficulties were mentioned here by the British Representative; the five-day
week and the special conditions of railwaymen. May I say frankly that I cannot under-
stand how anyone who has carefully studied the full text of the Hours Convention, and
particularly Article 5, could imagine that its stipulations would militate against the adoption
of either the five-day week or the maintenance of the railwaymen's agreement. Certainly,
we have not seen-and I do not think the Irish railwaymen, although our railway workers
belong to the same Union as their railway comrades in Great Britain have seen-any
precise statement of what the alleged difficulty really is. I do not like to seem to criticise
sincerest friendship for British workers that we venture to ask them and their Government
Great Britain: we have had occasion enough for that in the past; and it is out of the
to approach this question, not merely in a spirit of vague goodwill and of pious hopes for
Much more hangs upon this than the welfare of British workers. A
the future, but with a resolute determination to secure ratification without further delay.
fundamental social reform for the benefit of millions of workers all over
Europe depends upon the British ratification, and even from the point of
view of Britain herself it is important, because it is perhaps not too
much to say that the leadership and prestige in social reform and indus-
trial progress which Great Britain undoubtedly held in the XIXth century,
and well into the XXth century, will be lost completely if the Hours
Convention is not ratified by that country.
M. SCHURCH (Switzerland) followed, and during the course of his remarks, said :-
They un-
favourable effect on Switzerland, from the point of view of international competition;
but public opinion prevailed and the results were most favourable for Swiss Industry.
Another attempt to go back on the eight-hour day in Switzerland was rejected by a decisive
majority after a referendum in February, 1924. This shows that public opinion in Switzer-
land is thoroughly favourable to the eight-hour day. The workers, however, consider
that exceptions to the present eight-hour day are granted somewhat too easily by the
Government in Switzerland, and they greatly regret the decision of that Government not
to ratify the Washington Hours Convention.
MR. ARAYA (Argentina) said :- No reproach can be levelled at the Argentine Republic
in this respect, so far as the state of her legislation is concerned. The average hours
worked are not excessive; indeed, after the establishment of the forty-eight hour week,
there has arisen a movement in favour of the forty-four hour week.
MR. O'RAHILLY (Irish Free State) said: It is well, however, to remember that this
involves the duty on our part of attempting seriously to think internationally and a readi-
ness to adjust our national legislation in the wider interests of the workers of the world-
a standpoint which, I must say, is not always evident in the Government discourses we
have been listening to here. A medley of individual national apologies does not make
an international conference, they merely make a multi-national meeting; and I venture
to express the hope that ultimately we shall not have these prolonged proceedings here,
but that we shall have a special commission to enquire into the reasons why such and
such a country has not ratified such and such a Convention. We want an objective,
business-like examination of those reasons and of the difficulties which countries have found,
and we cannot find this in the series of eloquent discourses in a general conference here.
I hope that we shall ultimately have a commission, especially if we adopt the system of
amendment.
For instance, I cannot understand what is the difficulty about railway workers which
prevents the ratification of the Hours Convention, and I venture to express the opinion
that no one else listening here can understand either. I trust that we shall in the near
future, as I have said, have a business-like separate commission for the examination of
such national difficulties and excuses.
MR. RIDDELL (Canada) said :- British Columbia has taken a forward step and has been
the first Province in the Dominion of Canada to accept the eight-hour day and to put it
into operation. I also made a thorough study of legislation, and the legislation in our
Provinces would enable them, with practically no amendments to that legislation, to accept
more than 50 per cent. of our Draft Conventions and Recommendations.
ITALY has recently ratified the agreement: states that until Great Britain does so
they will not enforce the same.
AUSTRIA, BELGIUM, and FRANCE are in a similar position.

Mr. E. L. POULTON ON FORTY-EIGHT HOURS WEEK.

On Monday, 25th May, 1925, speaking at the I.L.O., Geneva, MR. E. L. POULTON,
J.P. (British Empire) said: I wish to speak on the wider question of the lack of ratifica-
tions of the Conventions that have been passed in the various Conferences since 1919,
I beg to express, on behalf of all the workers, their sense of keen disappointment at the
fact that we have to meet here this morning and can record approximately 150 ratifications
only, when there ought to be between 800 and 900. This to the workers is a very great
disappointment. Their hopes in 1919 were very high; they kept those hopes at that
high altitude for some considerable time, but as the years pass and they find this lack of
ratifications, their hopes gradually begin to go down. We learn this morning that, whilst
the Director is able to say that some advance has been made, the great majority of the
Conventions nevertheless remain unratified by the countries assembled here, and I am
asked on behalf of my colleagues to appeal particularly to the Government Representatives
here, when they go back to their several countries, to do all that is possible to ensure
the putting into effect of these Conventions to which the workers attach so much import-
ance, having regard to the effect that they would have upon the lives of themselves, their
wives and their children.

If we refer to the Director's Report itself, we find that since 1921 there have been no
fresh Conventions passed. It cannot, therefore, be said that we are asking as workers
to go at an extraordinary pace in ratifying Conventions. It was said in the earlier days
that we were attempting too much. We were endeavouring to make the pace so fast as
workers that neither Governments nor employers could keep up with what we desired.
I have said from the tribune in past years that one of the main reasons for the desire of
the workers was the lack of labour legislation during the years that are now behind us and
for which we have to make up the lee-way, and I do hope that from this Conference we
shall go home determined to do what we can to ensure the putting into force of the rati-
fications as portrayed in the Director's Report itself.

In that connection I was very delighted to hear the Bulgarian Minister state that he
hoped Governments would give all the information possible to the Office and to this Con-
ference, as the Bulgarian Government was determined to do everything possible to ratify
all Conventions. I congratulate the Bulgarian Government on arriving at that decision
and I hope that other Governments will arrive at the same decision in relation to the
ratification of Conventions. The same Minister added, however, what was to me a very
important point and one which ought to go home to all the Delegates. He said: we
cannot however remain almost alone as regards the hours question. Now that is to us,
as workers, going to the vital principle, when a country which has ratified tells us that
she desires to ratify every Convention, but nevertheless has to make in the speech of one
of its Ministers of State such an important reservation as that to which I have just referred.
I trust before we have done with the Director's Report, we shall have many statements
from Government Representatives and from Employers' Representatives that they are
going to carry out the Preamble to Part VIII. of the Treaty of Versailles. Having made
that statement in regard to the general ratifications, or lack of ratifications, I now want
to come to two in particular, because these two stand out in the eyes of the workers as the
most important of all and upon which they have set their hearts for many years past.

We have heard this morning the statements of the Belgian and British Representatives.
If I understood aright the statement of the Belgian Minister, he said Belgium was a small
country but was also very desirous of going forward. It could not go forward, however,
without the help of other countries, and therefore Belgium would ratify Conventions as fast
as other countries did so. Mr. Betterton, on behalf of the British Government, made a state-
ment with which personally I was very disappointed-not for what he said, but for what he
omitted to tell the Conference. I was hoping that Mr. Betterton would have been able
read from Hansard, That statement, while not altogether disappointing, is not one on
had gone
cannot find a way out of the difficulties with which they are surrounded, so that they may
be enabled to ratify the Hours Convention. I hope that as a result more ratifications will
ment of the Minister of Labour made in the British House of Commons, which Mr. Betterton
be secured.
Mr. Betterton stated that one of the great difficulties in Great Britain was that certain
operations in regard to trade and industry would make it difficult for the British Govern-
ment to ratify the Convention. I wish to say, in language which I hope will not be mis-
understood, that there are Articles in the Convention itself which will enable the British
or any other Government, if they have the necessary desire and goodwill to put the Hours
Convention into force, to deal with those difficulties. Articles 4, 5 and 6 of the Convention,
as I have told the Representatives of my own Government time after time, will enable
those difficulties to be surmounted. I trust we shall not hear in future that Governments
are refusing to carry out the Convention because of such difficulties.
---

---
Every country is bound to have some difficulty in carrying out a Convention which
has been passed here in the international spirit, but it is for each country to bring its laws
into conformity with the Conventions which are adopted here in these Conferences, and
which we believe to be in the highest interests of all.
"Belgian legislation on the eight-hour
I want to refer to one or two of the statements with regard to this matter which are
printed in the Director's Report. It states:
eight-hour day its interest clearly requires that it should do all in its power to bring it
day goes beyond what is required by the Washington Convention. As Belgium has an
about that its competitors in the world markets should also accept the same system,"
That was said by a Representative of the Belgian Government. At the top of another page,
with reference to France, the Report states:
the Bill "that is, a Bill which was to be brought into the French Senate" it is indicated
that the Act of 23rd April, 1919, on the eight-hour day in its main lines fits in with the
provisions of the Conventions."
"In the statement of reasons which heads
I want to add to that in Great Britain we have a day of eight hours or
less for millions of workers in force by voluntary agreement, and for
millions of other workers-miners and those under what we call the
Trade Boards by law, and by what is equivalent to the law of the land,
an Order in Council. That also applies to railways.
In the case of three great countries, therefore, one has a law which it says is better
than the Convention, another a law which in its main lines fits in with the Convention,
and the case of the third I have just mentioned. Despite this, they do not ratify the
Convention. Why? Why, if in their main lines their laws conform, do they not ratify
the Convention? Why, if in some respects their laws are better, and if in another case
millions are already working a day of eight hours or less, do they not ratify? Surely
the least they can do to show their good faith is to ratify, so that the workers can see one
more of their aspirations embodied in Acts of Parliament, so that their hours of labour
may be safeguarded against any unscrupulous employer who may attempt to make them
work longer hours. That is what the workers are afraid of; that is what they fear. Such
an action on the part of unscrupulous employers would precipitate a great industrial
struggle, and I submit that at this time of day we ought not to be compelled to resort to
industrial struggles merely to maintain an eight-hour day or a forty-eight hour week.
The second question I should like specially to stress is that of white lead. Those
who have been to the Conference for a few years will know that we had a battle royal one
with the other. Day after day, in Committee, we were fighting one against the other,
but ultimately we came to an agreement. Here is an industry which is affected very seri-
ously so far as the rights of the workers are concerned, by the use of this white lead, and
yet, if you look at the Director's Report, you will find that, of the 56 nations which are
connected with us in this great International Conference only five have ratified the Hours
Convention and only seven the White Lead Convention. I submit that that is eminently
unsatisfactory. When we raise the hopes of the workers as we do here, and when the
eyes of the workers are concentrated upon this great International Conference, they
expect that when we pass resolutions after serious and long discussion, we are going to
honour our word and carry out our bond when we get back to our own countries. I there-
fore hope that our appeal as workers will not fall on deaf ears. It has been suggested
to me already that this is not a place for propaganda. I submit that it is a place for
propaganda-for the propaganda of great humanitarian views and great humanitarian
ideals as expressed in these Conventions which we pass from time to time, and it is not
an unreasonable request which we as workers make, that when we assemble in 1926 we
may be able to read with pleasure and gratification that these Conventions-particularly
the Hours and White Lead Conventions-have not only been ratified, but put into working
order in the countries which are associated with us.
I.L.O. GOVERNING BODY.
Mr. E. L. Poulton, J.P., again Elected at Geneva.
GENEVA, Monday. -Mr. E. L. Poulton, of the British Trades Union Congress General
Council, was at the International Labour Conference again selected by the Workers'
Group as a member of the Governing Body, receiving, as on the previous occasion, the
highest number of votes.
The Convention dealing with equality of treatment for all workers in a country,
native or foreign, as regards compensation for industrial accidents, was passed by the
Conference, with 125 votes cast for and none against the measure. That such a large
number of votes should be given for a Convention without opposition is very unusual,
and Mr. Poulton is to be congratulated on the success which attended his efforts.
carried into effect, would limit the autonomy of the Groups, especially the Workers'
In the Standing Orders Committee proposals have been made from Italy which, if
Group. These proposals are strenuously opposed by Mr. Poulton and other Labour
representatives. —Daily Herald, 9th June, 1925.

After examining this new text, the Selection Committee has decided to submit to the
Conference the following Draft Resolution :-
Whereas the Labour part of the Treaties of Peace provides not only for the protection
of industrial workers, but also for the protection of agricultural workers, and whereas
the international Court of Justice has confirmed this intention of the Treaties;
Whereas by virtue of this decision, and of the general principles of the Labour Part,
rural workers must be accurately represented in the various bodies of the International
Labour Organisation;
And whereas the special nature of agricultural labour and the great variety of cate-
gories of agricultural workers in every country render the question of their organisation
and representation especially difficult and delicate, and whereas it is important to have
full knowledge of the facts in order to avoid possible misunderstandings or even disputes;
The Conference requests the Governing Body to instruct the International Labour
Office to prepare and submit as early as possible a report showing what are the various
categories of agricultural workers in every country, and also the principles upon which
they are organised and where necessary represented both in their national institutions
and in the International Labour Organisation.
For, 100; Against, o.
Asiatic Labour.
Draft Resolution concerning an enquiry into conditions of labour in Asiatic countries,
submitted by Mr. Joshi, Indian Workers' Delegate.
The Selection Committee, after a preliminary examination of the Draft Resolution
concerning an enquiry into conditions of labour in Asiatic countries, submitted by Mr.
Joshi, Indian Workers' Delegate, the text of which was published in Provisional Record
No. 1, has received a revised text, darfted in agreement with Mr. Joshi.
After examining this new text, the Selection Committee has decided to submit to the
conference the following draft resolution:—
The Seventh Session of the International Labour Conference expressed the hope that
the International Labour Office will continue to collect and publish all available informa-
tion regarding the conditions of labour in Asiatic countries; and also requests the Governing
Body of the International Labour Office, in consultation with the Governments concerned,
to undertake, as far as possible, a documentary inquiry into the conditions of labour in
those countries, more especially in China, India, Japan, Persia and Siam, and in the
colonies, protectorates and mandated territories in Asia.
For, 91; Against, o.
Mining.
The Selection Committee, after a preliminary examination of the Draft Resolution
concerning the standardisation of working conditions of miners, submitted by Mr. Mertens,
Belgian Workers' Delegate, the text of which was published in Provisional Record No. 1,
has received a revised text, drafted in agreement with Mr. Mertens. After examining
the new text, the Selection Committee has decided to submit to the conference the following
draft resolution :-
Considering that the Committee of the International Miners' Federation at a meeting
held in Brussels, on the 28th April, 1925, adopted the following resolution:
Whereas in certain coal-producing countries economic difficulties are such as to lead
to continually increasing unemployment among miners and the lowering of their standard
of living;
Whereas these economic difficulties arise very largely from competition and com-
mercial rivalry in the international coal market;
Whereas this competition and commercial rivalry is more acute and embittered by
reason of the differences in the working conditions existing in the principal coal-producing
countries;
The Committee decides that efforts should be made to secure the standardisation of
working conditions of miners on an international basis;
Since the Committee desires to be fully informed before it formulates definite and
concrete proposals it decides to organise, in agreement with the National Miners' Federa-
tions, an international investigation in the principal coal-producing countries. This
investigation shall deal with hours of work, annual holidays and wages paid by employers
to the various categories of working miners;
In order that the proposed investigation should be as thorough as possible and that
the enquiry may be completed with the least possible delay, the Committee also decides
to appeal to the International Labour Office for assistance and the co-operation of the
means of investigation at its idsposal.
For, 87; Against, o.

LABOUR WOMEN'S CONFERENCE.

DELEGATES' REPORTS.

I attended the Labour Conference of Women only, held at Birmingham, 27th and
28th May, 1925. Over eight hundred women delegates were present, from all parts, to
voice their opinions and pass resolutions.

The Chairman, Miss E. Wilkinson, M.P., gave an interesting opening speech, and
spoke of points in the Budget, with special interest to women.

The Conference listened with great attention and interest. Resolutions were passed
on Housing, Emigration, Education, and the World's Food Supplies.

A special report by the Standing Joint Committee of Women's Organisations, on
Bread and Socialism, which was meant to be instructive and educational to all women
present, was very fully discussed and digested.

The Conference each year improves, both in the standard of mentality and procedure.
When the Trade Union leaders of this country are as much alive to the organisation of
women into our Unions as are the Labour Party in enrolling members into the Labour
Party, then we may expect a Conference of Trade Union Women as large, and our trade
union membership of women very much increased.
M. J. BELL-RICHARDS.

The National Conference of Labour Women, held at Birmingham, 26th and 27th
May, 1925. There were 860 delegates present from all parts of the country, presided over
by Miss Ellen Wilkinson, M.P.

The delegates present representing were: Women's Sections Labour Party, 731;
Trade Unions, 52; Co-operative Guilds, 17; Fabian Society, 7; Independent Labour
Party, 30; Railway Women's Guild, 23.

The Conference opened with an address from Miss Wilkinson, who remarked that we
had met under a rich man's Budget, and stated the injustices of the same.

Dr. Marion Phillips presented the report of the Labour Party in the women's interests
at home and abroad.

Resolutions were passed demanding a non-contributory scheme for Widows' Pensions,
the speeding up of building houses under the Wheatley Act, also the Labour Socialist
International. To press for the institution of an annual international conference of women
delegates. Other subjects considered were: Health in the Elementary Schools, Price of
Bread, the Purity and Cleanliness of Food Supplies.

Mrs. Harrison Bell, the Labour representative on the Overseas Settlement, spoke on
Emigration. This resolution created a lively debate.

Other resolutions passed dealt with Internationalism, Armaments and Peace, Treat-
ment of workers under Unemployment Insurance Acts. The strengthening of Trade
Union Movement amongst women workers by better organisation. Birth Control also
came in for a long debate, several speaking for and against.

The Conference, I thought, was very interesting, educatively and politically.

(MRS.) L. H. GUMSLEY.

Over one thousand delegates attended the Labour Women's Conference, held in the
Town Hall, Birmingham, on 27th and 28th May, 1925, and were welcomed by Mrs.
Councillor Howse.

MILLIONAIRES’ BUDGET. - It was reported the Budget would relieve a quite moderate
millionaire to the extent of £40 a week in super-tax, some would be richer by thousands
a year, but the relief to small earned incomes was counted in shillings.

Labour's idea of Mothers' Pensions was to allow the mother to make a home for her
children, and to take her, if she so desired, out of the labour market. The pensions under
the Budget scheme were utterly inadequate. The widow with two children would receive
18s., with three children only 21s. This, compared with the Army pensions for a widow
of a Private with two children 44s., and with three children 50s.

UNEMPLOYMENT. - Referring to sneers about workers learning to qualify for insurance,
Miss Wilkinson declared that was no doubt to agree to the turning down masses of un-
employed on the ground that they were shirkers, and the women should make a strong
stand in their own homes against such tactics.

BIRTH CONTROL. - The resolution advocated that it should be permissible for doctors
employed in any public funds to give information on Birth Control to those who desire it.
The debate was of a lively character. Mrs. Laski, speaking in favour, said capitalists did
not want working women to have scientific information about Birth Control, as they would
