TRADE UNIONS. A resolution calling for the strengthening of the Trade Union
Movement amongst women by better organisation was passed unanimously.
WOMEN LABOUR CANDIDATES. There was much applause for the motion by the
delegate for West Willesden, that the Conference call upon members of women's sections
immediately to levy themselves a small weekly sum as a political fund for the express
purpose of running working women candidates for Parliament.
JUSTICE. Judicial and penal questions were dealt with in the form of a composite
resolution which included a request to magistrates not to allow corporal punishment
for children, and urged that probation should be used to the utmost, especially for offenders
under 21.
Other resolutions protested against the harsh treatment of women workers under
the UNEMPLOYMENT INSURANCE ACTS, and for the ESTABLISHMENT OF HOMES OF REST
for persons suffering from nervous breakdowns or mentally deranged.
It was decided the Labour Party deal stringently with any representative of Labour
who would not support sex equality economically, educatively and politically.
The resolution further declared that no proposals for equality of suffrage between
men and women will be acceptable which is based on raising the age for men voters and
reducing the age of women to 25. The resolution was carried.
WIDOWS' PENSIONS.-The Government's scheme next came in for vigorous denuncia-
tion. Mrs. Dollan (Glasgow) moved a long resolution protesting against the proposals
to establish Widows' Pensions on a contributory basis, and to transfer Old Age Pensions
to the same basis. Miss Evans, seconding, pleaded for the young widows with no children,
and Mrs. Holland (Swansea), who spoke as a widow with five children, denounced the
pensions scheme as a fraud. "The only time the State recognised me in fifteen years,"
she said, "was when Conscription was brought in and they took two of my sons and
returned one to me disabled for life. The Poor Law is infinitely superior to this scheme."
The resolution was carried.
The resumed Conference of Labour Women at Birmingham opened on a note of
sadness. Miss Ellen Wilkinson, presiding, announced that Mrs. Holland, of Blackpool,
one of the most ardent Labour women in Lancashire, who was to have attended as a
delegate, had died suddenly of heart failure on the eve of her departure for the Conference.
Delegates expressed their sorrow by standing in silence.
LAND POLICY.-It was distressing to learn the Labour Party had not given a clear
lead on the land question.
Miss Heagney, Fraternal Delegate from Victoria, conveyed greetings from the Labour
Union of Australia, and stirred delegates with a glowing account of what the Labour
Government had done. They have pensions of 17s. a week for women at 60, and there
is a maternity allowance of £5 for all women- these on a contributory basis.
A. SMALLEY,
Delegate.
I attended the National Conference of Labour Women held at the Town Hall, Bir-
mingham, on 27th and 28th May, where over 860 delegates were present. Miss Ellen
Wilkinson, M.P., presided, supported by Dr. Marion Phillips, and others.
After being cordially welcomed by Mrs. Howse, President of Birmingham Labour
Women's Council, and Mr. Ager, of the Birmingham Labour Party, letters of greeting
were read from various bodies.
Resolutions dealing with Widows and Old Age Pensions, Housing, Health in our
Schools, Emigration to British Dominions, Internationalism, Armaments and Peace,
and Birth Control, were put before the Conference for discussion.
Mrs. Dolan (Glasgow) moved a resolution denouncing the proposal of the present
Government to establish Widows' Pensions on a contributory basis and transfer Old Age
Pensions to such a basis. It also condemned the inadequacy of allowances for widows
with children, and the proposal to make the workers pay contributions as enough was
already extracted from the workers. It also pointed out that Old Age Pensions at 65
were inadequate to meet the present day hardships, and it also urged that people incapaci-
tated before that age and unable to work should be taken into consideration, and advocated
the pension at an earlier age. The resolution was carried unanimously.
Miss D. Jewson put to the Conference a Resolution dealing with Housing, urging
that pressure should be brought to bear on Local Authorities to compel them to use every


---
opportunity afforded them by the Wheatley Act to make progress in building houses for
people before big profits. After some discussion the resolution was carried.
Emigration to British Dominions, seconded by Miss Bondfield, to the effect that family
A great deal of discussion took place after Mrs. Harrison Bell moved a resolution on
emigration was the only satisfactory form of emigration put into operation in co-operation
and an Amendment agreed to that further investigation be made by the Standing Joint
with the Dominion of Canada Land Settlement Scheme. The Resolution was defeated
Committee and a further report be made. Many delegates denounced emigration as no
cure for unemployment, and that we should colonise our own country, and not until every
acre of land was cultivated should we think of emigration. Miss Jessie Stevens, after a
recent experience of conditions in Canada, very much condemned emigration, as she declared
things were quite as bad there with regard to unemployment as here.
Yours faithfully,
V. HOWARD,
Delegate.
The Conference opened with Miss Ellen Wilkinson, M.P., in the chair. There was
a splended attendance of delegates, and a striking feature of the Conference was the serious
manner with which the women followed the business during the whole of the time.
Several of the speakers appealed to the gathering to push forward the organisation
of women into their trade unions, and appealed to them to make the unions their Approved
Societies for Health Insurance.
Mrs. Hooper opened a discussion upon the question of a Labour Party Programme
for the taking over of the land for the people, and stated that she was in favour of con-
fiscation. Other delegates felt that we ought not to separate the Land question from
other things, and asked why we should not include property when discussing these matters
Mrs. Heagney, the fraternal delegate from Australia, was given a warm welcome by
the Conference. She stated that she brought greetings from the Labour Union of Australia,
and stirred the delegates by her account of what a Labour Government had done for the
people in Victoria. In that country, she said, women have Old Age Pensions of 17s. per
week at 60 years of age, and a Maternity allowance of five pounds on a non-contributory
basis.
Mrs. Harrison Bell moved a resolution, "That this Conference is of the opinion that
family migration is the only satisfactory form of migration, and views with sympathetic
interest the Land settlement scheme just being put into operation in co-operation with the
Dominion of Canada." She stated that ex-service men had been induced to leave this
country by misleading advertisements, and to invest their savings upon farms where there
was little hope of success, and the work of the Overseas Committee was to prevent this
happening. This was seconded by Miss Margaret Bondfield, who stated that while we agree
that it is necessary to settle our people upon the land in this country, we also ought to have
a scheme to help and protect those who were desirous of going abroad to try and make
good.
Important resolutions upon the subject of Health occupied a considerable time, and
it was during this discussion that one of the speakers urged the necessity of doctors being
permitted to give advice to married women who desired it, upon the subject of Birth
Control. One of the delegates, who evidently took strong exception to this matter being
discussed at the Conference, created quite an uproar, and it was only owing to firm handling
of the meeting by the Chairman that the discussion was able to be kept in proper order.
A very popular decision was one that Women's Sections throughout the country should
levy themselves weekly for the purpose of creating a political fund in order that working
women could be run as Parliamentary Candidates.
Resolutions dealing with War and Disarmament were carried, and an emergency
resolution protesting against the persecution of the workers in Bulgaria under the Tsankoff
terrorist government, was carried, including one that the British Labour movement should
press our Foreign Office to make the strongest representation to the Bulgarian Govern-
ment.
Space is not sufficient to deal with the whole of the matters discussed by the Con-
ference, but I feel that the interest shown and the ability with which the delegates dealt
with the various problems confronting the movement, speaks well for the future of the
Women's Labour Movement, and the early return of a second Labour Government with a
working majority.
K. BANNARD,
Northampton.

DISEASE-INFECTED HOVELS OF EARL SHILTON.
But District Council Vetoes Building Scheme.
Earl Shilton, if credit is to be given to a rumoured opinion of the county health
authorities, is not in need of any more houses. This unique distinction has been conveyed
to the township by way of the Hinckley Rural District Council, whose plan for a further
colony of 37 houses has not met with the approval of officialdom on account of the "lack
of need when compared with other districts. Eighty houses have been built in the town-
ship since the war, and the new scheme is for 150, 37 to be built in the boundaries of Earl
Shilton. It is understood that the county health officials' opinion is that the houses,
if built, would be occupied by natives of the district who would gladly change from the
village of their birth and upbringing for the chance of a house. That is a fact, but Earl
Shilton can fill these houses on its own, abundant proof of which was given to me in a tour
of the township to-day.
So bad is the shortage that the many couples who were married at Whitsun have
none of them the remotest hope of getting a house for a long time to come. The 80 Council
houses already erected could have been filled five times over, as in many other cases, and
the number of homeless naturally increases every day. The Council, the chairman of
which is Mr. Daniel Astley, J.P., is repeatedly implored to do everything possible for the
relief of the homeless, but in face of opposition from the authorities they are helpless.
These facts are similar in greater or less degree in hundreds of other cases up and
down the country. But a greater and more serious horror makes Earl Shilton district
the more gruesome an example. Earl Shilton is the most consumptive township in
England. The dread disease started many years ago, and has grown to appalling pro-
portions since the war, helped by the pitiful conditions in which the inhabitants have
to exist. In a recent inquiry, it was stated that out of 18 recent deaths only one was from
a cause other than tuberculosis. Yet Earl Shilton is powerless. In my tour, I saw three
dwelling-places, two of them with the taint of the disease, and all of them in such a state
as would be incredible to anyone who did not see them.
One cottage was inhabited by a one-legged South African war veteran and his wife.
Both were hollow-eyed and weak with consumption. The man was a living wreck. He
was living in a thatched cottage - one of a row. It originally consisted of four rooms,
two up and two on the ground floor. The top-rooms are uninhabitable, and open to the
sky in patches up to a square yard in dimension. The walls are bulging, and the sun
streams in through large splits where the roof has fallen away from the walls. The roof
is now in imminent danger of total collapse. The man and his wife sleep in the bed in the
living room, but when it is raining their bed is soaking wet from water that has come
through the roof and ceiling. Even downstairs the sun and the rain can pour through
the tops of the walls. The man pays 2s. 3d. a week for this.
Next door is a shoe clicker, his wife and four children. They, too, have been driven
from their upstairs rooms, and all sleep in one room. On the floor are tin baths to catch
the rain water. The woman told me that she would be willing to pay 6s. a week if she
could get a cottage fit for humans to live in. Here again they are living in imminent
danger of death from a total fall of the thatched roof. Not one single night in the year
are the sleeping children safe. In another cottage in the village lives an old woman who
has not been in a bed for a fortnight. There are great gaps in her roof, and in the recent
windy weather she was out in the street until one o'clock in the morning through fear of
her cottage tumbling about her ears. She cannot get the bed down the shaking stairs
and sleeps on a sofa. Her husband was consumptive.
There is no use in condemning these houses and evicting the tenants. They would
starve in the street. Until houses are built they will exist in the infected buildings while
they hold together. One of them collapsed completely a short time ago. The medical
men in the village have given up the struggle against the disease unless hope of more
houses is coming. There can be no fighting against it under present conditions. It will
remain a village of death until the District Council can start to build. They have the land
and they have the builders. Only officialdom " does not see the need." Meanwhile, the
green fields of Leicestershire surround a plague spot. - Leicester Mercury, 8th June, 1925.
[The Council have constantly bombarded the Ministry of Health (and Insurance
Commissioners) since 1913, but very little headway has been made. - E.L.P., G.S.].
GOSSIP.
Old Wolverhampton trade unionists, as well as a large circle of the general public,
will feel interested to know that I spent a pleasant time on 1st June, 1925, in the company
of four of the best known and most respected veterans of the Labour cause in this district,
including Mr. Harry Moreton, who entering on his 88th year, can look back with pride
on his many years' service as secretary of the Boot and Shoe Rivetters' Society, member
of the Board of Guardians, and first Labour representative on the old Wolverhampton
School Board. - Express and Star, Wolverhampton.

BOOT REPAIRING FIRM FINED.
Report of Proceedings.
TRADE.-Boot and Shoe Repairing.
FIRM.-Mrs. Muriel Taratooty, 93 Chippenham Road, Paddington.
DATE OF HEARING.-12th March, 1925.
CHARGES.-I. Against Mrs. Muriel Taratooty for the underpayment of five workers,
2. Against Mr. Nathan Taratooty, manager of the firm, for causing to
be made a false record of wages in respect of one of the workers.
CIRCUMSTANCES. As a result of visits paid to this firm at a second inspection there
appeared to be no doubt but that the workers were pieceworkers although the firm
attempted to show that they were timeworkers.
RESULT. The magistrate decided that the defendants had deliberately defied the
Trade Boards Acts. On the first charge he convicted and fined Mrs. Muriel Taratcoty
£2 or 21 days in respect of five summonses. Mr. Nathan Taratooty was convicted and
fined £10 in respect of the second charge. £5 5s. od. costs and arrears of wages amounting
to £27 5s. 11d. were ordered to be paid.

EXTRACT FROM MR. MACDONALD'S WEEKLY
LETTER TO THE "HERALD "-JUNE 13th, 1925.

1,250,000 Workless.
There are now close on one and a quarter million unemployed, nearly a quarter of a
million more than at this time last year, and 60,000 more than last week.
"Our great industries are languishing; over
there rests black depression and the threat of wage cuts.
"And what is the Government doing in the House of Commons? With its enormous
majority it is using the 'relay' system to suppress effective discussion on a Budget that
protects the rich, and on fiscal proposals that will further raise the cost of living, and offers
a quack remedy for Nottingham's ills.

"Tory dominance is in the saddle. Power is being used with a callous brutality to
break us physically or to compel us to cease our critical examination of the business before
us."
"The House of Commons," said Mr. MacDonald, "can be used or abused. We used
it last year; this year the Tory Government, with its servile majority and undemocratic
mind, is abusing it. This aggravates the problem, for in any case the pressure of work is
getting too great.

That is the
The remedy lies in Devolution-in taking a lot of work away from us.
only way to give us proper business hours and to make the Parliamentary machine function
properly.
"We should devolve greater responsibility on to municipal authorities, and enable
them to do much more work on their own initiative. They cannot do this now except by
coming here and promoting private Bills.

Relieving Congestion.
"If municipalities were given such powers, we should not have to deal so much with
general legislation, because good municipalities would carry out progressive programmes of
work in their own areas, or in conjunction with other authorities-they would get on with
their own job much more expeditiously; they would save themselves money and save the
House of Commons very valuable time."
"Would it not be an advantage if the House of Commons procedure was remodelled
somewhat on municipal lines?" Mr. MacDonald was asked.
"I have always failed to understand," he replied, "how that view is practical. The
difference is one between legislation and administration. On a city or borough council you
get your reports of work done or contemplated by the committees, and these reports are
generally dealt with very expeditiously and quickly, but it is all a record of administrative
work.
"When you come to initiating legislation it is a very different thing. In Parliament
you must have discussion; you must express views; you are divided by vast differences
of principle and interest. The simple unavoidable fact, for good or ill, is that the House
of Commons can never be a made municipal administrative council.
"I'm sure," he concluded, "that the reform of the House of Commons does not lie
in that direction; but it is quite certain that the passing into law of our Local Authorities
Enabling Bill would do a great deal to relieve the congestion."

CHANGES IN COST OF LIVING: STATISTICS FOR 2nd JUNE, 1925.
Summary: Average Increases since July, 1914.
All Items included ... ... ... 72 per cent.
Food only ... ... ... ... 66 "

FOOD.
At 2nd June the average level of retail prices of the principal articles of food showed
a slight decline as compared with that at 1st May. This decline was mainly attributable
to reductions in the prices of butter, milk and sugar.
As a net result of all the changes recorded, the average increase over the level of July,
1914, in the retail prices (in Great Britain and Northern Ireland) of the articles of food
included in these statistics was about 66 per cent. at 2nd June, as compared with about
67 per cent at 1st May and 60 per cent. at the corresponding date in 1924-The Ministry
of Labour Gazette, June, 1925.

COURT OF APPEAL

Awards Northampton Workman 1d. a Week. Interesting Case.
To-day the Court of Appeal, consisting of the Master of the Rolls and Lords Justices
Warrington and Atkin, remitted to the Northampton County Court Judge an application
by Wilson, Lovatt & Sons, builders, of Clarence Street, Wolverhampton, for review of
the weekly payments under the Workmen's Compensation Act to Tom Johnson, brick-
layer, of Green Street, Northampton.
In October, 1922, Johnson injured his right leg while working for Wilson, Lovatt &
Sons, at the Northampton Electric Power Station, Hardingstone, a stone mullion which
he was erecting slipping on to his right leg.
Under a recorded agreement he was paid 35s. a week until August, 1924, then the
employers applied for a review.
The County Court Judge held that the man's incapacity had ceased and made an
order for the termination of the weekly payments. Against this the workman appealed.
The Master of the Rolls, giving judgment, said the employers' application was for
review and diminution. There was no application for termination, therefore the County
Court Judge was not entitled to terminate the payments, though he might well have reduced
the payments to an insignificant amount.
The case would be sent back with a direction that the right award to make in the
circumstances was one penny per week. The Lords Justices concurred. Northampton
Daily Echo, 11th June, 1925.
[This proves that it is quite legal for Compensation to be revised, and to so reduce the
amount as to keep it "alive."-E.L.P., G.S.J.
NATIONAL SUMMER SCHOOL, RUSKIN COLLEGE, OXFORD.
August 15th to 29th, 1925.
Under the auspices of the Trades Union Congress and the Labour Party, a National
Summer School will be held at Oxford during the two weeks from Saturday, 15th August,
to Saturday, 29th August.
The School will be open to men and women who are members of organisations affiliated
to the Trades Union Congress or the Labour Party, and it is hoped that Trade Unions,
Trades Councils, local Labour Parties, Women's Sections, and Co-operative Societies and
Guilds will send members.
Oxford is centrally situated and easy of access. The city is full of interest and is an
admirable centre for a Summer School. The School will be held in Ruskin College, which
has been placed fully at the disposal of the two National Executives by the College Authori-
ties. A Common Room for members of the School will be provided at Ruskin College
and the College Library will be available.
As regards the general arrangements of the School, there will be lectures and dis-
cussions each morning and shorter "group" discussions after tea. There will be lectures
on the Political Outlook, the Industrial Outlook, Trade Union Organisation, Unemploy-
ment, Labour and the Commonwealth, Labour's Foreign Policy, Socialism and Industry,
Labour and National Finance, Social Insurance, &c.
The afternoons will be free for visits to places of interest such as Colleges and University
Institutions, the Bodleian Library, Museums, Galleries, &c., and for excursions and river
trips. The members of the School will be asked to co-operate in the evening arrange-
ments, which will be of a social character.
Members will be accepted for either the first or second week of the School or for the
full fortnight.
A registration fee of ros. per person is payable on application. This fee is for the
purpose of covering the expenses incurred by the Joint Research Department in organising
the School, &c.
The cost of board and lodging will be about £2 IOS. per week. The lodgings will be
lodging direct to their landladies. The weekly charges cover the period from Saturday
arranged by the Department, but the members of the School will pay for their board and
tea to the following Saturday breakfast, inclusive. A limited number of men will be
housed in Ruskin College.
It may be possible to arrange for reduced railway fares for those visiting the School-
Details of application for membership of the School may be made to The Secretary,
Joint Research and Information Department, 33 Eccleston Square, London, S.W.1, and
envelopes marked on the top left-hand corner "Summer School."

**THE NEXT WAR!!**

There is in Europe, as it exists to-day, almost every condition tending to war, except financial competence, and the lack of that we are supplying as far as we can by our failure to insist that the money now spent on armaments shall be applied to the satisfaction of our Continental debts. War over the Eastern frontiers, sooner or later, is not only possible but probable, and it is sheer folly to assume that it can be localised and that obligations entered into in the West will not be affected by events in the East.

Let us assume, however, that Germany does accept the French proposal, and that later she breaks her undertaking in the way provided against. France at once invades Germany, with Great Britain standing benevolently aside. France may win, in which case a grave problem would at once be created for this country, which cannot desire to see the Rhine a French river and Belgium practically a French dependency. Germany may win, and the threat is restored to get rid of which the British Empire sacrificed a million lives and ten thousand millions of treasure.

The people of this country must realise, before it is too late, that a Security Pact, whatever form it may take, is not a Pact of Peace but a Pact of War. **War on the Continent may be inevitable in any case. It will be, humanly speaking, ensured if we bind ourselves in advance to participate in it.**

[The above is an extract from the leading article in the London Evening Standard, 16th June, 1925, and is, in other words, a statement which we are constantly bringing to the notice of our members. Unless we are wide awake we shall find another war sprung upon us. It should be our determination to do all possible to render negative all the efforts made by the warmongers, and to strengthen and support the efforts made for peace and goodwill.-E.L.P., G.S.].

NATIONAL UNION OF BOOT AND SHOE OPERATIVES.
Branch for Dundalk,
At a meeting on Saturday to form a branch of above Union a number of men employed
by the Celt Ltd., the local boot factory, attended.
Mr. Geo. White, Belfast, was present.
Mr. Clarige (Chairman) said it was most essential that they have a union. The
National Union of Boot and Shoe Operatives was recognised all over the world. In one
place where he had been before the members joined it, the weekly output was 3,000 pairs
per week, at present it was turning out 10,000 pairs. The Union helped that factory to
increase its output. It would do the same for the Dundalk factory. The Union would
see that they got good wages and good working conditions as well as helping the industry,
There had been talk in town about scab labour in the factory. No such thing existed.
One could not expect that men would get the maximum or even a minimum wage when
learning the trade; learners must start on a small wage and realised that. When they
became more experienced they would qualify for a better wage. In some trades men had
to pay a fee to learn. He did not think they had been treated badly. Their employers
were very considerate, but they must always be working for better conditions, both for
themselves and for the industry.
Mr. White detailed the advantages of joining the Union.
Mr. Wall proposed that a branch be started. This was passed unanimously.
The election of officers resulted: -Chairman, Mr. Clarige, Secretary, Mr. Brett,
Treasurer, Mr. Kearney Committee, E. Wall, M. Walls, L. Maguire. Dundalk Times,
13th June, 1925.
INCREASING POPULARITY OF BOOTS & SHOES.
It is estimated that in the East Lancashire district alone there are now something
like 1,250 boot and shoe makers and repairers in business, which is 25 per cent. above
the average for pre-war days. The introduction of ex-soldiers into the trade has, of
course, added to the number, but the increase is mainly due to a change
of fashion. Clogs, which were formerly in vogue, are declining in
popularity on the part of textile workers, and with the decrease in the
number of cloggers there has been an appreciable increase in boot and
shoe repairers. Clogs are now a less distinctive characteristic of Lancashire life as
are shawls. Shoe Trades Journal, 29th May, 1925.
"STICKING TO THE LAST."
Shoemaker Brothers of 91 and 80 Years.
A family of six, all over 80, and two over go, live at Cassland Road, South Hackney.
Their aggregate ages total 525 years.
The "baby" of the family, William Hews, is 80 years and five months, and he and
his elder brother John, who is 91 years and three months, are engaged in making hand-
sewn boots, at which trade they have been for over 50 years.
June, 1925.
The other nonagenarian of the family is a sister, Mrs. Curtis. Lloyds News, 7th

INTERNATIONAL LABOUR OFFICE NEWS.
CHILD LABOUR IN CHINA.
Child Labour in Rugmaking in China.
The shops in which rugs are made in Peking are dark and overcrowded, with no
attempt at sanitation, and the workrooms serve also as dormitories for the workers. The
working hours are approximately those of daylight; only three shops in Peking are working
less than 12 hours a day.
Almost three-fourths of the employees in this industry, according to a special supple-
ment to the Chinese Social and Political Science Review, quoted in the Industrial and
Labour Information of the International Labour Office, are apprentices, who as a rule
receive their food and clothing and very little money, but no regular wages. The boys
are brought in from the country at the age of 11 or 12, and sometimes younger, to serve
under a contract for three years. During this time they are entirely under the control
of the shopowner, living in the shops in which they work and receiving no moral or educa-
tional training and no physical care. Large numbers of them contract tuberculosis and
other diseases.
The system is practically one of indentured child labour. Moreover, the apprentice-
ship is practically a" blind alley," and for most of the boys there is no future, as, when the
apprenticeship period is completed, other boys are brought in to take their places.
INTERNATIONAL LABOUR CONFERENCE, SEVENTH ANNUAL
SESSION, 1925.
Forty-six States.
The Seventh Annual Session of the International Labour Conference of the League
of Nations was held at Geneva from 19th May to 10th June, 1925, forty-six States being
represented. These included not only the important industrial countries of the world,
with the exception of the United States and Russia but including Germany, but also
many of the smaller and of the less advanced States. Among the distant countries were
Japan, China, Australia, India, South Africa, Canada, and the South American States.
Complete delegations consisting of two representatives of the Government and one each
of the employers and the workers were sent by all except ten, and these were mainly of
lesser industrial importance.
The President and Committees.
Dr. Benes, the Czecho-Slovakian statesman, was unanimously elected President
on the proposition of Mr. H. B. Betterton, M.P., Parliamentary Secretary to the Ministry
of Labour and senior British Government delegate. Speaking at the close of the Session,
Dr. Benes referred to the feeling that the International Labour Conference was a per-
manent institution with a definite place in modern international life, and added "it has
become a centre for the development of a wise international spirit, moderate, but at the
same time profound."
Following its custom, the Conference appointed six committees (the members of each
Committee being selected equally from each of the groups, Government, employers' and
workers'), to study the details of the questions before it and report to the full Conference.
Three "Second-Reading" Votes.
A provisional system of a "second reading" for Conference decisions had been adopted
at the 1924 Session. It had been felt that many Governments had been unable to ratify
Draft Conventions agreed upon by earlier Sessions by reason often of quite small points,
sometimes in the wording, and that, if they were given an opportunity of suggesting
amendments which would facilitate their own ratification before a final vote was taken,
the speed of adoption of international labour legislation would be increased. Provisional
decisions were therefore taken on three subjects last year and Governments given time
to forward such amendments before the present Conference.
Accident Compensation Equality for Aliens.
The first of these questions was equality of treatment of aliens regarding workers'
and accident compensation. After discussion in committee and in full Conference a
Draft Convention was adopted by 125 votes to none. By its provisions States who ratify
undertake to grant to the nationals of any other State which is a member of the Inter-
national Labour Organisation who suffer personal injury due to industrial accidents in
their territory the same treatment regarding workers' accident compensation as is given
to their own nationals. A Recommendation was also adopted by 128 votes to none on
to question of disputes arising out of the application of this system and on allied problems


---
Here is the extracted text:
Asiatic Labour Conditions.
361
The Conference, on the proposition of Mr. Joshi, the Indian workers' delegate,
instructed the International Labour Office to make a documentary inquiry into the con-
ditions of labour in Asiatic countries. A further resolution was adopted requiring the
Office to assist in the collection of information regarding the hours of work, wages and
holidays in the coal-mining industry throughout the world. This arose from a demand
made recently by the International Miners' Federation.
The "Two-Reading" System.
The "two-reading" system which had been provisionally adopted in 1924, as men-
tioned above, having resulted in the rejection of any international agreement on glass-
workers, and in the decision of the Conference to take the final votes on workers' co
pensation immediately, the Conference decided to remit a number of amendments which
had been proposed to it to the Governing Body which is to report on them to the 1926
Session.
A New Governing Body.
One of the duties of the Conference was the election of a new Governing Body of the
International Labour Office, which holds office for three years. Of the twelve Govern-
ments represented, the eight States of chief industrial importance, including Great Britain,
India and Canada, must be included. The Argentine, Spain, Norway and Poland were
elected to the other four places, the Argentine and Norway replacing Chile and Finland.
The six employers on the outgoing Governing Body were re-elected; they include Sir
James Lithgow, former president of the Shipbuilding Employers' Federation; and Mr.
W. Gemmill, of the Transvaal Chamber of Mines. The workers' group is also practically
the same and includes Mr. E. L. Poulton, Secretary of the National Union of Boot and Shoe
Operatives, and Mr. Tom Moore, President of the Canadian Trades and Labour Congress.
Ratifications of International Labour Conventions.
During the Session, 15 formal ratifications of International Labour Conventions
were announced, bringing the total to 161; the total in May, 1924, was 96.
It is noteworthy that the 15 announced during the Conference included the first
ratifications registered by France and by Germany; and the first by a South American
State (the Argentine Republic).
The total strength of the Soviet army is 1,058,000.

raisins, 24.4 per cent.; currants, 6.15 per cent.; dried and preserved plums, 5.36 per
cent.; figs and fig-cake, 0.01 per cent.
*
*
*
During the first three months of this year, the average royalty on each ton of coal
raised was 6+d.
*
*
*
Imports of wheat into Great Britain during 1924 were 117¼ million cwts., as compared
with 104 million cwts. in 1914; wheat meal and flour 11 million cwts., as compared with
10 million cwts.; and barley 21½ million cwts., as compared with 16 million cwts.
-From The Labour Press Service, 18th June, 1925.

Every precaution is taken by us to ensure accuracy in these Reports, our correspondents
being men on whom we can rely; should, however, any errors creep into them, upon the
same being pointed out to us, we shall be most happy to give the same prominence to the
correction as to the original matter. As these Reports are exclusively for the use of Members
the information contained therein must not be divulged.

MONTHLY REPORT.
27TH JULY, 1925.
FELLOW MEMBERS,
STATE OF TRADE.
Trade during the month is about the same as last month. There are,
however, a large number of members in receipt of Unemployment Benefit, the
amount paid out being 37 per cent. higher than last month. The figures
recorded in the Ministry of Labour Gazette are as follows :-
BOOT AND SHOE INDUSTRY.
A considerable proportion of boot and shoe factories closed down for a complete week
at Whitsuntide. Employment after the holidays was generally depressed, and in most
centres was worse than in May and worse than in June of last year. Much short time
was worked.
The percentage of workpeople unemployed, as indicated by the unemployment books
lodged at Employment Exchanges, was 10.2 on 22nd June, 1925, as compared with 10.1
on 25th May, 1925, and 7.7 on 23rd June, 1924.
Returns from firms employing over 41,000 operatives in the week ended 27th June,
1925, showed that 35 per cent. of these operatives worked short time in that week, to the
extent of about 10 hours each, on an average.
Employment was quiet in London. It declined at Leicester, and was slack, short
time being general. At Northampton employment was quiet, most firms being on short
time. In the Higham and Rushden district employment declined and was bad. At
Raunds it was fairly good, and much better than in June of last year; but at Welling-
borough it was very depressed, and worse than in the previous month; short time was
prevalent, and a large number of operatives were unemployed. At Kettering only a few
factories were on full time, and many were working from one to two and a half days short
per week; employment in this centre declined, and was poor.
Depression continued in the heavy boot trade of Leeds, but factories producing light
footwear continued busy. Very little short time was worked at Stafford, and employment
continued fair on the whole. There was not much change at Norwich, employment
remaining fair, with few operatives on short time. In the Bristol and Kingswood area
employment, though poor, showed some improvement, a little less short time being worked
some firms were on full time.

In spite of this long list of settlements, we would remind members that
lump sums of money are not obtainable by law, but only by agreement,
proving, once again, what may be done for our members when the Insurance
Companies know that there is a powerful organisation at the back of them.

THE HALF-YEARLY STATEMENT OF ACCOUNTS
is presented herewith. The details are given on the following pages. Despite
the heavy demands on the Sick and Unemployment Funds we are pleased to
record the fact that there is an increase in the Central Office funds. It should
be noted that the Sick and Funeral Benefits have increased from £15,740 5s. 10d.
for last half-year to £22,988. gs. 2d., or an increase of 46 per cent., whilst the
Unemployment Benefit has increased from £18,440 18s. 6d. to £24,228 Is. 3d.,
an increase of 31.5 per cent. In connection with the Benefits paid through the
State Unemployment Section, it will be observed that the increase is 37 per
cent.; this is a great advantage to the members by being enabled to receive
State Benefit through the offices of the Union, and we trust that every branch
will see the advantage in administering the same.
The loss recorded on the Political Fund is due to the Union's activities in
connection with the work carried on on local bodies, which work is of distinct
advantage, not only to one section of the community, but is of a general
character.

JOINT INDUSTRIAL COUNCILS AND VOLUNTARY AGREEMENTS.
At the forthcoming Trades Union Congress in September we have a
resolution upon the agenda dealing with the above matter, and also one on
Unemployment.
The resolutions are as follows:-
VOLUNTARY AGREEMENTS.
"This Congress instructs the General Council to urge upon H.M.
Government the desirability of giving to National Agreements voluntarily
entered into and approved by Joint Industrial Councils the same validity
as awards under the Trade Board Acts, 1909 and 1918, with the object
of ensuring the observance of fair conditions of labour by all engaged in
the industry whenever requested by the parties to the agreement."
UNEMPLOYMENT.
"That this Congress views with the greatest concern the serious
volume of unemployment now existing, and deplores the lack of effective


---
Here is the text extracted from the image:
385
measures on the part of the Government to attempt to alleviate the
situation. It further declares its emphatic opposition to the spurious
remedies of lower wages and the degradation of established working con-
ditions, and calls upon the Trade Union Movement to offer its utmost
resistance to any attempt to increase the hours of work. It asserts the
unquestionable right of the unemployed worker and his dependents to
adequate maintenance, and reiterates its demand for the establishment
of a National Employment and Development Board for the purpose of
devising and co-ordinating measures whereby the extent of unemployment
may be reduced."
We hope that both resolutions will be passed. The one dealing with
Voluntary Agreements has been, in the past, somewhat misunderstood. We are
only asking that any Union which has a proper agreement between themselves
and the Employers' Association, should, if both sides desire the same, to
make it applicable to all engaged in the industry, and thus prevent "under-
cutting" of wages and conditions at the expense of the worker. This surely
is a sound principle, and one that will make for the elimination of those
who want to reduce the standard of living.
THE POLITICAL AND INDUSTRIAL WORLD
is, from the workers' standpoint, very unsatisfactory indeed; especially so is
this the fact if looked at from the proceedings during the past few weeks in the
House of Commons. Of talk there has been an unending stream, and appeals
for something to be done to relieve the present abnormal circumstances. When,
however, Labour puts up its case through the Labour M.P.'s, the net result
is that "Parliament can do but little to remedy the situation," and later on,
when votes are taken, the big majority by which the Conservatives were returned
shows itself in constantly voting down the Labour proposals. The attitude of
the Government upon the great and serious question of unemployment, as
portrayed by the speech of the Premier, the Rt. Hon. Stanley Baldwin, M.P.,
is indeed one of great disappointment, and ought to be a speech to satisfy the
workers that their hopes lay in a Labour Party in the House of Commons,
with a sufficient majority to make it a real power. We are being constantly
reminded of the fact that the Labour Party was in office last year and did not
solve the problem; what is omitted is the fact that the Party was in a minority
so far as the number of members making the House of Commons was concerned,
and to a very considerable extent was therefore handicapped by the limits
which were imposed upon the Party.
Further, the very people who "taunt" the Labour Party with its short-
comings conveniently forget that whilst Labour had nine months wherein to
bring into being new methods, the older parties of the State have been in power
and authority for hundreds of years, and the present condition of affairs is
due very largely to their lack of statesmanship, and certainly reflect no great
credit upon them. We appeal, therefore, to the membership to keep steadily
---

---
```text
386
near future, a sufficient number of members in the House of Commons who will
and persistently at work to build up such a Labour Party as will ensure, in the
not only insist upon drastic reforms, but will be in sufficient authority to put
upon the Statute Book of the Realm such Acts of Parliament as will bring into
being a system which will relieve the workers from many unjust burdens they
at present are carrying.
OBITUARY.
It is with sincere regret that we have to record the death of another
Branch. Mr. Rawlings has for many years been an active member of
officer of the Union, MR. J. RAWLINGS, Secretary to the London South
the Branch and did yeoman work on behalf of the members in South
London. Upon the death of Mr. G. Standeven, in January, 1924, he
was elected Branch Secretary.
Mr. Rawlings was buried on Thursday, the 16th inst., at 2.30p.m.,
and we were represented at the funeral by the General President and
General Secretary.
We trust that the efforts put forth on behalf of the workers by Mr.
Rawlings will be an incentive to the younger men to carry on the work
he has been called upon to lay down.
To the relatives of the deceased officer we have tendered sympathy
for their great loss, and also appreciation of the work of our late colleague.
We hope it will be some consolation to them in this their loss to know
that Mr. Rawlings was held in esteem by his comrades.
We have further to record the very sudden death of MRS. BLEAKLEY,
the wife of Mr. C. Bleakley, who himself died as recently as May last.
It is indeed a sad blow to the family to lose both parents by sudden
deaths and especially within such a short period of time. We beg to
assure the family of the sympathy of the membership of the Union in this
their time of trouble and loss.
UNEMPLOYMENT.
A Special Conference was held on the 24th inst., called by the General
Council of the Trade Union Congress, at the Central Buildings, Westminster.
The basis for representation being different from that for the Annual Congress,
we decided that inasmuch as the membership of the Union could not be con-
sulted, we were represented thereat by the following:-Mr. T. F. Richards,
Mr. W. R. Townley, Mrs. M. J. Bell-Richards, Mr. L. Smith, Mr. W. R. Smith,
and Mr. E. L. Poulton. The Conference was well attended, and after con-
siderable discussion upon the present disgraceful condition of affairs regarding
the vast army of unemployed and the attendant suffering occasioned, a series
of resolutions were passed dealing with the question of insisting upon the
```

---
387
Government dealing with this paramount question. A report will be found
on page 441.
We are pleased our colleague, Mr. W. R. Smith, drew attention to the
most important item in which the Labour movement believes- the develop-
ment of home resources, particularly in regard to the land, much of which is
derelict. Although Mr. W. R. Smith's speech was of nearly ten minutes'
duration, we regret the Daily Herald only gave him the following in their
report of the Congress :-
"Mr. W. R. Smith (Boot and Shoe Operatives) regretted that the
resolutions made no reference to the development of home resources,
especially the land, much of which was derelict."
MINERS.
At the Conference named above, a full statement was made regarding the
position of the Miners and the attempts being made to still further reduce their
standard of life, a standard already too low. There was no doubt about the
mind of the Conference as to supporting the Miners, and while it is hoped a
way may yet be found whereby a stoppage will be prevented, we want to make
quite clear to the membership that the Trade Union movement as a whole is
expected to loyally support the Miners in their resisting the onslaught if forced
upon them. Their case is quite clear; they have already been forced down
to a standard below which they are unanimous in stating they cannot go
further. Their case has been before the General Council of the Congress and
they are unanimous in declaring for doing all possible to back up the Miners.
We cannot say more at present except that the members need not be surprised
if we have to call at short notice meetings of the members to ask for further
powers than we have at present to render assistance to other Unions.
WOOLLEN TRADES.
A statement was also made at the same Conference regarding the attempts
that the Employers in such industry are making to reduce the workers' wages.
Here also the workers are determined to resist such proposed reductions, and
a number of operatives have already ceased work rather than submit to any
reduction. We hope that success will attend them in their resistance. It is
indeed ironical to find on the one hand complaints as to restricted trade, and
on the other these proposed reductions of wages which will further restrict
trade, and this on the plea of increasing trade. These cases ought to wake
the workers up to the absolute necessity of so rousing the conscience of the
nation as will ensure the withdrawal of the proposals which our comrades are
confronted with. The Trade Union movement is going through a very trying
time, and we appeal to the members to stand loyally by and help as far as
possible those who are called upon to bear the brunt of the battle.
BALANCE SHEETS.
We beg to call the attention of those Branch Secretaries who have not yet
sent in their balance sheet for the quarter ending May last. On reference to

Circular No. 357, it will be seen that a period of eight weeks was allowed to

RECEIPTS FOR BENEFITS.

JUBILEE HISTORY OF THE UNION.

THE "DAILY HERALD."
We trust that all our members will take every available opportunity to
push the Herald, the only daily paper owned and controlled by the movement.
By purchasing the Herald daily a full report of the political world as seen by
Labour can be read.
REMEMBER THE FIFTEENTH!
Once again we feel bound to call the serious attention of Branch Secretaries
to the fact that the FIFTEENTH day of each month is the latest day for
returns reaching the General Secretary. If Branches fail to send off their
returns to reach the Central Office before the 15th of the month it tends to
upset the organisation of the work in the General Secretary's Office. We beg
to intimate that unless returns are in before the 15th of the month, the Secretary
has been instructed to put into force the Rules governing the same. Returns
Sheets MUST also bear the signature of both the Branch President and Branch
Secretary, or they will reluctantly have to be returned for completion.
TRADE UNION STAMP.
This is a matter which we have stated before rests with the members to
make a success. It is in the interests of members themselves that "Stamped
Footwear" should be increasingly purchased; therefore, push the Trade Union
Stamp. Members and their families should wear only "Union Stamped
Footwear."

GENERAL PRESIDENT'S REPORT.
FELLOW MEMBERS,
TRADE. The state of trade warrants all of us to look for the cause, and, if possible,
point to the remedies. At once let me say that all countries appear to be in the same
plight, as shown by the facts of our international relationship. Other countries have
been quoted, and we have therefore informed ourselves of the position in other countries
as here in this one. Our confrères in other lands cannot press home their industrial
demands unless the operatives are willing to be organised, and in this connection we could,
and would, like to stop home competition by getting all Operatives in the Union and all
Employers in the Federation; and owing to the self-centred interest inherent in the
unlearned, these impositions are put upon those who are conscientiously trying to do the
right thing. We have evidence of this where several manufacturers have been called upon
to abide by the National Agreement, and the pity is that some small penalty is not imposed
upon them; in fact, we here in this country appear to be imposed upon by the Non-
Unionist and Non-Associated because of our desire not to be either vindictive or unkind,
and whilst we may wish it. We are able to check bad wages and worse conditions in other
countries and we are doing it; that is more than the Employers are doing, they fully
believe in underselling each other. We don't, and our influence in the shoemaking coun-
tries, some twelve in number, only one exceeds the 48 hours, which at least is an achieve-
ment for the Unions so organised. Then we must always bear in mind that the purchasing
power of money differs in different countries and also the economic position has to be
considered. For instance, let us take Messrs. Bata Bros., Zlim, Czecho-Slovakia or
Moravia. They employ four thousand workers and are capable of turning out sixty
thousand pairs per week of women's and children's glacé kid, patent, willow, satin canvas
and leather. They manufacture everything necessary on the spot; they make their
own leather, having their own pine forest for tanning bark; make some of their machinery;
a wood-working department, paper and cardboard factories, printing works, and print
a weekly newspaper distributed among the workers. They have their own dairy farm
from which the workers are supplied with flour, milk, meat, cheese, eggs, &c. The firm
is also erecting a garden town of four hundred houses, schools, library, baths, the building
materials being supplied from the firms own brick-kilns, quarries and forests. The firm
have one hundred and eighty retail shops in Czecho-Slovakia, and one hundred and twenty
abroad, or a total of three hundred retail shops. They also manufacture felt and cloth
shoes for Bohemia and Moravia. The estimated turn-out is put at one hundred thousand
pairs per day, of which sixty thousand pairs are turned out by machinery and forty
thousand pairs by hand. Including the cottage industry it is estimated that in the country
of Czecho-Slovakia no less than forty thousand people are engaged in shoemaking, and
their output is about forty million pairs per year. If one carefully considers the above
it will be seen that such a firm can get all the wealth they need from any two or three of
the above investments, for apparently this firm is practising the Socialistic system by
having and being the overlord, but one wonders where the ordinary small private trader
can successfully compete with a system of co-ordination as this appears to be. Of one
thing I am concerned and that is we ought to know more of this firm and its conditions.
MANSFIELD.- Messrs. Faire Bros., of this town, employ some hundred and twenty
girls, and we succeeded in joining about one hundred and eighteen in January last, and
promised them as they had been members before, after improving their position, they
allowed themselves to be intimidated and left the Union, but we promised to see the
firm before Easter if they would remain loyal. We learned in a few weeks that the manage-
ment had had the girls in the Office and tried to intimidate them from belonging to the
Union. I asked for, and obtained, an interview with Sir Samuel Faire and his brother, in
Leicester, who promised me that they would have no trouble with us and that no inter-
ference with the operatives would be permitted, but further interference has taken place,
and to make sure twenty-five girls were discharged with the object of causing them to leave
the Union. Many girls stopped paying. We wrote the firm who again assured us in face
of this fact, that they were not interfering with the girls and their membership, and although
the six months has elapsed, asked for by the firm, they have not put themselves in order.
It makes one lose confidence in mankind when men of position act in this manner and allow
others to drag their name into the public gaze, when the conduct displayed by the manage-
ment at Mansfield is such that we must inform the Industry of the fact that this firm is
acting contrary to the letter and the spirit of the National Agreement, which all honourable
firms adopt, and we must ask the employers to help us to respect their own agreement,
and also respect the attitude of our members who refuse to work up materials produced
under unfair conditions. We are told that the law of libel is great: well, we are prepared
to put that to the test by making the facts known.

were permitting at the auxiliary firms like the above, and also Looms', of Market Har-
borough, and other firms who cut leather and make stiffenings and heels, all of whom make

SUPERANNUATION. - The Sub-Committee appointed reported to a special Council
Meeting on this matter, and there appears to be only one way of dealing with this matter
at present, that is, by all future recipients to pay an additional contribution, and one
wonders how many members there are prepared to do that. Beyond this I will not divulge
at present all the other points that may appear in a Memoranda later.
ADVISORY COUNCIL OF TRADES BOARDS met at Montagu House, to consider, among
other things, the method of Inspectorship in connection with the Trade Board Acts, but
owing to the grave crisis in the coal industry and the general Council Meeting precisely
at that time, and several of our members being on that body, after carefully looking
through the Annual Report to be presented to Congress at Scarborough, we adjourned
the meeting with the Council, Todd and the Chief Lady Inspector until a later date.
LONDON SOUTH.- The General Secretary and myself had the sad experience of attend-
ing the funeral of Mr. J. Rawlings, Secretary of the Branch, who passed away after a very
few days illness through pneumonia. This branch only a few months ago lost their old
Secretary, Mr. G. Standeven, and this calamity falling so soon must make the older members
feel very sad indeed.
WATERFOOT. I have also paid a visit to Waterfoot with the avowed object of trying
to prevent friction between us, but as the Secretary had had a special call, I could only
write him very fully asking for an opportunity of meeting the Executive of that Union
of 6,000 strong, and by such methods avert the friction that may arise on any question
in which both may be interested. I have told Mr. Taylor that at present all I am doing
is unofficial, but if I can get something tangible to place before the Council of the National
Union, I should be only too pleased, as we shall never get unity until units unite. Bodies
are always made up of these units, and it is necessary that these must make the first move
which, to say the least, was the recommendation of the General Council's Dispute Committee.
All members are very loyal to the Union who buy and read the Daily Herald, and act
upon its advice in Trade Union matters, especially if they wear STAMPED SHOES, or at
least be sure they have been made under Trade Union conditions.
Yours fraternally,

M. Richards
General President.
60 LADY SOMERSET ROAD,
KENTISH TOWN, LONDON, N.W.5.
19th July, 1925.

THE AUDITOR'S REPORT.
THE CO-OPERATIVE WHOLESALE SOCIETY LIMITED,
AUDIT DEPARTMENT,
99 Leman Street,
London, E. 1.
The Executive Council,
National Union of Boot and Shoe Operatives.
I beg to report that I have audited the Accounts of the Union for
the Half-year ended 30th June, 1925, with the Books and Vouchers
relating thereto, and have obtained all the information and explanations
I have required, and am of the opinion that they exhibit a true and correct
view of the Union's position according to the information given, and as
shown by the books.
The Deeds of Investments have been examined, and found to be
in order.
The system of accounting is an excellent one, and the books are
exceedingly well kept.
15th July, 1925,
Yours faithfully,
A. E. NEWMAN,
Public Auditor.

BRANCH REPORTS.
ARBROATH. — Trade is still bad, and now the middle of the year brings
round the need for statutory holidays. Needless to say our members could
do with work and not with holidays, but they will have to accept a further
interval which will pass as a time of rest and pleasure. We have written before
of how the holiday hits the short-time worker, and again the stop will entail
a stop in benefit which is always needed. We have been awaiting in vain for
the shrieks of indignation over the new proposals of the Government re Un-
employment Insurance, and once again must we confess we cannot understand
the British worker. He seems willing always to be kicked or squeezed or
diddled or hoodwinked, and even ready to kiss the hand that hands over the
"medicine," if one can be allowed to confuse the metaphor. How insured
contributors should permit say the idle benefit being spoken of as the "dole,"
how they should tolerate further meddling with waiting period and with
ordinary rights to benefit, how they should lie down to this bare-faced project
to reduce unemployment by depriving claimants wholesale of benefits, is some-
thing which will be of enormous interest to the students of psychology of the
future, but which remains a perfect nightmare to those of the present. After
a period of approximately five years' depression, when all kinds of resources
are long since swallowed up, and when after a fitful summer we are approach-
ing a British winter season with its cruel demands for greater expenditure,
the screw will begin to turn which will leave huge numbers with no alternative
but the workhouse or the grave. The new codified instructions for the adminis-
tration of Unemployment Benefit have been issued, and several very unsatis-
factory issues appear to have been in the mind of the Ministry when this was
prepared. Throughout the period between one set of regulations and another,
the permanent officials are exerting their cuteness in finding fresh difficulties
to put in the way of those who claim, and if those restrictions fail to rouse
serious opposition, the underlying idea is then embodied in a definite instruction.
How this works can be seen from the treatment of juveniles who become
insured for the first time. When the right of "waiver" was first allowed to
the 30 stamp conditions, those juveniles were admitted to benefit after their
first stamp, the view being held that if they had worked at insurable employ-
ment between the ages of 14 and 16 years, they had proved their right to be
regarded as being normally insurable, while only being eligible for stamps at
16 seemed to prove they had got all the reasonable amount of insurable employ-
ment that was possible in their case. This went on for some time, but at last
some bright brain discovered a technical flaw, and held that no employment
was insurable before a person became 16 years of age; therefore the statutory
condition was not fulfilled until a longer term of stamps was necessary to prove
"normality." Various restrictions were imposed until no one in an official
position knew what was what, but now it is laid down that three months of
insured employment must be proved before a juvenile gets benefit. And all
the time only a few kicked. If a greater volume of protest had been made
this objectionable finding would have been prevented. Many other instances
could be given to prove that very oppressive conditions have grown up gradually
which only required a united opposition from Unions to kill at their inception.
BURNLEY. — We would like to see an Organiser as promised. Our
branch is dying of apathy and indifference.
CHESHAM. — Trade here still remains slack.
CHESTERFIELD. — We have to report trade bad at the time of sending.
We have several members out of work, and our sick list is rather heavy for the
time of the year. The prospects do not look very bright for trade improving
at present.

DUNDALK. - Trade is very bad indeed, so much so that members are
being dismissed and short time is the general rule. This being a new branch
started by men employed by a new factory, matters are rather unsettled.
management was approached and asked to allow all to share alike as regards
We regret to state we had trouble here this week, ending 4th July. The
short time. This was refused, so our Branch President, an Englishman, volun-
teered to stay off too. The members considered this a great sacrifice. At
our monthly meeting the members strongly resented the management's action,
and presented their local President with a very handsome pipe in case, as a
mark of respect and esteem for his laborious toil in connection with the Branch,
at the same time wishing him every success in his future occupation. He is
leaving Dundalk on the 6th inst. for Waterford, where his family are domiciled,
he having been employed for seven years under a Waterford firm previous to
his coming to Dundalk. We may mention also that the sewer and stitcher-
the only one we had-packed up on Friday evening, the 3rd inst., and returned
to his home in Northampton-had enough of Dundalk management. Further,
we understand enough stock is on hand to supply the requirements of the firm
for three or four months, and it is difficult to understand what is to happen next.
In all cases the employees have been dismissed without notice. On Friday,
the 3rd inst., the forelady was dismissed without notice. Now we have the
foreman finisher's wife in her place.
EDINBURGH.-Trade in Repairing Section fairly busy during the month-
We are sorry to report the death of Mrs. C. Bleakley only six weeks after her
husband and late Colleague C. Bleakley. Our sympathy goes out to the family
in their double bereavement. Up to the time of sending returns away the
response to our appeal has been very gratifying. We shall publish a full
list in next month's Report. This response goes to show that if a man has
convictions and states them, even if you don't always agree, one has to respect
the man. On looking over one of the Organisers' reports we see the statement
that operatives are always pleased to see any of the National Officials of the
Union, acting, as it does, to encourage the outlying districts. This is exactly
he position the members all over Scotland feel. A visit from any of the
ficials is ever welcome, even if some knotty questions are asked. Let
otland have a visit from one of the Organisers, it would be greatly appre-
ated. We are pleased that the firm recently organised by our late Colleague
have now started paying to National Agreement as far as wages are concerned,
but have not adopted the Holiday Scheme. Of course we shall endeavour to
have same applied as it is part of our Agreement. Male members with this
firm are proving staunch to Union, but the females are rather a difficult pro-
position. The Branch thanks all those who have contributed towards our
appeal. Owing to the death of Mrs. Bleakley we propose putting all the
money collected into a banking account on behalf of the three girls left at home.
Trusting this will meet with the approval of all contributors.
HIGHAM AND RUSHDEN.-During the month trade has gradually
become worse, with the exception of Raunds, who fortunately have Govern-
hardly know how to keep going.
KINGSWOOD AND DISTRICT.-Trade in this centre is far from active,
and in view of the industrial situation a decided improvement cannot be


---
expected immediately. The Kingswood Boot Trade has figured prominently
in the local Press during the past week, one anonymous scribe declaring that
(in Kingswood) the industry will be wiped out entirely. Strong exception
unless the attention of the Government is called to the serious state of affairs
outlook has been taken by another well-known manufac-
turer, who is reputed to possess a comfortable bank balance, and who has
pointed out that when employment generally improves, Kingswood will more
than justify its existence in the great boot manufacturing industry and so
say all of us-but what of the operative? If Kingswood is to justify its future
existence as a prosperous boot centre, the operative will have to be considered,
and the time to do that is now, with special reference to the rising generation,
and we contend that what is badly needed in this District, is ample opportunity
for technical education. The day of the outworker and the backyard work-
shop is past, and instead of looking for Government assistance, the whole of
the Kingswood manufacturers should get together, cultivate the community
spirit, come down off their perches, and discuss matters with their men. This
will do more to stimulate pride in craft, which is essential in modern boot
manufacture, and help to remove that frequently expressed contempt for the
industry which is so prevalent. The members at Branch meeting moved a
sympathetic vote of condolence with the widow and family of our late friend
and colleague, Chris. Bleakley, who will be remembered in Kingswood for
sound organising work for the Union. The Branch voted the sum of £2 2s. od.
to the fund organised by the Edinburgh Branch.

LIVERPOOL.—The glorious summer weather during the past month has
been grand for holidays, sports, and making hay, but has caused a slump in
boot repairing in the majority of shops. When the weather breaks we may
expect a rush, some of our unfortunate members say let it be soon. We note
that some of our members are behind with their contributions. Eight weeks'
arrears is the limit for receiving benefits according to rule.

NORTHAMPTON.-Trade remains somewhat slack for time of the year,
causing some to be discharged and others to work short time. But of course
where men are on Supplementary Wage Basis they are able to earn a little over
standard rates whilst at the factory. Our members were very proud of the
honour attained by our General Secretary, Mr. E. L. Poulton, J.P., and passed
the following resolution :-

"To offer Mr. E. L. Poulton our heartiest congratulations on the
honoured and responsible position, he having been elected on the League
of Nations, and from personal knowledge of his abilities, feel he will carry
out those duties with credit to our Union, and the People's cause generally,
and that any action he takes, or advice given, will be a result of con-
sidered judgment."

It was with regret we heard of the death of C. Bleakley.

PLYMOUTH.—During the month we have been occupied with a claim
by one of our members for State Unemployment Insurance Benefit. The local
Labour Exchange having referred the matter to the Chief Insurance Officer,
and he disallowing the claim, we determined to present the case before a Court
of Referees. We are pleased to report that the Court recommended the pay-
ment of the claim, and the Chief Insurance Officer has now agreed with their
recommendations. Our member had been given one week's notice to terminate
his engagement as a dayworker, after nine years' service, and offered piecework
on the seat, which he refused, contending that it would be impossible to earn
a living on the seat as all the best work was done by machinery, and only the
most difficult and unremunerative remained. We stated before the Court
that our Union had a National Agreement with the Masters' Federation of
Great Britain and Ireland, which showed that it was only common justice


---
Here is the extracted text from the image:
```text
before changing from day to piecework that four weeks' notice should be given,
and notice in writing to the Branch Secretary; this had not been done. We
won our case. Thus we see once again the benefit of Trade Unionism and
organisation as exemplified in the National Agreement, taken as a standard
of morality and fair play for the trade, influencing the minds of impartial
authorities and establishing a precedent which must stand for all time as the
play-fair standard of our trade, and the death knell of "battledore and shuttle
cock."

STONE. It is with sincere regret that we have to report the death of
one of our oldest members, Albert Smith, 60307 (brother of the late Thomas
Smith, the first General Secretary of our Union). He joined our Branch at
the commencement, 17th September, 1887, and was largely instrumental of
us joining the National Union. He was President of our Branch some years
and always worked for the benefit of Branch and its members. As a Labour
member he was elected, with others, in April, 1894, to the Stone Urban District
Council, and retained his seat for 12 years, when he resigned. A good record
for a Labour member over 30 years ago. We extend our sincere sympathy
to his wife and family on their sad and sudden bereavement.

WATERFORD. - Trade still remains good.

WELLINGBOROUGH. Trade is still very bad, the district experiencing
a shortage, which in the opinion of some of our older members is the worst for
many years.
```

EMPLOYMENT OF LABOUR DIRECT SAVES MONEY OF NATION
says the Postmaster-General, Sir Wm. Mitchell Thomson.
A large part of the transport work of the Post Office is carried out under a system of
contracting. That system of contracts has been universally applied to the carriage of
the mails. For reasons of economy the Post Office staff looked round in
certain directions to see whether under certain conditions they could not
carry on a road service more economically by employing their own
vehicles, and not those of the contractors. This, however, does not
apply generally; it only applies under certain conditions. There is no
doubt that the work we have carried on in that way has meant that
we have been able to operate our own motor vehicles more cheaply
than by contract.
I hesitate to put an actual financial figure, but the State has affected
a saving over this work somewhere in the neighbourhood of £60,000.
In three instances, during recent times, reductions in the tenders of the
contractors have meant a saving of £50,000 yearly.
[Extract from Statement of Postmaster-General in House of Commons, July 20th, 1925.

FACTORIES AND WORKSHOPS: CHIEF INSPECTOR'S ANNUAL REPORT.
The Annual Report of the Chief Inspector of Factories and Workshops for the year
1924 has been issued.
The Report states that, taking industry as a whole, trade conditions have again
been bad, though there have been particular industries and branches of industry which
have been very prosperous and where employment has been good, and there are others
where, though conditions cannot be described as good, they have certainly been better
than they were during 1923.
Reference is made in the Report to the continued development of new industrial
areas, to the steady increase in the removal of factories from the centre to the outskirts
of London, and to the continued and rapid increase in the generation and use of electricity.
The number of registered factories in Great Britain again increased during 1924,
the total being 142,494, as against 139,920 in 1923. Workshops declined from 140,850 to
133,729. Factories thus for the first time exceed workshops in number; the decline
in workshops is largely due to the introduction of electrical power, the use of which con-
verts premises from a workshop into a factory. There is, too, the tendency for the small
places to be absorbed by the big; in particular, the small dressmaker and milliner,
it is stated in the Report, can no longer compete with the large stores.
The only new legislation affecting the Department which became operative during the
year was the Workmen's Compensation Act, 1923, which imposed new duties on the staff
both in regard to notices and registers required to be kept for workmen's compensation
purposes, and in regard to first-aid. Section 28 of the Act, which came into force at the
beginning of the year, abolished the dual standard of reportability of accidents (viz., one
day's absence from work in the case of machinery accidents and seven days' absence in
the case of all other accidents), and established one uniform standard, of three days'
inability to earn full wages, without any differentiation as to causation. - The Ministry
of Labour Gazetle, July, 1925.

BOYS' NIGHT WORK.
Five Inspectors for 6,000 Factories.
There are only five inspectors for 6,000 factories and workshops in the large industrial
area of Bradford, Halifax, and Keighley, and a considerable amount of night work is done
by boys.
These facts were brought out by a deputation representing workers in the textile
trade which waited on the Home Secretary (Sir William Joynson-Hicks) with reference
to the dearth of factory inspectors.
The Home Secretary promised to recommend to the Cabinet that steps be taken to
remedy the shortage of inspectors.
-Westminster Gazette, 21st July, 1925.

D. MCCARTHY.
During the past month we have endeavoured to deal with the firm of Looms', Market
Harborough. This firm is chiefly engaged in Heel Building, and cutting stiffeners for the
trade; no Holiday Fund is in operation, and the wages generally are below the recognised
scale. We have only a few members engaged here and have made continuous attempts
to organise the operatives by frequently meeting them and endeavouring to persuade
them to become members. As a result of our efforts we were enabled to induce a number
of them to become members, and there seemed every prospect of increasing the number
the following week, but it at once became apparent to us that the firm were determined
to interfere with the liberty of the operatives. When we followed up our work the following
week we were informed that six members had been dismissed, three of whom had recently
joined the Union, with the usual excuse that it was shortness of work. This for the present
time has had a deterrent effect, though it will not interfere eventually with the determina-
tion of the men to join when trade improves. This attitude reflects very little credit
on the mentality of the firm, whose ideas of limiting the rights of how the operative should
spend his money would not be tolerated if the operative dictated to the employer what he
should do with his profit that he had produced for him. Other operatives who have
joined the Union have since been discharged.
We have also visited Desborough and found trade very bad, but we had a meeting with
the operatives at Bird's and Riley's. There were a few girls working there who were not
members, but promised to join.
We have visited Olney, and met the operatives at Hinde & Mann's, also Cowley & Sons,
Unfortunately both these firms were working at Hinde & Mann's, also Cod number of
youths and girls not members, and there is need of further improving our membership here.
Whitney & Westley, at Burton Latimer. On two occasions we met the operatives, a good
number of whom were resident in the Finedon district. There were about 30 non-members,
We met the officials of the Kettering Branches who asked us to visit the firm of
very efficient method that is adopted by the two Branches with reference to the details
and after canvassing them they all promised to join up. It is very pleasing to record the
of organisation which enables them to keep well in touch with their members. For two
weeks we have been in Mansfield endeavouring to deal with the position at Faire Bros.
