Some time ago 130 girls joined the Union, and we understand Mr. Fewkes, Director, took
upon himself the responsibility of dismissing 25 girls and has since denied the right of the
girls from joining the Union. We have frequently met the girls and tried to induce them
to again take up their cards. Despite our efforts, the girls seem terrified, because of the
action of the firm, although we understand that some small increase of wages has been
given. The conditions at this firm are far from satisfactory, and is a negation of the
National Agreement. The girls are fully conscious of the conditions that operate here,
but they are compelled to accept the same and would gladly pay their contributions if
the firm would allow them the freedom of becoming members. Thus you have the deplor-
able spectacle of the only way in which persons can successfully carry on their business
is to terrify the employees and then prevent them from having any rights. This idea
may have been a very excellent one in the seventeenth century, but we cannot allow
those ideas of that period to dominate a period where the Schoolmaster had tried to do his
work very efficiently.
We have also endeavoured to organise the girls working at Thrall's, who are chiefly
engaged on Stiffeners. The wages paid here are exceedingly poor, and there is great need
and though there are about only 40 engaged here, the conditions are so bad that
drastic alterations are required if this firm is to be brought up to our Agreement.
We made a special attempt to hold a meeting for the whole of the firms in the town,
but we did not get a very good response, only from the Mansfield Shoe Co. We are very
pleased to report that as a result of our efforts at Mansfield Shoe Co., 50 new members.
have been made with fairly good prospects of increasing that number.
LEN. SMITH.
J. H. BAUM.
The early part of the month was spent in the Sileby area. At every factory in Sileby,
Mountsorrel, and Rothley, meetings have now been held and operatives canvassed outside
as to joining the Union. In each case the reception has been good, and if the results are
not so helpful as we would like, it is well to remember that the state of trade does not tend
to make the work of organising so easy as we would like, but the effort that has been
made, will, I hope, be productive of results later on when the condition of trade is better
than at present.
My strong opinion is that this district should be given all the attention possible,
from the point of view of organising. There is scope for such work, and the area is import-
ant in the sense that it borders upon our largest centre, and we should protect our position
in every way possible.
The National Labour Party made application for my services in connection with the
By-Election in the Forest of Dean, and the Central Office having agreed to same, I spent a
week in connection therewith. I was asked to make a special effort in the rural part of
the Division, and the majority of the meetings I addressed were on village greens, and they
were in every respect, most encouraging; the attendance was good, and the attention
paid to the speeches were all that could be desired. I am persuaded that the effort helped
very substantially in these rural parts, and I am glad that our Union was able to con-
tribute, through myself, towards the victory so handsomely won.
I feel I cannot leave this subject without a reference to the cause of this election,
viz., the death of Mr. J. Wignall. He was one who had won the regard and affection of all
who knew him. Kindly disposed, generous to a fault, a man of strong conviction, who on
many occasions in the House of Commons, on such subjects as Unemployment, spoke with.
such first-hand knowledge and earnestness that he commanded the respect of all. His
death is most regrettable, and I feel I have lost a close personal friend.
On Friday last I attended with my colleagues, the Special Trades Congress at the
Central Hall, Westminster, regarding Unemployment. Three resolutions had been
drafted by the General Council, which, so far as they went, could not be taken exception
to, but I feel most strongly, and in this I feel I have my colleagues with me, that in any
suggestions or proposals put forward to deal with this great depression, foreign markets,
though of great importance, do not cover the whole position. I therefore raised the
question of home resources and the proper cultivation of the land.
I do hope our movement will not lose sight of this question. In my opinion it is of
the greatest importance, and sooner or later the sooner the better it will have to be taken
up. The land of this country is far too vital to the interests of our people to be left as
something to be controlled by individuals as a private concern.

UNEMPLOYMENT.
A Special Trades Union Conference on Unemployment was held in the Central Hall,
Westminster, on Friday, 24th July, 1925. Chairman: Mr. A. B. Swales, President of
the General Council.
The Chairman, in his opening statement, called attention to the gravity of the situation
drastically with such an appalling situation, affecting as it does the lives of millions
with its long continued roll of such a vast army of unemployed, and the necessity of dealing
being intimated by the Chairman that in view of the gravity of the situation it might be
of workers. The following resolutions were, after discussion, unanimously passed, it
necessary to call the Conference together again within a few days.
(1) That this Special Conference of Unions affiliated to the Trades Union
Congress regards with the utmost apprehension the appalling increase in the number
of unemployed as compared with the pre-war period, and declares that this increase
diminished. As our industries are to a large extent dependent on export trade,
can, with effective governmental legislation and administrative action, be materially
the manifest duty of the Government is to pursue a foreign policy calculated to develop
amicable diplomatic relations, and trading relations through the medium of the
Overseas Trade Acts and the Trade Facilities Acts, with all countries, including
Russia.
(2) The Conference views with the strongest resentment the declared intention
of the Government to reduce the benefits now being paid to unemployed workers
by means of a lengthening of the waiting period from three to six days and the cur-
tailing at the discretion of the Ministry of Labour of the right to extended benefit.
It declares that as unemployment is admittedly inherent in the present system, any
deprivation of the workers of adequate maintenance is inhuman in the extreme.
It asserts the right of every citizen to work or full maintenance.
(3) The Conference declares its determination to exert the utmost possible
pressure upon the Government to take such steps as are necessary for the remedying
of the present critical situation, and urges all Trade Unions and other Labour Organisa-
tions throughout the country to press these demands vigorously upon their local
Parliamentary representatives and upon the Government.
During the past six years a chronic feature of our Social life has been that one
million workers have been unemployed, which, together with their dependents, has
meant at a modest estimate that three millions of our fellow human beings have been
permanently existing on the border-line of starvation. In addition, "part-time
employment has intensely aggravated this deplorable condition.
This Conference records its intense conviction that such a state of affairs cannot be
allowed to continue indefinitely, and that organised labour must not only protest
vigorously against the present apparent indifference towards this social injustice,
but, in addition, if redress is not speedily forthcoming, it will be compelled to take
such action as conditions and opportunity dictate.
Statements were also made regarding the present serious situation of the Miners
and the termination of their agreements and proposals of the Mine Owners to reduce
wages and lengthen working hours. Also the proposals of the Employers in the Woollen
Industry to reduce wages. In both cases it was made clear that the workers would, to the
utmost, resist these retrograde proposals. They therefore asked for the full support of
the Trade Union movement to enable them to resist successfully. We feel sure such an
appeal will not be made in vain. There must be at this time loyalty and help given to
those who have to resist the present demands of the Employers, and we feel sure it will be
given, and thereby enable a successful resistance to be put up.
Our colleague, Mr. W. R. Smith, drew attention to one of the most important items
in which the Labour movement believes the development of home resources, particularly
in regard to the land, much of which is derelict.
T. F. RICHARDS, General President.
W. R. TOWNLEY,
MARY J. BELL-RICHARDS,
LEN. SMITH,
W. R. SMITH,
E. L. POULTON, General Secretary.

HOW TO GET BACK TO PROSPERITY.

Mr. H. J. Bostock on Fallacy of Reducing Wages
and Importance of Developing the Home Market.

On the 14th inst., the evening prior to the business meeting of the Boot Manufacturers' Federation, the President (Mr. John H. Marlow) and the committee of the Northampton Boot Manufacturers' Association received the delegates at a dinner, which was held in the Masonic Hall, Northampton.

"To the Prosperity of the Federation," was the theme of the chairman's toast. He expressed the great pleasure Northampton felt in welcoming the leaders of the Federation, and referred to the importance of the shoe manufacturing industry, employing as it did over 100,000 operatives. The Federation controlled over 90 per cent. of the employers, and the relations between themselves and the National Union was reflected in the fact that for 30 years there had been no official strike or lockout, as a result of which the shoe trade was looked to with respect and envy by the great industries of the country.

In responding to the toast, said: "Aggregations of capital could be justified only if the owners thereof used them to promote the public weal."

"In these days," Mr. Bostock proceeded, "everyone was asking what was ahead. The country to-day, instead of being a land fit for heroes to live in, was one in which many who fought for it had now to exist on the dole, and were often abused as a consequence. The dole was sometimes abused, but if there had been no dole there would have been revolution."

Continuing, the president said, "there was no greater fallacy than to think they were going to get back to prosperity by reducing wages. The home market had lost purchasing power and could recover it only by increasing production. They needed to rearrange the balance of production. There were too many engaged in some trades such as coal and engineering and not enough in other industries such as building.

No pre-conceived political notions should stand in the way of promoting better production, in which connection he particularly mentioned food. With regard to manufactures he pointed to the changed positions of the country and the rest of the world. There was export trade to be done, but it was to be fought for. They had in the home market a rich field, but it must be developed on new lines. Old methods would not suit present-day conditions."

In conclusion Mr. Bostock stated that he believed in advertisement. It would pay this country to advertise its advantages as a tourist resort for Americans. The United States had no history, and Europe offered many attractions to the wealthy of the United States, who came with much money, and left a considerable amount behind them. Anything that increased the flow of money would have a beneficial effect not only on the hotel industry but also upon the manufacture of antiques which was now an important trade in this country. (Laughter.) -The Shoe and Leather Record, 17th July, 1925.

BOOT MANUFACTURERS' FEDERATION.

Annual Meeting in Northampton.

The following are extracts from the reports issued in connection with the annual meeting the thirty-fifth - held in Northampton on the 14th and 15th July, 1925.

In opening the proceedings Mr. H. J. Bostock, J.P., said amongst other things "that without an increase in the purchasing power of consumers in the great industrial areas, the home demand for shoes could not be stimulated. The time was long overdue for the problem of unemployment to be seriously attacked at its roots."

Annual Output.
Statistics of output for the year 1923 were compiled from information at the disposal of the Federation for the purpose of the memorandum of evidence submitted by the president, Mr. H. J. Bostock, before Sir Arthur Balfour's Committee on Industry and Trade on January 29th, 1925, and so useful was the information considered that it was decided to complete the Federation records as far as possible for the years over which statistics of the industry had been collected. The results had been checked by an outside source, and in submitting them for the use of members, the committee desired to emphasise that they were to be regarded as estimates of output based upon a partial survey of the industry only.

The figures were:—Output for the year 1920, 91,136,000 pairs; 1921, 77.744,000; 1922, 96,053,000; 1923, 100,490,000; 1924, 111,383,000.

On the 15th inst., Mr. C. W. Horrell was elected to succeed Mr. H. J. Bostock as President of the Federation. We can only hope that the same broad outlook will be continued under the new presidency as was the case under the presidency of Mr. Bostock, and trust that the trade will, within the near future, emerge into more prosperous times.

TUBERCULOSIS.
Decrease in Child Mortality. Scientists on Need of Public Education.
Tuberculosis in childhood was discussed at the Eleventh Annual Conference of the
National Association for the Prevention of Tuberculosis, at the Hall of the Royal Society
of Medicine, Wimpole Street, W., yesterday. The Hon. Sir Arthur Stanley, chairman of
the Council, presided, and he was supported by Sir Kingsley Wood, M.P., Parliamentary
Secretary to the Ministry of Health. Public Health authorities throughout the British
Isles were represented, and members of the medical profession were present from America,
Austria, the Argentine, Australia, Denmark, Russia, Spain, and Switzerland.
SIR KINGSLEY WOOD, in opening the conference, paid a tribute to the work of the
Association. Dealing with the prevention of tuberculosis, he said its chief
birthplace and home was the sunless, ill-ventilated and overcrowded
dwelling, and it was for that reason that the Ministry of Health was
giving great attention to the housing difficulty. They were more hopeful with
regard to tuberculosis in childhood. In ten years the mortality among children from
pulmonary tuberculosis had fallen by about 46 per cent., and from non-pulmonary tuber-
culosis by about 42 per cent., while the number of new cases notified of both types of
the disease had also substantially diminished.
PROFESSOR PIRQUET, of the University of Vienna, in a paper on "The Significance
of Tuberculosis in Childhood and in Youth," referred to investigations in Vienna which
went to show that tuberculosis in children was not so dangerous as we formerly believed.
But for middle age, he added, tuberculosis represented the very worst
disease. The Times, 7th July, 1925.
[One more testimony to what, for many years we have been preaching, viz., If the
people had good homes; where the sun could get into the rooms and overcrowding was
done away with, still further improvements would be brought about, and Consumption
still further reduced. It, further, is interesting that Sir Kingsley Wood should make the
said statement, and also add to it, "that the Ministry of Health was giving great attention
to the housing difficulty."
It is to be hoped that after such a statement the "attention" will be in a greatly
concentrated form and quickly result in such a number of houses being erected as will
fully meet the demand. Such a policy would also have another result, viz.: Find work
for a great many people, who at present are without employment.-E.L.P., G.S.]
TUBERCULOSIS IN SHOEHANDS.
Hinckley Medical Officer of Health on High Death-Rate.
That tuberculosis is prevalent among the boot and shoe operatives of Earl Shilton
and Barwell was the statement made by Dr. J. E. O'Connor (medical officer of health),
at this afternoon's meeting of the Hinckley R.D.C.
He said that at Earl Shilton the number of deaths had been exceptionally high.
Referring to smallpox, he observed that if contacts were vaccinated within three days
they would in all probability escape infection.
If vaccinated within four days it was very doubtful whether they would do so.
Commenting on the value of receiving medical reports, Mr. Dan Astley, chairman
of the Council, recalled that it was not so long ago when the Council refused to let the
medical officer of health read his report, because they considered it to be "all bosh." —
Leicester Mercury, 20th July, 1925.

SOME OF THE THINGS THE WORKERS WANT.
By Fred Hewitt, Editor Machinists' Monthly Journal.
The workers want employment.
The worker wants steady employment. He wants to bring about a condition whereby
he will feel that each day's work may not be his last.
He wants to work not more than 44 hours per week.
He wants his fair share of the wealth he helps to produce. He wants to be employed
in surroundings as comfortable as the nature of his employment will permit.
He wants sanitary workshops.
He wants protection against unnecessary risk.
He wants to enjoy a full measure of freedom guaranteed all citizens of a free country.
He wants compensation laws passed for his and his family's protection
He wants the right to live and let live.
So far, partial success has crowned their efforts to obtain these, but a full realisation
of their desires will only come when they join in a common cause. Then, and then only,
will the workers obtain those things they want, and to which they are entitled.

THE WORLD WE CREATE.
By Emma Playton Seabury.
A song for the idle who roam at will,
And a shrug for the men who shirk,
But who will sing of the prick and sting,
Of the bitter taunt and the cruel fling,
To the man who has no work
The man who will delve if he had the chance.
With saw or with plane would build.
Oh, who can tell of the bitter shame
To sink from man's estate,
And to humbly take for his children's sake
The coin that is flung! Oh, the hearts that break
In this world that we create!
For the plan of God was the same for all,
The right to be and do;
If only we would learn to see
That the world was made for you and me,
And not for a chosen few.
Oh, the saddest sight in the world to-day
Is our neighbour passing by
With a weary pace and a blanching face,
Who is out of work and out of the race.
And we make it you and I. Justice, U.S.A.
*
*
*
Then let us make a right New World;
Where joy and peace shall be,
And work for all shall be the plan;
Based on true equity.
A world wherein real help is given;
Where men are truly free.
Where loads are light and life is bright;
For all, for you and me.-E.L.P., G.S.

WASHINGTON HOURS CONVENTION: RATIFICATIONS.
In answer to a question in the House of Commons on the 24th June, asking how many
countries had ratified the International Eight-Hour Day Labour Convention, the Parlia-
mentary Secretary to the Ministry of Labour made the following statement:-
The Washington Hours Convention has been ratified by Austria, Bulgaria
Czecho-Slovakia, Greece, India, Italy, and Roumania. The ratifications of Austria
and Italy are conditional upon ratification by other States. The Ministry of Labour
Gazette, July, 1925.
CHANGES IN COST OF LIVING: STATISTICS FOR 1st JULY, 1925.
Summary: Average Increases since July, 1914.
All Items included
Food only
FOOD.
73 per cent.
67
"
At 1st July the average level of retail prices of the principal articles of food showed a
slight upward movement as compared with 2nd June. This advance was mainly due to a
rise in the average prices of eggs, butter and potatoes (old potatoes having been displaced
by new potatoes at higher prices). There was relatively little movement in the prices
of the other articles of food included in the statistics.
As a net result of all the changes recorded, the average increase over the level of
July, 1914, in the retail prices (in Great Britain and Northern Ireland) of the articles of
food included in these statistics was about 67 per cent. at 1st July, as compared with
about 66 per cent. at 2nd June and 62 per cent. at the corresponding date in 1924. The
Ministry of Labour Gazette, July, 1925.

SPECIAL NOTICE TO MEMBERS.
Every precaution is taken by us to ensure accuracy in these Reports, our correspondents
being men on whom we can rely; should, however, any errors creep into them, upon the
same being pointed out to us, we shall be most happy to give the same prominence to the
correction as to the original matter. As these Reports are exclusively for the use of Members,
the information contained therein must not be divulged.

MONTHLY REPORT.

24TH AUGUST, 1925.

FELLOW MEMBERS,

TRADE

during the past month has been somewhat slack. The figures in the Ministry
of Labour Gazette are set out hereunder :-

BOOT AND SHOE INDUSTRY.

Employment showed a further decline in most districts, and on the whole was worse
than in June, and worse also than a year ago. An increased amount of short time was
worked during the month. As has been the case for many months, employment was worse
in the heavy boot trade than in the light trade.

The percentage of workpeople unemployed, as indicated by the unemployment books
lodged at Employment Exchanges, was 11.8 on 27th July, 1925, as compared with 10.2 on
22nd June, 1925, and 7.5 on 28th July, 1924.

Returns from firms employing 36,000 operatives in the week ended 25th July, 1925,
showed that about 38 per cent. of these operatives worked short time in that week, to the
extent of about 12 hours each, on an average.

In London employment was quiet and slightly worse than in June. At both Leicester
and Northampton a decline was reported, and employment was generally depressed, with
much short-time working, though there was a slight recovery at Northampton in the last
week of the month in consequence of holiday orders. Employment was bad, and worse
than in the previous month, at Hinckley; few firms were on full time. At Raunds employ-
ment remained fairly good, as a result of work on Government contracts; but in the
Rushden district it declined, a large number of women being unemployed. Practically all
operatives at Wellingborough were on short time, and employment continued bad. At
Kettering less than threequarter time was worked on an average, though here, also, as at
Northampton, there was a slight recovery in the last week of the month; although women
were fairly well employed, there was considerable unemployment among male operatives

Employment was poor, and worse than in June, at Leeds, though, as in previous months,
factories making light footwear remained fairly busy. At Stafford employment was slack,
and showed a decline, short time again being prevalent; at Norwich there was a decline,
nearly all firms being on short time. A slight improvement was reported in the heavy
boot trade of Kingswood, many firms reverting to full-time working; but at Bristol


---
Here is the extracted text:
469
LOANS FROM GUARDIANS.
The General Council of the Trades Union Congress has recently had
submitted to them the question as to whether Boards of Guardians could
recover relief granted to necessitous persons on the basis of a "loan." The
reason the matter was brought before the General Council being due to the fact
that in one County Court the Judge decided that Guardians could not recover,
whilst in another Court the Judge decided that the Guardians could recover.
The General Council have therefore taken legal advice upon the subject
and the opinion of Counsel consulted is that Guardians CAN RECOVER.
This is a most important matter and the opinion of Counsel will be found on
pages 484-485, from which it will be seen that there is no doubt about the
question, which is one of considerable importance.
INDUSTRIAL AND POLITICAL.
The past month has been a month wherein history has been made, if for
one matter only, viz.: the ferment in the industry of Mining. The position
is a notable one when the vast issues involved are considered. The threatened
reduction of wages and worsening of conditions of employment in the said
industry were only actually faced up to by the Government when it was seen
that there would be a unity in the ranks of the workers which had never on
such a scale been achieved before. The General Council of the Trade Union
Congress called together the Executives of the various Unions on the 29th
July, 1925, for consultative purposes, as also to report the actual position.
The net result of the meeting was to promise aid to our comrades who were
likely to be called upon to bear the brunt of the fighting. Had matters
developed, we should have had to follow up the circular of the 31st ultimo
authorising us to deal with the question from a financial standpoint.
The Trade Union movement will, as a result of the present-day develop-
ments, undoubtedly have to alter some of its machinery, especially on its
financial side, with a view to greater and speedier help being given to those
who have to stand off in disputes, and thereby render effective assistance to
ensure success in carrying out a policy wherein all have had opportunity in
shaping. The setting up of a commission by the Government, likewise the
subvention to the Mining Industry, has not by any means settled the differences
that exist; on the contrary it has only secured that employment goes on for
the time being while an enquiry takes place as to what are the actual conditions
obtaining in the Mining Industry.
Meantime, it is incumbent upon the whole Trade Union movement to take
such action and adopt such methods as will make our machinery work effectively
in practice as well as on paper if called upon to operate. The times, while
somewhat dark, are also full of possibility for a move forward provided that
wise methods and tactics are adopted in pursuance of the carrying out of
policy.

The Labour Party did its best to improve these measures from the workers'
standpoint. The bald fact is, however, that what counts is the number of
votes recorded in the various Divisions, and here the Labour Party is in a
hopeless minority, proving once more how necessary it is to increase the number

strength which will be a majority vote instead of the present minority. The
lesson is plain-Work, work, work in and for the cause of Labour so that we
are ready at all times for an Election.
MANCHESTER DISPUTE.
For a considerable time past we have paid attention to the boot and shoe
factories in Manchester, and visits have been paid both by the General Officers
and the Organisers to the district.
Quite recently the firm of Messrs. Johnson & Co. refused to honour the
National Agreement, and on the 8th inst., the operatives on our instructions,
left work, and were on dispute until the 19th inst., when Mr. D. McCarthy,
with Mr. J. Mason, the local branch secretary, entered into an agreement with
the firm, whereby a considerable advance has been made. A number of the
operatives resumed work on the 20th inst., and arrangements were made for
the others to commence within one week.
This proves once again our determination to bring all manufacturers in
the industry up to, at least, the National conditions.

THE GENERAL PRESIDENT'S REPORT.
60 LADY SOMERSET ROAD,
KENTISH TOWN,
LONDON, N.W. 5.
17th August, 1925.
FELLOW MEMBERS,
THE MINING DISPUTE. Probably the most important happening during the past
month has been that in connection with the Miners. So serious was the outlook that the
General Council of the Trades Union Congress immediately rose to the occasion by calling
a special conference. Whilst those of us, and the whole movement generally, have been
clamouring for peace, the Miners for seven months have been endeavouring to inform the
Owners that under no circumstances could they accept a worse position than they had,
and for the time being were prepared to negotiate, so long as the position was not made
intolerable. To this the Owners paid no heed, and so within two days of the notices
expiring, the General Council called an extraordinary conference of the whole of the
executive councils of all unions affiliated to that body. As a result the Central Hall,
Westminster, was fairly full. After a very serious consideration of the position placed
before the delegates in private assembly, they, with an united voice carried an unanimous
resolution that if the lockout notices were enforced, every trade unionist in the Kingdom
would stand by the miners in their trouble. That fiat was issued and made known to the
Cabinet. They (the Cabinet) decided they would provide sufficient money for the Owners
to make up the difference they claimed would be lost if the miners refused the reduction.
This means the Owners are getting their way, and are imposing their will upon all. They
are not reducing the miners' wages, but are receiving the proposed reductions from the
taxpayer.
We need not stop to enquire what form this strike would have taken had the Owners
refused to give way by withdrawing the lockout notices. One thing is certain; the
refusal to handle coal would have brought about a stoppage in all industries-including
the boot and shoe industry. We should then have taken the members into our confidence
and advised them as to the course to pursue. We are convinced that we should have
supported the Miners, both morally and financially. It must not be forgotten that that
Trades Union Congress with its 4,328,235 members; 2,000,000 ex-members, and at least
4,000,000 sympathisers would have brought the country to a complete standstill. What
would have happened then? No one knows.
We have always been satisfied that only madmen recommend a decrease in wages,
because it is wages and wage-earners that create 80 per cent. of the trade of any country.
We are pleased to know that for the moment this matter is to be investigated-once
more-although it would be interesting to know what this stupid Government will do
when the Report is presented. Will it be shelved as was the Sankey Report? It should
be remembered that the natural tendency of every executive-trade union executives
are not exempt-is always to be loath to accept reservations of a smaller number of persons
than themselves.
We are led to understand there will be four or five persons on this new Commission.
Who can imagine the British Cabinet-all tories to the backbone, and invariably all stupid
-would agree to accept the findings of five persons, no matter how eminent they may be ?
The Labour movement must not lose sight of the situation. We can only say that one
hopes those people who voted for the present Tory administration will remember they are
responsible for the position and should go in "sack-cloth and ashes."
UNEMPLOYMENT.-A special Trades Union Congress was held a week prior to the
above to deal with the question of unemployment, a report of which appears on page 441
of the July Report.
This question has been the sole subject for which some of us have tried to find a
remedy for over forty years. It is still with us. Three years ago we recommended at a
conference in the Kingsway Hall, London, that all trade unionists should stop for one
day as a protest against the treatment of the unemployed. One of the most prominent
labour leaders protested against the suggestion adding that it would be better to give the
unemployed one day's pay. No stoppage has taken place; none of the unemployed
had the pay. Another prominent labour leader said if the operatives stopped work, it
would be difficult to get them to restart. These people were reminded that we could
have two days off for a coronation, but not one for ourselves. Many felt that if a stoppage
B

above to deal with the question of unemployment, a report of which appears on page 441
of the July Report.

had taken place the employees may not start when required. I agree that even in our
Union we have instances when some of our members come out on strike as a protest and

Still, we are living in serious times, and the only solution is a majority of Labour

WOOLLEN INDUSTRY. —After the Congress had dealt with the Miners' question, the
position of the woollen industry was explained by Alderman Ben Turner, president of
Textile Workers' Union. Although this organisation have a Guarantee Fund similar to
our own which has been in operation for twenty-five years, the Employers brought about
the stoppage. A Mr. Martin corrected my statement made at our Jubilee conference,
that we were the only union in the world with a monetary Guarantee against a strike or
to reduce wages at a time like the present. The sympathy of the Congress went out to
Mr. Turner and his people. It is to be hoped that all Unions will endeavour to give all
lockout, and I shall now be able to remind him that he has broken his parole by wanting
the financial assistance they can. This dispute has been settled for the time being.

SILEBY has again been visited only to find that Mr. F. Leader, the branch secretary,
is still away, and owing to bad trade an old member of ours who is doing the secretarial
work feels organising is likely to be slow for some weeks to come.

LEEDS. —I have had to intervene in a dispute at the Co-operative Wholesale Society's
Meanwood Road Works, Leeds, where, because the management could not get their prices
for a skiving operation in the closing room, the matter was taken to the Board of Arbitration
by my colleagues in that City. Before a settlement was reached, two of the four women
operatives who had given evidence were paid off and dismissed, being given wages in
lieu of notice. The other operatives came out in sympathy and refused to return to work.
My presence at their meeting only had the effect of them asking me to see the Directors
at Leman Street, London, E.1, the following day. I met the Directors, not as a trade union
official, but as an old co-operator. They refused to discuss the matter until the operatives
had returned to work.

I returned to Leeds the same evening, and advised the operatives to return to work,
although I felt the Directorate through their Management had aggravated the situation
by tampering with witnesses by discharging them whilst the prices were still before the
Board of Arbitration. I appealed to the Secretary to the C.W.S. —who is a lawyer—as to
what a judge would have thought of counsel tampering with witnesses. However, we did
our best to get these girls reinstated, and strange to relate another woman had been started
girls had been promoted to fill the positions. To still further obtain benefit for their own
piecework, which we should say was done to cheat
the piecework prices. Although this information was placed before the directors four
days after resumption of work it did not seem to penetrate into their minds. My impression
is that they are determined to stand by their own Management. I have expressed to them
the opinion that we are disappointed with the settlement which was to the effect that one
of the girls would be reinstated within six weeks. The other one they would not reinstate
because she lost time - with the firm's consent. This is the most unsatisfactory dealing
I have ever had in connection with the C.W.S. factories. To prove our contention was
the correct one I have quoted the request of Sir Arthur Steel-Maitland, Minister of Labour,
to the woollen manufacturers, to allow the operatives to return to work on old conditions
pending investigation; this they did, but the C.W.S. Directors refused my suggestion:—
That old conditions should prevail and all operatives return to work pending investigation.
As stated above, they refused, and I have informed them I am not surprised that some
of my colleagues describe C.W.S. Directors and Committees as being more difficult to deal
with than private employers.

LEICESTER. A visit has been paid to one of the large firms in Leicester, because
they had made arrangements to "stand off" their operatives for three weeks at a time.
It was evident from the examination made, that whilst no blame can be attached to our
members, the employer and management or the employer through the managers and
foremen, had been made acquainted of the fact that after twelve days "standing off"
our members are entitled to Suspension Benefit. We shall now look upon this firm with
suspicion as they did not hesitate to tell us that our members were entitled to the benefits
for which they paid. It was my duty to inform the firm that if this was the spirit in which
members of the Federation were going to deal with the Union, then our funds could very
easily be diminished by such action.

I have not given up the idea, and shall consult my colleagues in Leicester, as to whether
this question ought not to be reported to the Boot Manufacturers' Association in Leicester.
Personally, I shall fully report the facts to Miss Ruth Tomlinson, secretary to the Boot
Manufacturers' Federation, because any unfriendly action by a firm against the Union will
not make for harmonious working, but will have a contrary effect.

TRADE BOARD PROSECUTION.
Repairing Shop Handed Over to Employees.
On Tuesday, George Quick Latham, managing director of the St. Crispin Boot
Repairing Co. Ltd., Bristol, was summoned at the Bristol Police Court for offences under
the Trade Boards Acts.
Mr. C. L. M. Langham presecuted on behalf of the Ministry of Labour, and Mr. W. S. A.
Brown represented the defendant, who pleaded guilty.
Mr. Langham said that on 4th February an inspection was made of the defendant's
wages book, and it was found that he was employing two adult workers and one apprentice,
all of whom had been paid less than the rates fixed by the Repairing Trade Board. The
adult workers were Hart and Dunn. Hart was being paid 42s. 6d. instead of 60s. for 48
hours, and Dunn 48s. instead of 60s. Hart had been underpaid £84 18s. 11d. and Dunn
£56 2s. 8d.
On 19th February the defendant gave an undertaking to pay the arrears before 7th
March. Subsequently receipts were sent to London alleging that the arrears had been
paid. These proved to be false, no arrears having been paid. This was the serious part
of the case.
Evidence was given by Dunn that the defendant told him he could not pay the arrears,
and if he had to the works would have to be closed. Rather than have this happen witness
signed the receipt. There was no compulsion, Similar evidence was given by Hart.
For the defence Mr. Brown referred to the financial trouble the firm had had, and said
it was well known that the boot trade was bad at present. The works had now been
handed over to the employees to carry on and make what they could out of them.
a lenient course because the defendant had
The magistrates fined the defendant £2 10s. in respect of each case and ordered him
to pay £5 costs. They said they adopted
derived nothing from the offence. - Shoe and Leather Record, 31st July, 1925.

FELLOW MEMBERS,

In my last report I mentioned that notice to cease work had been given at Messrs.
Johnson, Newton Heath, Manchester. Up to then the firm refused to entertain the
Holiday (Provision) Scheme in any form and often offered the opinion that the Manu-
facturers' Federation were wrong in recognising such a scheme. Their workers, who are
now all members of the Union, thought otherwise and, at their request, Mr. Mason and I
made a further application for Holiday Pay. Though the firm is not federated, as none
of them in this area are, they wanted us to make all pay regardless of our membership in
any particular shop. Mr. Mason and I agreed, with the members' consent, that if Messrs.
Johnson would guarantee holiday payment for one year and put the wages right of those
not getting the rates within four weeks, we, on our side, would guarantee that the Union
would use all its powers to make the other firms pay at the end of that period. This did
not satisfy the firm. The notice expired before the holidays. The shop was picketted
on 10th August, and none went into work. After eight days we settled on the definite
offer to pay four days' holiday money next year (the 48 hours are worked in five days),
and the other day in all probability would be paid, but that might depend on how the
Union was dealing with other firms. This meant two days next Christmas, one at Easter
and one at Whitsuntide, and the probability of a day at August, making a week's pay
right in four weeks. We placed the firm's offer before the members on 19th August and
without contributions from the workers. The wages, with one exception, would be put
they agreed to accept it as it came so near to the request made for a twelve months' guar-
antee for holiday payment. It was our desire in the first instance to avoid a conflict.
and because of that we made our claim exceptional and moderate. During the month we
have added about 120 members to the Manchester Branch.

D. McCARTHY.
During the month further efforts have been made to reorganise the operatives working
at Faire Bros., Mansfield, trading as Premier Stiffener Co., where pressure has been put
upon the workpeople to break away from the Union, and a general system of terrorisation
has been put into operation by discharge and threatened discharge. Every difficulty is
put in the way by the firm, and the general position is one where methods of publicity will
have to be adopted in order to draw attention of the trade in general to firms of this character.
Thralls & Co. Ltd., are in the same position. About 50 girls are engaged at this firm.
Fear of discharge operates here, a difficulty which is surprising when the position of the
district is taken into consideration, this being a large mining area and a vast majority of
these girls being daughters of men engaged in this depressed industry. Still there are
some compensations. We have proceeded with the organisation of the operatives working
at the Mansfield Shoe Co., about 60 new members being made and good prospects for a
further increase. We hope to attain in the very near future 100 per cent. in the Union.
Statements are compiled for each department, and to assure their proper application it is
essential that both male and female operatives should belong to our organisation.

Attention has also been given to the firm of Ward & Co. All our conditions are
observed by this firm and, strange to say, this is used as an excuse by the operatives work-
ing here why they should not join the Union. It is an unusual psychology, but still, with
persistent propaganda, we hope to overcome this difficulty.
LEN SMITH.
25th August, 1925.
J. H. BAUM.

I concluded my last report with a reference to the miners' dispute, which, at that
time, looked like developing into the biggest thing in the history of the Trade Union Move-
ment of this country. The news of settlement, or shall I say postponement, came as a
surprise to many, as the attitude of the Government had been one that appeared to leave
the issue to be fought out. One cannot help thinking (and the Prime Minister's speech
in the House confirms this) that the solid front presented by the Trade Unions was responsi
ble for the change af attitude, and while the way out cannot be looked upon as a final
solution, it must be the duty of the Movement to use its forces to see that the ultimate
decision shall be on lines which will be both sound and just to the workers and the comm-
munity.

The work of organising during the month has been somewhat hampered by the holidays,
to say nothing about the state of trade which one has to admit is very bad. This, of course,
is one reason why the operatives should join our Union, having regard to the way our
benefits protect them in these days of trade depression. Both men and women can resist
temptation better if there is some income entering the home, and so the conditions won can
be maintained and the possibilities of further improvement brought nearer.

Here is the text extracted from the image:
477
The places visited during the month are Whetstone, Blaby, Cosby, Sileby, Shilton,
Anstey and Wigston. Meetings have been held in most of these, when the attention
shown was quite good, and the interest displayed was all one could desire, and we feel that
it is only a question of time when we shall have the united support of the workers in these
areas.
But persistent and continued effort is needed in some of these places. The differences
between them is very great, on the one hand there is a strong robust feeling behind the
branch or section, on the other there is apathy and indifference. No one objects to our
conditions in the sense that they are not good and desirable, but the impression one gets is
that some operatives think it will all come to them as a matter of course, and that they
need not specially exert themselves to secure them.
While not desiring to pillory any employer, experience teaches that only by perfect
organisation can we get the full recognition which is essential for our purpose. The con-
ditions of to-day are not final ones; there can be no such thing as finality in our work, and
at the moment with thousands of operatives out of employment in the country, no centre
being from this problem, surely a further reduction of working hours becomes an urgent
necessity.
The average figures for this year show an increase of 16 per cent. over last year, and
when to this is added the short time obtaining at many factories it is apparent that our
industry has reached a stage when a revision of working hours has become a matter of
great urgency.
But the only way by which we can bring this about is by the workers recognising the
need through membership of the Union. It is for this and other reasons we must continue
our work of educating and instructing those who fail in their duty towards their neigh-
bours. There is also the further point: we are frequently told that wide and drastic
changes are needed in our social system if justice is to be done to the workers. This few
would deny, but I am persuaded that working-class advancement will come sooner and
better if we have working-class organisation.
22nd August, 1925.
WALTER R. SMITH.
INTERNATIONAL CONFERENCE OF LABOUR AND SOCIALIST WOMEN.
The Labour and Socialist International is calling a Conference of women delegates
of their affiliated organisations to meet on 21st August, the day before the full Congress
opens at Marseilles. The agenda will deal with the present state of organisation among
Socialist women in the different countries on which a report is being compiled, on informa-
tion sent by each, and of discussion on resolutions dealing with the aims and methods of
the Socialist Movement amongst women. Representation at the Conference will be on
the same basis as that of the full Congress, and the delegates will therefore be appointed
by the Labour Party and the I.L.P., their votes being allotted in the proportion of 29 to 1.
The Labour Party delegates so far appointed are Mrs. Harrison Bell, Mrs. Dollan, and
Dr. Marion Phillips. The I.L.P. have appointed Miss Bondfield, Miss Dorothy Jewson,
and Miss Pallister, and it has been arranged that any organisations represented on the
Standing Joint Committee of Industrial Women's Organisations may add to the number
and they will then be included in the delegation. Mrs. M. J. Bell-Richards (Boot and Shoe
Union), and Dr. Bentham, Mrs. Tawney and Miss Rose Smith Rose (Fabian Society),
have been thus added to the delegation. The discussion will be in the main on the organisa-
tion of women politically, and a very full report of this in the different countries has been
prepared, which we propose to publish in a shortened form next month. Other questions
will be the aims and methods of Socialist Women, and the Standing Joint Committee has
prepared a resolution dealing with these subjects, which will be submitted after agreement
of the Labour Party in the name of the British Delegation. — The Labour Woman, August,
1925.

BRANCH REPORTS.
ARBROATH. —Trade still remains depressed in our area, and seems
likely to continue. We had hopes that something might develop out of sample
orders received here, but nothing further has been heard so far. If we fail to
hear good news of employment, the Government is determined to make us hear
bad news where unemployment benefit is concerned. All readers of the
Monthly Report will be aware of the ferocious conditions attached to receipt
of extended benefit after September. The public seem fairly indifferent to
the cruelty of it all. Without excuse and simply to harass the poor "out-of-
work," all the rotten conditions of past periods have been resuscitated again
to squeeze out thousands from benefit, and so lessen unemployment. During
the past special periods each of those conditions have been tried, and then
been abandoned after severe criticism, yet they come up again as though
should not lie down to it. We have witnessed recently what a united front can
accomplish with the enemy, and this matter calls for a further display of the
same strength. The twelve millions who are working should stand by those
who are idle, and see that they get emergency or extended benefit as it is called
instead of being forced to go to Parish Councils for relief. It has been freely
mooted for some time back that the Government meant to introduce other
machinery to deal with unemployment distress. They were to confine the
Insurance scheme to rigid principles, and not make it a compassionate fund,
but instead of asking those who were rejected to go to the Poor Law, they
were to establish a scheme of “Distress Relief Grants” to meet the position
of idle workers who failed to qualify under the Insurance scheme. No one
would quarrel with that plan if it had matured, but here we are with Parlia-
ment on holiday, with winter rapidly approaching, and with no provision of
this kind brought forward. If the Labour Party had devoted their time to the
task of securing an alternative, subsidiary, and intermediate scheme for the
relief of the unemployed, instead of waging such a hopeless fight against the
tightening of regulations of Unemployment Insurance, then there might have
been a useful compromise effected. The Trade Union Congress and the Labour
Party should still fight for an alternative scheme of relief, and they will get the
backing of every Parish Council and Poor Law Authority in the country.
We are looking forward to the visit of our General President, and hope we shall
get good meetings of our members to hear him. Mr. Richards has been often
enough in Scotland to understand the conditions here, and we hope great
good will come out of his visit. He will be interested in seeing our factories
again after a lapse of two years.

CHESHAM.— Pleased to report a slight improvement in trade here.
CHESTERFIELD. —There is a slight improvement in trade here, but
still we have members out of work. We must congratulate the Trade Union
Congress for bringing about solidarity in the Miners’ fight. We shall want
more of it in the future, as the real fight with Capitalism has commenced,
and with solidarity we can win every time.

CORK.—Trade still continues good, the Lee Boot Co. building additional
premises.
DUNDALK.—Trade, though showing a slight improvement, is as yet far
from what might be desired. The fact that some of our shopmates are on the
dole, while others are doing labouring work speaks for itself. All our members
will be returning to work on Monday after one week’s holiday minus pay.

EDINBURGH.— The members are pleased that the General President is
going to pay us a visit at the end of the month. We trust they will turn out
and give him a welcome. The firm recently organised have now given all the

HIGHAM & RUSHDEN. - Trade during the month has generally been
very poor, with a few factories running on full time, most employers stating
that they have extreme difficulty in keeping on. There seems no sign of
improvement at present. Most factories are closing for a long holiday.
KILMARNOCK. Trade has been fair during the last month, but unfor-
tunately our Kilmaurs members are still out from the lack of work. The
repairing trade is fairly steady, due, no doubt to the approaching holidays.
We are pleased to report that one of our members has been selected by the
N.C.L.C. as one of six students to attend international summer schools held
abroad under the auspices of the International Federation of Trade Unions.
Our shopmate, Thomas Campbell, is the only one N.C.L.C. student selected
from Scotland, and along with the two others will attend a school at Brunnsvic,
Sweden, for fourteen days, and three other students are going to Prague,
Czecho-Slovakia. We are very sorry to hear of the death of Mrs. Bleakley, and
are sure we echo the sentiments of the members of this branch when we extend
to the now doubly bereaved family our deepest sympathy.
KINGSWOOD AND DISTRICT. - Trade is, to use a local colloquialism,
"hanging in the breeching," and a good deal of uncertainty exists as to the
prospects of a busy season in view of the serious industrial situation. Our
best wishes go out to the Miners in their great struggle for the right to live.
LEEDS No. 1. We regret to report that so far as the heavy and medium
trade here is very bad, but so far as the lighter class of work is concerned is
fairly moderate. We wish to heartily thank our General President, Mr. T. F.
Richards, for his services in the C.W.S. dispute. We would once more draw
the attention of our members who are in arrears, that if at all possible it is
hoped they will make an effort to see that should sickness or unemployment
overtake them, that they are entitled to Union benefits. We also regret to
report that our old Branch President, Mr. John Buckle, J.P., is seriously ill,
and we sympathise with him and his family during his long and serious illness.
LEICESTER No. 2. - We are sorry to report that trade in this centre is
very bad. Quite a number of manufacturers have made the holiday the
opportunity of a long suspension, which means that we are having to deal with
a good number claiming Suspension and Out-of-Work Benefit. There are times
when we feel that those working men and women who helped to return the
present Government have become awakened to the fact they have backed
the wrong "hoss." Where are the good times and full employment which
were promised? Echo answers "where."
LIVERPOOL. Trade has been on the quiet side during the past month.
NORTHAMPTON.-Trade remains quiet here resulting in large numbers
discharged and suspended. Sickness normal. We regret having to record
that Jas. Gribble has been compelled to leave the town, but are pleased to know
he has got into a suitable business at Hastings-a Boarding Hotel and when
he gets settled we feel sure he will be pleased to welcome friends. At anyrate,

PLYMOUTH. We have been watching the development of the coal
crisis with interest, and wondering what would be the end if the colliers' wheels
ceased to spin and no more coal was landed on our shores We can only see
the few remaining markets closed indefinitely, perhaps for ever, semi-starvation
for the miners, and short commons for thousands more of our fellow workers,
illions of money frittered away in the struggle, pits closed never to be opened
ain, hundreds more added to the ranks of the unemployed, and the standard
life considerably lowered throughout the land; then bloodshed and revolu-
cion such as no Englishman can contemplate without a shudder. We have no
desire to see our country reduced to chaos, but if a stoppage occurred our sym-
pathies would unreservedly be on the side of the miners, for their wages appear
to be on the borderline of starvation, and should in the name of humanity
and reason, rise and not fall. How this can be accomplished, by greater
efficiency, amalgamation, unification, nationalisation, or a fortunate turn in
the international situation, we must leave, but we must say without hesitation,
that the nation should adopt any system known to the genius of man, in order
to secure a living wage for the miners. It was Asquith, we believe, said on one
occasion, "that there was nothing higher and nothing deeper in the Liberal
faith than this: that the interest of the few must give place to the interest
of the many." Neither can we imagine a Prime Minister so dull as to think
that the miners formed a small minority on one side, while on the other stood
the community ready to protect itself at his bidding; the facts are far different
and if our Premier would only take a vote of the electorate, on the question:
"Are you in favour of a living wage for the miners of Great Britain?" he
would soon find the community on the side of the miners, whatever the change
involved in the coal-mining industry. Do not let us be deceived by such

NORTHANTS DISTRICT COUNCIL.
Minutes of Quarterly Meeting of the Northants District Council, held in the Co-
operative Hall, Desborough, on Saturday, April 18th, 1925, commencing at 2.30 p.m.
PRESENT: Mr. W. Langley, J.P., the President, presided; a large number of delegates
also attended. There were also present, as representing the National Council, Mr. C.
Bates. Mr. W. Townley, Mr. L. Poole, and Mr. T. F. Richards, the General President.
The Executive Committee of the Desborough Branch also attended the meeting.
MINUTES. The report of the previous meeting having been circulated, it was resolved
that same be accepted as read and approved. Same were signed by the Chairman.
Mr. T. F. Richards, General President, then introduced a discussion on the subject
of "THE LEGALISING OF THE NATIONAL AGREEMENTS."
At the outset Mr. Richards dealt with the unique organisation of our own industry,
Unlike most industries, the boot and shoe trade was catered for entirely by our own Union.
We covered every department in a boot and shoe factory. Comparison in this respect
was made with the Engineering industry with its 48 unions, and with other trades similarly
placed.
The growth of Trades Unionism, particularly in its relationship to the State and the
various enactments legalising trade unions, were then outlined. Mr. Richards claiming
that progress had been largely brought about by the thought, energy and farsightedness
of a few individuals.
The developments in our own Union arising out of the National Lockout of 1895
were then commented on. The advantages of arbitration, would not, Mr. Richards urged,
be successful but for our National Agreement. That was the safeguard, and very little
attempt had been made by the manufacturers to take away from us any of the advantages
contained therein. The employers know, and we know, that it would not be good for
us to have an upheaval.
The history and costs of the disputes in the trade since 1895 were then given by
Mr. Richards as follows :-

The settlement in four of the cases were totally unsatisfactory, i.e., Nantwich, Mel-
bourne, Preston, Eyam and Stoney Middleton. Generally speaking, we have no desire
to even renew similar conflicts, as we feel that the other side has been willing for a satis-
factory settlement as well as ourselves in all these disputes. In the four cases mentioned,
it was merely the right of man to belong to a trade union that was at stake, and in these
cases the liberty of the subject was bound to be defended by us, although the members
affected had perhaps in some instances not been members of ours for more than four or
five weeks. With these facts in front of us, and then again realising that according to the
most reasonable estimate that can be given of the number of people working at the industry
being round about 120,000, and seeing that in our own Union we have 80,000 members
only, the old body approximately 2,000, and the Rossendale Slipper Union round about
6,000, one feels inclined to ask why that other 32,000 of a floating population is not with
us. When we look at the above-mentioned four places, where we have few members,
and then consider the efforts of our organisers during the last two years in the East End
of London, we think that if the Government of the day, whoever they may be, and what-
ever party they may belong to, can be of any assistance to an industry, our Union Executive
is willing that the Resolution carried at the 1922 National Conference, moved by Mr.
H. J. Bostock and seconded by myself-" That the Government should be urged to
give National Agreements voluntarily entered into, and approved by the Joint Industrial
Council, the same validity as wages under the Trade Board Acts." I want all our members
to know, and I tried to make clear at the Conferences, the need that "National Agree-
ments voluntarily entered into" which would mean that those agreements being subject
to confirmation or amendment every two or three years would only be enforceable during
their lifetime, and at the will and pleasure of both sides. To my mind this should eliminate
the fear in the minds of those anti-compulsory arbitrationists, who like ourselves, do not
look upon voluntary arbitration as compulsory arbitration. We enter the arbitration
wish to lay the foundation stone for compulsory arbitration, but at the same time do not

room with the intention of arriving at a settlement without resorting to or throwing the
responsibility on the presiding chairman or Umpire to do for us what we ought to do for
ourselves, and when there is a large body gathered together of from 15 to 30 persons on
each side, as we invariably find in our National Conferences with the Employers, it has
been our success up to the present to find that among them there is always an air of
reasonableness in an assembly of that kind, with the result that we are pleased to record
the fact that for 30 years there has been no general stoppage of work.
Our disputes had done but little good. Most of them had been fought in isolated
districts, with the very worst material and against employers who had little regard for
the principle of collective bargaining.
The value of the Boot and Shoe Repairing Trade Board, with its 30,000 employers,
was then instanced as to the value of legislation. What could be done with a legally
enforceable agreement with a multitude of small employers, could, it was claimed, be
equally, if not more successful in our own highly organised trade.

MR. LYNE (Northampton No. 2): I believe legalisation of agreements to be a good

them seems so obvious that I can never understand why it has not been adopted before,
and by many other Unions. The backward districts have the power of determining the
progress of our own industry. Legalisation would have a very large influence where the
conditions are bad. By having the legal force behind us it would help us to demand a
higher power that we can exercise at the moment. Progress is determined by the power
which exists at the moment, and the greatest progress has been made during times of
prosperity. Legalisation would bring equality of power and advantage.

BOOT & SHOE FACTORIES IN NEW ZEALAND.
The following information will doubtless be of interest to our members seeing that
it has been taken from the official statistical report on the Factory Production of the
Dominion of New Zealand, 1923-4.
In connection with the manufacture of boots and shoes there are 81 factories in the
Dominion, at which 1,449 males and 859 females are employed. The salaries and wages
giving an approximate average of £4 per week for males, and £2 2s. 6d. for females for
50 weeks each year.
The production for the year 1923-4 was: Boots and Shoes, 1,471,093 pairs; Slippers,
17,217 pairs: Shoe-ettes, £56,299; Leggings, 53 pairs.

ALEX. MACMORRAN.
WHAT THE GERMAN EMPLOYERS ARE SAYINC ABOUT PRODUCTION.
FEDERATION OF GERMAN INDUSTRIES.
Need for Greater Production.
The Sixth General Assembly of the Federation of German Industries was held at
Cologne from 24th to 26th June.
Dr. Kastl, Director of the Federation, in explaining the programme of the Congress,
referred to the necessity of increasing production and of finding new markets when pro
duction was increased.
In Germany (he said), as in almost all other European countries, the machinery of
production is working too slowly. Waste of effort, incomplete utilisation of labour and
plant, difficulties in the transport system, lack of development on the technical side, and
other causes are preventing production from attaining its full capacity.
As regards the labour factor, the working classes, or rather the trade union leaders,
are unfortunately adopting a mistaken policy, Instead of endeavouring to help the
development of production, they are trying to reduce it, in the hope of creating further
possibilities of employment. Account must also be taken of the psychological factor-
the fact that the working class consider production and those responsible for it as the
incarnation of capitalism, which conception is entirely opposed to that held by the American
working classes, for example.
Both as regards hours of work and as regards wages, the workers should try to get
a better understanding of the situation. Although many arguments militate in favour
of the eight-hour day, the fact is that there is to-day a pressing need for a reduction in
the cost of production.
The workers have a false conception of the wage question. In order to arrive at a
system of high real wages (which is to be desired) it is necessary that a return should be
made to the system of piecework, seeing that a general increase of wages without an
increase of output would put German industry in a state of inferiority on the world market.
The heavy social charges, the burden of interest, the burden of taxation, and freight
rates, all of which are a heavy charge on production, should be considerably reduced.
Germany's Position in World Markets.
A special report was presented by the Chairman, Dr. Duisberg, dealing with the
economic situation of Germany, and, in particular, with the trade balance, and the question
of the share of the Reich in world commerce as a whole.
The report surveyed possible methods of increasing exports, in view of the fact that
Germany, which before the war held the eighth place in world commerce, now held only the
twentieth, It concluded by suggesting that it would be better to make an improvement
in the quality of German exports rather than in the quantity, in order more easily to
secure markets for German goods in competition with the goods of other countries.
[From the above statements it is quite plain that German Employers are using exactly
the same arguments about costs, &c., as are British Employers. It is also very
significant that according to the said reports Germany now holds "twentieth " place
in the world commerce as as against" eighth "before the war.-E.L.P., General Secretary.]

Two of the States included in the study, Missouri and Ohio, have laws limiting the day's

EMPLOYMENT OF SEAMEN.
Conference, held at Genoa in 1920.
Australia Ratifies International Convention.
Information has been received at the International Labour Office of the League of
Nations at Geneva that Australia has ratified a Convention dealing with the employment
of seamen which was adopted by the second annual session of the International Labour
By this Convention, the State which ratifies it agrees that "the business of finding
employment for seamen shall not be carried on by any person, company or other agency,
as a commercial enterprise for pecuniary gain; nor shall any fees be charged directly or
indirectly by any person, company or other agency, for finding employment for seamen
in any ship," and undertakes to provide punishment by law for any violation of this
provision. Such agencies are allowed to carry on their work temporarily under Govern-
ment licence during the period of reorganisation and the establishment of free public
exchanges for the finding of such employment for seamen. Committees of equal repre-
sentatives of shipowners and seamen are to be set up to advise on questions connected
with these exchanges while seamen and shipowners are to be guaranteed freedom of
choice.
Italy, Japan, Norway, Poland and Sweden.
The Convention has already been ratified by Belgium, Bulgaria, Esthonia, Finland,
This brings the total of ratifications of Conventions adopted by the International
Labour Conference to 160.
POSTAL WORKERS IN RUSSIA.
"Considerable Unemployment."
The following summary of conditions among postal workers in Russia, as shown
by reports to the Ninth Congress of Russian Postal, Telegraph and Telephone Workers,
a Union numbering 102,500 members, held at Moscow in June, is given in Industrial and
Labour Information, the weekly publication of the International Labour Office of the
League of Nations.
Whereas in industry wages are now on an average 78 per cent. of their figure before
the Communist revolution (60 per cent. in heavy industry), the average wage in the postal,
telegraph and telephone services is only 50 per cent. of its 1914 level, and in the provinces
it is even sometimes lower.
In view of the fact that there is no longer a deficit in the working of the postal, telegraph
and telephone services, but even a certain excess of revenue over expenditure, the delegates,
in agreement with the Central Committee, demanded that the Commissariat of Posts,
Telegraphs and Telephones should increase wages 40 per cent. The employees also
demanded that the wages of provincial employees should be brought to the same level
as those of employees in the great centres, and that there should be an end of the existing
difference in the wages of telephone operators, who were generally better paid than tele-
graphists and postmen.
The Commissary for Posts, Telegraphs and Telephones stated that he hoped to be
able to increase wages by 25 per cent. during 1925, but was unable to promise any further
increment.
The greater majority of the delegates pronounced in favour of an uninterrupted day's
work (with the exception of a break for meals), and against the "spread-over" system.
The delegates urged the necessity of supplying all employees, in accordance with the
Labour Code, with special working clothes. At the moment only employees in the big
centres benefited by this regulation. It was also stated that housing conditions were
extremely unfavourable and prejudicial to output. Employees were often compelled
to live at a considerable distance from their work and to spend several hours a day in the
train. They were thus tired when they came to work, and the work suffered.
It was also stated that many employees were living in deplorable conditions of over-
crowded and insanitary dwellings. The result was a general lowering in the average level
of health, which could not but be harmful to efficiency.
The Central Committee was accused by many delegates of paying insufficient attention
to the question, and of not bringing sufficient pressure to bear on the Commissariat of
Posts, Telegraphs and Telephones, in order to obtain the sums ear-marked for the con-
struction of dwellings for employees, which sums it would appear that the Commissariat
was unwilling to pay on its own initiative.

**CHANGES IN COST OF LIVING: STATISTICS FOR 1st AUGUST, 1925.**

Summary: Average Increases since July, 1914.
All Items included . . .. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 73 per cent.
Food only . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 68    
FOOD.

At 1st August the average level of retail prices of the principal articles of food showed
a slight upward movement as compared with 1st July. Eggs, butter, margarine, cheese,
and milk were all dearer than a month earlier, but these advances were largely counter-
balanced by a seasonal decline in the prices of potatoes and by a slight reduction in the
average prices of flour and of bread. The remaining articles included in the statistics
showed no appreciable change in price.

As a net result of all the changes recorded, the average increase over the level of July,
1914, in the retail prices (in Great Britain and Northern Ireland) of the articles of food
included in these statistics was about 68 per cent. at 1st August, as compared with about
67 per cent. at 1st July and 64 per cent. at the corresponding date in 1924.

SPECIAL NOTICE TO MEMBERS.
Every precaution is taken by us to ensure accuracy in these Reports, our correspondents
being men on whom we can rely; should, however, any errors creep into them, upon the
same being pointed out to us, we shall be most happy to give the same prominence to the
correction as to the original matter. As these Reports are exclusively for the use of Members,
the information contained therein must not be divulged.

MONTHLY REPORT.
21ST SEPTEMBER, 1925.
FELLOW MEMBERS,
TRADE.
We regret to have to report that trade upon the whole is worse rather than
better, and the immediate prospect from the point of view of employment is
not at all a hopeful one. The figures in the Ministry of Labour Gazette are as
follows: -
BOOT AND SHOE INDUSTRY.
Employment remained depressed during August. In a number of districts the
factories were closed for extended holidays, while after the resumption a very considerable
amount of short time was worked in the majority of centres. Compared with August,
1924, employment generally showed a decline.
The percentage of workpeople unemployed, as indicated by the unemployment books
lodged at Employment Exchanges, was 12.5 on 24th August, 1925, as compared with
11.8 on 27th July, 1925, and 8.7 on 25th August, 1924.
Returns from firms employing over 40,000 operatives in the week ended 29th August,
showed that 48¼ per cent. of these operatives worked short time in that week, to the extent
of over 12 hours each, on an average.
Employment remained quiet in London and was generally worse than in August,
1924. At Leicester and Northampton many firms extended the holidays; and after
work was resumed employment was generally depressed, with much short-time working.
At Hinckley employment remained bad. At Rushden, where most factories closed
down for to days at the holiday, employment was bad, with general short-time working,
though there was a slight recovery towards the end of the month. On the other hand,
employment in the Raunds district was again good as a result of Government contract
work. The majority of the workers at Wellingborough were on short time, and employ-
ment remained bad and was worse than a year ago. At Kettering there was little change,
employment remaining poor and only a few firms working full hours.
At Leeds employment showed little change, and was poor in the heavy boot section
and fair in the light footwear section. Employment was very slack and slightly worse
than in the previous month at Stafford, short time again being largely worked. At Norwich
a number of factories were working short time, and employment was only moderate and
worse than in August, 1924. At Kingswood and Bristol employment was fair on the whole,
and better than in July; but at Street it continued poor and was much worse than a year
ago. In the slipper trade of the Rossendale Valley employment was fair on the whole.
B

Here is the extracted text:
517
Apart from the question of unemployment in our own industry the growing
volume of unemployment in the country is indeed very serious. The Govern-
ment, however, so far as outward appearances go do not appear to be looking
upon the matter in a way which the gravity of the position demands. The
fact of such a vast army of unemployed, and their dependents added, makes
it the main problem in our opinion which the Government should tackle very
seriously.
We would urge upon the members the necessity of keeping this subject
prominently to the front in their several districts, it being a matter upon
which there ought to be no division of opinion-either employment should be
found, or maintenance provided. We much prefer employment being found,
because despite statements that one reads and sometimes hears as to the
workers not wanting work, we know this is a gross libel upon them. Here and
there may be found a few (as in other grades of life) who do not want work,
but we know the great majority of the workers prefer work rather than idling
about. For ourselves, we resent the statements which are sometimes made,
reflecting as they do upon the workers as a whole.
RUSSIA.
It is generally known that a short time ago, a number of women went over
to Russia to enquire into the general conditions obtaining in that country.
During their visit they had a meeting in connection with the women employed
in the shoe industry, and Miss Loughlin, one of the members who went over.
to Russia, was entrusted with a red leather banner printed on one side in
Russian, and the translation in English on the other side, to hand over to the
Women Leather Workers and Boot Workers of Great Britain. Miss Loughlin
handed this over to the General Secretary during the Trades Union Congress
week. We highly value this banner, and shall take the necessary steps to have
it properly framed, and next month we propose having a photo of it produced
in our Monthly Report with a statement of good wishes that the Russian
Women entertain towards the Women Workers of Great Britain.
We hope in the not distant future, whatever the differences may have been
in the past, such differences will be wiped away and a proper understanding
arrived at with the workers in all parts of the world. We may not be able to
agree upon many matters and methods, but we ought to be able to agree to
come together to try and understand one another's point of view, and thus
remove differences in the struggle of the workers for a better and fuller life.
THE TRADES UNION CONGRESS
was this year held at Scarborough, under the presidency of Mr. A. B. Swales.
On the whole the Congress was a very successful one, and once more confounded
the prophets who for some time past have been telling the public of the divisions
existent between the Trade Unions and the Labour Party, and the definite
split that would take place at this Congress.
