Here's the extracted text from the image:

**Page 518**

With two such vast movements there naturally is some difference, and differences will from time to time arise from various standpoints. This is not only inevitable, but tends to bring about more likelihood of success because of the discussions that from time to time take place. For ourselves we should look upon it as a backward step if there was any serious division between the Trade Unions and the Labour Party.

The Congress represented 4,350,982 members. The President in his address made a strong appeal—after surveying the position—for a still wider outlook, and a greater determination to resist any attempted reductions in wages or lengthening of hours, and finally stated: "It is the duty of all members of the working class to so solidify their movements that, come when the time may for the last final struggle, we shall be wanting in neither machinery, nor men, to move forward to the destruction of wage slavery and the construction of a system of society based upon co-ordinated effort and world-wide mutual goodwill and understanding."

As usual, a considerable number of resolutions were dealt with, and the report of our delegates appears elsewhere. We feel bound, however, to refer to the two resolutions standing in the name of the Union, both of which were passed.

The one on UNEMPLOYMENT was as follows, and was carried unanimously :-

"That this Congress views with the greatest concern the serious volume of unemployment now existing, and deplores the lack of effective measures on the part of the Government to attempt to alleviate the situation. It further declares its emphatic opposition to the spurious remedies of lower wages and the degradation of established working conditions, and calls upon the Trade Union Movement to offer its utmost resistance to any attempt to increase the hours of work. It asserts the unquestionable right of the unemployed worker and his dependents to adequate maintenance, and reiterates its demand for the establishment of a National Employment and Development Board for the purpose of devising and co-ordinating measures whereby the extent of unemployment may be reduced."

The second dealt with the question of VOLUNTARY AGREEMENTS :-

"This Congress instructs the General Council to urge upon H.M. Government the desirability of giving to National Agreements voluntarily entered into and approved by Joint Industrial Councils the same validity as awards under the Trade Board Acts, 1909 and 1918, with the object of ensuring the observance of fair conditions of labour by all engaged in the industry whenever requested by the parties to the agreement."

This resolution was strongly opposed by the Rt. Hon. J. H. Thomas, of the N.U.R., Mr. J. Hill, of the Boilermakers' Society, Mr. Hallsworth, of the

Thus the Congress has approved of a serious attempt being made for
Voluntary Agreements—when asked for by both sides—being enforceable by
law. This is indeed gratifying to us, and we can only hope that ere long we
may see upon the Statute Book of this country, an Act giving such authority.
As previously stated the Congress was a very successful one, and we venture
to say is increasingly making its influence felt in all parts of the country.
POLITICAL.
Although Parliament is not sitting, many momentous matters are being
dealt with by Statesmen which might easily bring about disturbances between
nations. We would remind the membership that although THE WAR which
was to end war has ended, nevertheless there are more men under arms to-day
than was the case in 1914. Wars are still going on in various parts of the
world. Europe is still very unsettled, and it needs wise and courageous
statesmanship to deal with the situation in which the nation finds itself con-
fronted.
We are more and more convinced—and it may be even against themselves
—that Statesmen will be driven to deal with the abnormal situation largely
upon the lines which Labour has pointed out to escape from the troubles with
which mankind is beset. Meantime, a good deal can be done to bring about a
changed outlook among the people of this country to ensure labour views and
labour proposals securing ascendancy. We appeal to the membership to do
their part in accelerating the time when this state of affairs have become a
positive fact.
WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION ACT.
In March last, MR. A. T. WALTERS, of the Kingswood Branch, died from
Tetanus owing to a small wound under the left finger nail, whilst he was carrying
on in connection with his occupation of laying electric cables in the district.
We are pleased to report that the question of compensation has now been
satisfactorily settled, and the sum of £300 has now been paid into Court on
behalf of the late Mr. Walters' dependents.
THE "DAILY HERALD."
We trust that all our members will take every available opportunity to
push the Herald, the only daily paper owned and controlled by the movement.
By purchasing the Herald daily a full report of the political world as seen by
Labour can be read.

THE GENERAL PRESIDENT'S REPORT.
60 LADY SOMERSET ROAD,
KENTISH TOWN,
LONDON, N.W. 5.
16th September, 1925.
FELLOW MEMBERS,
I regret not putting before you the agreement made between our Union and the
C.W.S. Directors in 1913 in my last report, and upon which we claimed the Management
at Leeds and ultimately the Directors, on the advice of an expert in law, their Secretary.
The agreement is as follows:-
"That no strike or lockout take place before the whole of the facts in dispute
are submitted by the Union, first to the Management, and then, failing settlement,
and the Executive Council of the National Union of Boot and Shoe Operatives."
to a Joint Meeting of the Productive Committee of the Co-operative Wholesale Society
(Signed) WM. LANDER, Chairman.
It will be seen that the Union ought to have had an opportunity to investigate the
cause of the victimisation of two members for acting on Trade Union instructions, which
is in strict accord with the National Agreement governing the trade, and the full support
of the Council of the Union, who claim the right to advise members to lower their quantities
when an employer reduces the earnings. The Directors have already been informed that
a repetition of the C.W.S. conditions, and no orders will be given to return to work until
old conditions prevail, as in the case of the Woollen Textile dispute, and the advice given
by the Minister of Labour, which was, that all return to work as they left off. The directors
now know the prospects for the future.
The above statement is made for the benefit of many co-operators who do not think
it reasonable that operatives in C.W.S. Factories should ever be driven to take immediate
action.
LOOM'S, MARKET HARBOROUGH. This firm has again been visited as the outcome
of some correspondence and attitude I have taken up with them, and also Faire Bros.,
of Mansfield, but of Leicester fame, and that is to ask these firms if there is any reason
why we should not appeal to their customers and point out the actual conditions, as far
as we understand them. At this firm I was shown the Wages Sheets for the week previous
to my visit, and they showed that at least sixty-six out of a total of eighty-one are in
receipt of our scale and over, and the firm is also paying the Holiday Pay that week, so
they informed me. The only two points to put them equal with any other firm is to
regulate the boy labour to its proper proportion and remove the embargo on Trade Union-
ism. I was pleased to find the firm in a much better condition than I expected, and we
hope the firm will do its best to meet us in order that we could give them a clean bill of
health.
We are bound to appeal to all Manufacturers and Merchants, as we have to the Govern-
ment, to only deal with those firms who respect and pay and work to our National Agree-
ment.
THE TRADE UNION CONGRESS by nearly two million majority, have encouraged us
to ask the Government to compel all firms connected with shoes, to work and pay to our
national conditions.
It has been my pleasure to interest myself in the competition we have from abroad,
and although I have never attended the International Conference of Boot and Shoe Opera-
tives, my interest has not been void, and the most important place to which I wish to
direct members attention is Czecho-Slovakia. The wage in this country is about 14s. 6d.
against our 60s. for 48 hours, but they have all food at cost price, and doctors are provided.
I have also been in close association with a friend in the largest Shoe Factory in
Austria, and my visit to Brussels with attendant inquiries and ultimate results whet my
appetite to know more, and now I am in correspondence with two gentlemen who have
both worked at Bata's, Zlin, Czecho-Slovakia, who began in 1894, employ 6,000 workers,
(4,000 Machine Makers, 2,000 Hand Makers,) and work on the American system. Every
man has a set quantity to do per day, the firm provide Dinner and Supper and other food
at cost price. Hospital, Theatre, and Cinema; owns most of the houses (building more),
they also own Woods, Sawmills, Brickyards, Herds of Cattle, Butcheries, Tanneries,
Linen Factories, Paper Factories, Box Factory, Printing Press, Ships, and even Railways
are owned by the firm. This firm have just given the operatives three weeks' holiday
with pay, accepts workers' savings, and gives about 10 per cent. interest on all monies,
and all monies remain in the firm. They pay no extra for overtime, and the worker
has to do his quantity day by day even if he stays late. This firm is from America and
is subsidised by the Czecho-Slovakian Government. I am still getting more information.

FAIRE BROS., LEICESTER AND MANSFIELD. — I am in close touch with this firm and
expect developments this week.
522
MANSFIELD. — The factory of the Sherwood Shoe Co. is also receiving attention respect.
ing several matters, and a further visit has been made with not very satisfactory results,
but here we want to quietly persuade, rather than to unduly press. It looks as though
we shall have to organise some places by public appeals with such bright prospects we
may wait a little longer.
THE CONGRESS AT SCARBOROUGH has been a success, as all Congresses are, and those
who thought the movement would make a colossal blunder, have been mistaken. The
members refused the One-Union motion, but it has come and is coming much nearer
unification, as was shown by the support they gave to many resolutions affecting the whole
movement. Power to buy was refuted; our Unemployed resolution was carried, and our
power to compel non-federated and non-unionists to observe our National Agreement was
carried by 2,790,000 to 942,000, a majority of 1,848,000. The General Secretary moved,
and I seconded. Mr. Sherwood supported, but neither of these are mentioned by the
Daily Herald. Mr. Bevin and Mr. Rowlerson also supported. We had the support of the
Miners, Transport Workers, and all the Labourers, and the opposition of Boilermakers,
Railwaymen, N.U.D. & A.W. and the Garment Workers.
In spite of the Daily Herald's bad treatment, I ask you to buy it, and some day it will,
Daily Herald were too busy "puffing pals," and so missed the best speeches of Congress,
photos and empty pictures. The General Purposes Committee of Congress improved
without prejudice, print others' speeches as well as their friends—put in matter instead
Congress business on two important points: Local papers recorded the fact that the
We shall again ask them directly for an explanation, and before this report reaches your
hands, if I can add their usual excuse I will do so, but buy the Daily Herald, we then have
the right to give to them a piece of our mind.
My visit to Scotland was undertaken to assure the members that the demise of
Mr. Bleakley being so sad in itself, may have caused an impression that such an event
may cause the Central Executive to be less vigilant respecting the interest of our Scottish
members, because the newly-elected Council Member being an employee and in work
was out of work, other than the Council gave him for several years. He had made two
attempts on business lines to become his own employer as a repairer, but we took him
may be prevented from giving as much time to the work as Mr. Bleakley, who unfortunately
away so much that even in the initial stages, he was bound to give up because he was
often away on Union business at a time when his customers wanted him, and like his
predecessor he had to forfeit that small effort.
I have, therefore, assured the Scottish members that we shall do our best not to harass
or embarrass the new Council Member, but undertake to attend to all his difficult cases in
or embarrass the new Council Member.
Therefore, at Kilmarnock, we had a meeting of the " faithful," who took,
and do take much interest in the Union's activities. Although the meeting was not
Education was a theme considered, but it was that we should as a Union support a scheme
largely attended, I hope that the members will agree it was immensely interesting.
which is neither Ruskin or the W.E.A.; these latter the Union support, and we are inter-
ested in educating all members, especially the absent ones and the old ones, and would

let the mistaken ones learn by the newcomers' good work and stand or fall by their own
have come to earth then let them build up their good work on their own foundations, and

AYR.—The Ayr meeting was said to be much larger than usual, and much interest
was exhibited by the members in the activities of the Union in its political outlook especially;
supporters of the Labour Party, even if they differ with its Socialist aspects. One
support has undertaken a self-denying ordinance until such time as Ayr Burgh is one
for Labour; he thinks this constituency is at present the bulwark of the reactionary
party, and it is for this reason that he and others, like myself, cannot imagine any Trade
Unionist putting a cross against the name of any member of any party which is opposed
to his own wage-earning political party.
MAYBOLE.—Here we had a very good meeting in the dinner hour, the major portion
of the Maybole workers attending, and I addressed them on the great need of organisation,
quoting especially the need for another 30,000 members in the standing of our Union and
in the Labour world, with emphasis as to the benefit of the Holiday Scheme. The meeting
in the evening was not so big as usual, owing to the annual visit for one day of a Broncho
Bill Circus and Exhibition, which certainly depleted our gathering. I also had to investi-
gate the discharge of a young man who had been discharged for alleged bad language
indulged in by both the foreman and the employer, who were informed that we did not
defend bad language, bad work, or bad time, neither should we admit the right of foreman
or employer to use bad language to our members, who themselves were to blame if opera-
tives copied them.


---
GLASGOW. Here the two branches of our Union had been trying to organise and
improve the conditions of Messrs. A. Strachan & Co., Graham Square, where there were
girls in the Finishing Room. As the firm which has been in existence some twenty-five
years have had very little attention paid to them, have now become of interest to us, as
they are willing to comply to the National Agreement in detail, the only difference being
the time when these girls should be transferred from this Department. We tried to obtain
the services of the Manager of the S.C.W.S. as Umpire, but after seeing him a second time
he begged to decline for several important reasons. We again saw the firm and agreed
to a date; the relationship of our branches and the firm is very cordial and bids fair to be
an important help, as two of our old Secretaries have found employment there.

EDINBURGH. Here I found the branch interested in the firm of which Mr. Rankin
assures me they have no antipathy to Trade Unionism, but as the work is mainly rubber,
construction, and can make allowances for that portion who are engaged in any other
but to help, and the Shoe Section is the part we must preserve for all engaged in shoe
he hopes we will bear that factor in mind. Needless to say we are not anxious to embarrass,
occupation. We had an interview with the firm, whose Manager informed us that he was
prepared at an early date, say October, to put into operation the Holiday Scheme, which
will give immense satisfaction to the operatives, and as we have already all but a few
members, we hope that this action of the firm will have beneficial results to all concerned.

The meeting of the members was as usual attended by those who really feel that
Trade Unionism is a vital force in their lives, and they appear never to miss an opportunity
to try and make an official visit as pleasant and successful as possible, but they all expressed
themselves that our visits were not frequent enough, but as explained before, they may
see more of some of us if there is a real demand for our presence.

DUNDEE. - Dundee being visited found the faithful in attendance, and good intent
was shown in the address, and the many phrases of Union work caused much surprise
that we had any further outlook than the objects of our Union, and I was reminded that
it was just thirty-three years ago since I moved and got carried the Socialist resolution
which at that time made no references to the sexes, which finds many opposed to who are
supposed to favour the same.

ABERDEEN was next visited and again there was nothing approaching the size of the
ment of things as they are, and as Morrison's have closed down the factory and only now
factor, the few employed by them have had to seek other employment. This leaves the
branch to consist now of repairers only, and here, as elsewhere, the members think that an
Organiser would do good. I had to tell them that to send an Organiser to go round to each
place where one man was employed would be too costly, and advised them to form a small
Committee, of two say, and let them try their hand during their spare time, get and keep
a detailed report, send it on to the Council after three months' work, and ask them to make
a grant towards it, they would then be able to judge of its effect.

ARBROATH was next visited, and I called on our old comrade and ex-Council Member,
Alex. Smith, who was busy with his Registrar work. He looks well, and appears to me
better in health than he has been for many years. I also visited Fairweather's factory
whilst there, and had an informal chat with one of the employers. Like other firms,
trade with them is flat, but as I had arranged an evening meeting at Carnoustie, we called
Fairweather's men to a meeting at five o'clock, and they turned up well. They listened
intently and did not prolong the meeting as they had not had their tea. The officers
were all pleased with the splendid attendance, as this to them was a new innovation.

CARNOUSTIE was then visited, and the night was very cold indeed, which may account
for the smallness of the meeting, coupled with a benefit night of the Pierrots. The
accustomed faithfuls were there as is their desire - come weal, come woe, they can always
be relied on. I had to leave here rather early to reach Scarborough for the Trade Union
Congress General Purposes Committee, who were warned to meet on Saturday morning
in order to arrange the business of the week, and to get as many of the composite resolutions
in the hands of the printers as soon as possible, This was our work for the Saturday, and
on Sunday all our Union delegates met to consider what support we could give the various
resolutions, and make any other arrangements affecting us during the rest of the week.

Yours fraternally,
W. T. Richards
General President.

BRANCH REPORTS.
BELFAST. - Trade both in the manufacturing and repairing continues to
remain in a deplorable state without any signs of an early improvement.
BURNLEY. - Our members have been watching with keen interest the
negotiations concerning the miners, as we know they are literally starving here.
They are working-those who are working-about three days a week, and are
not able to get a living. We do not know what happens anywhere else, but
we do know some miners have to go down the pit and risk their lives to get
coal for IS. IId. to 2s. 8d. per ton. The men who retail the coal get from IOS.
to 15s. per ton. We can all see, whatever our political outlook may be, that
there is something wrong. If an industry cannot provide a living for the
people engaged in it, it is time it was closed down. To starve the producers
to pay for motor cars for the investors of capital in the industry is to ask for
trouble.
CHESHAM. - The slight improvement in trade reported last month has
been maintained, but we still have some members unemployed, and some
working short time.
CHESTERFIELD. - There is an improvement in trade, yet we have some
of our members still out of work.
DERBY. - Trade in Derby continues very bad, many operatives only
making about three days a week. A number of clickers at Clemson's have
been suspended during the last eight weeks. Clickers at C.W.S. are also only
making about four days a week.
DESBOROUGH. - Trade with us is very bad, and very few are fully
employed. Long holidays have been the rule, whilst a great many are only
working three days per week. A few of our members have not yet restarted
since the holiday, which makes our Unemployment Pay this month rather
heavy, but it is very welcome to those who are suspended.
DUNDALK. - Trade remains uncertain, but as the all-share-alike system
is pursued to some extent, the workers are quite agreeable to the half-loaf
policy. This, though a very natural attitude, deserves commending, as most
of the members are inexperienced in regard to Trade Union principles. We
feel sure the employers will find it to their advantage also, as the men seeing
they are treated equally, will give entire satisfaction when the industrial
depression rolls by. At any rate by adhering to this policy of reciprocity
they will not be incurring the risk of being looked upon in a philanthropic
sense. It is also well to remember the tree of industry will never bear fruit
unless it is watered from the well of justice, equality, and fair play. Some
members, because they receive the minimum rate of wages, consider pay-
ing a sort of monotony; still, if ever they appear on the unemployed list,
the benefits to be derived from Union membership will not be above their
consideration, or what guarantee have they of even a minimum continuity?
None save the Union itself. The Union alone was responsible for the 48-hour
week and the minimum pay they receive. Ancestorial contributions gave the
Union birth. If we are to do something worthy of our posterity's appreciation,
then by being loyal members we are taking a stride in the right direction,
and when acting rightly and legitimately, fear of consequence should not
deter us from performing our moral obligations. New members should con-
sider the great future the Union affords them. A living wage for an honest
week's output, out-of-work benefits, &c. Those principles should appeal to
every intelligent worker, male and female alike. Up to the time of writing
we have not heard from Emyvale. Probably they have started a branch on
their own.


---
DUNDEE. Trade reports are such depressing reading that we have no
desire to dilate on such a theme, as our trade barometer is very low indeed at
present. There is, however, a slender streak of the proverbial "silver lining."
A report has just reached us that there is greater activity in Blackford than
has been experienced for a considerable time. May it continue. September
Ist was a "red" (not a Communist) "letter night" in our branch. We had
our genial General President with us, from whom we had an excellent discourse
on what had been achieved by our organisation during the past year. In
passing he mentioned that next to the trade of compositors, who were the
highest paid workers, our trade came second. This is very creditable to the
Council, whose duty it is to keep a watchful eye on the interests and advance-
ment of the members of our Union. At the close of the meeting the hope was
expressed that Mr. T. F. Richards might consider the advisability of paying an
annual visit to Scotland. Failing his ability to do so, our General Secretary,
or some member of the Council. These visits would be highly appreciated
and good results would accrue. It would act as a stimulus, especially to the
younger members, and break down the apathy which is apt to grow without
stimulation.

The Branch wish to thank all who have subscribed so liberally in making
this appeal so great a success. The Misses Bleakley wish to thank the Union
for their kindness.
GLASGOW. Trade with us has slowed down since the holidays, and a
number of members have been suspended, while others are working short time.
We hope this may only be temporary. Generally the repairing section of the
trade is fair. We were glad to have a visit of the General President, Mr. T. F.
Richards, for whom we arranged a joint branch meeting, but although this was
fully announced, the attendance of members was disappointing. However,
those present appreciated the spirited and encouraging address they heard.
We are also grateful for the attention and services rendered by Mr. T. F.
Richards on Union business while here.
HIGHAM AND RUSHDEN. The month has been a bad one for our
Branch. Extended holidays at beginning of the month generally, in some cases
meaning 14 days away from work. The last two weeks of the month trade has
improved, Rushden being better now than prior to August holiday, Raunds
fair, Finedon poor, Irthlingborough worse than for months past. Irchester has
suffered through extended stoppage of Closing Factory (now restarted) of T.
Brown & Co., Leicester. Wollaston and Bozeat, slight improvement.

KINGSWOOD AND DISTRICT. -Trade in this centre has improved
slightly, and generally speaking much difficulty is experienced in making full
time. There is therefore a reluctance to set on men unemployed. The apathy
and sluggishness affecting the volume of trade seems also to infect the opera-
tives, and we have to be constantly on the alert-educating, agitating, organising.
LEICESTER No. 1. - We are sorry to report that trade is still very bad
amongst our members, no less than £1,608 15s. od, being paid during the
month in Out-of-Work Benefit, almost half of which is Suspension Benefit.
Even this does not reflect the true position, as quite a good number of our
members have, unfortunately, drawn their full allowance, and are still out.
We do hope to be able to give a more favourable report of trade next month.
We trust our members will not fail to notice (and REMEMBER at the proper
time) that the unemployed will shortly "enjoy" more of the "benefits," of
a Conservative Government, i.e., six days waiting instead of three, as from
the 1st October. During the month a deputation from the Branch visited
Waterfoot, Lancashire, and valuable information was obtained regarding the
conditions of working in that district. Every facility was given by our friend,
Mr. Taylor (Secretary) and his Executive. We are much indebted to them,
and we hope to be able to return the compliment in the near future. We have
had Mr. W. R. Smith (Union Organiser) in this district during the last month
or so. He has worked very hard amongst the several sections in the County,
and although he has been seriously handicapped owing to the very short time
being worked, he has been successful in obtaining good results, quite a number
of new members joining.
LIVERPOOL. - The boot repairing trade has shown a marked improve-
ment since writing up our last report, which is probably due to the change in
the climatic conditions, and that the holiday season is nearly over, and also
the fact that the troubles in the Labour world are not so acute as they were,
although we are living in a critical age. We desire to draw the attention of
our members to the Council's Address in the August Report, which contains
interesting items which affect every member, on page 470. Another item of
interest is to be found on page 481, dealing with the history and cost of disputes
since 1895.
MANSFIELD. -Trade here is very fair. During the past month the
General President, Mr. Richards, with Mr. Len. Smith and Mr. J. H. Baum,
have been in Mansfield endeavouring to organise the operatives at the firms
of Messrs. Royce & Co. and Faire Bros. Although they have not been able
to deal with the situation as reported at Faire Bros., quite good progress has
been made at Royce & Co., where over 60 new members have joined, and four
from Ward Bros. have joined us, and there is every prospect of further increasing
our membership.
MAYBOLE.-Trade is much better, Messrs. J. Lees & Co., R. Crawford &
Sons and J. M'Creath & Sons, all working full time. James Ramsay & Co. are
still working short time. We had a visit from Mr. Richards during the month.
We had an outside meeting in the dinner hour which was well attended, but
we were disappointed at the evening meeting, and while there were other
attractions at night it is no excuse for members not turning up. Mr. Richards
and the Secretary visited one of the firms on a small matter, and we believe
this visit will do no harm. At the evening meeting Mr. Richards agreed to
take up the question of an Arbitration Board for Scotland, and we hope he
will succeed.
NORWICH. -Trade in Norwich during August suffered a further set-back,
many additional operatives becoming unemployed; in fact, but for three leading
factories in the city which are running very well, the industry is in a very
shaky condition.

ORGANISERS' REPORTS.
FELLOW MEMBERS,
In my last report I said that we had made about 120 new members at Manchester.
I should have stated that 110 of those were females engaged in the Rubber Shoe Making
at Messrs. Frankenburg's. Since then nearly another 100 have joined. A good many of
them belonged to the Rubber Workers' Union a few years ago, but the firm refused to
recognise this Union only on the side where workers were engaged in the manufacturing
of rubber. This left the girls in the closing and binding of the canvas uppers, as well as
the girls who shaped the shoes and put the rubber soles on, unprotected, from a trade
union point of view. The Council gave me permission to set about organising these females
if the other Union would agree to forfeit all claims to them. On a few visits from Mr.
Mason and myself to the General Secretary of the Rubber Workers' Union, this was
agreed to. One of the directors of Messrs. Frankenburg's said that if our Union could
organise the other four firms engaged on this class of goods, that it would be a good thing
for the trade and for the workers employed in it. I followed this work up during a few
weeks of this month at Manchester and Liverpool. There is a big firm at Walton, Liver-
pool, engaged in the making of rubber, rubber cable and rubber shoes. The term " rubber
shoes" does not strictly define the meaning of the goods, as the bulk of the work would be
black, brown and white canvas uppers with rubber soles.

On 8th September I went to the Walton Rubber Works, and gave several hundred
bills to the females in the dinner hour and evening. To me it was sad to hear the com-
ments of many of the girls when they were comparing the wages they were getting with
our Union's scale on the leaflets I had given them. The girls have had no voice in making
the piece rates which they work to. Many of the girls agreed that they needed a union
badly, but the usual difficulty presented itself to them: “Who would make a start?"
"If the others join I'll join." The men employed here in the making of rubber and rubber
cable belong to the Rubber Workers' Union. It is our business now to come to the rescue
of the girls, and if they will help us like their sisters at Manchester have done, by joining us,
Walton will be made brighter for them. We had a fine meeting of the newly-joined
females at Manchester on the 9th. Three Shop Presidents were elected and two from
each of the three departments were elected as a Shops Committee to assist them. It is
worth placing on record that 130 females on a cold wet night came to a trade union meeting
and gave two hours discussing matters touching their lives in the workshop.
D. MCCARTHY.
23rd September, 1925.
FELLOW MEMPERS,

During the past month I have chiefly been engaged at Mansfield, endeavouring
to follow up the work of the previous month. I have frequently met the girls working
at Messrs. Faire Bros., and despite the efforts that I have made the position has not altered.
The girls are fully conscious of the inadequate wages they receive, and are desirous of joining
the Union, with a view of obtaining substantial increase in their wages. Up to the present
time the firm is absolutely refusing to them their rights, which, if the girls wanted to interfere
with the rights of Mr. Tewkes, would no doubt soon be resented. To terrify girls by
dismissal is sometimes the only defence some persons have, and though it may appear to
be an eminently satisfactory thing to do, it sometimes has the same results upon those
types of individuals when the same methods are adopted, with regard to their business
methods. I have continued the work at the Mansfield Shoe Company; though the progress
in membership has not increased so rapid as last month, the membership is still increasing,
but there is still a further need of increasing our members. I am fairly confident
that over a period of time this firm will eventually become well organised. I have met the
girls working at Thrall's, stiffener makers; the wages paid here are exceedingly poor, and
it is almost impossible to organise them. I understand that a well-known Leicester
gentleman has financial interests in this firm, and it would be advisable for him to pay
some attention with regard to the wages paid at this firm, because out of their scanty
earnings they can hardly afford to pay a few pence towards a trade union.

I had occasion to visit the firm of Betts & Broughton, Huthwaite, a little difficulty
having arisen in one of the departments, and after meeting the operatives and the manager,
matters were adjusted satisfactorily. This is not a very large firm and we are well organised
and all our conditions are observed. The firm of Ward's has again been visited, and six
of them have joined the Union. All our conditions are adhered to, but somehow the men
don't seem to respond, but I think now that a beginning has been made and a Shop Presi-
dent appointed, no doubt others will soon follow. The firm of F. & C. Brown, repairers,
were called on, though there are only six men engaged here. After explaining to them the
work of the Union, they eventually all decided to become members at the end of
the week.


---
I have again visited the firm of Looms’, Market Harborough. Since my last visit
the firm have adopted the Holiday Scheme, and improvements have taken place with
regard to wages. The men seemed very reluctant to talk to me, and I thought that they
rather resented the stoppage of payment for holidays, in view of the fact that they were
working very short time. But by the distribution of literature dealing with the Scheme
the men seemed more satisfied. I am not altogether satisfied with the attitude of this
firm with regard to the men joining the Union. I am fairly confident, however, that this
attitude will eventually cease, but until it does cease, there is very little prospects of in-
creasing our membership at this firm.
During the month I was afforded the opportunity of speaking to the Mansfield Trades
Council. I felt that some statement was necessary in order to call their attention to the
position of the girls at Faire Bros. and Thrall’s. Though Mansfield is chiefly a mining
centre, unfortunately Trade Union organisation is not very strong, which is reflected in
the organisation of girls in the town. I impressed upon the Council the need of giving
some attention to women workers, because of the information I had received. I found
there was not any factory in the town which was paying anything near our scales. As a
result of this meeting the Trades Council have decided to convene a conference of the
various unions catering for the trades, with a view of a united effort to organise the women
in Mansfield.
J. H. BAUM.
21st September, 1925.
FELLOW MEMBERS,
In the first two weeks of the past month I continued my work in the Leicestershire
area that is covered by the Leicester Branches. The places visited were Syston, Wigston,
Blaby, Oadby and Earl Shilton. Meetings at factories were held at three of the above,
the results on the whole being good. Members have been made, and at the factories
where the membership is practically complete, appreciation was shown of a visit from
a national official.
In most of the places the state of trade is a handicap to organising, but the seed thus
sewn will give a return later on. Looking at the position nationally one cannot help a
feeling of satisfaction in the steady growth of membership in spite of bad trade, and the
propaganda work now being done will help to consolidate that membership, and one hopes,
I would like to express my thanks to the branches for the support
add to it later on.
given to me in my work in this area.
The No. 1 Branch as the one most affected readily
granted assistance in the service of a member of their Committee, who is able to canvass
for members while I am speaking outside the factories.
Perhaps the most important event of the month has been the meeting of the Trade
Union Congress at Scarborough, and though the main decisions will be given in a general
report, the gathering merits a word or two of special reference.
I am afraid the general result has proved disappointing to our critics. A large section
of the Press was looking forward to a clash between contending sections, with the rout of
the one they were particularly opposed to. The disappointment lies in the fact that there
was no clash as they expected. There were some keen debates, but the tone all the way
through was mainly that of trying to find the right path to achieve the end we all have in
view, rather than to accomplish a sectional victory by dialectical skill.
True, some of the resolutions were of a nature that call for a detailed consideration
by affiliated bodies. There is always a danger that at times of a great crisis, we may in
our anxiety to find a solution of our difficulties take a line that perhaps on more mature
consideration might be different, it may not be so here, but whether or not, one can say
that if the decisions are right ones, then the sooner we give the rank and file of our members
the chance of realising their obligations and responsibilities in regard to them the better,
for it is certain that in these matters any movement or change without their intelligent
support will be fraught with danger.
One question of great importance was dealt with on lines that gives time for considera-
tion as to what is best to be done. There is a desire to give to the General Council greater
powers. There has been a steady movement in that direction for some time. The estab-
lishment of a General Council on its present basis, in place of the old Parliamentary Com-
mittee, had that object in view.
The suggestion now made seeks to give them powers in regard to all wage movements,
to render assistance to any Union on strike-evidently in a more direct form than pre-
viously—as it is suggested they should have power to levy all affiliated members, to give
them power to call upon an affiliated body to bring out all or part of their members in sup-
port of another Union who is defending a vital trade principle, and to arrange with the
C.W.S. to make provision for the distribution of food, &c., in the event of a strike or other
action calling for same. And the Unions are asked to make such alteration in their rules
as will regularise the same.


---
Here is the extracted text:
530
The Congress refused to come to any decision upon this matter apart from referring
the whole subject to the new General Council for them to consider and report to next
Congress. This is a wise course, as it would be nothing short of madness were decisions
reached, that would prove useless when it was sought to put them into operation. It
must not be forgotten that different unions have different policies, that what might be
vital to one might not be so vital to another, and further that a Union in giving support
to another might place in jeopardy their own position, which so far as wage arrangement
is concerned is satisfactory if not vital to them.
If any move is made in this direction there must be the fullest consultation with all
the main Unions, so that every consideration may be given to the difficulties that are
bound up with such a procedure. In any change that takes place I am sure our members
would wish to play their full part and for that reason I would like to see this matter
adequately discussed, if possible, at our next Conference. We might refrain from some
of the trifling subjects and get our minds on to these bigger issues. Whatever we may
think of the question, there is an undoubted tendency in this direction, whether it is
temporary or otherwise time alone can decide. It may be that the Miners' dispute has
given it an added importance, which will subside if a satisfactory way out of that problem
can be found; but if so, it may be only for a time, as undoubtedly something of that char-
acter is the logical development of trade union organisation.
The subject lost some of its importance by being brought forward by small unions,
one with less than 400 members. The thought which naturally arises in one's mind, is, that
if the time is ripe for such a step, why have we these small unions to-day? Would it not be
far better for them to link up with the larger bodies ? Could not the National Union of
Vehicle Builders find a way of joining the Transport Workers ? Is it possible for us to
operate successfully on a wide area, if there cannot be a linking up within these narrower
limits ? I would strongly urge that this must be our first step. I know a great deal has
been done in this direction, but there is room for a great deal more. It is up to every
Union to do its best in this direction. If by next Congress we had cut down the number
of unions by one-half, a not impossible thing to do, we should be much nearer to this
greater power, not merely in the passing of a resolution upon it, but in applying it with
advantage to the great problems before us.
By general consent the Congress was a great success. The attendance was splendid,
and the delegates were more seriously-minded than at any previous Congress I have
attended. It may be the Miners' dispute is responsible, or it may be that on all sides it is
recognised that we are approaching a period when our forces may have to be marshalled
to meet an attack on all fronts. The year 1926 promises to be a critical one for organised
labour. The Miners' question is not settled, but postponed. The issue next time may be
more difficult, but we must win through. To do this there must be no dissension in our
ranks, there must be no attempt to decry political action in favour of industrial. One
felt there was this tendency at the Congress. We claim they are the two arms of the
Labour movement, each to be used as occasion demands, as a means to achieving our end.
We may make mistakes, but they are not confined to one policy alone. There are as many
will give us all we want at once, but both acting co-operatively will in the end get us
made on the industrial field as on the political, while each have their successes. Neither
through.
I wish these important questions that were before Congress could be discussed at our
Branch meetings, they must be understood at the bottom as well as at the top. It is the
members who will have to act in the hour of crisis. We can but advise and lead according
to our lights, but leadership will be all the more effective if the bulk of the army under-
stand the purpose in view.
It only remains to be added that our Union was successful in its objective-Mr.
Richards was elected to the General Purposes Committee for next Congress (subject to
the enquiry as to the accuracy of the counting); Mr. Poulton was elected to the General
Council unopposed; while both our resolutions were carried, the one relating to the legalising
of voluntary agreements by an overwhelming majority.
19th September, 1925.
WALTER R. SMITH.
THE SPITALFIELDS DISPUTE.
On page 492 of the August Report a balance sheet appeared showing the amount
of money received from branches in connection with the appeal issued on behalf of the
operatives concerned in the dispute at Messrs. Jackson & Joseph. In this connection the
sum of £127 12s. 6d. was remitted by the Manufacturers' Federation for disbursement
to the dependants (under 14 years of age) of the operatives on strike.

TRADES UNION CONGRESS.
The Fifty-Seventh Annual Congress in connection with the Trades Congress was
held at the Central Hall, Queen Street, Scarborough, on Monday, the 7th September
and the five following days. The President this year being Mr. A. B. Swales.
His address, after reciting the sacrifices that have been made during the past year,
especially with the two outstanding cases of the Miners and the Textile Disputes, were
and on the other hand the solidarity that has been shown by the workers, dealing most
enlarged upon.
He also pointed out the necessity for a wider outlook regarding not only as may be
said to be strictly industrial, but also the economic and political side of our work, showing
increased necessity for Trade Union unity throughout the world, pointing out finally that
those who believe that a new order of society is inevitable before we can remedy the existing
evils-and to which in this address I have directed attention-"cannot do other than rejoice
at last there are clear indications of a world movement rising in revolt and determined
to shake off the shackles of wage slavery. Just as our people have passed out of slavedom
into serfdom, and out of serfdom into wagedom, so will they finally pass out of wagedom
into freedom."
The General Council were able to report an increase in membership over 1923-24,
the figures being 4,350,982 with a delegation of 724, the number of affiliated societies
being 200.
The report of the General Council dealt with a very considerable number of matters
and is a document of 250 pages, ranging over all phases of industrial work.
The question of new premises upon which there has been a great deal of talk and
unwarranted statements made as to differences between the Labour Party and the General
Council were finally disposed of, making it quite clear that negotiations must continue
between the two wings of the party with a view to working together on their respective
sides.
Full support was given to the General Council regarding the position they took up in
connection with the Mining and Textile disputes, and the feeling of Congress was un-
doubtedly in the direction of the General Council using its full powers to help Unions on
the one hand, and to prevent if possible serious upheavals of industry on the otheri
As usual there were a great many resolutions, too many for us to tabulate here. We
are glad, however, to be able to record the fact that both of our own resolutions were
carried.
The first one on UNEMPLOYMENT reads as follows :-
"That this Congress views with the greatest concern the serious volume of
unemployment now existing, and deplores the lack of effective measures on the part
of the Government to attempt to alleviate the situation. It further declares its
emphatic opposition to the spurious remedies of lower wages and the degradation of
established working conditions, and calls upon the Trade Union Movement to offer its
utmost resistance to any attempt to increase the hours of work. It asserts the
unquestionable right of the unemployed worker and his dependents to adequate
maintenance, and reiterates its demand for the establishment of a National Employ-
ment and Development Board for the purpose of devising and co-ordinating measures
whereby the extent of unemployment may be reduced." -Carried unanimously.
Our second resolution which created a very good discussion, was as follows:-
VOLUNTARY AGREEMENTS.
"This Congress instructs the General Council to urge upon H.M. Government
the desirability of giving to National Agreements voluntarily entered into and approved
by Joint Industrial Councils the same validity as awards under the Trade Board Acts,
1909 and 1918, with the object of ensuring the observance of fair conditions of labour
by all engaged in the industry whenever requested by the parties to the agreement."
This was also carried, despite the opposition of the N.U.R., Boilermakers, N.U.D.A.W..
and Garment Workers, by a majority of 1,848,000, the voting being :-
For
Against
2,790,000
942,000
Thus we have been able, after a time to secure the concurrence of Congress that
agreements (when both sides agree) should be adhered to by those in the industry and
should have the force of law behind them.
We hope that the resolution will be put into force within the near future, and that
we may be able to record the fact that the Government has given practical effect to the
same.

NORTHANTS' DISTRICT COUNCIL.
The Quarterly Meeting was held in the Foundry Place School, Daventry, on Saturday,
25th July, 1925.
Mr. W. Langley, J.P., presided, and a full complement of delegates were present.
There were also present additional delegates from most Branches, as well as Mr. C. Bates,
Mr. W. R. Townley, Mr. L. P. Poole, and Mr. E. L. Poulton, as representing the National
Council. Altogether 62 members of the Union attended.
In introducing Mr. Poulton to the meeting, the Chairman said how proud we felt
of the new Office held by the General Secretary, and expressed the general feeling that it
was a compliment not only to the personal qualities of Mr. Poulton, but also to the National
Union of Boot and Shoe Operatives. He then called upon Mr. Poulton to address the
meeting on:-
"THE WORK AND POLICY OF THE INTERNATIONAL LABOUR
OFFICE OF THE LEAGUE OF NATIONS."
MR. POULTON said: Allow me, first of all, to thank you Mr. Chairman and fellow
members for the compliment you have just paid me. I feel that it has been a great honour
not only to myself but to the Union that we occupy such a position in the first world
Parliament that has just closed; because I look upon this as the nearest approach yet
to an International Parliament. I entertain the hope that out of it will arise a real Inter-
national Parliament, not merely to determine conditions and policy, but also to determine
political policy as well, so that men and women shall learn to know one another and under-
stand the hopes and aspirations of each other, and thus develop a spirit which will prevent
them from plunging into war in the future. I hope that all members will be behind the
League of Nations, of which we form part. It will, however, only be as we ordinary
individuals press our views upon statesmen and members of Parliament from time to time.
that we sha'l prevent war taking place, because, whatever we may say, the fact remains
that more men are under arms to-day than there were before the War. for even during the
time that a recent Conference in Geneva was discussing the limitation of the manufacture
of arms by private firms, the smaller nations were greatly opposed to it. Their advocates
in dealing with the case said that if countries like Great Britain, France, Italy, America
and Germany were allowed to manufacture arms, and they were not allowed to manu-
facture them, they would be at the mercy of the big states, and therefore they should be
allowed to contract for arms with private firms. These are amongst the difficulties we
have to deal with and to counteract in pursuance of a peace policy. Nevertheless I do
not lose heart or feel dispirited because I believe that there is a generation growing up
whom we shall be able to influence, and with the additional education they will make it
increasingly difficult for statesmen and politicians to indulge in warfare.
The International Labour Office is, I think, an Office which, at the moment, very
largely unknown. I have spoken at a great many League of Nations meetings in various
parts of the country, and many times I have been thanked for bringing before the meetings
the wide ramifications and activities of the Office, and what it has done. I must make it
quite clear that we are part and parcel of the League of Nations, and one of the few good
things that has emerged from the Versailles Treaty is the idea of the League of Nations
and the Labour Office. I hope we shall be able to accomplish the work that has been given
us to do.
Whilst the League of Nations has for its object the idea of universal peace, such a
peace can only be established by the establishment of social justice. The Labour Office
assists by dealing with such injustices and hardships. It is engaged in the regulation of
working hours all over the world and the improvement of working conditions. For
example, the regulations as to hours worked include the establishment of the maximum
working day or week; the regulation of the supply of labour and the employment of
children. It also seeks to regulate the conditions as to the employment and protection
of women and young children and the provision of certain forms of technical education,
Certain rules and regulations are laid down as to how best we can do that work, but
I will not take up your time with these. The first meeting was held at Washington in
1919, and strange to say, the Labour movement in America, who took part in the business,
has not yet seen its way clear as a country to join the League of Nations. I regret this,
especially as they are always standing at the door and giving advice, without actually
taking part in the business and being prepared to take responsibilities for the things done.
I find that the great obstacle from the point of view of America is that they have a great
ideal of a combined American League, that is, the development of those many countries
which are outside and around the United States, and which, combined, could dominate
the world. The point which they emphasise, however, as their principal objection is that
Great Britain, or the Empire-to accept the wider sphere-has an undue preponderance
of votes and influence, because Great Britain and each self-governing colony send their
representatives, and the Americans object to this, and say that only an equal number of


---
representatives should be at the meetings to represent the Empire. This is the real
difficulty why America will not come into the League of Nations. It is, however, doing
useful work, and it has helped in moulding policy. It is to be hoped that ere long America
will take her place amongst the representatives of this world-wide League.
I look upon the question of hours as one of the most important that can be dealt
with at the present time. We have drawn up a convention dealing with the 48-hour
week; the provision against unemployment; women's employment and maternity:
night work for women: the employment of children of immature age; the age at which
children shall be employed at night minimum age for employment in agriculture: white
lead weekly rest, &c., &c. Since the Office has been established we have been working
very hard, and we have accomplished much that is vital to the welfare of the workers.
Some people grumble about the slowness with which the work proceeds, but I submit
that we have accomplished a wonderful amount of work in the six years we have been
established, having regard to the slowness with which a nation itself does its work, apart
policy.
Representation at the Conference is as follows: Governments have two represen-
tatives; the workers of each country have one representative and the employers one.
Advisers are also allowed to act in an advisory capacity. These delegates are selected
by the government from the most representative organisations existing in the country,
and the government of this country have decided that so far as Great Britain is concerned
the Trade Union Congress is the authority, and therefore every year the Government
sends to the Council of the Trade Union Congress, which body nominates the delegates
and also the advis rs. The Government also send advisers who act along with the repre-
sentatives of the Government and the whole body splits itself up into Committees. The e
were seven different Committees this year. As we sent a number of advisers there was,
with myself, a British representative on each of the Committees that was sitting. Now
these Committees have the duty of putting into words what they think should be the
method adopted regarding matters submitted to them by the Office. When they have
finished their decisions are set out in the form of a report and presented to the full Con-
ference. The Conference then decides whether they will pass that report as a recom-
mendation or convention. You can understand that with 52 countries, with their different
outlook and development and the backwardness in industry of some of them, that progress
is necessarily slow. But we had better go slow
but pass reports which we do not put into operation.
Each country has a questionnaire sent to them at the end of the year in the form of a
book of questions for the next year's Conference, and are asked what they consider their
Government would be likely to support. The answers that are received form the basis
on which the Committee work and upon which the Conference finally decides its policy
and the form such policy takes, either a recommendation or convention.
During the past four years we have passed Conventions and Recommendations
which are a milder form of Convention-upon the Hours question. A number of countries
have adopted them and put them into force, but I regret to have to say that the British
Government has not yet put it into force, and that is why it is being held up in other
countries. Great Britain is the foremost industrial country in Europe, and the smaller
countries say that we in Great Britain have not adopted the Convention to put the 48-hour
week into force by law, and I have been told both in the governing body and in the Con-
ference that they will not put it into force until Great Britain does so.
Our statesmen
tell us that we have already millions of our people working the 48-hour week under their
different craft agreements, or even less. We do not dispute that, but this is not the same
as if it were law, and we need something to put it into law. When you get back to your
districts ask your Member of Parliament what he proposes to do in the matter, and go on
asking him until he does something in the House of Commons, because the more questions
there are put, the greater the likelihood of Great Britain adopting it.
At Washington, Great Britain voted for this Convention, and not only voted for it,
but they sent a cablegram over here to our statesmen as to whether they should support
it or no, and the answer was "yes," and yet, strange to say, they have not yet brought it
before the House of Commons. It is something of a disgrace to this country, and the other
countries are continually using it against us.
We have also passed a Convention upon Unemployment and what help we can give
in this respect, and a number of countries have adopted these proposals, and are putting
them into operation at the present time. Regulations as to night work for women we
have passed because we believe that there should be a minimum of work not only for
women, but also for men at night, and as far as possible we should not have them working
at night. We have also dealt with the question of the minimum age at which boys and
girls should start work. We have also passed resolutions upon white lead poisoning in
paint. We had a tremendous fight to get this through, and I again regret to say that Great
Britain has not carried out her word in this matter, and again, as in the question of hours,
the other countries are playing upon this. We have passed regulations as to the age at
which boys shall go to sea, and provided for their being properly looked after whilst on
---

the boats. We have also passed regulations regarding the weekly rest. We look upon it as
and parcel of the life of the nation, and
weekly rest as quite the ordinary thing and part and

When we have done with the Conference and have gone to our respective homes, the
governing body, which is really the Executive Committee, carry on the work from one
Conference to another. That body is elected for a period of three years. There are 24
members of the Governing Body, 12 being government representatives: 6 employers and
6 workers' representatives.
At our last Conference I believe we had 27 workers' delegates because unfortunately

great privilege that I have been re-elected to represent the workers, and I think I may
fairly say that the other delegates feel that I have done something to uphold their work
This body deals with the programme and secures information and details which
affect the work of the Office in all parts of the world, and there is growing up a more and
more extensive bureau, and nations are looking towards the International Labour Office
for information on industrial matters, and it is becoming more and more a basis of research
work. In a period of six years we claim to have collected in our Office at Geneva the
biggest industrial library in the world, recording all matters, so far as they can be got
together, in any part of the world, and that information is available for any nation.
on the governing body.
The Director is a brilliant Frenchman who played a great part in the Socialist move-
ment in France, and some think if he cared to return to play an active part in the work
of his nation, he would yet be Premier of France, and so you see we have a man who has
been brought up in the best traditions of social justice.
We are now engaged upon another great work apart from its industrial side. There
are wandering about the world men and women and children, who have practically lost
their country in consequence of the war. Many of them belong to the unhappy country
Labour Office to either get these unhappy people back to their own country, or to get
of Russia, and who cannot or will not go back there. We are endeavouring through the
they are established. The
believes that we are the body who are most likely to do this work effectively, and I think
it is a great compliment that such a young body has been entrusted with this work.
by the efforts of the International Labour Office. It is work which will also show to
think it is an encouragement to feel that thousands of men and women are being helped
people, in my opinion, that we can rise above the ordinary routine, and from the stand-
point of humanity we are doing this work at the moment with a considerable amount of
success. The methods adopted by the I.L.O., in relation to its work is broadly as follows.
We try to get to know the present laws of a country and by convention or recommendation
try and induce them to bring new laws into being regarding the workers and to bring
influence from the more advanced countries to bear on the backward countries. We do
not lose any opportunity with countries that are not carrying them out in trying to secure
conformity to the various proposals passed at the Conference. Last year I reported to
my colleagues (and when I got back they put the report into the form of a pamphlet).
This year I have just presented my report to the General Council of the Trade Union
Congress, and they have decided to publish it as a pamphlet and circulate it throughout
the whole country, because they feel it is well worth while that other people should know
what has been done at the Conference just ended. This year, apart from the Conventions
that were passed, we made progress in other directions. For instance, when one of the
Conventions was being discussed it was somewhat remarkable that nearly the whole of the
plaints that we workers had made earlier in the proceedings, and apologising for their

pountries not having yet ratified the conventions, and wanting to assure us that they had
had no opportunities in their countries, but they would at once assist to put into operation

We have put in a good deal of work among the backward nations of the world. This

is a thing that is full of hope. Some of our conventions have been adopted by the Eastern

We have now got to this stage, and you will see how necessary it is for international action.
This year the Indian employers complained of the competition of Japan. Usually it
has been the Lancashire goods which have suffered and complained of Indian competition.
You will see how intricate trading has become, and how necessary it is that these nations
should be brought up to our standard, but of course their standard would always be a
different standard to ours, largely on account of the climate. My reference to the term
standard means of course the productive standard per person.
We had workers' representatives of India and Japan at the Conference this year, and
I hope next year we shall have a Chinese representative present. We have been talking
to these men and they are very anxious to know our conditions, and they are bringing
pressure to bear that we should send representatives to these countries and see what
actually is taking place.
I come finally to the question of cost. As you have doubtless read, or been told,
that the I.L.O. is a great waste of money and that we have a palace in which to do our
work, and wherein we waste the taxpayers money of this and other countries. As regards
the palace we have a staff of about 300 people in one building and a big building is neces-
sary for all these. It is quite true that the place we meet in at present is situated in a very
nice part of Geneva, and we can look across the Lake and see the great mountains, but so
far as furnishings are concerned, I go into many offices in this country which are furnished
much more elaborately than ours.
If you go into the Board Room you will find ordinary
boards for shelving so that we can save expense. This is where we do our work, and as
economically as possible. We must have our documents translated into various languages,
and nobody should object to this translation so that other nations may use them. We
spend less than £40,000 per year, on the I.L.O., that is Great Britain, but the country is
going to spend many millions of pounds on ships of war, and yet questions are constantly
being asked about the expenditure in " our nest of luxury" at Geneva. In bringing to
bear our intellects and knowledge and taking part in conferences with the rest of the world,
and gathering information of great value to the industrial world, we spend the sum of
£37,000. That money is better spent than on ships of war which may go to the bottom
of the sea in two or three minutes, and I am anxious that we should take up the challenge
and show what great advantages lie therein. I hope you will continue to give us your
backing. One of the foremost representatives of the workers on the Continent said to
me not long ago, "You have got to remember this, that you have had your legislation
many years regarding many matters affecting the lives of the workers, whilst some nations

disease or injury, and for old age, and so assist in making the world better and brighter
than it is now for so many millions of toilers.

MR. TOWNLEY moved:--

"That, this Council, representing 31,728 members of the National Union of
Boot and Shoe Operatives, hereby places on record its deep and earnest appreciation
of the services rendered to the International Labour movements and to the cause of
International goodwill and peace, through the International Labour Office of the
League of Nations, by Mr. E. L. Poulton, J.P. We note, with considerable pleasure,
the recent honour conferred upon Mr. Poulton, and trust the utmost facility will
continue to be afforded by the Union to enable our General Secretary to carry out the
arduous duties entailed."

I think that everybody will be satisfied with the enormous amount of work that Mr.
Poulton is doing on behalf of the workers of the world. We should all agree that Mr.
Poulton has done splendidly. It is work that calls for all the thought, energy and con-
sideration that can be given to it, and I am sure that in selecting Mr. Poulton for this work.
we shall all agree that the Trade Union Congress could not have found a better man. I
have much pleasure in moving the resolution.
MR. I. H. JOHNSON, in seconding the resolution, expressed the hope that every
member of the Union would interest themselves in the work of the I.L.O., and give to Mr.
Poulton their whole-hearted support in the magnificent work he is doing.
MR. POULTON, in reply, expressed his thanks and said how interesting and yet how
hard the work was. He would rather wear out than rust, and he felt that this work was
essential and helpful.

The delegates were then entertained to tea by the Daventry Branch, to whom warm
and appreciative thanks were extended.

Many of the delegates afterwards visited the new Wireless Station at Daventry,
the arrangements for which had been previously made by Mr. Johnson, the Secretary of
the Daventry Branch. The opportunity was much appreciated and the Council's thanks
were extended to the Manager of the works and also to Mr. Johnson.

APPENDIX.

EXTRACTS FROM OFFICIAL DOCUMENTS READ BY MR. POULTON DURING HIS SPEECH.

Organisation and Objects of Department.

Whereas the League of Nations has for its object the establishment of universal
peace, and such a peace can be established only if it is based upon social justice; and
whereas conditions of labour exist involving such injustice, hardship and privation to
large numbers of people as to produce unrest so great that the peace and harmony of the
world are imperilled; and an improvement of those conditions is urgently required;
as, for example, by the regulation of the hours of labour, including the establishment
of a maximum working day and week, the regulation of the supply of labour, the pre-
vention of unemployment, the provision of an adequate living wage, the protection of the
worker against sickness, disease and injury arising out of his employment, the protection
of children, young persons and women, provision for old age and injury, protection of the
interests of the workers when employed in countries other than their own, recognition of
the principle of freedom and association, the organisation of vocational and technical
education and other measures;

Whereas also the failure of any nation to adopt humane conditions of labour is an
obstacle in the way of other nations which desire to improve the conditions in their own
countries;
The High contracting parties, &c.


---
Apart from the spoilt papers it was quite evident that a considerable number of
delegates had been under misapprehension as to the number of votes they were allowed
to cast. In numerous instances unions had not used their full vote, but where there was
more than one vacancy in the group they had divided their total number of votes by the
number of vacancies, and had thus failed to exercise their full voting power. We consider
it desirable that steps should be taken to prevent this happening in the future and suggest
that the instructions printed on the ballot paper be fuller and clearer and also that the
Scrutineers be thoroughly instructed in order that enquirers on this point may be in turn
correctly advised.

The Scrutineers and Recount Committee further suggest that the General Council
should consider the advisability of making such arrangements as will secure that there is
not a complete change in the personnel of the Scrutineers each year.

The Scrutineers and Recount Committee suggest that it should not be competent for
properly nominated candidates to withdraw their nominations at a later date than, say
14 days prior to the opening of Congress. If this were done and ballot papers not printed
until after the last date for withdrawal had passed, it would obviate the possibility of the
names of persons appearing on the ballot paper who were not condidates.

(Signed) HERBERT SMITH (Chairman).

CO-PARTNERSHIP WORKSHOP.
Report of the Leicester Co-operative Printing Society Limited for the
Half-Year ended 1st August, 1925.

We have pleasure in submitting the 65th Half-Yearly Report and Balance Sheet.
The Sales for the half-year amount to £27,170 15s. 6d., a decrease of £825 15s. 1d. compared
with the corresponding period of last year. After providing for Interest on Share and Loan
Capital amounting to £1,034 11s. 11d., and depreciating Fixed Stock, Buildings, &c,
£1,336 6s. 3d., there is a balance profit of £705 9s. 2¼d., which, with the balance of £237 19s.
brought forward from last half-year, gives a total of £943 8s. 2¼d. for disposal.
We have added 13 new members during the half-year, 11 have lapsed, the present
number of Shareholders being 607.

The decrease in Sales and Profits is somewhat disappointing, but the corresponding
half-year with which the comparison is made was an exceptional one with regard to large
orders of a special character which could not be repeated. At the same time the half-year
just completed has been one of the busiest we have experienced, and in view of the general
condition of trade which is reflected in the printing industry the Committee consider
that the result is satisfactory. The London Branch is doing remarkably well and shows
a substantial increase in trade. Another valuable machine for rapid printing has been
installed there during the half-year and has already justified the rather heavy outlay.
The Kettering Branch has maintained its position fairly well and continues to render
good service.
Detailed figures for the half-year's trade are as follows --

Leicester Printing Works £ s. d.
Box Department 16,790 11 11
London Branch 5,891 8 7
Kettering Branch 3,365 15 0
1,123 0 0

We are glad to be able to report that a definite scheme for the rehousing of the Box
Department is now in hand. The Society has purchased a freehold building site in Gravel
Street, separated only by a narrow passage from the Printing Works in Short Street.
Plans are being prepared for consideration for the erection of a three storey, well-lighted
and up-to-date factory, with provision for an additional two storeys when future needs
warrant an extension. The advantages of having this department in closer proximity to
the general offices and works, with the extra accommodation and facilities generally that
this scheme provides are obvious, and we feel sure will lead to considerable expansion
and development of this important branch of our business.
The present half-year has opened well with a considerable number of orders.
