CORRESPONDENCE.
The Council do not hold themselves responsible for the views expressed by Correspondents.
INTERNATIONAL SUMMER SCHOOL, SWEDEN.
FELLOW MEMBERS,
I attended an International Summer School held at Brunnsvic, Sweden, from
1st to 15th August, as a student sent by the General Council of the T.U.C., through the
National Council of Labour Colleges.
These schools have been inaugurated in view of the ever-increasing interest being
paid by the trade unions to the educational side of their work, and the I.F.T.U. run two
schools this year, one at Brunnsvic, and the other at Prague in Czecho-Slovakia.
On our arrival in Sweden, the students to the number of about fifty were the guests
of the Swedish Co-operative movement on the Saturday and the Trade Union movement
on the Sunday. During both these days everything possible was done to demonstrate the
warmth of the welcome extended to us, and in our tour of Stockholm and the mills and
shops of the Co-operative Societies we were shown many interesting sights, some of which
I may give in more detail in a later report, as the briefness of this report makes necessary
the exclusion of some valuable information as to the work of the young and virile Co-
operative movement in this country.
On Sunday our comrades of the L.O. (the initials denoting the general T.U. organisa-
tion in Sweden) took us for a sail up the beautiful inland lochs, and on our arrival at Vasteras
we parted with the singing of the "Internationale," the students proceeding onwards by
rail to the school and our comrades of the L.O. returning homewards.
Work commenced at the school on Monday morning with a lecture on the working-
class movement in Sweden. This lecture was particularly interesting from the point of
view of a comparison with our own country. Although only a small labour movement
(Sweden has only a population of 6,000,000) the workers of Sweden maintain an extensive
system of Workers Education through a body known as A.B.F. This body is constituted
by representatives from all phases of the workers' movement and every shade of opinion
from Co-operators to Syndicalists is represented in the governing Council. The Brunnsvic
school is a permanent organisation to which trade unionists are sent for six months' training
during the winter months. The location of the school on a lakeside among trees is com-
ducive to study, and the fact that the nearest town (Ludvika) is five miles away, also tends
to make the school free from interruption.
Albert Falkenberg, an ex-Cabinet Minister from Germany, dealt with the trade union
movement in that country and its latest developments.
Richard Sandler, Prime Minister of Sweden, gave an extensive lecture on Socialisation
Problems in Sweden. This lecture dealt in part with the problems of the present govern-
ment in Sweden, and in part with the programme of the Social Democratic Party and
probable methods of application.
J. W. Bowen, a member of the General Council of the T.U.C., gave a lecture on British
Trade Unionism, and dealt with it effectively in the brief time at his disposal.
Z. Piotrowsky dealt with the Polish workers' movement and the educational move-
ment also. A member of the school from another post-war country, viz., Czecho-Slovakia,
gave a very good lecture on the problems facing the workers in that country. Belina's
lecture was one of the most interesting I have heard for some time, and he was able to
explain from the point of view of a native of the country, the very complicated racial and
economic problems in that country.
C. V. Bramsnaes, Finance Minister in the present Danish Government, gave us some
information with regard to the workers' movement in that country. The S.D.P. in Den-
mark have only 55 seats out of 144 total, but are the government of the country.
Englebert Graf, the noted German economist, gave lectures on "Modern Tendencies
in Capitalism," and the "Materialist Conception of History." The best parts of both
these lectures were lost in the translation, as the technical terms did not correspond and the
correct meanings were very difficult to get.
Spencer Miller, junr., M.A., who is the General Secretary of the Workers' Education
Bureau in the U.S.A., gave a series of lectures dealing with American history and the
American Labour and Educational movements. Miller is a very capable lecturer and the
W.E.B., of which he is secretary, has over 2,000,000 members attached to it.
Comrade Brown, of the I.F.T.U., who was in charge of the school, was late in coming
owing to the fact that he was a member of the committee of eight in charge of the coal
strike, and was unable to come earlier. As most of us had not seen an English paper for
over a week, we were very keen to hear all the latest news with regard to the Coal crisis
---

---
549
Comrade Brown gave us a lecture on the crisis and was able to give us first-hand information
on the various negotiations between the Government, the Miners, the General Council,
and the Labour Party. I may say in passing that the Swedish Trade Unionists are very
interested in the present trend of the British Trade Union movement, and I was questioned
several times about the Quadruple Alliance and the Minority Movement. Comrade Brown
also lectured on "Migration," dealing with the emigration question over the last 100 years,
and another lecture was on the "International Trade Union Movement."
greater degree than usual.
The discussion on the lectures was very keen and the fact that each country had
lecturers of international repute representing them made the data obtained reliable to a
During the whole of our stay everything was done to make us feel at home, and
especial thanks are due to Mr. and Mrs. Hugo, the genial Rector of Brunnsvic, and his wife,
for their kindness during our stay.
31st August, 1925.
Yours fraternally,
THOMAS CAMPBELL (96068).
ITEMS OF INTEREST.
During the first six months of this year the Empire has taken 42.6 per cent. of our
exports of British manufactured produce and manufacture, whilst foreign countries have
taken 57-4 per cent. Of our re-exports of imported produce, the Empire has taken 17.9
per cent. and foreign countries 82.1 per cent.
*
*
*
On 13th July the price of the 4lb. loaf in London was 10d. At the same period of
last year, when the Labour Government was in office, the price was 9d.
*
*
*
The profits of brewing concerns were £9,971,000 in 1913-14; £18,400,000 in 1922-23,
and £17,840,000 in 1923-24.
*
*
*
The cost of a battleship of the latest type, inclusive of torpedoes, ammunition, &c.,
is approximately £7,000,000. The cost of a battle cruiser similar to H.M.S. "Hood"
is £6,000,000. The corresponding figures for a modern light cruiser are £2,000,000, a
modern destroyer £325,000, a modern submarine £400,000.
*
- The Labour Magazine, September, 1925.
*
*
As against the above, the British contribution to the League of Nations (including
the I.L.O.) is approximately £100,000 per annum. The whole of the expenses of the
League of Nations (including the I.L.O.) are under £1,000,000 per annum; the work of
the League is to save life; raise the standard of working conditions, and improve the
lives of the people. WHICH IS BEST? One million pounds for the League of Nations,
or the above-named sums spent on battleships ?
*
*
*
In the month of August this year, after eight months of Tory Government, the number
of registered unemployed increased by over 28,950 in one week. There was in registered
unemployed an increase of 176,085 over the number registered twelve months earlier,
when Labour was in office.
*
*
*
Instead of trade improving, the figures quoted show unemployment is again increasing.
So far from improving trade, the loss of this spending power has helped considerably to
destroy the home market. Every branch of the trades and industries supplying the home
market is necessarily affected by the loss of purchasing power by these successive cuts.

Contrast the position of the wage-earner with the investor of capital. Figures show
that 1,411 companies, after payment of debenture interest, distributed profits as follows:-
In 1923, £130,759,424; in 1924, £139,362,273; an increase of £8,602,849. There was a
total return in capital invested in these 1,411 companies in a single year of more than
£110,250,000.
The average rate of net profits being about 10 per cent., the investor in these com-
panies gets his capital back in profits every ten years.
While wage-earners suffer reductions, the banking accounts of directors and employers
increase, as the following figures show:-In 1918-19 the receipts from super-tax were
£35,500,000; in 1923-24, £62,500,000; an increase of £27,000,000.
In 1918-19 the receipts from death duties were £30,750,000; in 1923-24, £59,500,000,
almost double, and being an increase of £29,250,000. - Speakers' Notes.

ENGLISH C.W.S. ALL-ROUND INCREASES.
Another Dividend of 2d. in the £.
ONE MILLION POUNDS OF TEA PER WEEK SOLD.
Increases of 10 per cent. in the trade, 21 per cent. in the output from
the productive factories, and a net profit of £487,444, are the most
attractive features of the C.W.S. Committee's Report for the half-year
ended 27th June, which is now issued and will be considered at the forth-
coming meetings of the shareholders on 3rd and 10th October.
The total sales for the half-year were £37,160,319 an increase of £3,543,552 on the
The total value of the supplies sent out from the
corresponding period of last year.
productive works was £13,563,668, an increase of £2,362,993.
The net profit, or surplus, after providing for all expenses, interest on capital, and
depreciation of property, amounted to £487,444; to which there is added the balance
£114,249 brought over from the previous half-year. This leaves a total sum of 601,693
available, from which it is proposed to pay a dividend of 2d. and of 1d. in the pound on
the purchases of members and non-members respectively; 186,936 1s. 2d. is proposed to
be written off buildings, fixtures, accounts, &c.; and this leaves £132,548 13S. IId. to be
carried forward.
The society's reserve fund stands at £742,270, and the insurance fund at £1,181,146,
The bank deposits and withdrawals for the half-year reached the remarkable sum of
£290,827,808, which is an increase of £26,891,538 on the corresponding period of last
ferred to the reserve fund. There are 19,459 current accounts, of which 5,632 are with
year; and the bank shows a surplus of £69,342, which it is recommended should be trans-
individuals and the rest with co-operative societies, trade unions, friendly societies, clubs,
and other mutual organisations.
The sales of tea show a net increase, 1,355,535 lbs. for the half-year; but Newcastle

sale of tea is 26,168,838 lbs.; or more than 1,000,000 lbs. per week,

A big increase, 424 per cent., is shown in the trade of the flour, meal, and provender
mills. All the ten mills did well in sales, and all showed profits except the Liverpool
Provender Mill, which registered a deficit of £2,498; but the Liverpool African Oil Mills
showed an increase in trade and also earned a profit. -Co-operative News, 19th September,
1925.
S.C.W.S. PROGRESS.
Mr. Robert Stewart, J.P., presiding at the half-yearly meeting of the S.C.W.S. at
Edinburgh last Saturday, spoke of the continued progress of the society. Despite all the
critics said, they were marching on to greater triumphs. The sales for the half-year
were £8,673,158, an increase of £132,198 over the corresponding half-year, whilst the
productive departments showed an increase of £354,398, or 13.1 per cent., over the corres
ponding period of last year. A dividend of 4d. to members and 2d. in the pound to non-
members was recommended. There was a grand total of over £9,005,157 in reserves.
The S.C.W.S. was one of the largest distributors of milk, and in regard to fishing, the
curing works at Aberdeen had received several good orders from Russia and Poland.
Notwithstanding the smaller catches, they had good orders from Rus the orders. He
pleaded for better support of the soap works. -Co-operative News, 19th September, 1925.

TECHNICAL EDUCATION.
BOOT AND SHOE MANUFACTURE.
The Executive Council of the Union desire the attention of members
to be called to the following in the hope that the classes may be
both interesting and helpful to members.
LEEDS TECHNICAL SCHOOL.
The work consists of Practical Instruction in all branches of the Trade, in addition
to Lectures and Demonstrations.
During the Session, students have the advantage of working on any machines in a
Evening Classes are held in Pattern Cutting, Clicking, Closing, Bottom Stock Cutting,
selected Department and on various classes of Light work.
&c., Handsewn Making, Hand and Machine Lasting, Machine Making, Hand and Machine
Finishing.
NORTHAMPTON TECHNICAL SCHOOL.
This scheme of classes is as follows:--ー
1.-Day Continuation School for boys and girls under 16.
Day Continuation School.
2. Branch Technical Schools for those who, for any reason, are unable to attend the
3.- Advanced Day Courses in Boot and Shoe Manufacture and Leather Manufacture,
which may be attended either full-time or part-time.
4.- Senior Evening Classes in the following departments: (a) Boot and Shoe Manu-
facture; (b) Leather Manufacture; (c) Commerce; (d) Domestic; (e) Engineering
(f) Building; (2) Science.
LEICESTER TECHNICAL SCHOOL.
full-time and part-time Day Students. The series of lessons is designed to meet all possible
A complete Course of Instruction in Boot and Shoe Manufacture is provided for
requirements of the Trade. The subjects dealt with are as follows:-Anatomy of the
Cutting and Design Clicking Closing; Bottom Stock Cutting and Preparation; Lasting
foot; Leather Testing by Physical and Chemical Methods: Leather Selection: Pattern
and Bottom Stock Attaching by Hand and Machine Methods: Finishing: Costings:
Factory Organisation and the Principles of Commerce.
A thoroughly representative plant of modern machinery is used throughout the
College, and such additions as may be deemed advisable are made from time to time.
Boots and shoes in sufficient numbers are put into work during the session to allow
of a liberal amount of practice, and all students are expected to reach a satisfactory stage
of mechanical proficiency before the conclusion of the Course.
COUNTY COUNCIL OF NORTHANTS.
At Welingborough the Boot and Shoe Industry work is divided into Junior, Senior,
Advanced and Honours Courses.
THE JUNIOR OR PRELIMINARY TECHNICAL COURSE is intended for boys from 13 to
17 years of age.
THE SENIOR TECHNICAL COURSE, for students of 17 years of age and upwards, includes
Lecture Classes in Applied Science and the Principles of Boot and Shoe Manufacture,
together with practical instruction in different branches of the trade. This Course should
ordinarily occupy two sessions, and consists of two parts-Elementary and Intermediate.
The Practical Classes include Pattern Cutting (at all centres), Hand-Sewn making
(at Daventry and Long Buckby), and Clicking, Closing, Bottom Stock, Lasting, Attaching
and Finishing at the Practical Schools of Boot and Shoe Manufacture.
to those who have passed the Intermediate Stage in the particular branch selected. Elemen
As the accommodation for the Machinery Classes is limited, preference will be given
tary and Intermediate students who desire later to attend classes in Machine Lasting,
Attaching or Finishing, should first attend a course in the corresponding hand process.

CITY AND COUNTY OF NORWICH EDUCATION AUTHORITY.
BOOT AND SHOE MANUFACTURE. — Classes will be held on five mornings each week
and the instruction will cover the whole subject of Boot and Shoe Manufacture including
organisation and management. These classes will be especially suitable for “pupils”
in factories and for the most promising departmental students preparing for the higher
and responsible posts in the industry.

REPAIRING TRADE BOARD.

Half-Yearly Report.

The following report on inspection and enforcement in the boot and shoe repairing
trade (Great Britain) for the six months ending 30th June, 1925, makes interesting reading
The number of firms on the Trade Board lists on 1st July, 1925, were 27,350; the
number of firms inspected for the first time during the period of six months were 933;
the number of inspections made during the period were 91; making a total number of
1,024.

The total number of complaints received during the period of six months was 175, and

Arrangements have been made for the investigation of 49 complaints outstanding.

The results of inspection are as follows: - 856 firms were inspected on routine and
168 firms were inspected on complaint. The number of firms on whose premises current
notices were not posted were 194 on routine and 49 on complaint. Dealing with the
question of records. The number of firms in which no records were kept or inadequately
so were 629. These refer to persons engaged on time only. The firms who were found
to be keeping no records of wages or inadequately so were 319.

The total number of firms found to be underpaying workers on inspection of routine
or complaint were 150, and the total arrears claimed from these firms was £1,879 13s. 9d.,
whilst the total amount of arrears paid was £1,221 15s. The total number of workers
underpaid under inspection of routine and complaint was 198, and the total number of
workers to whom arrears were paid was 157.

Of the total arrears outstanding £54 13s. 9d. - due from 10 firms - is being paid by
instalments. The remainder, due from 25 firms - was claimed comparatively recently.

There were 913 inspections occupying one day or less, 85 occupying 2 days, 18 occupying 3 days, 4 occupying 4 days, and 4 occupying over 4 days. There have been many visits which have not involved inspection; 725 have been paid to establishments, whilst 154 have been paid to workers; 45 visits have been paid to Trades Union Officials, Association
secretaries, and others, whilst 8 visits have been paid in connection with permits of exemption, making the total of such visits amount to 932. The importance of the above inspections is the fact that where an inspection is made it is usually done with great thoroughness and often involves a considerable time being spent upon the premises. The fact that during the last six months 85 inspections have been made, occupying 2 days' time in each case, bears out the above statement.

WAGE BOARD IN SOUTH AFRICA: NEW ACT.

The Under Secretary of State for Dominion Affairs has transmitted to the Ministry
of Labour a copy of an Act recently passed by the South African Parliament which provides
for " the determination of conditions of labour and of wages and other payments for labour,
the appointment of a wage board and investigations as to wages and conditions of labour."
Persons exempted from the application of the Act are those engaged in agriculture and
forestry, domestic servants in private households, officers of Parliament, those whose
wages are governed or regulated by any Act relative to the public service or the railways
and harbours service; workers covered by an award or agreement which has been made
binding under the Industrial Conciliation Act of 1924 and which provides for wages not
lower than those in force under the new Act; and apprentices under the Apprenticeship
Act of 1922.

THE PASSING OF MR. FRED BRAMLEY.
It is with sincere regret we have to record the fact that Mr. FRED
BRAMLEY, Secretary to the General Council of the Trades Union Congress,
passed away very suddenly whilst attending a meeting of the Inter-
national Federation of Trade Unions in Amsterdam on the 10th inst.
It is well known that Mr. Bramley had been ill for some time he felt.
however, after a recent sea voyage, somewhat improved, and determined
to take up the work in which he had spent most of his life.
It was seen both at the Congress at Scarborough and the Labour
Party at Liverpool, that he was not at all well. His desire, however,
to help the movement was so great that he felt compelled to carry on the
work, and it may be truthfully said he died in harness. As a matter of
fact he died after attending a meeting, and passed peacefully away in
his sleep.
Mr. Bramley did yeoman service for the cause of trades unionism
generally, and it was hoped he had some years of service in front of him ;
this was not to be.
We can only hope that the loss of Mr. Bramley will be a spur and
incentive to others to continue to carry on the work of humanity in
which he spent most of his life.
To Mrs. Bramley and the family we beg to tender our sincere con-
dolences and trust the appreciation and high regard in which Mr. Bramley
was held by his colleagues will be some consolation to them in their
irreparable loss.

FIVE-WEEK MONTH.
There being five Saturdays during this month, Secretaries are asked to

THE "DAILY HERALD."
We trust that all our members will take every available opportunity to push the Herald, the only daily paper owned and controlled by the movement. By purchasing the Herald daily a full report of the political world as seen by Labour can be read.
REMEMBER THE FIFTEENTH !
Once again we feel bound to call the serious attention of Branch Secretaries to the fact that the FIFTEENTH day of each month is the latest day for returns reaching the General Secretary. If Branches fail to send off their returns to reach the Central Office before the 15th of the month it tends to upset the organisation of the work in the General Secretary's Office. We beg to intimate that unless returns are in before the 15th of the month, the Secretary has been instructed to put into force the Rules governing the same. Returns Sheets MUST also bear the signature of both the Branch President and Branch Secretary, or they will reluctantly have to be returned for completion.
TRADE UNION STAMP.
This is a matter which we have stated before rests with the members to make a success. It is in the interests of members themselves that "Stamped Footwear" should be increasingly purchased; therefore, push the Trade Union Stamp. Members and their families should wear only " Union Stamped Footwear."
TRADE UNION BUTTONS AND BROOCHES.
Buttons and brooches can be had upon application, price 6d. each, cash with order. Secretaries please note, when ordering buttons or brooches, cash should ALWAYS be sent with order.
We remain,
Yours fraternally,
THE COUNCIL.
T. F. RICHARDS, Gen. President.

Please Note. - CHEQUES NOT "PRINTED PAPERS."
The Postmaster-General draws attention to the fact that considerable numbers of cheques are posted in open envelopes prepaid d. only. Cheques, whether crossed or not, and paper money generally, are inadmissible at a lower rate of postage than that applicable to letters, and if packets containing them are posted insufficiently prepaid they are liable to be surcharged double the deficiency.

THE GENERAL PRESIDENT'S REPORT.
60 LADY SOMERSET ROAD,
KENTISH TOWN,
LONDON, N.W. 5.
19th October, 1925.
FELLOW MEMBERS,
Buy no boots unstamped or unbranded; the makers are usually ashamed of
them.
The work for the month calls for great comment, inasmuch as the LABOUR PARTY
CONFERENCE by overwhelming majorities decided to prevent any new organisation from
disrupting the Party by personal attacks on the leading and Executive members, which
has been the policy of a section calling themselves Communists and based upon the Russian
régime, most of them openly advocating the change made in that country as a legitimate
means for a similar movement to take place here; but history counts in the matter, and
although all of us, proud of the term Socialist, know full well that the communal life of
mankind is worth striving for; also that dictatorship by any section or class is the direct
opposite to the policy advocated by the genuine Communists such as the late William
force put forth as a remedy, one would think that only by such means can a national
Morris. When these new organisations come forward with old ideas, rehashed, and physical
change be brought about. Our opponents were never tired during the past twenty years
in telling the public. My own experience in West Wolverhampton was mainly taken
up during the seven years of my close attachment to that constituency. We were con-
stantly assailed with the statements that the advent to power of the Labour Government
or Socialists would see a violent revolution, but Labour has been in office, although not
in power, for nine months, and left the state of the country better than they found it,
and not a single institution suffered nor been afraid. The attitude of these so-called
Communists has left a lasting impression on the movement since their attack on the
Chairman of the Labour Party and Leader in the House at the Woolwich By-Election,
and their belated friendship is looked upon with suspicion. If they think they have a
claim on the workers for support, then let them proceed with their propaganda anywhere
and everywhere that they have the chance. Those of us who admire their enthusiasm
find it difficult to distinguish between their policy and that of the Anarchists of old, and
the Syndicalism of a few years ago, but we only have to look at their ideal country, Russia,
and we shall not be happy until the Soviets, instead of electing themselves, go to the
polling stations and take their power from the elected of the people. We are informed
by deputation from the General Council of the Trades Union Congress that the imprison-
ment of the Russian Social Revolutionaries is being continued, and this is because they will
not give an undertaking from doing the very self-same thing that the present régime did
towards the revolutions when Kerensky was in power. Without the ballot box this
method appears in all countries where violent revolutions have taken place, that having
served power when having no one to fight, they fight each other, and if war is bad, it is no
better having vanquished the enemy to then turn your attention to your friends. In
Italy (Mussolini), in Ireland (the deaths of Mr. Griffiths and Michael Collins remind us),
in China several would-be dictators, and in this country the Communists only believe in
Parliamentary action for a purpose, and it is for these reasons that my colleagues and myself
voted against these attempts to be party of a party whose constitution and policy they
were opposed to. Their motto should be "Do as we say, not as we do."
In spite of all this, I consider it a most disgraceful thing if a Minister of the Crown has
publicly gloated over any of this country's citizens being placed under arrest. There
will be time enough after the trial to express his joy, but at present one can safely say
that the Home Secretary will be greatly disappointed if he cannot bring about a conviction
in the light of his alleged hilarity. It is only those of us who have been condemned behind
our backs that can vividly feel for others so placed.
NORTHANTS BOARD OF ARBITRATION had referred the price of a Blucher to be dealt
with by the Army Board, and upon inquiry it was found that a special price allowed some
years ago, and then withdrawn, had been maintained. This was a very unusual thing,
and if permitted, once the facts were known, would have resulted in a firm being permitted
to make something in leather at a rate less than the statements provide for under the
heading of "all other leathers." That in itself provides for this particular article, and
ultimately the Army Board came to the conclusion that the statement already
provided for this boot, and the same was referred back on that understanding.

C.W.S. BOOT PRODUCTION.
For the half-year ended 27th June the total sales of the Co-operative Wholesale Society
Ltd., were £37,160,319, of which £13,563,668 was from the Society's own works. Included
headquarters, and of £309,213 at the London branch, these being increases of £56,646 and
in these totals were sales of shoes, leather, findings, &c., of £551,179 at the Manchester
£50,248 respectively. At the Newcastle branch the shoe and leather sales were £214,353,
as compared with £227,033 in the corresponding period of 1924.
The distributive sales in the shoe and leather departments were £1,074,745, while the
supplies from the Society's own works, tanneries and fellmongeries were £1,207,809, an
increase of over £130,000, or about 12 per cent. The value of the production in the boot
works at Leicester was £304,685, followed by the Rushden works with £228,305. Leeds
with £86,311. Heckmondwike with £63,950, Norwich £29,367, and Derby £26,191. The
supplies from the Pontefract fellmongery were £349,410, and from the tannery at Grappen-
hall £46,555.
Apart from the fellmongery at Pontefract, all the boot and shoe works made a profit
after charging interest on capital and depreciation. At Rushden the profit was £7.586
and it ranged down to the £591 obtained at the Norwich works.--Shoe and Leather News,
24th September, 1925.

BRANCH REPORTS.
BELFAST. - Trade continues to remain very dull in Belfast and district,
while the Derry Section sends the encouraging report that trade is good with
them. A meeting of the Trades Board (Northern Ireland) was held on the
23rd September. This was the first meeting of the reconstituted Board,
which is composed of thirteen members on each side. Our Union has five
seats allocated to them on the Board, all of whom we are pleased to state were
present at the meeting. The most important items on the agenda for the
meeting was the motion from the employers' side, moved by Mr. Smyth and
seconded by Mr. Kerr, "That the Time and Piece Rates be reduced by 25 per
cent." There were two motions from the workers' side sent forward by the
Branch Secretary; one was for a 10 per cent, increase on the present Time and
Piece Rates, and the other motion was one complaining of the unsatisfactory
system of inspection, and urging upon the Government Department responsible
to increase the staff of Inspectors so that the regulations made by the Board
would be better complied with. After considerable discussion, which occupied
the whole day, it was agreed to defer further consideration pending reports
from District Committees which have been set up till the next meeting of the
Board.
CHESHAM. - We are pleased to report a further improvement in trade
for the month, and that some of our members who have been unemployed for
a long period have obtained work again.
CHESTERFIELD. - Trade is still improving; we have, however, several
members still out of work.
DERBY. - Trade in Derby has slightly improved, but it is still very bad.
We have had two of our National Organisers with us, Messrs. J. H. Baum and
Len. Smith, who are doing good work, their only difficulty in making a good
number of new members is the short time that is being worked. Mr. Baum
addressed a Special Meeting of the Branch, when we had a fair number of
non-members present. He gave us a very good address, and answered a
number of questions satisfactorily. We, in Derby, are of the opinion that if
we could have the services of our organisers more often in the district we should
not be one of the backward centres.
DUNDALK. - Trade remains very uncertain, with future prospects
nothing more than hopeful, notwithstanding the fact that this, the boot indus-
try, happens to be one of the numerous tariff protected industries which exist
in the Irish Free State. Basing one's remarks on, if not authoritative, at least
pretty authentic information, we regret to say more pairs of boots and
shoes, particularly in the better class have, and are being imported into the
Irish Free State than before Protection. Something is wrong. We wonder if
the ministerial advocates of Protection fail to see the opening Protection
affords (profiteering!) Or are there only going to be two elements which
shall derive benefit from Protection-first, the Government; second, the
capitalist. The Government, by way of revenue raised from imported con-
signments; second, the capitalist by availing himself of the opportunity of
putting an additional three shillings on every pair. Boots or shoes usually
sold for £1 may now be sold for £1 3s. od. This may suit the classes, but what
about the masses? Are they beneath consideration? Painful rumours are
afloat in regards to solely appointed agencies extracting or rather endeavouring
to extract a 90 per cent. profit. Further, it is rumoured interesting aspirants
must do likewise or go without the goods. This state of affairs, if true, is
desperate, without even waiting to get hold of the market as business concerns
do. We are heading for the precipice of catastrophe, present omens prog-
nosticate industrial and commercial stagnation. The consumer, who pays the


---
piper, should name the tune. We are pleased to report that as a result of a
preliminary discussion, quite cordial relations exist between the employers and
our Union. Apparently those gentlemen consider combined organisation has
not destruction for its aims. We give it as our word of honour, and in doing
so speak for all fellow members, that it is our intention to assist any well.
meaning company to conduct its business in accordance with principles of
kindness first, and hope to demonstrate that social service is compatible with
industrial and commercial enterprise.
EDINBURGH. Repairing Section have been fairly busy during the
past month. New Workers are still quiet. Like other centres the Ministry of
Labour working of new regulations are showing more workers who are being
refused uncovenanted benefit from Labour Exchanges. Could not the Labour
movement organise a protest against the treatment meted out to workers
unable to find useful work. We see by Unionist Conference that the present
from the tyranny of the Trade Unions. As far as our knowledge goes they
Government are asked to pass into law a Bill to protect Unionist members
Branches have had another successful meeting in connection with Advisory

KILMARNOCK. - Trade in Kilmarnock not so good this month, but

KINGSWOOD AND DISTRICT. - The general volume of trade in this

LONDON SOUTH. Trade in London South continues to be very bad
on the 8th of September in connection with indoor workshop question in the


---
Sew-round trade. A long discussion took place between workers' side and
employers. Some of the employers are in favour, others against. Finally
decided to call a full meeting of the Masters' Federation to see if they can come
to terms. The Board adjourned until the 21st. Another Board Meeting was
held on the 21st in connection with the Sew-round trade. The employers and
workmen have had this question before them several times. The employers
want a 4th quality glacé kid sew-round, our side objects. Finally it was sent
to the Umpire, who decided on a 4th quality, but the workers' side still refuse
to accept the Umpire's findings, and the meeting adjourned until the 10th
October for a further report.
NORWICH. -Trade becomes rapidly worse and the immediate signs do
not hearten one to optimism in the future. Unemployed claims are heavy
and likely to remain so. The new members joining the branch in the early
weeks of the year are being lost in many cases through unemployment, although
we are confident of regaining them when work improves. The turnshoe
section has slightly improved in one or two factories on the evening shoe trade,
and we wait with some anxiety the coming of Spring orders.
NORWICH No. 2. Trade is not at all flourishing. With two or three
exceptions every factory is working short time as well as standing the men off.
Yet for all that it would not be surprising if the output for the City is not in
excess of what it was before the war. It is very apparent, excepting for a
short period during the year, the trade (under our economic conditions) is
carrying more labour than it can utilise during the normal week. What was
considered a fair sized factory a few years back is only considered a standstill
affair to-day. We believe we are holding our own with the other shoe centres,
though that is rather poor consolation. Our sick experience has been rather
heavy, due no doubt to the very changeable weather and to a mild epidemic
of influenza.
PLYMOUTH. -September has been very slack with us, and we are re-
minded by one of our unemployed members that October has brought with it
the "six waiting days," instead of the three as previously, before Unemployed
Insurance Benefit is paid. This means two weeks' unemployment before one
penny-piece is secured to keep the home fires burning; but it must be pointed
out that this is only part of the price to be paid for placing in power the Con-
servative Party after a big majority of working men were made to see red."
Now we see it was only a red illusion to attract attention while the swindlers
rifled the pockets of our poor down-and-outs. It only makes nine shillings
difference, but nine shillings to a poor unemployed man is much more than
nine thousand shillings to a rich one. Apparently the Government considered
that the unemployed benefits were too easily obtained, and encouraged working
men to be idle. They grew suspicious and could not trust the workers, there-
fore they made it a little more difficult and discouraged idleness by cutting
off nine shillings from the first week of unemployment, so please allow us to
record the following : October, 1924: The British working man entrusted
the Conservatives with the reins of government and graciously paid them four
hundred pounds per annum as a minimum only, Cabinet Ministers more accord-
ing to size of hat. October, 1925: The Conservatives show their appreciation
of the British working man by distrusting him, and recognise his kindness
by cutting off nine shillings from the first week of unemployment, then blandly
tells him it is a little pick-me-up for his moral fibre, that he is relying too much
on State assistance, that he will really be better without the nine shillings
than with it. When these platitudes are uttered at political meetings there
are some working men who actually nod their approval and bray like donkeys.
Yet there are intelligent people who say "man came from monkeys."
STAFFORD. Trade in this centre still remains very slack.
WATERFORD. -Trade was fair during the past month,

D. MCCARTHY.
During the past month I have chiefly been engaged in the Derby district, visiting the
following firms: C.W.S., Clemson's, Maden & Ireland's, and the Derby Boot Company
Unfortunately trade is not very good, and has therefore interfered with any marked
improvement in organisation. I have frequently met the operatives working at Maden &
Ireland's, where our organisation is very weak, many of the operatives having ceased
membership some time ago. They have been working very short time for some months
now, but by coming in personal contact with them and explaining the work of the Union,
further interest has been added, which I think over a period of time will eventually have
beneficial results. No Holiday Fund is in operation, and the operatives consider that as
two other firms in the town are paying, the position is not fair. This appears to be an
excellent reason why they should all belong to the Union, but as I have pointed out to them,
that until they show a better appreciation of the power of organisation, the matters that
they complain about can never be adjusted. With trade improving, I think that by
consistent work the operatives' confidence can be regained, whereby negotiations could
proceed which will gradually improve their conditions. Clemson's have also been working
very short time, and a fairly good number have lapsed, but definite promises are being
made that as trade improves they will eventually commence paying. Conditions are
decidedly bad at the Derby Boot Company, the operatives are not very responsive, only a
few of the girls seem to have any spirit, the rest accept the conditions and just grumble.
Fear appears to be the usual driving force at this firm, and no doubt the employer would
be at a loss if he depended upon anything else to conduct his business, and the only way to
end this condition of affairs is for the men and women to assert their position as human
beings, and demand humane conditions of employment.
I called a meeting of the whole firms in the town, which was fairly well attended.
A good discussion took place on the work of the Union. As a result of this meeting several
matters were raised with reference to the C.W.S., which no doubt will be adjusted.
I have again visited Mansfield, and met the operatives working at the Mansfield
working at Faire Bros., but no improvement is possible until negotiations are completed
Shoe Company. Our organisation is steadily improving at this firm. I also met the girls
with this firm. The girls resent very much the interference of the firm with their rights,

HULL.-Trade at the present is very bad and the prospects of improve-
ment do not look very bright. We were pleased to notice in the Trade Press
of our President's intention of asking for a 50 per cent. advance at the next
Board meeting, as we can all do with it and we hope he will be able to secure
that amount. We noticed also that the nominations of the District Committees
have gone through, and earnestly hope they will soon get to business. Our
Organising Campaign has ended favourably, considering the bad times and a
local fair on hand. We also wish to state we would have done better had the
age limit to include sickness been raised at the last Conference. We heartily
support our Plymouth brethren in their last report, and hope the Council

(b) the entry in or upon Insurance books or cards of particulars of contribu-

16. Unemployment Benefit.
(1) The statutory conditions for the receipt of Unemployment Benefit by a person
insured under this Scheme (in this Scheme referred to as " an insured contributor") are-
(i.) that he proves that not less than twenty-five contributions have been
paid in respect of him under this Scheme;
(ii.) that he has made application for Unemployment Benefit in the pre-
scribed manner, and proves that since the date of the application he has been
continuously unemployed;
(iii.) that he is capable of and available for work, but unable to obtain
suitable employment;
(iv.) that he has not exhausted his right to Unemployment Benefit under
this Scheme;
Provided that a person shall not be deemed to have failed to fulfil the statutory con-
ditions by reason only that he has declined-
(a) an offer of employment in a situation vacant in consequence of a stoppage
of work due to a trade dispute; or
(b) an offer of employment in an occupation different from that which has
hitherto constituted his usual employment, except that the Committee may
decide that either generally or in a particular case a stated occupation is suitable
employment in which case this provision shall not apply.
(2) For the purposes of this Scheme-
A person shall not be deemed to be unemployed on any day on which he is following
any occupation from which he derives any remuneration or profit, unless that occupation
has ordinarily been followed by him in addition to his usual employment and outside the
ordinary working hours of that employment, and the remuneration received therefrom in
respect of that day does not exceed three shillings and fourpence, or, where the remunera-
tion is payable in respect of a period longer than a day, the remuneration does not on the
daily average exceed that amount.
(3) A person shall be entitled to receive Unemployment Benefit for any period not
exceeding twenty-five weeks in all at the rates stated in the First Schedule to this Scheme,
and such benefit shall be payable from the first day of Unemployment, providing that if a
person is unemployed for less than three consecutive days no Unemployment Benefit
shall be payable in respect of that period of unemployment.
17. Under-employment Benefit.
Under-employment benefit shall be payable at the rates and under the conditions
stated in the Second Schedule.
18.
(1) An insured contributor who has lost employment by reason of a stoppage
of work which was due to a trade dispute at the factory, workshop, or other
premises at which he was employed shall be disqualified for receiving Unemploy-
ment Benefit so long as the stoppage of work continues, except in a case where he
has, during the stoppage of work, become bona fide employed elsewhere in the
occupation which he usually follows or has become regularly engaged in some
other occupation.
Provided that an insured contributor who has lost employment by reason
of a stoppage of work which was due to a trade dispute in which he is not directly
concerned shall be disqualified for receiving Unemployment Benefit for a period
of six working days; and this period of six working days shall be dies non both
in respect of Unemployment and Under-employment Benefits.

Provided that where upon a recommendation by a Court of Referees Un-
employment Benefit is, in accordance with regulations made under this Scheme,
determination of the claim, the benefit shall, except where the regulations
paid during any period intervening between the claim for benefit and the final
otherwise prescribe, be treated, notwithstanding the final determination of the
question is adverse to the claim, as having been duly paid and shall not be recover-
able from the employed person under the provisions of this Scheme or otherwise.
(5) The Arbitration Act, 1889, shall not apply to proceedings under this
Scheme except so far as it may be applied by regulations under this Scheme.
The Committee may appoint such officers, inspectors, and servants for the purpose
of this Scheme as it may, with the sanction of the Council determine, and there shall be
paid, out of moneys provided by the Scheme, to such officers, inspectors and servants,
such salaries or remuneration as the Committee may determine and any expenses in-
curred by the provisional Committee appointed by the Council to prepare this Scheme or
by the Committee in carrying this Scheme into effect, to such amount as may be sanctioned
by the Council, shall be defrayed by moneys provided by the Scheme.

receipts paid into the Unemployment Fund established under this Scheme on account of
income, shall, in accordance with regulations made by the Council, be applied as an appro-
priation for the purpose of such salaries, remuneration, and expenses as aforesaid.
23.
(1) A Court of Referees for the purpose of this Scheme shall consist of one
or more members chosen to represent employers, with an equal number of members
chosen to represent insured contributors, and a Chairman appointed by the
Council.
(2) Regulations under this Scheme may provide that any claim or question
which is reported or referred to a Court of Referees may, with the consent of the
claimant or the person or association in whose case the question arises, but not
otherwise, be proceeded with in the absence of any member or members of the
Court other than the Chairman, and in any such case the Court shall, notwith-
standing anything in the Scheme, be deemed to be properly constituted, and the
Chairman shall, if the number of the members of the Court is an even number,
have a second or casting vote.
(3) Panels of persons chosen to represent Employers and insured contributors
respectively shall be constituted by the Council for such districts and such trades
or groups of trades as the Council may think fit, and the members of a Court of
Referees to be chosen to represent Employers and insured contributors shall be
selected from those panels in the prescribed manner.
(4) Subject as aforesaid, the constitution of Courts of Referees shall be
determined by regulations made by the Council under this Scheme.
(5) The Committee may authorise the payment of such remuneration to
the Chairman and other members of a Court of Referees, and such travelling
and other allowances (including, subject as hereinafter provided, compensation
for loss of remunerative time) to any such Chairman or members or to any persons
required to attend before any such Court, and such other expenses in connection
with any Referees as the Committee, with the sanction of the Council, determines,
and any such payments shall be treated as expenses incurred by the Committee
in carrying this Scheme into effect;
Provided that compensation for loss of time shall not be paid to any person
in respect of any time during which he is in receipt of remuneration under this
section.
24. Financial Provisions.
(1) For the purpose of this Scheme there shall be established, under the control and
management of the Council, a fund called the Unemployment Fund, into which shall be
paid all contributions payable under the Scheme by Employers and employed persons
and out of moneys provided by Parliament, and out of which shall be paid all claims for
Unemployment Benefit and any other payments which, under the Scheme, are payable
out of the Fund.
(2) The accounts of the Unemployment Fund shall be audited in such manner as the
Treasury may direct.


---
29.

Where an employer has failed or neglected to pay any contributions which, under
this Scheme, he is liable to pay in respect of any employed person in his employment, or
has failed or neglected to comply, in relation to any such person, with the requirements of
any regulations relating to the payment and collection of contributions, and by reason
thereof that person has lost in whole or in part the Unemployment Benefit to which he
would have been entitled under this Scheme, he shall be entitled to recover summarily
from the employer as a civil debt a sum equal to the amount of the Unemployment Benefit
so lost.

(2) Proceedings may be taken under this section notwithstanding that proceedings
have been taken under any other provision of this scheme in respect of the same failure
or neglect.

30.

Section 26 of the Act shall apply to this Scheme as if it were incorporated in this
Scheme.

31.

In determining whether out-door relief shall or shall not be granted to a person in
receipt of or entitled to receive Unemployment Benefit or benefit under this Scheme, the
Committee shall not take into account any such benefit except in so far as it exceeds ten
shillings a week.

32.

(1) The regulations made under this Scheme shall provide for the return to a person
who is not an employed person within the meaning of this Scheme, and to his employer of
any contributions paid by them respectively under the belief that that person was an em-
ployed person within the meaning of this Scheme, subject, in the case of the employed
person's contributions, to the deduction of any amount received by him in respect of
Unemployment Benefit under a similar belief:

Provided that no return of contributions shall be made under this provision except
on an application made in the prescribed manner and within the prescribed period, not
being less than one year from the date on which the contributions were paid.

(2) Where under regulations made under this Scheme any sum has been paid out of
the Unemployment Fund by way of reward for the return of an Insurance book or card
which has been lost, the person responsible for the custody of the book or card at the time
of its loss shall be liable to repay the sum so paid, not exceeding one shilling in respect of
any one occasion.

33. Powers of Inspectors.

(1) An inspector appointed under this Scheme shall, for the purposes of the execution
of this Scheme, have power to do all or any of the following things, namely :-

(a) To enter at all reasonable times any premises or place other than a pri-
vate dwelling-house not being a workshop, where he has reasonable grounds for
supposing that any employed persons are employed;

(b) To make such examination and enquiry as may be necessary for ascer-
taining whether the provisions of this Scheme are complied with in any such
premises or place;

(c) To examine, either alone or in the presence of any other person, as he
thinks fit, with respect to any matters under this Scheme, every person whom
he finds in any such premises or place, or whom he has reasonable cause to believe
to be or to have been an employed person, and to require every such person to
be so examined, and to sign a declaration of the truth of the matters in respect
of which he is so examined;

(d) To exercise such such other powers as may be necessary for carrying
this Scheme into effect.

(2) The occupier of any such premises or place and any other person employing any
employed person, and the servants and agents of any such occupier or other person, and
any employed person shall furnish to any inspector all such information and shall produce
for inspection all such registers, books, cards, wages' sheets, records of wages, and other
documents as the inspector may reasonably require.

(3) If any person wilfully delays or obstructs an inspector in the exercise of any power
under this Section or fails to give such information or to produce such documents as afore-
said, or conceals or prevents or attempts to conceal or prevent any person from appearing
before or being examined by an inspector, he shall be liable on summary conviction to a
fine not exceeding five pounds.

He told me they had tried to get out a scheme for cheap boots and shoes, but that
the scheme met with failure. He also stated they had raised the wages of boot operatives
to amongst the very best in Germany, but that the conditions generally were very bad
and very serious for the population there.
What was interesting was the length of time which some of the speakers took to
address the conference, varying from one and a half to two hours without expressing for
or against the resolution, and then at another time quite a number of delegates would
want to speak at one and the same time.
At the public meeting the general trend of all the speeches was for toleration and
broad mindedness and a determined attempt to try and understand the varying conditions
generally as to help forward the movement of the workers for a better standard of life and
obtaining in the several countries with a view to seeing if such action could be taken
better conditions appertaining to the people as a whole.

E. L. POULTON,

ORGANISER'S REPORT.
DUBLIN,
FELLOW MEMBERS,
October 19th, 1920.
Since my last report, September 20th, I have been working in London. I also
paid a visit to Manchester and am now in Dublin, where I worked 26 years ago.
I visited several shops at Manchester on the 21st September, on questions of wages
and prices. One firm had not carried out their agreement with us in regards to charging
the machinists for cotton. Last Easter there was a definite promise given that the cotton
would be supplied free for the future. The pledge was given again this time that no charge
would be made for the future. The girls were to blame for not letting the local Secretary
know about this breach of faith.
Mr. C. Mayes, Council Member, and I, visited Chesham on the 23rd over a number
of our members working overtime and not charging for same. It appears that the
employers called the workers together in several factories and asked them to work over-
time without charging the extra demanded by our rule. They were told a nice plausible
tale about the order being a lean one, &c. The local Secretary and President did all
they could to keep their members up to a sense of responsibility and loyalty to a vital
trade union principle, but sad to say that in the cases of four shops they failed. There
is no clause in all of our agreements that a stiffer fight had to be put up for than this one
on pay for overtime, and yet our members thoughtlessly fritter away their rights and
undermine the very structure of the Union.
On September 24th the President of the London Metro. Branch, and I, visited some
shops in Bethnal Green. We visited many shops up to October 2nd, and found an extra-
ordinary variation in prices, particularly in the clicking, according to the position of each
shop in the way of organisation and trade union spirit. At the firm of Zimmerman's,
where we joined about 20 members, the conditions are bad. The Consol operator was
doing about £7 worth of work for £4. We had a good meeting of the workers of this firm
on the 30th. and they all agreed to stand by one another, and left it to us to try and bring
about an improvement in the factory. Mr. Mason and I had a long interview with Mr.
Zimmerman on October 9th, and I have hopes that as a result of this that a considerable
advance will be effected.
Our Union has tried its best to unite the whole of the workers in every branch of the
trade into one united body. The need for this was brought home to me when I visited
the firm of Messrs. Raynes, Waterloo Road, and found that there were as many as four
unions represented there and the majority outside any union.
When the General President and myself were in Ireland some weeks ago the manu-
facturers we met asked for piecework rates to be got out for the different shops. I am
in Ireland at present trying to do something along these lines. The organisation of some
of the shops makes this a difficult problem. A deputation of our Irish members visited
Leeds, Leicester and Northampton to see for themselves how the conditions differed in
the way of organisation, pay, &c. They unhesitatingly say that they could accept the
English rates of pay if the conditions all round were similar. The deputation are more
than thankful for the generous way in which they were received by the officials and members
of the branches named. Personally I join them in my thanks for the comradeship and
good feeling shown wherever they went.
D. MCCARTHY.

SEATS FOR WORKERS IN FACTORIES AND WORKSHOPS.
The strain caused by prolonged standing at work makes it important that seats should
be provided to enable the workers to sit at their work where practicable, or, where the work
which almost invariably occur in the course of a spell of work. Change of posture is
cannot be done sitting, to take advantage of the occasional opportunities for resting,
that such variation, by preventing unnecessary fatigue and other troubles, promotes
helpful and beneficial to the health and comfort of the workers, and experience shows
efficiency.
The provision of seats at particular works or classes of works is one of the matters
with which the Home Office is empowered to deal by Welfare Orders under Section 7 of
the Police, Factories, &c. (Miscellaneous Provisions) Act, 1916; and Orders requiring
seats for women and girls have already been made for several industries. The provision
of seats is thus recognised as an integral part of good welfare arrangements.
importance of this question, and to assist them in taking the matter up by placing before
The object of this pamphlet is to direct the attention of employers generally to the
Majesty's Inspectors of Factories, and by indicating how the difficulties sometimes met
them a short summary of the information and experience on the subject gained by His
from employers, managers and others as to their experience in this matter.
with can be overcome. The Home Office and the Inspectors would welcome information
The subject may be conveniently considered on general lines, under three main heads,
as follows:-
(a) Seats for work which can be done sitting.
(b) Seats for occasional rest when work must be done standing.
(c) Seats outside the workrooms for rest during intervals.
The types of seats suitable for use under heads will vary considerably according to
the character of the work and the occupations of the workers.
2. SEATS FOR WORK WHICH CAN BE DONE SITTING.
possible. Workers are often unnecessarily made to stand at their work either through
The aim in every factory should be to arrange for the work to be done sitting wherever
the mistaken idea that they will work harder and better, or simply because it has always
been the tradition or custom of the factory to do so.
It is certain that by some readjustment of tables or benches, the adoption of suitable
chairs, the introduction of mechanical conveyors, or other similar means, much work
now done standing could equally well be done sitting.
Two things have to be considered-(a) the type of seat, (b) the adjustment of the seat
to the work.
(a) If the beneficial results looked for are to be obtained the kind of seat used must
not be left to chance but must be selected with due regard to the nature of the work. A
badly designed or badly adjusted seat produces a cramped position of the body and inter-
feres with the proper functions of the body. The abdominal organs may become distorted,
predisposing the worker to disorders of the digestive system; or a position may be neces-
sitated which prevents full expansion of the chest and so tends to weakness of the lungs
and impoverishment of the blood. Again, a seat, which is too short from front to back
and has a sharp front edge or which is fixed too high from the ground causes pressure
on the back of the thighs and by hampering the blood supply produces cold feet. The
seat should be so designed and its position in relation to work so adjusted as to allow
the worker to carry his body in a normal position while at the same time relieving his
legs of the weight of the body.
(b) Where it is impossible to adjust the work to the worker, the worker must be
adjusted to the work by a proper arrangement of the seat. A rule suggested by the
elbows will bear the same relation to the working level when he is sitting as they would
Gilbreths in their "Fatigue Study" is that the seat should be such that the worker's
if the workplace were properly adjusted for him to do the work standing. Different
workers require seats of different height, and it must not be overlooked that seats which
are perfectly comfortable to one worker may be very uncomfortable for another. This
point can be met by providing chairs of different sizes, but the better and more usual
way is to provide adjustable seats.
as vertically.
seats should in general be easily movable, or they may be made to swing aside. But
In order that the workers may have the opportunity of sitting or standing alternately,
be fastened to the ground, and in such case it should be adjustable horizontally as well
where a worker is seated before a dangerous machine (e.g., a power press), the chair should


---
833
No general rule can be laid down as to providing backs to seats. In many cases
seats without backs are preferred, and are found to be more convenient in practice. Where
backs are provided they should be inclined only slightly backwards from the vertical,
and should be of a height which will afford support to the lower part of the back, if made
too high they interfere with the free movement of the arms.
A separate seat should be provided for each individual. Forms are inconvenient
for the worker, and if used should be made short and should accommodate at the most
three or four persons.
For occupations where the work extends over a considerable area, and cannot be done
from one position, a sliding seat may be adopted. The use of seats may sometimes be
facilitated too by making the top of the work-table adjustable, as in the case of "burlers
and menders."
Where the seats must necessarily be so high that the worker cannot rest his feet on
the ground, foot-rests should be provided, and if the work is carried on in a room where
the vibration is considerable, a spring attachment to the legs of the chair is very desirable
to minimise its effect, or blocks of rubber or other similar material may be placed under
the legs of the chair.
The shape of the seat should be square, of ample breadth, and saddle-shaped. These
will be found more comfortable for prolonged sitting than a round flat seat.
3. SEATS FOR OCCASIONAL REST WHEN WORK MUST BE DONE STANDING.
Even if it is impossible for the work to be done sitting, opportunities occur in most
kinds of work for occasional rest, e.g., during machining operations when the worker has
for considerable periods merely to watch the machine, at intervals between the conclusion
of one operation and the commencement of the next, or on unexpected occasions, as when
a machine breaks down or the supply of material runs short. Workers should be given
every facility for taking advantage of such opportunities, and the greater the physical
efort involved in the work the more urgent of course is the need for rest. The use of
makeshift seats, such as piles of material or an upturned basket, crates or boxes, so often
observed in factories and workshops, affords ample evidence of the workers' feelings.
Where such natural opportunities of resting do not often occur, the employer will be well
advised to consider the question of arranging for short pauses for rest during the working spell.
The seats should be provided in the workrooms if practicable, and will generally
consist of forms or stools placed along the walls or at other convenient points.
Difficulty may sometimes arise in providing seats in the workrooms without causing
congestion or interfering with the work, more particularly in factories closely laid out with
machinery or plant, but such difficulties are apt to be exaggerated and can generally be
overcome. Flap seats fixed to the workroom walls, or other structures, or attached to
machines, circular seats round support columns, and seats fixed so as to swing in under the
machines, are some of the expedients which have been resorted to.
4. SEATS OUTSIDE WORKROOMS FOR REST DURING INTERVALS.
Even if seats be provided in the workrooms, there should also be sufficient seating
accommodation outside for workers who remain on the premises during rest intervals.
These seats should be as easy and comfortable as possible so as to allow the workers
to rest themselves thoroughly, and should include reclining chairs, such as deck chairs,
more particularly where women and girls are employed. Such provision is sometimes
made on rather elaborate lines, but it can also be made on quite simple lines. In suitable
cases and under suitable conditions arrangements may be made for workers to rest out of
doors.- Extracts from an Illustrated Pamphlet issued by the Home Office.

THE BOOT REPAIRING TRADE BOARD.
Administrative Committee (Training).
The Administrative Committee of the Trade Board, sitting as the Central Training
Authority, met on Wednesday, September 29th, Mr. W. Addington Willis presiding.
Messrs. H. Crabb, T. Malone, W. B. Noble, J. Parsons, and J. Westley, with Councillor
Tallant and Mr. T. John represented the employers, and Messrs. P. Brennan, J. Buckle,
T. F. Richards, W. Orchard, W. J. Jarratt, and J. T. Rees, the employees.
It is understood that the question of the delay in the supply of materials to certain
boot and shoe classes was gone into, and action taken thereon.
Administrative Committee.
The Administrative Committee met on Wednesday afternoon. After formal business,
correspondence from the Ministry of Labour in respect of the interpretation of certain
terms in the notices issued by the Trade Board was considered.
The question of the objections raised by the Surgical Instrument Manufacturers'
Association was then discussed. The points raised by the deputation from that association,
which waited upon the committee at a previous meeting, were:- The definition of a sur-
gical boot maker, and why, being a maker, he should come under the Boot and Shoe
Repairing Trade Board. It was presumed that the 2s. minimum was merely to be paid
to a man when he was making a surgical or cork boot. It was contended the piece work
rates were not in proportion to the time rates, and were too low. Why was a foreman
shoe makers entering the surgical shop to be trained as surgical boot makers, the advisa-
to be paid 80s. per week when men working under him got 96s. ? The question of ordinary
bility of consulting surgical boot makers when any alterations are made in future, and the
prices for various jobs not mentioned, such as "T" straps, valgus plates, &c., were points
raised.
The committee then considered points put forward by trade correspondents, including
such questions as "What is the wage to be paid to a girl of 18 who cleans shop for an hour
or so in the morning, serves and attends on customers, does a little on patching machine,
puts ink on edges and irons them?" This came from Yorkshire. From a home county
town: "All coloured work (including covers), Is. 4d. Who is to find the covers, the
employers or the workmen?" From Scotland: "Is a benchman supposed to file the tops
of the rivets and level the bottoms and square them up ready for finishing?"
Subsequently, the committee dealt with applications for the registration of male
indentured apprentices, and considered the draft model form of indenture of apprentice-
ship, while considerable time was as usual spent in examining applications for permits
of exemptions.
Full Board Meeting.
The full Board met on Friday, under the chairmanship of Mr. Willis, when the nomi-
nations of employers' and workers' representatives on the District Trade Committees
were made.
The report of the Administrative Committee was received (as already reported) and
adopted with one or two minor alterations. It was agreed that in the case of one person
employed on various occupations, the rate of pay should be that laid down by the Trade
Board for the time occupied on each operation concerned, where such occupation was pro-
vided for under an order of the Board, and that in the case of benching the operative need
not file the tops of rivets.
Lord Haig and the Board.
Mr. J. H. Boraston, representing Lord Haig, attended at the invitation of the Board.
The Chairman detailed the various efforts that had been made by the Board Committee
to employ ex-service men, and instanced the large numbers of men who had actually been
drafted into the trade. Statements were also made by Mr. Baucutt, the chairman of the
semployers' representatives, and Mr. Richards, the chairman of the employees' repre-
sentatives. Mr. Boraston said he had listened with interest to the facts laid before him,
and he should convey the information to Lord Haig.
The motion, of which notice had been given by Mr. Baucutt, calling for the registration
of all boot repairers in order that it should be made possible to see that the orders made
by the Board from time to time were complied with, was referred to the Administrative
Committee to consider and bring up a report at the next meeting of the Board.
The question of apprenticeship and boy labour also occupied attention. - Shoe and
Leather News, October 7th, 1920.

EMERGENCY POWERS BILL.
This Bill, under cover of making provision for supplying the people with
the necessaries of life during an emergency was, and is in reality a Bill to
give the Government authority to put into operation powers which were
really more drastic than the famous Act known as D.O.R.A. The Labour
Members' fight against the Bill was explained to the Congress, and as a result
of their opposition very considerable alterations were agreed to by the
Government. The Conference, after full discussion, unanimously passed the
following resolution :-
"This Congress, representing the organised Labour Movement of this country,
condemns the action of the Government in forcing through Parliament the Emer
gency Powers Bill, which, in our opinion, strikes at the existence of the Trade
Union Movement.
"We hereby enter our emphatic protest against the Government's action
and instruct the Parliamentary Committee, in conjunction with the Labour Party
Executive and the Parliamentary Party, immediately to consider the whole
matter in order that a Special Conference may be called if necessary to deal
further with the question."
If it is found necessary another Conference will be called to further deal
with the question. The passing of such an Act-because, as amended, it is
now an Act (though we doubt whether it will be put into operation, there
being really no moral force behind the same) - ought to shew the workers how
necessary it is when an election comes along to return such members to the
House of Commons as will ensure legislation being passed which will benefit
the people instead of passing repressive legislation. The arguments for the
Bill were to ensure food, &c., being supplied to the people; we venture to
suggest that the offers which we know were tendered to the Government to
ensure this being done, had they been accepted, would have rendered it altogether
unnecessary for any such Act. We trust, therefore, that the lesson will not
be lost sight of, because an election may be sprung upon the country at any
time. Members should therefore get all their machinery in order, so that
when an election does come along it may find us ready for the same.
After this resolution had been dealt with, the report given in to the Congress
was that there had arisen between the Government and the miners some points
which it was impossible to settle; under such circumstances the Congress
adjourned shortly after 6 p.m. until 9.30 a.m. the next morning, as it was
fully expected that the matter would by then be settled. It was, however,
found, when the Congress re-assembled next morning, the 28th October, no
agreement had been reached, and a further adjournment was decided upon
until 2.30 p.m. At 2.45 p.m. the Congress had the satisfaction of knowing
that an agreement had been concluded between the Government and the
Miners' Executive. A statement on behalf of the miners was made by Mr.
F. Hodges and Mr. R. Smillie, stating the broad outlines of the proposals agreed
B

UNION'S WAR MEMORIAL.
As a mark of respect to those of our members who were either killed
in action or died of wounds in the Great War, we have during the month sent
a beautiful Memorial emblem to each Branch of the Union-copy of which
appears in this Report with the request that the same shall be hung in the
Branch Offices of the Union, thus keeping in constant remembrance the fact
of their sacrifice-
abolition of war.
"They died that men might live."
We sincerely hope that the result of the great sacrifices made will be the
The loss of life has indeed been terrific and the attendant
misery is beyond words to describe; surely the people will not any longer
tolerate a few men being able to plunge the nations into the maelstrom of
war again.
The outlook even now is not one to greatly encourage us; nevertheless,
we must not lose heart, but ever press on and strenuously support the idea
of differences being settled by the arbitrament of reason.
If this is done,
great as has been our loss and grief-as we feel the loss of our comrades-they
will not have died in vain.

To the relatives of 2,591 members of our Union who have laid down
their lives in the Great War, we once again wish to express our deep
sympathy.

The Union paid in all, over £81,000 to keep nearly 25,000 of our members
serving with the Colours in benefit; £12,500 has been paid out to the relatives
of the men as death benefits, and over 6,000 men have been trained in the
industry since their discharge, the overwhelming majority of whom were in
other trades before the war.

During the month a meeting of the above has been held to deal with a

November 16th, 1920.
DEAR SIR,
With reference to your communication of 5th November, relative to the request from
your Board that the National Joint Standing Committee be asked to give a definite answer
to whether or not the 10 per cent. is to be paid on the 1919 Kingswood Hand-Making
Statement, we beg to inform you that this matter was duly considered by the Joint
Standing Committee, on Tuesday, 16th November.

If your Board asks the Joint Standing Committee for an interpretation, they are agreed
that they have nothing to add to the March 1920 Interim Agreement, which states that
to per cent. shall be added to all Piecework Statements compiled or revised under the
Terms of the 1919 Agreement, and which came into operation on or before 31st December,
1919.

If on the other hand, your Board requests the Joint Standing Committee to act as
Arbitrators in the case, it must be remembered that the Statement to which you refer
was one of which the National Conference had no knowledge whatever, and the Joint
Standing Committee would have no other alternative than to give the same decision as
was given for the Northampton County Clickers' Statement, that is an additional 5 per
cent, on the Statement already in existence.
Yours faithfully,

DEAR SIR,
With reference to your communication of November 5th, we beg to inform you that
the resolution passed by the Leicester Board of Conciliation and Arbitration relative to
a claim made by the Operatives' Union for an advance of 25 per cent. for all Dayworkers
and Pieceworkers, was duly considered by the Joint Standing Committee on Tuesday,
November 16th, 1920.

After careful discussion of the matter the Joint Standing Committee unanimously
agreed that the question raised by your Board cannot be properly be submitted to this
Joint Standing Committee for decision, their province being solely to interpret National
Agreements. The resolution of the Leicester Arbitration Board raises an entirely new
question, and is one for settlement by your recognised local machinery.
