---
DAVENTRY.- Trade is still very bad here. This continued slump is
a very serious matter for our members, who find it quite impossible to make
ends meet on the scanty wages they have received during the past few months.
and we hope the trade will speedily improve so that there may be a much
brighter outlook for all those engaged in the industry. We wish to heartily
thank all those Branches who have so generously responded to the appeal
made on behalf of C. S. Coleman; the subscriptions to hand amount to £19 5s.,
and this has been the means of relieving him of a good deal of anxiety, for
which he is most grateful.
EDINBURGH.-Trade is very quiet at present; some of our Sections
are only working two days per week, especially in the mining districts. Repair-
ing section expect that with the miners' strike being settled work will become
more plentiful. We are glad that the Council are taking up the matter of
members being suspended from work to become entitled to out-of-work benefit.
Labour in our District at Town Council elections have had a severe defeat, as
Edinburgh and Leith became amalgamated, where in Edinburgh Town Council
there were six Labour members now there are only four; not one being elected
for Leith. The great fight in Scotland is, Shall we have Prohibition? At time
of writing the "wets" are winning, although some have gone "dry," so
that travellers will have to be on the outlook if they want a "Scotch."
Congratulations to General Secretary on his promotion to Chairmanship of
Parliamentary Committee.
HALESWORTH.-Trade is still slow and during the month several
hands have had to stand off, some only doing 2 to 3 days per week; the
shop has been closed on Saturdays for a decent while now, and during the
remaining five days we work 61 hours. Once again we wish to draw the atten-
tion of members about our Monthly Meeting; we could have the meeting in
the dinner hour, it would save expense.
HIGHAM AND RUSHDEN. - There has been an improvement in most
factories, although there are a number in a far worse position. The whole of
the workpeople in Bozeat were out the last week of the month and do not
know when they will resume work. Two out of three firms closed at Irchester;
T. Lilley Ltd., Irthlingborough, one of the oldest firms in the trade, only twenty
at work, others suspended. Most other firms are doing much better than
previously.
KENDAL. Trade has been very quiet during the month, the depression
affecting us in common with other parts of the country. This has been
accentuated by the restricted hours of lighting for power purposes in con-
sequence of the Coal Strike. We anticipate an extension of hours shortly.
A lengthy discussion took place at the Monthly Meeting regarding the two
proposals issued by Council as to the continuation of the present supplementary
system of working or a day-work system with limited output and wages. The
reasons for the slump are not easy to ascertain, but we venture to think that
boots and shoes are being produced too rapidly, hence the depressing period
in which the shoe industry is passing through. If there is a likelihood of
going back to the old days when the trade alternated between overtime and
short time, and which the supplementary system of intensive production
makes possible, then we think a day-work system should be evolved, whereby
operatives will be more regularly and uniformly employed. We are pleased
to say that our President, Mr. J. T. Rooke, was returned at the head of the poll
in the recent Municipal Elections; both President and Secretary are thus
members of the Town Council-a somewhat unique position.
KILMARNOCK. -Trade is fair in this district. We are at present
working 33 hours per week, this being put down to the Coal Strike, but now
the strike is finished we hope for better times. At our Branch Meeting we

LABOUR DAMNS "DORA."

Special Congress may be Called.

Dora's latest daughter, the Emergency Powers Bill, was discussed yesterday by the
special Labour Conference called by the Parliamentary Committee of the Trades Union
Congress.

When the conference resumed after waiting for a report on the miners' negotiations,
J. H. Thomas, M.P., took the chair, and announced that the chairman (E. L. Poulton,
J.P.), and the secretary (C. W. Bowerman, M.P.) had gone to Downing Street to ascertain
the views of the miners on the coal situation.

The Congress, which was private, thereupon proceeded to consider a resolution put
forward by. J. H. Thomas on behalf of the Parliamentary Committee in these terms :-

This Congress, representing the organised Labour movement of this country,
condemns the action of the Government in forcing through Parliament the Emergency
Powers Bill, which, in our opinion, strikes at the existence of the Trade Union move-
ment.

We hereby enter our emphatic protest against the Government's action, and
instruct the Parliamentary Committee, in conjunction with the Labour Party Execu-
tive and the Parliamentary Labour Party, immediately to consider the whole matter
in order that a special conference may be called, if necessary, to deal further with
the question.

Action in Commons.

The Daily Herald understands that Mr. Thomas justified the action of the Parlia-
mentary Labour members in the House, and cited numerous valuable amendments, which
tended to diminish the powers of the Bill against Trade Unionism.

James Sexton, M.P., seconded, and outlined objections to the Bill from the trade
union point of view.

J. Bromley, of the Locomotive Engineers, spoke in favour of more drastic action,
and Jack Jones, M.P., described the Bill as a concentrated attack on the trade union
movement. He was in favour of the resolution, however, for he did not want any hasty
action.

The feeling of the Congress was well expressed by J. R. Clynes, who declared that
we were living in difficult and dangerous times, when people in authority talked of the
rights of the people, but Labour was confronted with a new Taff Vale decision. It was
that decision which had created the Labour Party, and he was confident that the new
legislation would solidify both wings and make them one.

Robert Williams also spoke in favour of the resolution, which was then carried.

The Congress will remain in session until the miners' dispute is settled.-Daily Herald,
29th October, 1920.

Excess Profits to Vary. 903
As part of the settlement hereby concluded, the Government undertakes to make
an order under Section 3 of the Mining Industry Act which shall provide for the variation
of the one-tenth share of the excess profits of the industry payable to the owners under
the Coal Mines (Emergency) Act by the deduction therefrom or addition thereto of one-
quarter of the said tenth part for each 6d. by which the men's advance is reduced or
increased.
The certificate of the Secretary for Mines as to the amount of the proceeds and the
advances payable shall be accepted as final.

STATEMENT ON SCHEME.
Hodges and Smillie Thank Miners' Comrades.—" Remain on Guard."
The provisional settlement between the Government and the miners' leaders was
announced during the afternoon to the waiting conference of trade union executives called
by the Trades Union Congress.
Mr. E. L. Poulton, J.P., presided, and, the Daily Herald understands, immediately
called upon the miners for a statement.

Hodges' Hopes for Peace.
Mr. Frank Hodges' opening sentence "We have arrived at a settlement," was received
with enthusiasm.
But, he was careful to add, it is a temporary settlement. It did, however, show pro-
mise of a permanent settlement, and he hoped would usher in a period of peace in the coal-
fields for several years to come.
"You will notice we are not wildly enthusiastic," he remarked, "but we have got a
settlement which we believe is acceptable."
This had been brought by the solidarity of their organisation, and they appreciated
the desire of their fellow-workers to do all they could to support them in claims which
were right and just.

"Be Ready."
Mr. Robert Smillie, after referring to the strain of the negotiations, said that for
a long time they had endeavoured to establish a National Wages Board, and now they
believed it would be accomplished.
The way in which organised labour had supported the claim of the miners had been
most helpful, and he suggested that they should hold themselves in readiness in case
their help might yet be needed.
Had there been a Labour Government in power, he did not believe the miners' dispute
would have existed.

Labour Solidarity.
Mr. J. H. Thomas moved a resolution congratulating the miners on their stand and
in effecting a settlement. Mr. Harry Gosling seconded. Organised Labour, he said,
had taken off their coats. Let them see to it that they did not put them on again.
The Chairman intimated that the Congress would be called together again if necessary.
-Daily Herald, October 29th, 1920.

"WHO PAYS FOR THE ATTACKS ON LABOUR?"
The Independent Labour Party Information Committee follows up "The Capitalist
Press" with a most useful pamphlet entitled "Who Pays for the Attacks on Labour ?"
In this the various strike-breaking and propaganda Agencies of Big Capital are exposed-
The British Empire Union, Middle Classes Union and the like. Besides giving the facts of
the capitalist interests concerned in these organisations, the pamphlet gives useful and
amusing details of the aims of these organisations and the methods they adopt to further
their ends. The facts show that the organisations "represent," not the British people,
the British Constitution, Liberty, the Commonwealth or any of those high-sounding phrases
used to fool the ignorant. They represent Big Capital. It is Big Capital organising its
blacklegs, undermining the Trade Unions and poisoning the minds of the workers against
their own class, their own interests and the highest welfare of their country.”

LEICESTER SHOE TRADE.
Application of Operatives for More Wages.
A meeting of the Leicester Boot and Shoe Trade Board of Conciliation and Arbitration
was held yesterday, when there were present: Mr. G. Parbury (in the chair), Messrs.
W. H. Tomlinson, G. W. Hunt, J. W. Morton, J. A. Sabin, W. Russell, H. Hollingworth,
and T. W. Smith, representing the manufacturers. Messrs. T. Adnitt, M. A. Parker,
H. Cramphorn, G. Knight, A. Hill, W. H. Lowe, and G. Smith, representing the operatives,
and Mr. A. E. W. Chamberlin (secretary).
Additional piecework prices agreed upon by the clicking and lasting joint committees
were approved and confirmed by the Board.
The operatives' side submitted the following application:-"That, owing to the
continued increase in the cost of living, an immediate advance of 25 per cent. is claimed
for all dayworkers and pieceworkers." The manufacturers' side submitted the following
proposition:-"That the manufacturers are unable to entertain the application as, in
their opinion, the claim is one which should be made to the National Conference under
Clause 25 of the National Agreement of 13th February, 1919."
It was resolved by the Board, "That without prejudice to the powers of the Board
under its rules, the question as to whether the claim of the Operatives' Union for an
advance of 25 per cent. for all dayworkers and pieceworkers should be dealt with nationally
or locally, be referred to the Joint Standing Committee under Clause 23 of the National
Agreement of the 13th February, 1919, for adjudication." -Leicester Mail, October 3rd,
1920.
PROFITEERING ACT.
CENTRAL COMMITTEE, 54, VICTORIA STREET,
LONDON, S.W. 1, 2ND NOVEMBER, 1920.
A Mr. Coward complained of the price charged him for Soda Crystals by Messrs.
Manger & Son Ltd., of Kingsland Road, and the complaint came before the Tribunal
last June.
The Respondents put in evidence their invoices, which showed that the high price
they had charged was due to the price they had had to pay Messrs. A. G. Collins & Brother,
of Cardiff, and in view of this evidence the complaint was dismissed.
The Department in working up the case for the Tribunal found out the names of the
various merchants from whom Collins had purchased the Soda and the prices they had
paid for it.
The Tribunal considered that the case should not end with the dismissal of the Com-
plaint, but the transactions of Messrs. Collins should be thoroughly investigated and laid
before the Preliminary Investigation Sub-Committee. This was done, and the Com-
mittee decided that Messrs. Collins' profits were so unreasonable, that the case should be
referred to the Director of Public Prosecutions, with a view to proceedings being taken.
This case is a very good example of the thorough way in which the Tribunal does its
work, as owing to the order that the transactions of Collins and the other merchants
concerned should be investigated, one profiteer has already been fined and it is anticipated
that at least twelve other cases will be sent to the Director of Public Prosecutions.
REX V. COLLINS.
On 29th October, 1920, at North London Police Court, at the instance of the Com-
plaints Committee, Mr. L. G. Collins, the Manager of Messrs. A. G. Collins & Brother,
Manufacturers and Shippers, Cardiff, was fined £475 and 45 guineas costs for profiteering
in Soda Crystals (wholesale). Twelve summonses had been taken out, six of which were
for aiding and abetting his firm in the transactions, but as the defendant assumed entire
responsibility, these six were dismissed.
In giving the decision the Magistrate (Mr. Clarke Hall) said:-
This case is one of very serious public importance, and therefore I make no complaint
against the time it has taken, because it was necessary that everything should be brought

SOME OF THE CAUSES FOR THE BAD STATE OF TRADE.
In our opinion the bad state of Trade has been largely brought about owing
to the fact of the money exchanges of the world having been completely upset
by the war; the lack of transport to a very considerable extent; and the
need for a system of international credits to be set up to enable the wheels
of commerce to again be set going between the various nations. We would
once more call attention to the fact that money for the carrying on of the
Government of the country has to be found, and there has either, in our
judgment, to be a Capital levy enforced or a Forced Loan, and it must not
be forgotten that financiers of Europe of some repute feel one or the other
MUST shortly take place if an entire collapse is not to ensue, as a result of
the present chaotic condition of the World's commerce.
The present situation is indeed a tragic one. On the one hand, a vast
multitude of people asking, nay entreating, that they be allowed to work to
enable them to procure the wherewithal to live and on the other hand,
millions of people lacking the necessaries of life, and in many instances dying
because they cannot procure the barest minimum of those things that would
enable them to keep alive. Such a serious situation calls aloud for the
application of such remedies as will abolish the present terrible state of affairs.
We are convinced that with the will to alter the same it can be done: but it
will not be accomplished until there is such a live interest taken in all such
matters as will compel statesmen to not only talk, but act in a way to
relieve the awful situation at present existing throughout a great part of
the world.
INTERNATIONAL FEDERATION OF TRADE UNIONS.
A special Congress of the International Federation of Trade Unions was
held in London from the 22nd to 28th November, 1920, at which our General
Secretary attended as representing the Parliamentary Committee of the Trades
Union Congress. As a full report appears on pages 973-977, we do not think
it necessary to reiterate any matters here, but hope as a result of the
Conference that there will be in future a closer unity between the workers
of the world than has been shown in the past.
UNEMPLOYMENT INSURANCE.
Acting upon our instructions the General Secretary made application to
the Ministry of Labour for powers to administer the Unemployment Act, 1920,
but up to the time of going to press it is not possible to give any further official
advice upon the matter.
In furtherance of our own scheme, meetings have been held during the
month with the Manufacturers' Federation relative thereto. So far we have
nothing tangible to report.

RESTRICTIONS OF MUNICIPAL SERVICES.
It is significant of the present state of society that although we are being
implored by Statesmen and politicians that economy on the one hand and
efficiency on the other are desirous-with which we agree-that when real
attempts are made by municipal bodies to bring about such a state attempts
are at once made by interested parties to thwart municipal effort in this
direction. Two recent cases will shew the trend of the opposition in this respect.
One of a London municipality-FULHAM-who set up a municipal laundry,
whereby the ordinary people could have their washing done at a reasonable
price, and thus help to bring about a lowering of the present excessive prices.
The aid of the law was at once enforced to prevent any such policy being
pursued by the municipality, the decision of Mr. Justice Sargent being that
whilst a municipality might provine building and machinery for other people
to use, they themselves could not use it although the community might
benefit thereby.


---
Here is the text extracted from the image:
951
In another case the LONDON COUNTY COUNCIL endeavoured to
link up their present system of trams by suggesting that buses should be used
and thus supplement the existing facilities for taking the millions of London
working people to and from their homes. The matter was referred to a Select
Committee of the House of Lords, who have thrown out the provisions of a
Bill which was being promoted for this purpose. It should further be pointed
out in this connection that a private company can run its buses without special
powers, subject of course to the ordinary regulations connected with traffic
being adhered to, but a public authority has to go to all the expense of pro-
moting Bills to present to Parliament (and in the case named above the
expense is now waste).
We hope this reactionary tendency will be taken note of by the workers,
and at the proper time when appeals are made to elect persons to either
parliamentary or municipal positions, the vote will be used in such way and
manner as to ensure those being returned who will act in the interests of the
whole of the community, rather than in favour of vested interests.
ANNUAL CONFERENCE, 1921.
Under the new Constitution of the Union the first Annual Conference
will be held in LONDON on May 16th, 17th, and 18th, 1921, and if any Branch
has questions of procedure or principle they wish to raise, the same should
be sent along AT ONCE to the General Secretary.
FUNERAL ALLOWANCE RECEIPTS AND BRANCH RETURN SHEETS, Etc.
The above are continually being received by the Central Office incom-
plete the President of the Branch having omitted to sign. In many cases
too the "Exact Occupation" of the deceased member is not stated in the
place provided for the purpose on the Funeral Receipts. This information is
required for statistical purposes, and Branch Secretaries are requested to see,
in future, before sending to the Central Office, that all the particulars asked
for are given.
MONTHLY RETURNS.
We feel bound once again to call the serious attention of Branch Secretaries
to the fact that the FIFTEENTH day of each month is the LATEST day for
returns to reach the General Secretary. Last month many returns were late.
Branches failed to send off their returns to reach the Central Office by the
15th of the month, and this tends to upset the organisation of the work in the
General Secretary's Office. We trust in future every Branch Secretary will
endeavour to get in his returns as soon as is possible after the month (or five
weeks as the case may be) ends, and NOT to wait until the latest date for
them to reach the Office; also before sending them to see that they are in
order and properly signed.

GENERAL PRESIDENT'S REPORT.
FELLOW MEMBERS,
UNEMPLOYMENT. - The appalling unemployment in many trades in the country
calls for immediate attention and practical help by Government assistance of a permanent
character and the adoption of a National subsistence allowance for all who cannot obtain
remunerative employment. When one remembers that there are at least 250,000 ex-
Service Men who had offered their lives in defence of their countrymen's unprecedented
institutions one would think at least that the present Government would have practical
sympathy with them who they pretend served them, the Government, in the Great War.
We Trade Unionists need not be surprised if the present Government, whose opposition
notice for reductions in some instances and the refusal to consider advances in others.
to organised labour is growing day by day, leads one to believe that with the troubles in
the Tramways, Electric, Engineering and Hosiery trades, who have practically been given
that we shall not get much relief from the Government in this respect. We therefore
need not be surprised if in the vote now taking place in our Union the members in their
belief that a set wage will improve the position, may vote for it. In any case, whatever
happens, I hope we shall not alter the position of our abilities, and carry the same out in
accordance with the wishes of the members and give any prospective system a trial.
although speaking candidly, my own experience, if employers who want a great output
are desirous of getting the same, and their operatives to-day when on full work are aver-
aging 44 to 14 ros. per week does nothing to prevent the employer from fixing the rate
consent. I hear in many directions men who say they voted one way but intended their
vote to count the other. Excuses of that kind cannot be admitted, and whatever the
from £5 or 15 IOS., and by that means set a very high standard of output with the members'

JOINT STANDING COMMITTEE. On the 16th of last month I attended a meeting of
the National Committee which decided that in the Leicester case in which the Operatives'
side asked for 25 per cent. increase in wages, that was a matter for the Leicester Board
tion which you probably saw in last month's report and up to the present we have heard
and not for the National Committee. With the Bristol case we also made a recommenda-
nothing further from either Branches.
PROFITEERING. On the 19th I attended another meeting of the Profiteering Com-
mittee to inquire into the cost of repairing and to find out, if possible, whether profiteering
in boot and shoe repairing was being indulged in. As this work is very largely private
Until the Reports are issued, I have no wish to go into the details, only to say how interest-
ing it is to find the method by which these small employers conduct their business.
Although an opportunity has been given to some of our members, who think it might serve
our purpose as a National Union to take up a similar thing with the employers, I find
that those who were the advocates of this plan have not made the slightest move to carry
it into effect. I can therefore conclude that they have come to the conclusion that such
enquiry would do no good.
COUNCIL MEETING. On the 21st and 22nd we held our usual Council Meeting and
had the opportunity of meeting once more our old German friend, Herr Simon, who was
here as a delegate for the Trade Union Internationale. He, like ourselves, had no sym-
pathy with the great struggle in which we were engaged from 1914 to 1918, and like our-
selves, not only wished such had never been, but hopes that the latest estrangement will
soon be forgotten. I think we can all express in similar words our wish that that may
be the same.
TRADE BOARD. On the 25th, as one of the special Deputation of the special Sub-
Committee, we met at Chancery Lane, for the purpose of correcting some of the Trade
Board anomalies in the various statements already issued and to correct some of the
prices in order to make such anomalies more tangible and reasonable. In this respect
I should like to say that when we arrived at the conclusion of hand-sewn work some of
the employers were very anxious to agree to a 25 per cent. increase on some of those
anomalies, but we at the same time pointed out that this advance and advantages could
not be accepted as adequate to meet our claim of the 50 per cent. addition, which matter
is likely to come up for consideration on the 16th of this month.
GENERAL FEDERATION OF TRADE UNIONS. On December 1st we held our Monthly
Meeting of the General Federation of Trade Unions, and at that made arrangements for
the Trade Union Internationale being held, to which we were for the last time sending
three delegates. This has given to the General Federation of Trade Unions a 21 years'
experience with the Trade Union Internationale. At this same meeting the question was
raised regarding the relationship of the General Federation of Trade Unions with the paper
known as The Democrat, and a resolution was carried that we were in no way responsible
for that paper. We were also informed by the Secretary that the paper is wholly and
solely under the control of his son, and in premises not connected with the General Federa-
tion of Trade Unions either directly or indirectly.


---
FELLOW MEMBERS,
Since my last report I have been working at Wolverhampton, Stafford, Atherstone
and Manchester. On November 15th Mr. Alex. Smith, of Wolverhampton, and
I had a good meeting of the workers at Edge's, of Bilston. We met with a good deal of
opposition, but at the finish there were nearly twenty gave in their names to join. This
factory was the only one in the area outside the control of the Union, and as the firm
contemplates considerable extensions it was timely that we should gain the right of entry
in bargaining for wages, &c. From the 16th to the 19th, I was engaged at Stafford with
the Secretary in trying to get women removed from men's departments and men's work
asking that the work and hours be equalised as far as possible during the short time. We
had occasion to visit Messrs. Bostock over the discharge of eight girls from their factory
at Stone. Mr. A. Smith, Mr. R. Dunn and I had a long interview with Mr. F. Bostock
on November 18th on this matter. The contention of the management was that the girls
were exceptionally slow and could not earn the £2 minimum which they were receiving.
We argued that the piecework statement to which they were working was solely of the
firm's making, and could not be taken by us as a fair basis whereon to judge any body
of workers. We got the admission that the discharges were somewhat irregular and that
it would have been better if the prices had been agreed to by both sides - the firm and the
Union. We asked for their reinstatement pending the compilation of piecework prices.
Mr. Bostock promised to consider the question and let us know of his decision.
November 21st and 22nd, I attended the Council meeting. On the 23rd the President
of the London Metro and I visited two shops over questions of wages and the division
of work during short time. On the 24th we had a good meeting of the repairers at the
"True Form" factory, Edgware Road. The men complained of dismissals, particularly
over the discharge of their Shop President, the unequal distribution of the work and the
playing off of weekly wage men against pieceworkers. We had an interview lasting for
nearly three hours next day with the Manager of the factory and it was only when we
said that the particulars would be taken to the headquarters at Northampton that he agreed
to go into details with us. He said there would be no more discharges and that the work
would be distributed as fairly as possible, that any men set on in the future would come
through the London Metro. Branch, and that the new Shop President would be welcome
to see him from time to time as the men required. It is difficult to convict men on their
motives. The men at the "True Form" believe that their late Shop President was
victimised because of his outspoken ways. The manager utterly denies this and we could
get no further in this case. Zimmerman's, of Hackney Road, has taken up a good deal
of our time since the men joined the Union a few months ago. Had they been members
when trade was good the shop would not be in the state it is now in in regards to wages
and conditions. Mr. Mason and I tried our best to patch up a settlement here.
On November 29th and 30th we spent several hours at the above firm trying to get
the clickers back to work. They were sent away indefinitely simply because we asked
that they be put on the statement. The management said it was because of slackness of
trade. We also visited a shop in Mile End to ask the men to elect a Shop President, and
they agreed that it was the right thing to do. The firm of Solomon's, in Brushfield Street
has engaged the attention of the officers of the Metro. Branch for some time. I visited
this place and found that Mr. Mason's report of the place was true. The finishing room
is full of girls and the highest wage paid is 31s. There are two men on general work,
getting only £2 a week. I could not help telling one of the owners that it was places such
as his that brought discredit on the religion and name of his people.
It is not often I get an opportunity of attending a meeting of my old branch, Leicester.
On December 1st I went to the branch meeting and got a resolution carried that the Branch
Committee be elected for twelve months instead of six months.
On the 2nd, 3rd, and 4th, I went to Hinckley and Atherstone. Mr. W. Warner went
with me to Atherstone, when we held good meetings of the workers at the factory there.
On the 6th I went to Manchester. On the 7th I visited Frankenburg's with the Secre-
tary, and got a few concessions in some minor matters. We then visited Libstein's. The
eight women working in the machine room were locked out because they would not work
to piece rates of 2s. a dozen for doing the work through. We submitted prices which the
women wanted 3s. Finally we got an offer of 2s. 6d., but the women reluctantly came
firm would not accept. We saw them again on the 8th, when 2s. 2d. was offered. The
down to 2s. 9d., but Libstein's would not go beyond the 2s. 6d. So there is a deadlock at
present. In the other slipper works of Manchester the skilled machinists get fairly good
wages compared to the past, but at Libstein's the highest wage was £2 5s., and the women
are capable of doing any sort of work. There are five on 35s., one on 30s., and one on 25s.
The last two are not as experienced as the others. Hoping that our branches may grow
in strength and fellowship, with the compliments of the Season to colleagues and members.
DECEMBER 13TH, 1920.
D. McCARTHY.

---
Ireland is still the skeleton in the Parliamentary cupboard, and Members can never
free themselves from the sense of its grisly presence. It has affected the lobbies and
smoking rooms in a peculiar degree during the week. It was believed, with good reason,
that negotiations were on foot to bring about a truce, or a reciprocal arrangement whereby
all acts of violence and other provocative acts, official and unofficial would cease while
representatives of the Government and of the real leaders of Irish opinion endeavoured
to come to terms on the constitutional question. These negotiations have so far come to
nothing. The Prime Minister's habit of blowing hot and cold from one day to another
is partly responsible for the failure; the belief of the Irish administration that it can,
by calling in Beelzebub, cast out devils, has been a factor in bringing these promising
discussions to naught; but the most potent factor appears to be the existence of a group
within the Cabinet which is opposed to any settlement by conference and conciliation.
Mr. Arthur Henderson was credited with having brought back from Ireland definite pro-
posals from the leaders of the Irish people, and was believed to be prepared to return to
Ireland to carry the discussions a further stage; he may still go back to Ireland, but it
does not seem likely that he will be in possession of any useful information or offers from
the Government which would enable him to explore further the avenues he found open
in Ireland leading to peace.

There is one feature of this Irish question that is worth notice, all sections state they
deplore the present state of things, but the Labour Party alone make any real effort to
find a way out. We who are supposed to be unfit to govern are singular in the endeavours
made for permanent peace in Ireland.
Perhaps the most disturbing matter affecting Parliament during the month, or it
may be said for many months, has been the attack made on Members by the "Yellow
Press." Rarely has the House been roused to indignation like that shewn on Wednesday
last. The honour of Right Hon. and Hon. Members had been attacked, men who had
been described as "hard faced" boiled with indignation, they had been called "wasters,"
they who had saved so much during the War, erstwhile clerks who had become men of
great wealth during the War, were being told they knew nothing of economy. Had the
authors of this calumny been struck like the people of Sodom and Gomorrah it would
have been a fitting punishment. For these people had offended against the law, they had
attacked their own kind; if they want to make attacks, if they must abuse and misrepresent
someone, let them confine their efforts to the workers, let them tell the world the cause of
all our troubles is because the workers drink too much, or better still, tell the world we are
in our present state of chaos because the working class, belieing their name, will not work,
that the reason why ex-soldiers cannot get employment is due to the fact that unemployed
boot operatives will not permit them being trained in their trade. There is plenty of scope
for the purists of the journalistic world, and for them to leave that sphere in order to turn
and rend their own kind! This is the most unkindest cut of all.

The House was determined to do something, they would pass a resolution telling
these scoundrels what they thought of them, and if they persisted in their wrong doing,
then they would consider what further could be done. It was certainly a case where it
was difficult to say what ought to be the punishment, but judging from the manner of
those on the Coalition benches, it should be something with boiling oil attached to it.
One can quite readily agree that this economy stunt is most mischievous in its character,
and if allowed to have effect will be damaging indeed to the people of this country. This
was most apparent in the debate on Thursday. A resolution was moved to limit the
amount which the Government should spend in a year, but without saying in what direction
expenditure should be curtailed. Is there any need for doubt as to what branch of our
activities would be curtailed; the main part of the criticism of expenditure is against
Education, schemes for the raising of National Health, and the Ministries responsible are
singled out for attack. The extent to which the economist will go in this direction is
manifest from their statements, and the Labour Party cannot be a party to this short-
sighted policy. We have been told the struggle between nations will be keener than ever,
and can we hope to hold our own with our people starved mentally and physically? Is
it at the expense of these things we are to economise? We say, you cannot save by
impoverishing the intellect or continuing the ignorance of the people.
If looked at from the mere commercial standpoint it is false economy, viewed from
the human side it is a tragedy. Are we still to remain a C3 population? Is the sacrifice of
four and a half years war to count as nothing? It is not as if we cannot do it, the means
are here. It is a question of method, of system, of policy. While we allow passion and
prejudice to govern our actions we shall fail to accomplish anything in the nature of pro-
gress. And further, while we permit a few to monopolise that which is essential for all,
there must be inequalities, some must lack that which is vital to their well being. As I
write I have an advertisement before me asking for capital in which it states the profits
were 15 per cent. in 1917, 24 per cent. in 1918, and 25 per cent. in 1919, and while we are

BRANCH REPORTS.
BARNSLEY.—We are sorry to have to announce that one of our oldest
members has passed away, Arthur Crosland (1803). He had been a member
of this Branch about 35 years, and was always clear on his card during that
long time; a very quiet man and well respected by the shopmates who came
in contact with him; a shopmate for some time with Mr. John Buckle, J.P.,
of whom he was fond in every respect; a good number of his late shopmates
and the Secretary attended his funeral. Trade has been slack here for some
time, but now the strike has ended there is a better prospect of regular work.
We wish all our members the Compliments of the Season and also to all the
officials of the Union, and hope that a brighter time for trade will be ushered
in with the new year.
BELFAST.—Trade still continues very quiet both in the manufacturing
and repairing. In reference to the application made to the Master Bootmakers
and Repairers' Association for a revision of the Piece and Time Rates, the
following offer was made on behalf of the Masters:—Three halfpence on Men's
Soles and Heels; one penny on Women's Soles and Heels and all other sizes;
a proportionate advance on Heels and Pieces ; no advance on Extras. Time
workers' claim to be considered at a later date. This was submitted to the
members at a Special Meeting. After very full consideration it was decided
to accept the offer. The new rates were payable from November 8th. The
Secretary was instructed to write the Masters' Association respectfully request-
ing that the claim of the Time Workers be considered without delay. Might
we again appeal to the members and urge upon them the necessity of taking
an intelligent interest in the workings of the Branch. We cordially con-
gratulate the General Secretary on the very high honour that has recently
been conferred upon him by being appointed Chairman of the Parliamentary
Committee of the Trade Union Congress. We extend to every officer and
member of the Union the Season's Greetings, and hope the dark cloud of un-
employment which is now hanging over us may soon pass away.
BRISTOL.—Our report this month is a very gloomy one indeed; trade
is so bad that we wonder sometimes if it is coming to a standstill. No doubt
many things have operated to bring us to the present state, but of one thing
we are certain, if Russia and Central Europe were opened up there would
certainly be a good trade open to us. At a very largely attended meeting to
discuss the system of working before the vote was taken, it was almost the
general opinion that the Quantities Statement piece system, with the rush
that goes with it, was not good for the majority of members, but that a system
of daywork properly arranged by Statement would result in a fairer distribution
of work.
BRISTOL AND KINGSWOOD No. 2.—We can only describe trade as
desperately bad, and it requires the spirit of "Mark Tapley" to give utterance
to the good wishes which is customary at this season. The sudden death
of one of our manufacturers, Mr. Bees, has caused the factory to be closed,
temporarily we hope, but over 100 operatives are at present idle, and we
hope that arrangements will speedily be made for them to resume work. Some
good meetings have been held during the month. These dealt with the recent
vote on Controlled or Uncontrolled output, and we favour Control. In our
view, after four years' unexampled prosperity in our trade, the present depres-
sion has pinched the operative more than the manufacturer, the position
being more manufacturers riding in motor cars and more operatives in want.
This result leads us to believe that a change is badly needed. We close with
the sincere wish that 1921 will bring a revival of trade to our own and other
industries, and that we shall enjoy the blessings of a real Peace on Earth and
Goodwill toward all Men.

NORTHAMPTON. - Trade
here is very bad, large numbers being out of
work and all others on short time this we are afraid is to continue for some
time, thus causing perpetual unemployment and general distress. What have
our highly-paid Government and their officials to say to their neglect of pre-

and our Ministers have not a settled policy; all of them are trusting to luck.
We had a large vote on the proposed new Agreement, the vast majority hoping
that a change of system of working will abate the unemployment which is
so acute here. Well, we must "wait and see."
PLYMOUTH. We wish all members the Compliments of the Season.
Trade is quiet here, but the question of daywork versus Piece-work livened up
our Branch Meeting considerably; this is one of those questions which go
straight as an arrow to the vital point. Our members have read the President's
arguments for Piece-work in the daily papers, and our Secretary read the letter
in full which Mr. Richards had written to the Shoe and Leather News, in order
to give the members the best information and argument obtainable, but the
members would have none of it, not even with the sugar and syrup of "un-
limited wages." Mark the words, lads! it's a new one to piece-workers.
What visions of unbounded wealth this brought before our minds. We have
some very old workers at Plymouth but they have never yet seen a millionaire
Piece-worker; probably we are rather slow in the South, but if there were no
limit we should get there alright. Our Branch has passed a resolution calling
upon the Council to take a ballot of the members on the straight issue of Piece-
work versus Day-work, without reference to quantities, being of the opinion
that Quantity Statements is but another form of piece-work; this is in accord
with our General President's declaration at the Special Conference held at
Leicester, February 20th, 1919, when, explaining the Agreement, he said,
"I know some of our members prefer to go on daywork, but if they work to
Quantity Statement they become piece-workers." We realise to the full
that the present depression of trade is largely the backwash of the War, but
under-employment and unemployment is no new thing to our trade, and our

present system of Piece-work unfortunately lends itself to it by allowing the
employers to bring hundreds of men to the trade without the responsibility
of finding them a week's work or a week's money, but merely keeping them
as a reserve army in case of a push.

PORTSMOUTH. -We have to report with regret this month that trade
does not seem to improve, and many men are still walking about in despair;
we know of many cases where no job has been taken in for several days, and
each morning the men hear the words "nothing to-day." We can only hope
the New Year may see a great change in industrial conditions. We refrain
from saying more at this time but hope to record signs of improvement in
our next report.

READING. -Trade is very dull here; there are a large number of
Repairers out of work, also all shops working short time. We have a large
number of small shops doing repairs at starvation prices; they do the work
themselves, so we cannot touch them on wages; there is also a man who comes
to Market on Saturdays and takes away repairs, doing gents' at 4s. 6d. and
ladies' 3s., which is not making it better for the men that are walking about.
Wishing all officers and members the Compliments of the Season.

SOUTHAMPTON. -Trade is slack in the boot repairing here, and also
amongst most of the trades in connection with shipping there is a slackness.
several large firms working short time in their various branches of manufacture
May we hope that the coming year will improve very much and that instead
of the present uncertainty all business will get more normal and steady. As
this is out final published report for 1920 we send out heartiest Greeting to
headquarters and all Branches, hoping with them to have a better time in the
coming year.

STAFFORD. - Trade still remains very slack here, in fact, instead of
any improvement it is the reverse, the oldest members of our Branch declaring
they have never experienced such an acute depression; everything points to
the festive season being one which will bring with it great suffering for a lot
of our members.

STREET. - There is no improvement in the state of trade in this district
and a very large proportion of our members are working less than half time.
We are glad, however, that with few exceptions our members remain loyal to
their Union and are determined to keep their contributions up to date. At
such times as these it is more than necessary that our organisation shall keep
up its strength. After long, careful consideration and discussion we have
definitely decided to carry the good work into the political field, and at a
special Branch Meeting held on November 16th, which was addressed by
Mr. Len. Smith, Council Member for Northampton, it was unanimously decided
to nominate Mr. Smith to contest our Parliamentary Division in the Labour
interest at the next election. Most of the polling areas now have their Labour
organisations, and there is no reason why we may not be successful in the
fight.

INTERNATIONAL FEDERATION OF TRADE UNIONS.
SPECIAL CONGRESS HELD IN LONDON, NOV. 22nd to 28th, 1920.
Synoposis of Business transacted thereat by E. L. Poulton, J.P., Gen. Sec.
The meetings were held at the Holborn Restaurant. Mr. W. A. Appleton having
resigned as Chairman of the Federation, Mr. J. H. Thomas, M.P., was upon the proposal
of Mr. E. L. Poulton, J.P., appointed in Mr. Appleton's place.
The business of such a Congress must of necessity proceed slowly owing to the diffi-
culty of there being so many languages spoken, the speeches therefore having to be trans-
lated, as a minimum, four times.
The Conference was a great success, there being a wonderfully good feeling shown
throughout the whole week. Seventeen countries were represented, some having as
many as twelve delegates. The principal business dealt with was as follows:

After the Congress had been opened by the Rt. Hon. J. H. Thomas, M.P. and M.
Jouhaux, the international position was discussed by E. Fimmen. After a full discussion
a resolution was proposed, protesting against all open and covert attempts of the dominant
class throughout the world to suppress freedom of organisation and action by the workers;
it raised emphatic protest against the military and commercial war still carried on against
Russia; declared that it is the primary duty of all national trade union centres and of
all workers to use every means to fight the world reaction which threatens the growth,
existence and very life of the trade union movement.
The opinion was voiced that, both nationally and internationally, the movement,
besides systematic pursuit for better conditions of labour, must join issue against Capitalism
and imperialism; that militarism must be combated in every form, and :
"The trade union movement of all countries should resort to general withdrawal
of labour and to international boycott as a stern and effectual weapon in the fight
against reaction and for progress."
An urgent appeal was made for co-operation "to impress on the working classes
the necessity for international mass action in the assault on reaction, in declaring war
against war, and for the realisation of a new social system."
The resolution was carried by 21,906,000 votes to 2,710,000, Italy, Norway and
Canada voting against.

THE INTERNATIONAL LABOUR CONFERENCE.
WASHINGTON, D.C., December, 1919.
It was our privilege, as visitors, to attend the International Labour Conference pro-
vided for in the Covenant of the League of Nations.
In addition to studying the Conference, our instructions were to study and promote
the interests of the International Federation of Trade Unions.
The Conference was held in the Pan-American Buildings, Washington, D.C., and
opened on the 29th October. On the first day there were 32 countries represented, the
principal European exceptions being Germany, Austria, Hungary, and Russia. Owing
to representations made in London prior to the Conference Mr. G. N. Barnes specially
interviewed the Supreme Council in Paris, his object being to secure the removal of any
obstacles to the attendance in Washington of the delegates of the Central Powers. The
American Embassy gave courteous and sympathetic assistance, and before the end of
September the restrictions imposed by the Treaty of Peace and the Covenant of the League
of Nations had been waived. In these circumstances it was confidently expected that the
German and Austrian representatives would be present. Unfortunately, their internal
difficulties were too great, and they were unable to make the journey.
The absence of nations which were certain to be serious competitors for the world's
business was regrettable, both from the moral and material point of view. Their presence
might have helped the healing of the world's wounds and the closing of the breaches
between the peoples. Not being present they could claim liberty to adopt, to modify,
or to reject any of the findings of the Conference in respect of wages, hours, or conditions
of Labour. That they do not regard these findings as binding may be inferred from their
attitude towards piecework, as illustrated at the International Conference of the Garment
Workers, which was recently held in Amsterdam. There they frankly argued that piece-
work offered their only means of industrial regeneration.
In fairness to these countries it must be admitted that they cannot pay the debts
incurred unless every individual within them produces very much more than is required
for his own maintenance and the maintenance of his dependents.
Much more serious than the absence of the Germans and Austrians was the official
abstention of the United States of America. Taking into consideration the object of the
Conference and the importance of the subjects to be discussed, it was more than unfortunate
that the country responsible, through its own President, for issuing the invitations to the
Conference was not officially represented. Unofficially, Secretary W. B. Wilson and Mr.
Samuel Gompers attended.
The attitude of the American people towards the Conference was reflected by the
American Press, which, after the first day, gave only limited consideration and space to
the proceedings. Generally speaking, there was a courteous suppression of actually
hostile sentiments, but there were many who doubted the practical utility of the Con-
ference and not a few who doubted the bona-fides of those who persisted in precipitating
the Conference upon an unready community.
Not all the Americans suppressed their opinions in order to maintain the national
reputation for courtesy. Senators Sherman, Reed, and La Follette all gave utterance
to pungent criticisms. The former, speaking at New York a few days after the Conference
opened, said that the delegates had been dumped there from all parts of the world, that
they were Radicals (Reds) working for no good purpose, and they were bent on fanning
into a flame the discontent in America. If he had the power he would deport the Inter-
national Labour Conference. It was no time for such a Conference. They had enough
stormy petrels in their own country without having the Internationals to swamp them.
Americans were often kind enough to say that Senator Sherman only represented
himself. Our inquiries, however, led us to believe that, while the language used might be
typical only of Senator Sherman, the thought behind was the thought of the great mass
of the American people. They saw no advantage to the world or themselves in discussing
what they regarded as platitudes, and they feared the effect of the discussions upon the
minds and activities of those who in America are hostile to American methods and institu-
tions.
The active opposition to the Conference may be to a great extent attributed to partisan
politics, because there are men in the Republican Party who feel that to the extent they
can belittle the Peace Treaty and its machinery to that extent they can weaken the position
of the Democartic Party and that of the President.
This situation could, and ought to, have been foreseen by the British Government,
and appears to justify our conclusion that the Government, in view of the opposition of
the Senate to the Treaty of Peace and to the action of President Wilson, made a great
mistake in pushing the arrangements for the Conference and in temporarily financing it.

arranged by consent of the Bureau and the delegates for 3 o'clock in the afternoon. This

the Pan-American Building on the afternoon preceding the date fixed for this particular
meeting for the purpose of assuring ourselves that no change in the programme was con-
templated. The assurances we received were definite, but the river trip prevailed.
We personally apologised to Mr. Gompers for the slight placed upon the American
Federation of Labour. We reiterate our regret that an international gathering of Trade
Unionists should have acted so carelessly at a moment when the Federation was gravely
concerned with the steel strike, the miners' strike, and the injunction which the Courts
of Law had granted against the mine workers of America.
This expression of the delegates' concern for Trade Unionism determined the length
of our stay in Washington. We discussed the situation as we saw it, and decided that
there was no justification for any longer stay in this city than was necessary to enable
us to fulfil our obligations to the American Federation of Labour. We, therefore, made
our arrangements for returning home.
During our stay we attended meetings, or attended at the places where meetings
had been convened, on the 29th of October and on the 1st, 4th, 5th, 7th, and 8th of Novem-
ber. We also took occasion to see many of the American Trade Unionists who came to
Washington for their special conference on the 9th of November.

**TRADE UNION CONGRESS PARLIAMENTARY COMMITTEE.**

**UNEMPLOYMENT INSURANCE BILL.**
Protest against its Administration by Friendly Societies.

TO THE OFFICIALS AND MEMBERS OF AFFILIATED SOCIETIES.

GENTLEMEN, -As you will doubtless have seen from the public Press, the Parliamentary
Committee of the Trades Union Congress found it necessary to record their protest against
an amendment to the Unemployment Insurance Bill, carried in Standing Committee C,
on Tuesday, the 23rd inst., by 24 votes to 16, the effect of which is to make Industrial
and Friendly Societies' and employers' organisations parties to the administration of
Unemployed benefit. The Parliamentary Committee happened to be meeting that day,
and, upon being informed of what had occurred during the discussion of the Bill in Standing
Committee, unanimously passed the following resolution, copies of which were forwarded
to the Prime Minister and to the Press :-

"That this Parliamentary Committee, representing the whole of the organised
workers in the United Kingdom, having been informed of the amendment passed
to-day by the Committee considering the Unemployed Insurance Bill, which enables
Industrial and Friendly Societies and employers' organisations to administer the
Unemployed Benefit, as provided in the Bill now in Committee, desire to inform the
Prime Minister that in the event of this clause not being deleted in the Report stage
of the Bill, they will be compelled to recommend to the whole of the Trade Union
movement to refuse to have anything to do with the Act."

The Parliamentary Committee are convinced that the claim set forth in the above
resolution will meet with the unanimous approval of organised workers throughout the
country, who will readily recognise that the administration of Unemployment Benefit is
essentially a matter for the Trade Unions to deal with, being absolutely distinct from the
sick and other benefits administered by the Friendly Societies.

The Parliamentary Committee therefore urge upon the Executive officials of affiliated
organisations to advise their members to communicate at once with their local Members
of Parliament calling upon them, upon the Report stage of the Bill, to vote for the restora-
tion of Clause 17 to the form in which it left the House of Commons upon second reading.

Yours faithfully,

THE PARLIAMENTARY COMMITTEE.

J. H. THOMAS (Chairman).
G. H. STUART-BUNNING (Vice-Chairman).
M. BONDFIELD.
H. BOOTHMAN.
W. J. DAVIS.
H. GOSLING.
J. HILL.
J. W. OGDEN.
E. L. POULTON.
A. A. PURCELL.
J. SEXTON.
H. SKINNER.
A. B. SWALES.
W. THORNE.
R. B. WALKER.
J. B. WILLIAMS.

C. W. BOWERMAN, Secretary.

March, 1920.

Here is the extracted text from the image:
313

You know what happens when a man spends more money than he makes; he goes
into debt. The same applies to our physical energy. If you exercise more than you should,
illness and sickness will follow. You should take your recreation in a quiet, rather than
a strenuous, manner until your lungs have become healed, which usually takes three to
four years. Avoid active play like dancing, lawn tennis, handball, football, skating, rowing,
swimming, hunting, horseback riding. Golf in a light form, croquet, and walks or short
hikes are most suitable. Gambling is the worse form of recreation. It is too exciting
and is often indulged in at night when players ought to be in bed sleeping. Do not gamble
if you want to stay well. Avoid excesses.

GUARD AGAINST A RELAPSE.

Every arrested case of tuberculosis may have activity again. You should learn the
important points of the disease. You should be on the alert to note the first signs of a break
down.

DANGER SIGNALS.

An undue sense of fatigue, loss of appetite, loss of strength, loss of weight, rise of
temperature, night sweats, increased cough and sputum, spitting of blood, rapid pulse,
are the most important danger signals. The most dangerous of these is the temperature
99.2° F., with headaches; 99.4° or over; difference between morning and evening of
over 2 degrees, are the points to be looked for. Every arrested case of tuberculosis should
know how to take his own pulse and temperature. Do not take temperature for 15 minutes
after smoking, or eating or drinking anything hot or cold. Active exercise raises the body
temperature. Rest at least one-half hour before taking it. Weigh yourself every two
weeks.

HOW TO KEEP UP A FIGHTING RESISTANCE.

Don't worry. Cultivate a cheerful disposition. Be an optimist. Your ability to
stay well depends upon your power to keep up a good fighting resistance to the germs-
tubercle bacilli.

Worrying, the blues, overdoing, poor food, bad housing, reduce your resistance. The
same may be said of colds, attacks of influenza, tonsilitis, or any form of illness. You can
not afford to treat these minor sicknesses lightly. It is your duty to seek in every way to
keep up your resistance your defenses against tuberculosis. Remember that rest and
medical care will aid you in making a more rapid recovery. When in doubt, consult with
your physician. "Safety first" is a good motto to follow. Better be called a health
crank and carry on than be called a good sport and be in the sanitorium again "chasing
the cure."

The more you know about tuberculosis the better you can protect yourself and preserve
your health. The National Tuberculosis Association, 381, Fourth Avenue, New York
City, and the Federal Board for Vocational Education, Washington, D. C., will send you
several booklets on tuberculosis free of charge. The Vocational Summary, March, 1920.

LEICESTER BOOT & SHOE TRADE BOARD OF CONCILIATION & ARBITRATION.

A meeting of the Board was held on 30th March. Present: Mr. A. Wilkes, in the
chair. Messrs. C. H. Roberts, W. A. Thompson, G. W. Hunt and T. W. Smith, representing
the Manufacturers. Messrs. T. Adnitt, G. Parbury, M. A. Parker, H. Cramphorn, G.

Sincere regret was expressed at the absence of the Chairman, Mr. T. Orton, owing
to a recurrence of illness, and the Board desired the Secretary to convey their sympathy
to him and their best wishes for his speedy recovery.
The claim of the No. 2 Branch of the Union, that an extra be paid to Clickers when
cutting work to quality, was referred to the Arbitrators.
Prices for Stitching and Loose Nailing agreed upon by the Lasting Joint Committee
were approved and confirmed by the Board.
Prices fixed by the Finishing Joint Committee for Heel Paring, Heel Scouring and
Heel Padding, were reported to the Board and ordered to be recorded in the Minutes,
the prices to come into operation and be paid as from and including the working week
commencing on the 12th, 13th or 14th February, 1920.
The question of the revision of Shop Finishing Statements for Canvas Work, was
referred to the Finishing Joint Committee.

THE REPAIRING TRADE BOARD.
Male Apprentices to be Registered and a Form of Indentures
Approved-Minimum Rates for Apprentices whose Work was
Committee's Repairers and the Shops (Early Closing) Bill-

The Public and Leather Substitutes.
A meeting of the Boot Repairing Trade Board was held at the Offices, Chancery Lane
their scheme for the registration and supervision of male apprentices, full details of which
W.C., on Thursday and Friday last week. The Administrative Committee submitted
appeared in the last issue of the Record. A form of registration was agreed upon, which
would shortly be issued by the Board, and would in due course be followed by a form
indenture, also to be drawn up by the Board. The scheme was made applicable to all
apprentices in the trade, and only those so registered would be recognised as apprentices
by the Board, the whole object of the scheme being to ensure proper training.
Employment of Young Workers.
With regard to the proposals for the limitation of the proportion of young workers
of a scheme having this object in view, such a scheme to apply to others than learners and
in the trade, it was decided to seek the opinion of the Ministry of Labour as to the validity
apprentices.
The Board agreed to amendments in the rates applicable to apprentices in rivet and
combination making and repairing. On a five years' apprenticeship the rates were fixed
as follows: First year, ros. per week: second year, 15s. per week: third year, 21s. per
The recommendation of the Administrative Committee that the fixing of general
piece rates for closing be relegated to the District Trade Committees, for further information
week; fourth year, 25s. per week; fifth year, 30s. per week.
and consideration, was agreed to.
War-Interrupted Apprenticeships.
The Board considered the application of minimum rates to apprentices whose appren
under the scheme prepared by the Board and approved by the Ministry of Labour. It was
ticeships had been interrupted by service in the Forces, and who were undergoing training
agreed that in no case should the wages for adult workers be less than 65s. for a week of
48 hours, and, further, that the State grant be withheld in all cases pending the production
of evidence that the worker had in fact been paid at that rate since the date on which
the minimum rates fixed by the Board came into operation.
The Board approved the Administrative Committee's report upon the setting up of
District Trade Committees, and of the areas as defined by the Committee. Under this
scheme there will be nine District Trade Committees in England, three in Scotland, and two
in Wales, each Committee consisting of 24 representatives, of whom eight would be
nominated by the Board, 16 to be local representatives (workers and employers in equal
proportion). The appointment of local representatives would be made after consideration
of names submitted by the Administrative Committee to the Board. The members of the
Administrative Committee of each side agreed to furnish the names for submission to the
Ministry of Labour, and it was hoped that they would be ready for selection at the next
meeting of the Administrative Committee on May 6th and 7th.
Shops (Early Closing) Bill.
The Administrative Committee submitted a report on one-man businesses and the
Shops (Early Closing) Bill. After consultation with a representative of the Home Office
as to the practicability of applying the measure to boot repairing businesses, it was agreed
to urge the Home Office to seek the necessary power to apply the Bill to such concerns.
The Committee, it was stated, had appointed a sub-committee for the purpose of examining
the provisions of the Bill, with a view to ascertaining whether they affected repairing shops,
and if necessary to draft an amending clause with a view to covering establishments of the
kind. The sub-committee had prepared a memorandum in support of such an amendment
and had transmitted it to the Home Office. Correspondence was read between the Home
Office and the Secretary of the Board, and from this it was gathered that the memorandum
was under consideration by the Home Secretary. It was decided that the Secretary should
might state their views in support of the suggestion that repairing shops should be brought
write asking that a deputation be received by the Home Secretary in order that they
under the provisions of the Bill.
A case was cited for the determination of the scope of the minimum rates in reference
to men who made up for themselves. The Board agreed that the case came within
the scope of the minimum rates.

Approval was given to the recommendation of the Administrative Committee that

Bill. The Committee had considered the provisions of the Bill, by which a special scheme
of unemployment insurance might be substituted for the general scheme in the case of the
boot-repairing trade, but they were not at present in a position to make a recommendation
on the matter. The Board approved of the Report.

Mr. T. F. Richards had on the agenda a motion, of which he had given previous notice,
that general minimum piece rates be fixed for stripping and preparing for machine. He
said he was not prepared to go on with the motion at the moment, and asked permission
to postpone its discussion. The Committee agreed to this course being followed.

In communication with Mr. F. W. Leggett, Ministry of Labour, on a variety of subjects
appertaining to the repairing trade, Mr. James Parsons, secretary of the repairers' com-
mittee of the Federation, has received a letter in which Mr. Leggett expresses the opinion
that the District Trade Committees, which are to be set up under the Trade Board, should
be constituted with as little delay as possible, in order that they might consider the effect
of the new rates in their areas, and report to the Board. By these means information on
the position will be readily obtained, and if any modifications may prove to be desirable
a sound basis will exist on which they can be determined. — The Shoe and Leather Record,
April 23rd, 1920.

RETAIL PRICES AND RENTS.

The general level of retail prices, taking into account food, clothing, fuel and light,
&c., as well as rents, at 1st April was about 132 per cent. above the level of July, 1914,
assuming the same quantities and, so far as possible, the same qualities of each article
purchased at the present time as before the War. — The Labour Gazette, April, 1920.

AUSTRIA, 1920.
Extracts from Report by Mr. W. A APPLETON, President, International
Federation of Trade Unions, upon his visit to Austria, to superintend the
distribution of the Food sent to Austria by the Trade Unions.
The International Federation of Trade Unions must take notice of the sorrows of
the workmen in any country. Austria at the moment affords a pitiful example of the
consequences of war. Many of her citizens are suffering grievously and her little children
are dying for want of nourishment. She has appealed for help and help is being sent, but
whether it is sufficient, or will arrive in time, is still a matter for unhappy conjecture.
The International Federation has already sent large consignments of food into Austria.
On the 16th February I accompanied the second of three trains of thirty-seven truck loads
of potatoes, margarine, corned beef, and condensed milk. Each of those trains costs
twenty thousand pounds. We travelled in an ordinary composite corridor car which was
attached to the food train, and took between six and seven days to get from Holland
to Vienna.
Before the war, Austria was an extensive empire including fifty-eight millions of
people, may of different races, many speaking different languages, but all, in theory at
least, governed from Vienna. To-day Austria is a republic with a population of fivel and
a half to six millions, all of whom are, presumably, of German nationality. The country
has been left with territory of little economic value and with a debt that was heavy when
placed on the shoulders of the old empire, but which becomes paralysing when placed upon
the few millions whom the statesmen of the entente have left in Austria.
Before the war, Austria drew upon the fertile plains of Hungary and of Slovakia
for cereals, roots, and other crops. These territories provided also industrial necessities
and some luxuries. From Bohemia the Austrians drew glass and coal, and from Slavia,
in addition to valuable agricultural products, they benefited by the operation of a number
of industries.
An elementary knowledge of agiculture will at once reveal the poverty of most of the
soil which covers the country passed through on the way to Vienna. It is thin and dry
and lacking in humus, and does not suggest the possibility of carrying any grain crop
heavier than rye or oats. The soil will grow potatoes and crops of a similar character, but
does not appear to offer any encouragement to the men who desire to grow wheat.
Mountains there are in abundance, and these are clothed with forests, but there is
a lack of transport, and whatever wealth lies in the forests can only be utilised slowly and
painfully.
Of water power, Austria has plenty, but she needs millions of capital from the outside
in order to develop this power, and she makes no secret of her preference for the British
capitalist.
It is only, however, when one reaches the cities that the full extent of the disaster
which has overtaken Austria can be realised. Then it is seen that the Entente has left
Austria with an industrial population, but has stripped her of all that industry needs in
order to operate successfully. She has also been left with a great population of non-pro-
ducers, including pre-war pensioners, unemployed civil servants, and workers in luxury
trades and in such occupations as are provided by the theatres, restaurants, and cafés.
Roughly speaking, there are in Vienna itself 2,500,000 people. In the other con-
siderable towns left to Austria there may not be less than 500,000. The agricultural
population is apparently the smaller, and out of a limited and poor soil it has to produce
enough to maintain the town populations.
However much assistance is given, Austria is bound to suffer very severely. The
soil that is left to her cannot supply her reasonable needs, while her debts and circumstances
affect the rate of exchange so badly that it is hardly possible for her to buy from the outside
world.
The industrial position of Austria is indeed a desperate one. Her people are clever
and artistic. They are said also to be very industrious. In every shop there are evidences
of skill and taste. Some of the factories I visited were large and spendidly equipped.
Messrs. Sieman's and Messrs. Shuttleworth's declared that, given fair opportunities, they
could find employment for four times the number of workers at present in their factories.
They had orders for electrical appliances and agricultural machinery sufficient to last
them for two or three years, even if they were working three eight-hour shifts. Their
great difficulty was lack of coal and lack of transport.


---
than my thumb. It was impossible to avoid the conclusion that in many of these cases
it would be better if the children died, and died quickly. There does not seem to be any
hope of them recovering sufficiently to become physically fit.
There are, in Vienna, 4,000 officers of the old army; and in many cases they are without
the means of purchasing civil clothing. There are 8,000 sergeants similarly situated. Army
officers are selling newspapers in the streets, and delivering goods to the houses. There
are generals with five or six campaigns behind them, men who have served on the staff,
who are now laboriously learning to mend boots. Their new occupations may be useful
but they are hard, especially to the older men, and they are made harder by the jealousy
and opposition of those who previously did this work.
It would be possible to multiply cases, but enough has surely been said to show the
gravity of the position in which the Austrians find themselves, and to emphasise the need
for help.
The help that Austria needs is of two kinds. First, it must be charitable and must go
in the shape of food and clothes. She cannot exist until the next harvest unless these
things are sent to her. There need be no fear as to the means of distribution. The fund
with which the Rt. Hon. Frederick Huth Jackson is connected has already sent goods or
money to the value of £200,000. The work of all English relief organisations is said to
be satisfactory. The Society of Friends is spoken of with gratitude and affection. Mr.
F. D. Lindley, the Chief Commissioner, is co-operating with the Austrians themselves.
The Trade Unions have splendidly organised the distribution of the food sent by the
International Federation of Trade Unions. Up to the present three train loads have gone.
Most of the money for these trains has been subscribed in Holland, where the workmen have
worked extra time and given the whole proceeds to the Trade Union funds. The central
Trade Union organisation in Vienna has issued food tickets, and the actual distribution
takes place through the Co-operative Societies without the slightest hitch or sign of dis-
order. The arrangements and the control in this, the tenth, district, were perfect. Part
of this success was due to the excellent organisation of the Trade Unions and part to the
fact that the International had concentrated upon a limited number of commodities.
Where small quantities of varied commodities are sent, the difficulties of distribution are
increased, and there is a possibility of ill-feeling developing amongst the recipients.
In addition to watching the distribution, I attended a great meeting of the workers,
and everywhere found that not only had the food itself helped to keep them alive, but the
action of the International Federation of Trade Unions in sending food had revived hopes
in the hearts of the people and had considerably improved their morale. It was pititul
to hear the expressions of faith in Britain and to feel that Britain might not live up to the
Austrian expectations.
Other help is needed, and this must be of a political character. It is impossible to
visit Austria without coming to the conclusion that all claims for indemnities must be
revised. Austria has already been stripped. To continue the effort to extract indemnities
will be just as useful as attempting to pick the pockets of a man whose clothes had been
previously stolen.
There must also be readjustment of the incidence of Austria's debts. Obviously
6,000,000 cannot liquidate the liabilities incurred by 58,000,000. The force of these state-
ments will be felt when it is understood that prior to the war, and during the war, Austria
borrowed on the credit of her then existing empire and population. The money borrowed
came to Vienna, but much of it was spent in Hungary, Czecho-Slovakia, and Jugo-Slavia
and countries which could produce food and raw materials. In these countries some
people have become exceedingly rich, and with the cunning that always characterises
the arch-profiteers, they have cleverly retained the money while transmitting the liability
for its repayment to the unfortunate Austrians.
Austria cannot exist as at present constituted; she must either be given means and
opportunities to work out her own salvation, or be permitted to attach herself to the King-
dom of Bavaria. She has potential possibilities, including waterpower, but she needs
capital to exploit these. Her people frankly demand the opportunity to work, while they
declare that they are not beggars and that they don't wish to eat the bread of charity a
moment longer than is necessary.
Events in Germany may be said to hinder the giving of help to Austria. Personally,
I do not think this is so. I do not think any Government in Germany will desire to oppose
the giving of help to the Austrians.
These people have not the temperamental austerity of the Prussian, and they freely
manifested their earnest desire to associate with Britain in any moral efforts to check
invasion from the east. The officers whom I met spoke with respect and sometimes with
keen admiration of the British Army. Particularly was this the case of an officer who was
with the section charged to capture the British guns in the retreat from the Piave. His
last request to me was that if ever I met the British officer who commanded the British
guns during this retreat, I would convey the congratulations of one soldier to another
