REPAIRING SHOP MANAGERS' WAGES.

Eighty Shillings Minimum Adopted after Protracted
Discussion by the Trade Board.

The Boot Repairing Trade Board further considered at their meeting held at the
Offices, Chancery Lane, W.C., on Thursday, the 15th inst., the proposal to fix a minimum
wage of £4 per week for managers and foremen of branch repairing shops. The Board
received a deputation which attended to lodge objections against the proposal, the deputa-
tion consisting of Mr. Bilton, of Messrs. J. Coombes and Co., who have over 160 branch
shops; Mr. Rowe (London), Mr. Williams (Liverpool), and a representative of Messrs.
Smith.

A Deputation Received.

The chief objection submitted by the deputation against the proposed minimum was
in regard to it being applied to managers of repairing shops in which the amount of trade
done was very small. The deputation instanced cases in which the amount of the takings
would not justify the payment of £4 a week to the man in charge. It was contended that
the payment of a guaranteed wage of £3 5s. for a 48-hour week was sufficiently high for
the amount of responsibility exercised. This point of view had, it was declared, the
support of the major portion of the trade, and also of the majority of the members of the
Trade Board. No objection was raised to the proposed rate being paid to foremen or
managers in boot repairing factories, but only to its application to men in charge of small
shops.

Many questions were asked by each side of the Board, in order to elucidate various
points, and when the deputation retired the proceedings had lasted close upon three hours.

A Stormy Discussion.

Discussion of the matter was resumed by the Board on Friday, the 16th inst., when
the proceedings were again protracted and at times stormy. The employers' representa-
tives submitted an amendment to the effect that the proposed rate of 80s. a week should be
made applicable only to adult persons in charge of and exercising sole responsibility over
two or more journeymen. This was defeated, a member of the employers' side of the
Board voting against it.

A further amendment was moved from the employers' side to the effect that the
proposed rate of 80s. should apply only in cases where a manager exercised supervision
over one employee. This was also defeated, the same employers' representative voting
against it.

Some Amendments Moved.

During the adjournment for lunch, the Secretary to the Board, acting on instructions
interviewed the Minister of Labour, of whom he inquired whether it was possible to make
a varying rate, according to circumstances, without again submitting the proposal to the
trade of the country. The opinion of the Minister was that this could be done, and accord-
ingly, when the discussion was resumed, a resolution on these lines was submitted from
the employers' side. The contention was that a £4 minimum ought to carry a certain
amount of responsibility, and it was feared that the rate, irrespective of the amount of
business done, would result in the closing of a number of shops owned by private traders.
The fear was also expressed that the universal application of such a rate for managers and
foremen would cause hardship to the men concerned, because many would be discharged,
and the shops in which they had been employed would become one-man businesses, over
which the Trade Board would have no control.

The Voting of an Appointed Member.

The employers' representatives therefore aimed at a further compromise by sub-
mitting an amendment that 70s. per week should apply where one man was employed in
addition to the manager; that 80s. per week should be paid where the employees numbered
more than one and up to five; and that 90s. a week should be paid where the men under
the manager numbered more than five.

On this amendment being put, the voting was equal, but it was lost by reason of the
fact that one of the appointed members voted against it.

The original proposal-8os.-was then put and carried, one of the employers' repre-
sentatives refraining from voting.— The Shoe and Leather Record, April 23rd, 1920.

Section I. The respective minimum rates set out in this Schedule shall apply subject
to the provisions of the Trade Boards Acts and this notice to all workers in Great Britain
who are employed during the whole or any part of their time in any branch of the Trade
specified in the Trade Boards (Boot and Shoe Repairing) Order, 1919; that is to say the
repairing, wherever carried on, of boots, shoes, slippers and all kinds of leather footwear,
including the making of bespoke handsewn, riveted or pegged leather footwear, but excluding
the manufacture of leather footwear on a large scale, the repairing of saddlery and leather
goods other than leather footwear and the retailing of leather footwear.
Section II. The Minimum Rates set out in this Schedule shall be paid clear of all
deductions other than deductions under the National Insurance Act, 1911, as amended
by the any subsequent enactments, or deductions authorised by any Act to be made from
wages in respect of contributions to any Superannuation or other Provident Fund.
Section III. - The Minimum Rates set out in this Schedule are without
prejudice to workers who are earning Higher Rates of Wages.

It is provided by the above-mentioned Regulations that :-
Secretary
Every occupier of a factory or workshop or of any place used for giving out work
to out-workers, shall, on receipt of this Notice, post up and keep posted up a sufficient
number of true copies thereof in PROMINENT POSITIONS in the factory, workshop or place
used for giving out work in such a manner as to ensure that in each case the Notice shall be
brought to the knowledge of all workers employed by him or on his premises who are affected
thereby. PENALTY for non-compliance, a fine not exceeding 40s.
Under the Trade Boards Acts, 1909 and 1918, it is provided amongst other things that :
1. Where any minimum rate of wages fixed by a Trade Board has become effective
an employer shall, in cases to which the minimum rate is applicable, pay wages to the person
employed at not less than the minimum rate clear of all deductions, and if he fails to do
so shall be liable on summary conviction in respect of each offence to a fine not exceeding
twenty pounds and to a fine not exceeding five pounds for each day on which the offence
is continued after conviction therefor.
In the foregoing provision, the expression "deductions" includes deductions for or
in respect of any matter whatsoever (other than deductions under the National Insurance
Act, 1911, as amended by any subsequent enactments or deductions authorised by any
Act to be made from wages in respect of contributions to any superannuation or other
provident fund), and notwithstanding that they are deductions which may lawfully be
made from wages under the provisions of the Truck Acts, 1831 to 1896, and where any
payment being a payment authorised to be received by an employer under section one,
section two, or section three of the Truck Act, 1896, is made by an employed person to
his employer, the employer shall, for the purposes of the foregoing provision, be deemed
to have deducted that amount from wages.
On the conviction of an employer for failing to pay wages at not less than the minimum
rate to a person employed, the Court may by the conviction adjudge the employer con-
victed to pay, in addition to any fine, such sum as appears to the Court to be due to the
person employed on account of wages, the wages being calculated on the basis of the mini-
mum rate, but the power to order the payment of wages under this provision shall not
be in derogation of any right of the person employed to recover wages by any other
proceedings.

THE ECONOMIC CONSEQUENCES OF THE PEACE.
No one who wishes to know the facts about the present condition of Europe and the
causes which led to them should be without a copy of this book. Mr. Keynes exposes the
actions of the Peace Conference and in particular of its chief protagonists, analyses the
economic provisions of the Peace Treaty and the indemnities, and their effect on European
society, and presents the case for immediate revision. The importance of the book may
be gathered from the fact that even of the comparatively expensive edition over 60,000
copies were sold within two months of publication. Of this book the Labour Research
Department is now able to offer a special edition at 2s. 6d. post free, to members of the
Labour Movement only. One free copy will be given for each dozen ordered. The offer
is only open until May 1st, and all orders must be received by then, from the Labour
Research Department, 34, Eccleston Square, London, S.W. 1.

17TH MAY, 1920.

FELLOW MEMBERS, PROFITEERING.

We have upon a number of occasions called attention to the question of
profiteering and the adverse affect it has upon the standard of living, and
beg once more to refer to the same, because it would appear that the Govern-
ment are very reluctant to deal with this matter in anything like the drastic
way it calls for. It is of comparatively small value to summon a small trader
as to whether he has made an excessive profit on 4 ozs. of sweets, a pound of
candles or a bottle of some patent medicine. The matter needs such handling
as will enable the wholesalers to be dealt with, and it is worthy of note that the
Minister of Food, Mr. C. A. McCurdy, M.P., stated when presenting his estimate
for the ensuing year that, in cases where de-control took place, and upon
representations, prices would go down, but they immediately went up, and
control had to be resumed. Further reference to this is made on pages 382-383.

It is also significant that when the Chancellor of the Exchequer (the Rt.
Hon. Austen Chamberlain, M.P.) presented the Budget for 1920-21 he said :-

"I come now to the Excess Profits Duty. I attempted last year to state fairly
the arguments for and against the Excess Profits Duty when I asked the Committee
to continue for another year at a reduced rate of 40 per cent. But there was one
factor in the situation which I have to confess I entirely failed to forecast. If honour-
able Members will cast their minds back to the circumstances in which I spoke last
year, I do not think they will blame me or pretend to greater prescience. Industry
was then disorganised, unemployment was rife, and there was every prospect of
declining prices and a great fall in profits. But the results have been far otherwise.
There has been no such decline; on the contrary, manufacturers are overwhelmed
with orders in excess of what they can execute. Prices have risen and the level of
B

THE STATE OF TRADE
shews no improvement during the month, but rather on the contrary; if
anything, there is more short time being worked than last month. So far as
one can see, a certain amount of slackness will obtain for some time yet, unless
there is such an improvement in the exchange markets as will give an impetus
to the export trade, which for a considerable period has been very quiet indeed.
The number of members signing the out-of-work books during the month is
1,073-
The figures as shewn in the Ministry of Laboar Gazette are as set out here-
under :-
In this trade employment was fair on the whole during April, but there was a further
decline in some districts and employment generally was not so good as a year ago. A
considerable amount of short time was reported.
At Leicester, employment was moderate and not so good as a month ago; much short
time was reported. At Northampton and in the surrounding district employment was
also slack. At Kettering there was a decline as compared with a month ago, but employ-
ment on the whole continued fair. At Norwich and Stafford employment continued good;
at Bristol, Kingswood and Leeds it was slack with much short time. In Scotland generally
employment was fairly good.

TRADE UNION BUTTONS AND BROOCHES.
A fresh supply of buttons and brooches has recently been secured and can
be had upon application, price 6d. each, cash with order. Secretaries please
note, when ordering buttons or brooches, cash should ALWAYS be sent with
order.
REMEMBER THE FIFTEENTH !
Once again we feel bound to call the serious attention of branch secretaries
to the fact that the FIFTEENTH day of each month is the latest day for
returns reaching the General Secretary. Last month many returns were late.
Branches failed to send off their returns to reach the Central Office by the
15th of the month, and this tends to upset the organisation of the work in
the General Secretary's Office. We trust in future every branch secretary
will endeavour to get in his returns NOT later than the fifteenth of every month,
and before sending them see that they are properly signed.

BRIGHTON. On the 22nd I went to Brighton and met the Executive Committee of
the Branch and advised as to future organisation and the control of the Union's affairs
in that area, respecting the methods of business as to the work of the district and the policy
adopted by the Trades Boards for the work of the future District Committees.
DISTRICT COMMITTEES for Hand-sewn and Repairing only under the control of the
Trades Boards. England will have nine, Scotland three, and Wales two.
Our Union will have three members on each District Committee, as follows:-
1. Durham, Cumberland, Westmorland to Guisboro'.
2. Yorkshire, East, North and West Riding, Derbyshire, portion Gainsborough
and Caistor, parts of Lincolnshire.
3. Lancashire and Cheshire.
4. Midlands-Hereford, Leicestershire, Lincoln, Notts., Burton, Stoke to Bir-
mingham, Dudley and Worcester.
5. London, Counties of Middlesex, Essex, Herts., Kent and Surrey.
6. County of Northants., Peterborough, Oxford, Hunts., Bedford, Bucks., Herts.

Borough of Yarmouth, Ipswich, Southend.
8. Dorset, Southampton, Isle of Wight, Sussex, Berks., Kent, Wilts., Surrey,
Bournemouth, Portsmouth and Brighton.
9. County of Cornwall, Scilly Islands, Devon, Somerset, Gloucester, Exeter,
Plymouth, Bath and Bristol.

We have no members.
South Wales. (1 representative). Cardigan, Radnor, Pembroke, Carmarthen,
Glamorgan, Brecknock, Monmouth.
We as a Union will have a share of the representatives appointed by the Government
from the Central Committee of London. These are composed as follows:-Chairman
appointed by Government, four operative representatives, four employer representatives
on each Committee, with eight worker representatives and eight employer representatives.
Their duties will be referred to them by the Central Committee. For the present they
will have no power to vary the rates of wages or hours of employment.
Will the Repairers of Wales please wake up and let us have branches in the Prin-
cipality?

I have been compelled to send the following letter to the Yorkshire
Papers, in reply to an A.U.C.C.E. Official charging our Members
with Blacklegging :
NATIONAL UNION OF BOOT & SHOE OPERATIVES.
DEAR SIR, 34, GUILFORD STREET, W.C. 1.
My attention has been directed to a statement in the Press to the effect that
an Official of the Amalgamated Union of Co-operative and Commercial Employees con-
siders the members of the National Union of Boot and Shoe Operatives are "black-legging"
in Yorkshire. Now, Sir, if the A.U.C.C.E. was acknowledged by the Trades Union Con-
gress or the National Labour Party as a bona-fide Trade Union, we should immediately
take them before one of those bodies and demand an apology; but they, being an un-
recognised body, we can only appear at the bar of public opinion and answer the vindictive
statement made.
Blacklegs are men who work contrary to the instructions of the Union to which they
do, or ought to, belong. The A.U.C.C.E. is not a Shoemaker's organisation, and therefore
have no right to speak on their behalf. Our Union have all the English and Scottish
C.W.S. employees, some 3,500 in the shoe industry, and by recent census among co-operative
societies, we have the majority of the Repairers engaged by co-operative distributive
stores as members of our Union. The census shows 37.9 belonging to our Union: 17
per cent. belonging to the Amalgamated Union of Rivetters and Finishers: this means
54 per cent. of the whole, and the remainder belong either to the A.U.C.C.E. or are non-
unionists.
The A.U.C.C.E. have been condemned by the highest Trade Union authority in the
land for poaching upon our Union, and have been ordered by the said authority to restore
the stolen members to our organisation, as we understand their wants and requirements
That is our answer to the A.U.C.C.E. so far as shoemakers are concerned.
With the above decision standing against them, we cannot accept their marching
orders; but a greater question of principle controls our action, because being co-operators
ourselves, we decline the A.U.C.C.E. methods of bleeding the working class movement,
known as the co-operative movement, whilst they stand by and fail to punish or make
the private employer pay at least as much as the co-operative movement. They only
attack co-operatives where they can get the assistance of well-tried trade unionists
(members of the various committees) to do their work, who smart at the wily tactics of an
organisation which imposes upon the willing co-operative movement, but dare not attack
the private trader. The body who call others "blacklegs" have no standing in the
national labour world, and where they have put themselves upon a par with other hon-
ourable trade unions, we shall decline to work with such a camouflaged organisation,
who hang on to organisations and then claim all the credit with the parrot cry of " alone
I did it." This Union takes our Statements and calls them "logs" because they are not
acquainted with trade terms; then they add from 20 per cent. to roo per cent. and call
the statement theirs, which they present to the co-operative societies on the "go one
better" principle, no matter what our rates are.
The method has seduced some of our weak members, but the honest decline to be
drawn,
Yours faithfully,

y.F. Tilhardy
General President,
Boot and Shoe Union, over 100,000 members.

ORGANISER'S REPORT.
FELLOW MEMBERS,
It has been a pleasure to me to work with the Secretary and Committee of the
Stafford Branch in trying to get the greatest amount of good out of the New Agreement
for the benefit of the members. So far I believe that no other branch can show such a
record in the benefits gained. The members here appreciate this.
On April 22nd I spoke on the settlement to the workers of "Lotus" Ltd., at Stone,
It was a good meeting, and as a result of it I trust that the minority of women and girls
who were outside the Union, have joined before now.
I attended the Council meeting on April 25th, 26th, and 27th. On the 28th I went
to Atherstone to learn of the conditions obtaining at the one factory there. Mr. Warner,
of Hinckley, came over, and we held a meeting of the workers on the 29th. The con-
ditions are bad, grievously bad. I took note of about fifty cases. The next day I went
to the factory to seek an interview with the manager. I got this and nothing more. For
over an hour and a half we talked and talked, but no increases in the minimum wage
packets came of it all. This great man did not look upon it as a reproach on his manage-
ment if he could not pay wages something near to others making similar work. A few
weeks ago a Consol operator at this firm tried to organise the workers. He succeeded well
in his efforts, and his reward for this bit of manly work was to get a week's wages in lieu
of notice. He is still sticking to the work of collecting the money and encouraging the
members with advice.
On May 3rd I had to be at Leicester over a claim of the Clickers for an extra for cutting
more than one quality of upper out of the one skin. The employers were strongly repre-
sented. Ald. Hill and myself put up the case for our side. The Umpire, Mr. Geo. Sedg-
wick, listened patiently to both sides.
In the afternoon I set out for Manchester. I had word from Mr. Poulton that there
was a strike at the firm of Gregg's there. The president of the branch and I saw the
strikers at 9.30 on the Tuesday morning. We got their permission to go and interview
the owners. We spent over six hours on the business. At a full meeting I read out the
terms of settlement and these were unanimously accepted as being satisfactory. In fact
they declared that it was the best victory ever gained in the history of the Union at Man-
chester. If these workers had not come out they would have got no increases or they
would have got them as they did twelve months ago in little driblets. This time men got
increases of 8s., 10s., and in two cases up to 12s. 6d. each. The women and girls in the
Closing Room were doing fairly well, but they, to their credit, came out in sympathy
with the men. I told the firm and I told the workers in spite of what we have gained
that this must not be looked to as the last word for some time to come. The strike began
on the Saturday and they were all at work on the Wednesday morning. I went to the firm
of Sutton's on the Wednesday and got increases similar to those gained at Gregg's. The
workers here were more than thankful at what was done. I then went to Libstein's
and they refused to open the question-their workers are all satisfied, they never complain
about their conditions, &c. We have only a few members at this firm. I visited Johnson's
at Newton Heath. They gave increases a few weeks ago, but in a few cases I managed
to get them moved up a bit. At Funey's we have six members. I thought of asking the
firm to deal with me for these only. The head of the firm, had he been in, I believe would
have accepted this position, but as he was not in I gave particulars of the increases given
at the other factories and got a promise that these would be considered. A few weeks
ago we got considerable increases all round at Frankenburg's. At the request of several
groups of the workers there I went in again this time to try and get some more concessions.
The manager practically conceded all that was asked for. This man is much attached
to the bonus system. A team in the lasting room are paid a fixed wage for a quantity,
all they do over is bonus. I discovered that the bonus work came out cheaper for the firm
than their other work. I got an increase of 20 per cent. on the bonus work, which brings
it fully up to the other.
Since I was last at Stafford there has been a considerable increase of female members.
I am sorry to say that Mr. Simmons, Branch Secretary, is in poor health.
D. MCCARTHY.

---
369

impartial tribunal would draw up the ballot paper, disseminate in an unbiassed fashion all
facts and information relevant to the question in dispute, and, of course, would not seek
in any way improperly to influence the votes of the Trade Unions concerned! The working
classes had some evidence of this "fairness" and "impartiality" during the railway
strike, when the daily papers were filled with Government advertisements giving what the
Government presumably considered to be the "real facts" of the case. The Government
are and must always be an interested and not indifferent party whenever any section of
the workers contemplate a strike, and the best way to ensure a decision in favour of
ballot would be for the Government forcibly to extend their own patronage or that of
"independent public officials " to a Trade Union ballot vote. Organised Labour will
not even look at such a preposterous proposal. When Trade Union administration is
taken out of the hands of its duly elected officials, Trade Unions will cease to exist; but
the workers have no intention of allowing their organisation to be scrapped.

The latest increase in the price of coal is calculated to intensify popular grievances,
for it must inevitably lead to a general increase in the cost of living and give a new impetus
to the futile race between wages and prices. During the past twelve months the Govern-
ment's fluctuating action in this matter has been a striking illustration of their ineptitude.
First they increased the cost by 6s. per ton; then they reduced it by 10s.; and now they
have raised it by 14s. 2d. per ton. During the whole controversy there has been an un-
fortunate absence of reliable official statistical data, and the the Board of Trade and the Miners'
Federation have consistently disagreed on important points and challenged and con-
tradicted each other's statements. In the discussion which the Party initiated on an
adjournment motion last week, the Miners' leaders refused to accept the increase and
demanded the facts upon which the Government based its case. It was pointed out that
figures could now be produced which would shew the whole position in a clear light, and
it was urged that before the price was raised the figures should be produced, whereby
the nation could judge for itself what was necessary. It was stated that no increase at
all was needed to avoid a loss, but no matter how strongly the question was argued, the
Government intended to put on the 14s. 2d. The whole thing is a scandal. Nobody
suggests that coal should now be sold below cost, but the needs of the nation are such
that large profits should not be made, and no speaker from the Government benches was
able to shew that this would not be the result of the increased price, but the voting gave
the Government a big majority. And so the people must pay, and with the price of other
commodities rising, the problem becomes intensified. But there is one bright spot on an
otherwise dark horizon, as we are told the demand for Rolls-Royce cars is still very great.
WALTER R. SMITH.

LONDON ARBITRATION BOARD.

The National Joint Conference Interim Agreement.

A meeting of the Board of Conciliation and Arbitration for the boot and shoe trade
of London was held on the 17th inst. at Basma House, 13a, Fore Street, E.C., when the
following resolution was passed unanimously :-

"That in accordance with the decision of the national conference, that piece-
work or payment by results shall become the general system in operation this Board
agrees that the piece-work statement for clicking shall become operative in all factories
in the London district by a date not later than the first working week in July. That
in the meantime weekly wages for males in all departments (where the minimum
wage is increased to 68s. per week) earning 57s. to 70s. inclusive, shall be advanced
according to the following graduated list on the wages paid on the first pay-day in
January, 1920 :-

57/- plus 11/6 68/6

The foregoing, the secretary, Mr. John Arthur Craig, announces, supersedes the
resolution of the committee of the London Boot Manufacturers' Association, which was
issued on April 20th last. The Shoe and Leather Record, May 12th, 1920.

DERBY. -" It's a long lane that has no pub" is an old saying, and we
are pleased to report after months of careful work we have at last got the
Melbourne employers and employees preparing to work together harmoniously
for the mutual benefit of all concerned. The employers at an interview ex-
pressed their willingness to comply with the Union principles and make their
firms trade union shops on consideration the workers would accept and adopt
the County Statements, and at a large meeting of the workers they unanimously
decided to agree to the said Statements. Seeing that Melbourne has been
organised by local effort it is most gratifying; the Section is now 250 strong.
Another development has occurred, vide the Derby Daily Telegraph:" Follow-
ing upon the recent announcement that the Co-operative Wholesale Society
have purchased the vacant premises of Brown's Foundry Co. in Abbey Street,
comes the news that they have now taken over the works of Messrs. Jackson
and Davis Ltd., manufacturers of Girls' Boots and Shoes. The latter employ
a large number of hands. It is evident that the Co-operative Society intend
making Derby a centre of manufacturing activity in connection with their
Boot and Shoe Department."
EDINBURGH. -The members here are a little disappointed at employers

week under protest. Given a full week's work the Statement should work out
better than the old one. The time-workers in Repairing shops consider that
they must have a substantial increase to bring them into a just proportion
with the piece-workers. To all appearances the makers of new work are

satisfied that the Council have done the right thing in getting Employers'
Federation to reconsider the present agreement. However, it is scarcely up
to what we expected, but we must bear in mind that the Federation need not
have met our side, but kept to the bargain already settled. We cannot see
what good the District Councils in connection with the Wages Trade Board
are going to do, as they have no legislative power, and therefore cannot alter
any item without the sanction of the Wages Board. Trade Unionists all over
the country should get up in arms against the present proposals of adding
40 per cent. to the rents of dwelling houses of the people. Let us hope that
the Labour Members of Parliament will do their best to defeat this measure.
Of course outside pressure might make their case a good bit stronger. We are
sending two delegates to a Conference to be held in Glasgow on May 22nd, to

HIGHAM AND RUSHDEN.—We regret the amount of unemployment
in the District; this is chiefly owing to the return of the demobilised soldiers

Statement has again received certain revisions, and we are confident if the
operatives would agree to work according to the Quantity Statement they
would receive considerable advantage. A large number are doing so, and the
result is fairly satisfactory, and with certain re-organisations in some factories
it would be a good thing for all our operatives. The Pressmen's Statement
has been revised with an increase of 75 per cent. (which includes the 10 per
cent. in new Agreement); they are now in hands of printers. We have con-
tributed £5 to Benefit Fund for the Widow of the late esteemed Secretary of
Kettering No. 2 Branch, Mr. John Taylor, and trust other Branches will do
their best in this case.

KETTERING.—The equipoise of any live Branch of the Trade Union
Movement presents a curiously attractive and interesting problem to a student
of social psychology. To watch the interplay of men's minds; the rapid
absorption of an idea—which does not necessitate deep thought; the response
to certain stimuli, and the effect of such response upon organised development
and spontaneous action, is to largely understand the divergent anomalies at
present existing, and to accurately judge of the future possibilities of organised
labour. Space is here too brief to follow out the problem in any detail, but
the position of a number of the members of the Kettering No. 1 Branch in
their relationship to the new minimum rates and subsequent happenings, well
illustrates the point of view it is our desire to bring out. First of all the differ-
ence in the percentage increase between day and piece workers was accepted
without any open hostility. Many, of course, expressed disapproval and were
desirous of amending the anomaly, but on all sides one could see and feel the
bubble of satisfaction which the increased wages gave. Note the pleasurable
complacency aroused in response to a certain stimuli. But also observe that
hardly one operative in ten consciously associated the increases obtained with
the Boot and Shoe Operatives' Union. This is a strikingly significant illustra-
tion of the average mind of the ordinary trade unionist. He will not—or
cannot—estimate the true value of the trade union movement, and the influence
the movement wields—and is still further capable of wielding—in the social life
and well-being of the community. Let us again illustrate the contention from

LEGAL CASES AFFECTING LABOUR.
TRADE UNION ACTS.
DISPUTE BETWEEN ASSOCIATIONS OF EMPLOYERS: RULES OF ASSOCIATION : WORKMAN
DISMISSED BY ONE MEMBER NOT TO BE EMPLOYED BY ANOTHER: ENFORCING
RULE AGAINST OFFENDING MEMBER: CONTRACT IN RESTRAINT OF TRADE:
RIGHT OF DISMISSED WORKMAN.
A contract in restraint of trade is illegal at common law in the sense that it is un-
enforceable; but there is nothing to prevent the parties from performing it if they think
fit, provided they do not commit any actionable wrong or use any illegal means against
a third party.
An association of employers was formed, the members of which were all the principal
firms carrying on a certain business in the district. Amongst the rules of the association
were the following:--
2. The objects of the association are as follows:
(a) To enable the members to meet from time to time for mutual counsel; (b)
to advise members of all defaulters; (c) to improve the tone of the trade generally
and create a more amicable feeling amongst the members.
8. A special meeting of the members may be called by the chairman and secretary
at any time, setting forth the object of the meeting in the notice convening the same
20. On an employee leaving an employer, who is a member of the association,
the employer shall (if so desirous) report the same to the secretary, who shall advise
all the members, and no other member of the association shall employ or supply him
for 12 months.
In February, 1919, a man employed as a branch manager by one of the members
of the association left that employment, after due notice, and immediately entered the
employment of another member of the association under a contract providing for a month's
notice by either party to terminate the contract. The man avowedly intended to use the
connection he had formed with the customers of his former employer to obtain business
for his new employer and intended to canvass those customers in the interests of his new
employer. The former employer was in fact the secretary of the association of employers.
In these circumstances the secretary wrote to the man's new employer asking him
to dismiss him in accordance with Rule 20. This the employer refused to do, and the
matter was brought before a meeting of the association. There was no rule providing
for the expulsion of a member disobeying the rules, but the persuasion of the other members
was sufficiently strong to induce the offending member to consent to dismiss the man in
question. Accordingly the employer gave the man a month's notice as required by the
contract of service stating that he did so in compliance with the rules of the association.
The dismissed man then brought an action in the High Court against the four officials
of the association, one of whom was the secretary, claiming damages and an injunction
to restrain the defendants from interfering with any person or corporation with a view
to causing such person or corporation to break his or their contract or contracts with the
plaintiff or to cease to employ him. The judge dismissed the claim (see LABOUR GAZETTE,
December, 1919, p. 551) and the plaintiff appealed.
The Court of Appeal dismissed the appeal and upheld the decision of the judge. They
held that clearly Rule 20 was void and unenforceable at law as being in restraint of trade
and so contrary to public policy. The judge was, however, justified in finding that no
threats, coercion, or unlawful pressure had been used by the association to induce the
employer to dismiss the plaintiff, though no doubt strong moral pressure was used to
persuade the member to observe the rules he had agreed to as a condition of membership.
There are two grounds only upon which a person who procures the act of another can be
made legally responsible for the consequences:-(1) Where he knowingly and for his
own ends induces that other to commit an actionable wrong; (2) Where the act does not
constitute an actionable wrong but is a detriment to a third party and is procured by the
use of illegal means directed against the third party. In this case the employer of the
plaintiff committed no actionable wrong, as he gave the plaintiff the month's notice to
which he was entitled and was entirely within his rights in what he did. And as no illegal
pressure, nor anything more than strong moral persuasion, had been used by the defendants
to procure the employer to dismiss the plaintiff the defendants were not legally responsible
for the consequences of that dismissal. Davies v. Thomas and others, Court of Appeal,
30th April, 1920. The Labour Gazette, May, 1920.

383

been most grossly exaggerated. Between 7th August and 12th March the quantity of
bacon sold by the Ministry of Food at £60 per ton or under, including inferior qualities
as well as actual wastage, was only 1 ¼ per cent. of the sales made during that period. All
this bacon was privately imported, and, so far from holding back stocks, the Ministry com-
pelled the actual importers to handle those stocks from the time that they landed, and forced
their clearance by every possible means.

I will deal with one or two of the main items of food supplies, taking sugar first. There
is no prospect of any immediate improvement either in supplies or prices with regard to
the sugar which is required in this country. The world's production of sugar is down
approximately by £3,500,000. The price of sugar in America to-day is more than nine
times the pre-War price. In the United Kingdom we have by a rationing system succeeded
in economising the consumption of sugar to the extent of 700,000 tons, which, unfortunately,
is almost exactly counter-balanced by the increased consumption now taking place in the
United States of America. If I might give an illustration of the slowness of the world to
re-establish the productive conditions which have to be re-established before normal supplies
can be once more made normal and prices brought down, I would refer to the unhappy
fact that last year there were something like 4,500,000 tons of sugar beet grown in Germany,
but owing to the disordered industrial conditions in that country, only 3,500,000 tons were
converted into sugar. The rest, for want of labour, was left to rot upon the fields. There
is a further difficulty with regard to the re-establishment of the European sources of sugar
supply, in the fact that sugar beet is an industry particularly suitable for cultivation on
large estates. The division of the large estates all over Germany into small estates arising
out of political changes, will be another obstacle to the restoration of Germany as a sugar-
producing country.

Let me turn to wheat, which, after all, is perhaps the most important commodity to
consider in the world if you are trying to estimate the future course of prices because
wheat prices are the most important factor in determining wages all over the world. Wheat
prices determine the prices of feeding-stuffs. They determine the prices of other cereals and
of alternative foods in a large measure. They determine the price of milk produce. It
is unhappily clear that we are faced in the coming twelve months with reduced world crops
of wheat. In the present cereal year the imports of grain into Europe amounted 18,000,000
tons, of which 9,000,000 tons came from the United States of America. Prior to the War
the average export of grain from the United States was 2,000,000 or 3,000,000 tons. It
was due to the exceptional efforts in stimulating production of foodstuffs carried out by
Mr. Hoover and the American Food Ministry during the war that they were able so largely
to increase their exports. Unfortunately, the enthusiasm of war as worn thin in this respect
and for the coming cereal year the United States of America will have 4,000,000 tons less
to export than they are exporting to Europe in the current cereal year. There is a reduced
production in the Argentine, and there is a large fall in the supplies of Australian wheat;
in fact, the only wheat-producing country in the world in which there is an increase is
India, where there is an increase of something like 1,500,000 tons, which will not, I am
informed, be wholly, if to any large extent, available for export. We are, therefore, faced
with the prospect of a considerable reduction in the amount of wheat available for the
needs of the importing countries the world over, and I am afraid, in the absence of wise
and prudent statesmanship on the part of all the importing countries, that a consequent
rise in price will be reflected in the price of every other commodity.

DISABLED MEN AND SHOE INDUSTRY.

QUESTION 30, MR. W. R. SMITH.—To ask the Minister of Labour, whether the National
Trade Advisory Committee of the Boot and Shoe Trade have made any recommendation
as to the future training of disabled soldiers; that there is no further opening in the
industry for men trained in boot-repairing, but that men can be trained in hand-sewn
making; and will he state whether they have recommended the setting up of national
factories whereby these men can be guaranteed employment after they have been trained.

REPLY.—The functions of the National Trade Advisory Committee for the training of
disabled men in the Boot and Shoe Trade have now been taken over by the Boot and
Shoe Trade Board and this Board have passed resolutions in the sense indicated by my
hon. friend.

It is correct that the National Trade Advisory Committee pressed for the establishment
ment of National Factories for the employment of ex-service men in this trade, for the
purposes of supplying the forces of the Crown with hand-sewn boots and shoes. After
giving the matter careful consideration, in consultation with the Supply Departments,
my Department was unable to concur.

TRADE CUSTOM AND SHORT NOTICE.
Clicker's Successful Claim Against Boot Manufacturers.
At the Old Street Police Court last week, Messrs, Robert de Lange and Co., boot
manufacturers, Crispin Street, Spitalfields, E., were summoned by W. F. Naman, clicker,
for £4 5s., a week's wages in lieu of notice. The complainant said he had been with the
firm for ten months and had received his weekly wages on Fridays. He was told on
April 20th that there would be no more work for him at present and he was dismissed.
The reason given was that there was so much made-up work on hand that no more was
wanted.
Cross-examined by Mr. Ross, for the defence, the witness said that some time after
April 11th the following notice was put up at the factory: "We hereby agree to be dis-
missed at a minute's notice." The workmen, however, refused to sign it.
MR. Ross: Were you not told by the work-room manager that if you and the others
did not agree to the new terms you could leave?
WITNESS: No.
MR. CLARKE HALL (magistrate): If you want to impose a new contract you must
dismiss everyone and begin again.
MR. Ross: Virtually everybody was dismissed.
MR. CLARKE HALL: You cannot impose new terms without any consideration, nor
force a new contract on old hands without first dismissing them.
MR. Ross: Every man was told.
MR. CLARKE HALL: It does not matter.
MR. Ross: It was brought to the notice of all the men, and they were told if they
would not accept the terms of the new contract they could go. No one objected.
MR. CLARKE HALL: They are not bound to object.
MR. Ross submitted that the custom of the shop was satisfied by the notice. Mr.
Clarke Hall said it would not do in this case, and gave judgment for the plaintiff, with
5s. costs.
On a second summons by Isidore Abrahams, clicker, who also claimed £4 5s., a similar
order was made. -The Shoe and Leather Record, May 7th, 1920.

"CO-OP" STRIKES IN YORKSHIRE.
Refusal to Recognise the Co-operative Employees' Union.
The members of the Co-operative Employees' Union employed by the Halifax Indus-
trial Society Ltd., gave notice to cease work yesterday (Thursday) evening, as represen-
tatives of the Union have been unable to settle the trouble which has arisen in connection
with the Society's journeymen repairers. The latter have been on strike a fortnight.
The Halifax Society are members of the Federated Hours and Wages Board, which
has declined to enter into negotiation with the Union on questions affecting craft trades.
In the neighbouring district of Sowerby Bridge a general strike of "Co-op." employees
took place last week. A meeting of members of the local society was held on Saturday,
at which great excitement prevailed. The meeting broke up in confusion, no decision
being arrived at.
The strike has also spread to the Huddersfield district, business having, as a con-
sequence, been suspended at the stores of the following societies:-Huddersfield, Hillhouse,
Honley, Berry Brow, Close Hill, Brockholes, Netherton, Meltham, Meltham Mills, Milns-
bridge, Hill Top, Paddock, Lane Dyehouse, and Crosland Moor. The trouble has arisen
owing to the dispute amongst the West Riding boot repairers, some of whom are in craft
unions, and others in the Co-operative Employees' Union. A settlement had been effected
between the co-operative societies and the craft unions without an agreement with the
Co-operative Employees' Union, which is not recognised by the District Hours and
Wages Board or the North-Western Hours and Wages Board, as the authority to deal
with craft workers. The Co-operative Employees' Union requested its members to cease
work to enforce the recognition of the union's right to negotiate for craft workers
belonging to the union, and also to bring about a settlement of alleged breaches of an
agreement last August in reference to other classes of workers in addition to boot
repairers. -The Shoe and Leather Record, May 12th, 1920.

BOOT REPAIRERS STRIKE AT BRADFORD.
Position Discussed at Meeting of Employers.

A joint meeting of the two sections of the Bradford Chamber of Trade connected
with the boot trade was held on Thursday last week with reference to the position created
by the operative repairers having ceased work the previous Tuesday without giving notice
to the employers.

No formal application for an increase in wages had, it appeared, been received by the
employers' organisation, but it was evident from statements made by different operatives
that there was a division of opinion as to the extent of the demand for an increase some
wanted 100 per cent, and others 60 per cent, on the piecework rates. In addition, it was
understood there was a demand for a £5 per week minimum on day rates.

After discussion, the following resolution was passed:— "That this meeting, as a body,
refuses to negotiate until such demands are formulated and sent in a constitutional manner."
The resolution was forwarded to the local secretary of the Co-operative Employees'
Union.

There are no further developments in the situation created by the strike of boot
repairers employed by the Cleckhetaon Co-operative Societies, who came out on strike
for a 30 per cent, increase above the Board of Trade award. The contention is that as
the Lancashire co-operative societies have given the additional advance, the Yorkshire
societies should do likewise. Shoe and Leather Record, April 30th, 1920.

CO-OPERATIVE WHOLESALE SOCIETY LTD.
Acquire Boot Manufacturing Businesses at Bristol and Derby.

Our Bristol correspondent states that Messrs. Parsons & Co., boot manufacturers,
Gerrish Avenue, Whitehall, Bristol, have disposed of their business to the Co-operative
Wholesale Society Ltd. It will probably be taken over as a going concern at end of June.
Messrs. Parsons & Co. carried on business for many years in Portland Square, Bristol,
before transferring to their present factory at Whitehall. Mr. W. W. Cann, one of the
directors, who has taken an active part in all matters relating to the local boot trade,
will, it is understood, retire. His numerous friends will wish him many years of happiness
and good health to enjoy the leisure he has so well earned.

The Co-operative Wholesale Society Ltd., have also taken over the business of Messrs.
Jackson, Davies & Co., Derby, manufacturers of boys' and girls' footwear. The Shoe
and Leather Record, May 21st, 1920.

DEATHS FROM CONSUMPTION.

When the medical officer submitted his report at the meeting of Rothwell Urban
Council this week, the chairman, Mr. F. T. Tebbutt, J.P., stated that during the past
20 years an average of 46 per cent. of the deaths of members of the National Union of
Boot and Shoe Operatives was due to consumption. In some years the average was as high
as 60 per cent. The doctor said that all factories were places where people were most
likely to contract consumption. The chairman stated that at the Avalon factory the
fans were a source of contention between the manager, the foreman, and the workpeople.
The workpeople would not have the fans going if the weather was a little bit cold. After
pressure the majority of employees voted for the fans. Nobody, he said, would credit
the difference they made to a place. -Boot and Shoe Trades Journal, April 30th, 1920.

THE HOUSING PROBLEM.
A Very Hard Case.

Judge Snagge was occupied for a considerable time at the Northampton County
Court, on Tuesday, in hearing applications for possession of houses.

In one case the owner of a house said it was being let at 7s. per week, while she has had
to pay £2 2s. a week for two rooms, with no attendance.—Northampton Echo, May 12th,
1920.

STANDING ORDERS of the BIENNIAL CONFERENCE.

BIENNIAL MEETINGS.
RULE 80.-Biennial Meetings of the Conference shall be held on Whit Monday
and subsequent days.

TIME OF CONFERENCE MEETING.
RULE 81. - On the first day of Conference the Delegates shall assemble at a
place provided for the purpose, at Twelve o'clock Noon prompt,
and shall proceed to the election of Door Keeper and Two Tellers, and
shall also consider the President's Address and Council's Report ;
after which the Conference shall adjourn. On Tuesday and each
succeeding day, the time of meeting shall be 9.30 a.m., adjourn
at 12.30 p.m., re-assemble at 2 p.m., and adjourn for the day at
5. p.m.

DELEGATES' QUALIFICATION.
RULE 82.-The Conference shall consist of Delegates who have been elected
in accordance with General Rule 69, which reads as follows:-
"Delegates shall be elected in accordance with the number of Financial
Members returned in the December Report previous to the date on which
the Conference is to be held. Delegates to be elected two months prior
to the Conference taking place."
The Council to attend by virtue of their office.

PRESIDENT OF CONFERENCE.
RULE 83.-The President of the Union, by virtue of his office, takes the chair
at all Conferences.

STANDING ORDERS COMMITTEE.
RULE 84.-A committee of five persons shall be elected from the Delegates
of the Conference, whose duty it shall be to examine the Delegates
Credentials, and report thereon. They shall also be at the service of the
Conference to consider any questions or matters that may be relegated
to it with a view to reporting thereon during the sitting of Con-
ference. For such extra services they shall receive additional pay,
to the amount of ros. each.

TELLERS.
RULE 85.—Two Delegates shall be appointed as Tellers, whose ruling as to
numbers shall be final, except in cases of dispute, when the Chairman
shall order a recount.


---
Here is the extracted text:
4
DOOR KEEPER.
RULE 86. A Door Keeper (not a delegate) shall be elected at the commence-
ment of the Conference, whose duties shall be to test all applications
for admittance to Conference, and also to keep the floor of the hall
strictly for Delegates only, and also to regulate the granting of
passes to members of the public to the gallery, and to perform such
other duties as may be required of him by the General Secretary.
Preference to be given to a local man, and his pay shall be 7s. 6d.
per day for any day or days required.
VOTING.
RULE 87. The system of voting shall be by show of hands, the decision
of the majority to rule.
LIMITATION OF SPEECHES.
RULE 88. Every latitude is to be allowed the Delegates for giving expression
to their views on the various subjects that may be brought before
them; but in the event of the Chairman seeing that the business
is not making satisfactory progress, he shall have power to appeal
to the Conference to limit the time allowed to movers of resolutions
to ten minutes, a seconder to seven minutes, and any subsequent
speaker to five minutes. No one shall speak more than once on a
question, except the mover of an original resolution. Should the
President be of opinion that there is no practical difference among
the Delegates, he shall have the power to stop the discussion.
AMENDMENT OF STANDING ORDERS,
RULE 89. Should any amendment of the Standing Orders of Conference be
proposed by any Branch, such amendment shall be forwarded at the
same time and under the same conditions as the amendments pro-
posed to the Rules. Notice of such amendment to be sent by the
General Secretary to the Delegates as early as convenient after
receipt.
DELEGATES LEAVING CONFERENCE.
RULE 90. No Delegate shall leave Conference without the consent of the
President, and Delegates absent one whole sitting without leave of
absence shall be named by the Chairman. The Conference shall
thereupon decide what action shall be taken with any such
delegate.

SUNDAY, MAY 23rd, 1920.

As a preliminary to the week's conference the Manfield Choir entertained the delegates
to Conference and their friends on Sunday evening, 23rd May, 1920, in the Free Gardeners'
Hall, Edinburgh. The Choir rendered the following items: "The Singers," " Sweet and
Low, whilst the ladies' section gave "Caller Herrin," and "Night." The part song for
male voices was "Comrades in Arms." Solos were appreciated by the audience from
Miss W. Marshman, Miss Violet Wills, Miss Clara Wills, Miss Manning, Mr. T. Phillpot,
Miss Alice Winter and Mr. F. Foskett. Duets were rendered by Miss W. Marshman and
Mr. F. Foskett and the Misses Wills.

Upon the completion of the programme, Mr. T. F. Richards, on behalf of the assembly,
said he was expressing the wish of all in offering cordial thanks to the Choir. The Choir
had gone out of their way to entertain the delegates, and he congratulated them upon
their magnificent tone and for the pleasant evening spent together. He also referred to
the help given to the choir by Messrs. Manfield & Sons, and expressed the desire that it
would be well if other firms adopted a similar practice. Mr. Richards thanked the
choirmaster, Mr. W. F. Marshman, and said he was pleased to know there was such a
choir in the trade, as everyone knows there is nothing like leather. He then proposed
a hearty vote of thanks, to which Mr. Cole (a director of the firm of Messrs.
Manfield & Sons) responded.

MONDAY.

The twenty-fourth Biennial Conference of the National Union of Boot and Shoe Opera-
tives opened in the Free Gardeners' Hall, 14, Picardy Place, Edinburgh, at 12 o'clock noon,
on Monday, May 24th, 1920, the General President, Mr. T. F. Richards, presiding. He
was supported by Alderman A. Hill (Treasurer), Messrs. A. J. Dobbs (Leeds), J. Manners
(Bristol), C. Mayes (Kettering), W. Hindes (Norwich), C. Bleakley (Edinburgh), and Mrs.
M. J. Bell-Richards (Members of the Executive Council), Messrs. J. Buckle, J.P., and D.
McCarthy (Organisers), and Mr. E. L. Poulton, O.B.E., J.P. (General Secretary).

Mr. T. Orton (Leicester) and Mr. H. Simmons, J.P. (Stafford), were unable to attend
owing to illness.

In addition the city of Edinburgh was represented by Mr. C. Allan, for the Boot and
Shoe Manufacturers; Mr. Jas. Bachop, representing the Repairers' Association; and
Mr. Caldwell, Chairman of the Edinburgh Trades Council.

The Chairman's Welcome.

THE CHAIRMAN offered a hearty welcome to Edinburgh on behalf of the National
Union of Boot and Shoe Operatives, and called attention to the fact that all sections of
the industry were present.

Welcome by Local Representatives of the Trade.

Union of Boot and Shoe Operatives, I have great pleasure in being here to-day amongst
you, and to welcome you all. It was fortunate for me that I did not previously know
that I should be asked to make a speech. If so I should have had hours of fear, but as
I have been called upon to speak now I have had no time to feel the effects or to think

He also reported that Councillor H. Simmons, J.P., and Mr. Thos. Orton, members

keep at his work; I am sure we hope he will be able to continue his good work.

THORNE, GENERAL WORKERS' UNION CONFERENCE, ABERDEEN.
Boot operatives beg to convey to you fraternal greetings and trust your Confer-

THE GENERAL SECRETARY: We invited delegates from the American and German
Boot Trade Unions and I regret to say in both instances we have met with failure. A
very nice letter was sent from America, which said owing to their being overwhelmed with
work they were unable to accept our invitation. Nevertheless they send fraternal greetings
and hope to send a delegate to some future conference.
In relation to the German Union the matter has been much more difficult. I sent a
long wire last week asking if a delegate would be coming along, to which I received the
following reply :-

It was decided to send an official reply from the Conference. It was also decided
upon the suggestion of the Chairman to print the letters from America and Germany
through the official copy of the Conference Report :-
DEAR MR. POULTON,
BOOT AND SHOE WORKERS' UNION,
246, SUMMER STREET,
BOSTON, MASS.,
February 28th, 1920.
I am writing to acknowledge your letter of January 26th, 1920, and to say that
the matter of our being represented at your annual conference in the city of Edinburgh
is a matter which will be considered by our General Executive Board within the near
future, and you will be advised of the action taken.
I am in receipt of a letter from F. Simon, former secretary of the International
Union of Shoe and Leather Workers, requesting information similar to that which
was furnished prior to the war.
In connection therewith and for your information, will say that at the last meeting
of our General Executive Board, acting upon two different communications from
former Secretary Simon, it was decided to take no action in connection with requests
from him until such time as the war situation had been definitely cleared up, and a
situation existing which would make it not only advisable, but possible to hold an
international convention, which in our opinion, should be for the purpose of estab-
lishing a new International Shoe and Leather Workers' Union, as such expression
as was given by the members of our Board indicated quite clearly that they were not
in favour of continuing the old International.
I do not know what opinions the officers of your organisation have in connection
with this subject, but I thought it might interest you to know what the officers of
our organisation have in mind.
I trust that you are enjoying the best of health, and remain, with personal regards
to yourself and Brother Richards,
Fraternally yours,
C. L. BAINE,
General Secretary-Treasurer,
MR. E. L. POULTON, J.P.,
General Secretary,
Boot and Shoe Operatives,
34, Guilford Street, London, England.

Here is the extracted text from the image:
24
BOOT AND SHOE WORKERS' UNION,
246, SUMMER STREET,
BOSTON, MASS.,
March 24th, 1920,
DEAR MR. POULTON,
It is with great regret that I am writing to advise you that it will be impossible
for the Boot and Shoe Workers' Union to be represented at the Biennial Conference
of the International Union of Boot and Shoe Operatives scheduled for May 24th next.
The situation after the war in our country is such as to make it necessary for the
active executives of our organisation to give the very closest attention to its
affairs.
There has been a general disposition among the employers of labor in our country,
particularly those who can be classed as unfair, to bring about a situation which will
permit them to reduce wages and increase the hours of work, and the propaganda of
employers and others of like mind who claim to represent the public is to the effect
that the cost of living cannot be reduced until Labor agrees to accept lower wages
and to work more hours per week so as to increase production.
Labor leaders of this country, however, insist that the cost of living, if reduced,
must be brought about by the employers either agreeing to, or being forced to accept
less profits, in addition to which a curb should be put upon speculation in the necessities
of life, and that production can be increased by reducing the number of people who
are neither employers or workers.
In this connection, it has been suggested that the Government adopt some
measures to force these non-producers to bear their share of life's burden similar to
the plan adopted during the war when the Government adopted most effectively the
slogan-" Work or Fight."
While the unions of our country have been quite successful in increasing wages
to meet the increased cost of living during the last five years, none of them haes
secured increases in wages beyond the percentage of increase in the cost of living
during the same period, but some of the unions have not been able to secure increases
to equal the increased cost of living, while in the poorly organised industries, the
average increases secured represent but little, if any, over 30 per cent. of the increased
cost of living and these increases were given in the form of bonuses, some of which
have already been taken away.
Briefly speaking, the slogan of the organised workers is that the wages did not
increase until after the cost of living had materially increased, and employers or the
public in general must not expect that there will be any reduction in wages until the
cost of living is materially decreased.
Referring directly to the shoe trade, will say that the year 1919 was the most
successful year in the history of the Boot and Shoe Workers' Union, and for your
information, I am enclosing herewith a letter recently issued by our General Executive
Board covering the progress of our Union during that year. We feel that we have a
right to congratulate ourselves upon the success achieved, especially in view of the
fact that we still have to meet the opposition and antagonism of independent factions
in our trade.
In a previous communication, I believe we expressed the views of the Executives
of our Union in connection with holding an international conference as suggested in
a communication received from former Secretary Simon.
We are still of the opinion that when the former freedom of intercourse which
prevailed prior to the World's war has been established between Germany and other
countries, a conference should be held for the purpose of establishing a new International
Shoe and Leather Workers' Union, and when such a conference is called, I feel certain
that the Boot and Shoe Workers' Union of America will be represented.
I trust that your Biennial Conference will be successful and result in great benefit
to the members of your union.
With sincere personal regards to yourself and colleagues,

BOOT AND SHOE WORKERS' UNION,

BOSTON, MASS.,
TO THE OFFICERS AND MEMBERS OF OUR VARIOUS LOCAL UNIONS.
in the history of our Uinion.
January 15th, 1920.
The General Executive Board at a meeting held in Boston, week beginning
January 5th, 1920, made careful review of the general situation, especially as applied
to the Boot and Shoe Workers' Union.
The General Board feels sure the membership generally will agree that there is
just cause for congratulation when they learn that the year 1919 was the best year
On January 1st, 1920, we had 231 active chartered locals as against 175 local
cinnati, Ohio, shows the largest percentage of increase in members, but is closely
unions, January 1st, 1919, with a net increase of 14,484 members for the year. Cin-
followed by Brockton, Mass., St. Louis, Mo., Chicago, Ill., St. Paul, Minn., Rochester,
N.Y., and Montreal, Que., while nearly every other local union shows an increase
for the year.
The large increase in membership in Cincinnati is due to a considerable extent
to the women stitchers, who for the first time since the days of the Knights of Labor
have again manifested interest in organisation.
The action of the Knights of Labor Cutters' Assembly of Rochester, N.Y., in
voleased our membership in that city, and we find that in spite of the claims of the
voluntarily joining the hot and she workers' Union in a body has substantially
United faction, many shoe workers outside the cutting departments are joining our
union and the prospects in that city are good.
Substantial increases in wages and a general reduction in hours was secured
for the members for the year.
In reviewing the financial condition of the Union, we find that although a year
of heavy expense, due largely to the holding of a convention, the balance on hand in
general funds shows an increase over January 1st, 1919.
The general tendency upon the part of our Union to appreciate the necessity
of maintaining contracts is also a feature for which we have a right to congratulate
the organisation. There have been a number of violations on the part of our members,
none of which, however, created any serious situation, but it seems essential to say
that the result in each case was failure and loss of employment to the violators.
There have been a number of strikes by groups of individuals or sections of
shops' crews without authorisation by the local executive board of the local unions.
Strikes without authority from the Union are unwise, contrary to the constitution
and policy of the Union, and should be condemned.
A fundamental principle upon which the Boot and Shoe Workers' Union was
founded is the bettering of wages and conditions of all shoe workers, and not of any
special branch.
When certain members of the Union employed in any one department create
a strike situation in violation of agreements, or without Union authority, they do so
without giving any consideration to the interests and welfare of their fellow members
in other departments, who have a right to be considered and have a voice in the creation
of any situation which may mean their loss of employment and the consequent loss
of wages.
Acts of this kind are also in violation of the motto for which the Boot and Shoe
Workers' Union stands, "Each for all and all for each," and are in violation of every
sense of honor upon which mutual agreements are based.
These violations are taken advantage of by the opponents of voluntary arbitra-
tion, who advocate compulsory arbitration and enactment of laws to make strikes
under any circumstances unlawful and the participants subject to penalties in fines
and imprisonment.
We urge our Unions everywhere to give this communication their earnest con-
that
of our contracts and constitution is maintained at all times and under all circum-
stances.
In view of the fact that large manufacturers operating strictly non-union factories
are attempting to place advertisements in the Labor Press, and in other ways seeking
of our Union that special efforts should be made to boom the Union Stamp.
to secure the trade of members of organised labor, it seems to be to the best interests

Credentials.
MR. G. PARBURY reported that all the credential cards were in order except that of
MR. J. H. HOOPER (Street), who had failed to hand his card in. Mr. Hooper stated he
would bring the same to the next session.
The Conference then adjourned at 1.5 p.m. until Tuesday morning.
In the afternoon a most pleasant and enjoyable time was spent at the invitation of
the Edinburgh Branch. A motor drive to Queensferry was taken and then by passenger
steamship round the Rosyth Dockyard, the delegates being given a chance to see some of
our famous warships, including Lord Beatty's flagship, "The Lion," "Queen Elizabeth,"
and others. After a most enjoyable trip lasting upwards of two hours the delegates
returned to a high tea provided by the St. Cuthbert's Co-operative Association. The
General President of the Union expressed the cordial thanks of the delegates to the Directors
of the St. Cuthbert's Co-operative Association for their kindness, and Messrs. Leng and
McGregor having replied the gathering dispersed.
TUESDAY.
Morning Session.
The Conference resumed at 9.30 a.m., the GENERAL PRESIDENT presiding.
THE GENERAL SECRETARY read the roll and reported that all delegates were present.
Upon the proposition of COUNCILLOR C. BATES (Higham and Rushden), seconded
by MR. W. N. BLISSETT (Derby), the minutes of Monday's proceedings, as read by the
General Secretary, were unanimously endorsed.
whilst on his way to Aberdeen to attend the conference of the General Workers' Union,
THE GENERAL SECRETARY reported, with regret, that The Rt. Hon. Will Thorne, M.P.,
had been taken seriously ill, and had had to be taken to a nursing home and undergo an
operation. He suggested that a letter be sent wishing him a speedy recovery, which was
agreed to.

Presidential Address.

THE CHAIRMAN : I am pleased to find a much larger number of delegates at this
Conference than we have had in the past. We certainly feel our members would require
much larger representation than what we enjoyed when we only had 29,000 members.
From that point of view I presume they have satisfaction for the time being. I well
remember the last time we met in Edinburgh 26 years ago. As Mr. Bleakley said yesterday
there are only four present to-day who attended that Conference the General Secretary
Mr. Alex Smith (Arbroath), my friend Buckle, and myself. It was in this ancient city we
carried the socialistic resolution which was added to our rules. We have not come across
resolution. It is all that is worth striving for this side of the grave. When it comes
many other Unions that have such a democratic object as there is contained in that
to a question of our entering into politics we hold that as one of the most important matters
in the Union. At the present time we find ourselves in the happy position of having
100,443 members, or an increase of 57,289 since the last Edinburgh Conference. Of this
number 26,802 are financial females or 46 per cent. of the membership. In 1894 we had
65 branches and sections, to-day there are 143 or an increase of 78. Our funds in 1894
stood at £47,408 12S. 9d.; to-day they have reached a grand total of £367,604 88. 4d.,
or an increase of £320,195 15S. 7d. I should like to say, although it looks well on paper,
if we had our 100,000 members to cater for in a national trouble, we should soon find our
funds gone. I hope that time will never come.
I have seen the means and methods by
which other people get the best of the good things of this life, and they do not get them
by striking but by subtle means. They do it by a policy of manipulation in which they use
mesmerism and hypnotism. They are on the box of the industrial coach and they hold
the reins, and I say the time has arrived when we should see our man on the box and the
coach going our way, and that is the way of the workers after toil whether by muscle or
brain. There are one or two features I should like to touch upon. One is we are not
very happy in our relationship at the present moment with some of the employers who
belong to the Employers' Federation. Some of my friends have been disturbed by a
foolhardy policy adopted by a local association, where owing to their action the girls of
were surprised to find local people have read into our agreement that we are not guaranteed
and never challenged before, and we have taken steps to have a conference with the em-
ployers in order that we might wipe away any doubt, because most of us would not have
looked at payment by results if the minimum wage had not been guaranteed for the opera-
tive whilst on the premises. I am pleased to say even the Government have not been
so narrow as that, and in their Trade Board regulations every person is entitled to the
minimum wage if he is on the premises for a full week. It has upset us and aggravates
the good relations which exist between the manufacturers and ourselves by them attempting
to disturb that understanding. I hope you will let the Central Office know of any attempt
to ignore that position. It has been the bulwark and pivot of the whole position and with
which we have done the major portion of our business. It has been for us to ask our
members to give it a trial, and if we lose our position we shall reconsider our policy. We
are hoping at this Conference with the employers to get over that difficulty. Now I will
refer to the position in which we find ourselves in Yorkshire. There have been two Unions
fighting one another. The A.U.C.C.E. (against whom we make a charge with the Parlia-
mentary Committee of the Trades Union Congress nine years ago) were informed they
must give up boot operatives to us who cater for boot operatives only. That has not been
carried out. In their judgment they thought fit to withdraw from the Trades Union Congress
and not force the issue. Since then, upon a number of occasions, they would liked to have
got back to Congress and joined the Labour Party, but they have not been able to do so,
because we wanted the resolution carried out. The Council offered to the A.U.C.C.E.
the condition that we would allow them to keep the members they had if they gave us a
guarantee that they would not go into any of the branches or sections in competition
with our Union. They would not accept that position; consequently they went on
poaching and only recently took the whole of the operatives at a Sheffield Co-operative
Society. As a natural consequence we can do nothing else but look after ourselves.
got a Sedgwick Award in 1918 and have secured further advances until we obtained
I have a letter stating this has been accepted by the
The A.U.C.C.E. now

demand roo per cent. on the Award. This is the policy they pursue. No matter what
We dare not bleed co-operative societies whilst private employers escape. It is not just.
we shall have to take the question to the Trades Union Congress. They have no control

THE GENERAL SECRETARY suggested the question should be raised on the Council's

1.
Council's Address to Conference.
FELLOW MEMBERS,
Since our conference at Leicester when the world was at war, we are
glad to be able to report that, speaking in general terms war has ceased, although
it may be said that some amount of fighting is still going on. This we trust
peace. Since 1914, the times have indeed been not only strenuous, but full
will speedily be brought to a conclusion, and that one may say the world is at
anxiety being attended by loss, suffering and pain quite unexampled
of anxiety arising out of, or in connection with the world war; such
present such a united front to any person or group of persons who may
in the history of the world. We sincerely hope that mankind will
intentions being put into operation. We would remind you however, that
attempt to bring about wars in the future, as to thwart and destroy any such
there is absolute need for constant vigilance on the part of the people to prevent
the war party from again becoming predominant, there being from time to
time what we cannot but think are inspired paragraphs as to preparations for
the next war.
In our judgment it is imperative that all workers should
arrive at such understandings with one another as to make it, in the
terms of the proposals which the Labour Party drew up in 1918, "that there
should henceforth on earth be no more war."
desire are not for the killing off of one another and we would here point out,
that if, unhappily there should be any big war in the future, science has devel-
oped to such an extent that words fail to convey the horrors that would fall
upon mankind should such an outbreak take place. This should bring home
to the workers all the more strongly the absolute necessity of unity and concord
-but wars against injustice, against disease, against any conditions that
handicap mankind from attaining to the highest and best humanity is capable
of attaining to.
2.
STATE OF TRADE.
During the period under review, the state of trade has, upon the whole,
been good. In the Closing Department there has been in some centres a short-
age of labour. Special schemes were set up to meet the exceptional circum-
stances brought about by the shortage which we think is only temporary.
3.
DEMOBILISATION.
During the two years-1918-20-not fewer than 18,000 of our members
have been demobilised, and, generally speaking, were re-engaged in their
old situations. In addition to these members having returned, 3,685 men
injured in the war, but who were still capable of working, have been
trained either as handsewn men or for boot and shoe repairing, as well as on
the manufacturing side of the industry. The schemes under which these
persons were trained were schemes drawn up by representative Committees
under the Ministry of Labour or Ministry of Pensions. The Union has had
from the setting up of such Committees representation thereon. Nearly all
of these 3.685 men were before the War connected with some other industry
than the boot and shoe industry, and we may fairly claim that as an industry

5.
BOOT AND SHOE REPAIRING.
Since we last met in conference, a new body known as the "Boot and Shoe
Repairing Trades Board" has been called into existence by the Government
J. Buckle, J.P. (Organiser), C. Bleakley, A. J. Dobbs (Council Members),
J. Osborne (Bristol Secretary), and W. J. Smith (Great Yarmouth Secretary).
Other societies are represented, along with representatives from the employers'
organisations, with Professor Hobhouse as chairman. The Board deals with
handsewn work and boot and shoe repairing. A considerable number of
meetings have been held and prices fixed up with a minimum wage for males
of 21 years of age and over 65s. per week; females, 45s. per week; 48 hour
week and extra rates for overtime. In addition a very complete piecework
statement has been drawn up for repairing, and 2s. per hour fixed for those on
surgical work; also a scheme for training youths and women. The great
advantage of these conditions is that they are national and have the force
of the law behind them, inasmuch as the Orders for their enforcement are
made under the authority of the Minister of Labour. We are fully satisfied
that despite any shortcomings, and we know there are shortcomings, the
setting up of these conditions has been a distinct advantage not only to the
workers, but also to employers, because here again the statement being
national, prevents so far as the labour cost is concerned-undercutting of one
employer against another.

AMALGAMATION.
We are not able although a number of meetings have taken place with
