8.
JOINT INDUSTRIAL (WHITLEY) COUNCIL.
A Whitley Council has been established in connection with the industry,
and ourselves. Attempts were made to ensure representatives of other organi-
the Council consisting of representatives of the Manufacturers' Federation
sations, both employers and employed, being members of such Council: it
was unanimously agreed to keep it to the two organisations.
Inasmuch, for so many years past machinery had been in existence in
the industry for dealing with many of the matters that Whitley Councils were
set up to deal with, it was decided at the outset to rule out of the jurisdiction
of such Council, such questions as
Hours,
Wages,
Conditions of Employment,
they being already dealt with by the Joint Committee of the Manufacturers'
Federation and ourselves.
A number of meetings have been held of this Council on questions
affecting the industry such as
Holidays,
Sanitary Conditions of the Factories,
Health of the Workers,
To encourage membership of the Union, etc., etc.
The Council has thus done some good work and we feel that with proper
safeguards it can be a useful body to the industry as a whole.
CONSUMPTION IN THE TRADE. This matter has been dealt with not only
by the Industrial (Whitley) Council but also by a new body known as the
Industrial Fatigue Research Board, of which the General Secretary is a member,
and several meetings have been held of this new Council and also joint meetings
of the manufacturers with ourselves and representatives of the Home Office
in connection with the question of consumption, and this disease has been one
of the prominent features which we have dealt with. At the present moment
a special enquiry is being held by the Industrial Fatigue Research Board
with a view to ascertaining, if at all possible, whether there is an excess of any
other disease in the shoe trade, and if so to ascertain if possible what steps
should be taken to deal with the same.
We regret we cannot report more fully upon the subject, the reason being,
that owing to the war, the services of the Medical fraternity were so called
upon to deal with the large number of wounded sailors and soldiers and were
thereby prevented from giving the necessary attention to this subject. We

Overtime.
19.-Employers shall give reasonable notice of their intention to work
overtime, such notice to be posted in the departments concerned. Overtime
on each working day except Saturday shall be paid for at the rate of time
and a quarter for dayworkers and 25 per cent. over the piecework rates for
piece-workers. Overtime work on Saturday shall be paid at the rate of time
and a half for dayworkers and 50 per cent. above piecework rates for piece-
workers.
Overtime shall only be paid to an operative who has worked the full period
of a normal week of 48 hours, unless an interruption in the normal week has
occurred for which the operative is not responsible; provided that overtime
worked to make up loss of time due to breakdown of machinery or due to
holidays given at the request of the operative, shall be paid for at normal
and not at overtime rates.
The staff employees in the Stock or Shoe Rooms shall not be subject to
any overtime regulations if they are paid for holidays.
Universal Application of New Minimum Rates.

but if at the expiration of the period for which this agreement has been fixed
it can be shown that the Union has failed to put such rates into general operation
or any of its local Associations to decline to consider any further application
throughout the non-federated districts it shall be competent for the Federation,
to increase such rates.

```text
Chairman of Conference.
A great many of the branches (as stated above) at once called their
Boards of Arbitration out and dealt with the question of bringing up to date,
in accordance with the Agreement, their quantities or piecework statements,
or framing new Statements, and in most centres this work has been accom-
plished. There is no doubt at all in our minds that, although the Agreement
does not secure all we should have liked, nevertheless, it has secured very
considerable advances and benefits for those in the industry. We are aware
of some feeling in various parts of the country against the Agreement,
nevertheless, we are convinced there have been many beneficial improvements
both from the workmen's standpoint and also from that of the employer, by
having a standard to which one can appeal so far as output is concerned.
It is true in just a few, very few, instances, some opposition has been put
up against the advantages of the newer methods, but we feel certain such
opposition has been based upon a misapprehension of the development in the
shoe trade. Development is bound to take place from time to time and what
we have to secure is that such development should not be detrimental to the
workers so far as wages and conditions are concerned, but an improvement;
and although it may be that payment by results is a system to which
objections can be urged, it is equally true to say any system which may be
framed is open to some criticism, and can be proved to have some short-
comings in connection therewith.
In our judgment, however, experience has fully justified the adoption of
quantity or piecework statements in connection with the boot and shoe industry.
It should be clearly understood that to those on daywork the statements can
just as easily be applied, viz.: Reckoning up the amount of work performed
and the sum of money such work calls for as measured by the prices set out on
the statements.

1919 AGREEMENT REVISED.
Owing to the fact that for only a few months after the Agreement was
signed the cost of living went down, and then began to take an upward curve
with the prospect of remaining high, we felt bound to ask the Manufacturers'
Federation to reopen the Agreement with a view to setting up a higher minimum
wage. We have expressed our views upon more than one occasion through the
Report that it would be the best thing in our judgment, to bring down the cost
of living and so help to break up this vicious circle in which we find ourselves
at the present time. Seeing however, we are in the circle we felt compelled
to press for an improvement in the minimum rates to meet such increased
cost of living. Our request for re-opening the Agreement being acceded to,
```

PARLIAMENTARY DEBATES.

HOUSING, EARL SHILTON.

MR. PETHICK-LAWRENCE asked the Minister of Health whether his attention has been
drawn to the housing conditions existing in Earl Shilton, in Leicestershire: whether
he is aware that many of the cottages are in imminent danger of falling down, that others,
of the shortage of houses many couples, who were recently married, have no hope of getting
the upper rooms of which are open to the weather, are inhabited, and that in consequence
houses; that consumption is rife in the township; whether any efforts are being made
to deal with the situation; what is the attitude of his Department towards the matter;
and whether he will take action to secure some improvement of the conditions prevailing.

MR. N. CHAMBERLAIN: My attention has been drawn to this matter, and I am in
communication with the local authority for the district, who have submitted proposals
for the erection of houses in this and other parishes.
--Parliamentary Debates, 2nd July, 1925.

RUSSIA.

MR. LOOKER asked the Parliamentary Secretary to the Overseas Trade Department
if he can inform the House of the total amount expended by Great Britain in the purchase
of Russian good and products in 1924; and whether Russia, during 1924, expended in
the purchase of British goods the full amount of the credit established in her favour for
this purpose?

MR. SAMUEL: The imports of Russian goods and products into Great Britain in 1924
amounted in value to, roughly, £20,000,000, while the exports to Russia of British products
and manufactures, during the same period, amounted to nearly £4,000,000, and of re-exports
from the United Kingdom of foreign and colonial merchandise to something over £7,000,000.
There is thus an unexpended balance of trade in favour of Russia, in so far as this country
is concerned, of about £9,000,000 sterling. The full amount of the credit established in
the favour of Russia has, therefore, not been expended in the purchase of British goods.--
Extract from Parliamentary Debates, House of Commons, Monday, 6th July, 1925. Vol.
186, No. 98, p. 18.

UNEMPLOYMENT.—It was stated that unemployment was considerable among postal,
telegraph and telephone employees, as the result of the numerous suppressions of posts
carried out with a view to economy.

Out of 102,500 members, about 15,000 were out of work. Of this number about
3,020 were receiving unemployment relief from their Union. Although the number of
post offices has been increased, and although the Commissariat of Posts, Telegraphs and
Telephones intends to develop yet further its service of post offices, it was stated that
it was improbable that the unemployed would find work in the near future, since the State
had not the funds to engage new employees. It was, therefore, thought probable that
unemployment would continue to be acute.

FACTORY INSPECTION IN JAPAN.

Osaka Figures.

Figures recently published with regard to the enforcement of the Factory Acts in
Japan, states Industrial and Labour Information, the weekly publication of the International
Labour Office of the League of Nations, show that in Osaka, where 3,673 factories
come under the operation of the Factory Acts, as employing more than 15 persons, penalties
for violation of the Acts have been imposed in 462 cases.

The offences which were the subject of these cases included:—

Employment of workers under the legal minimum age . . . . . 16
Employment of women beyond the legal maximum hours of work, 12
Other offences included neglect to make known to the workers the regulations concerning
compensation, &c., neglect to make rules for compensation, &c., and infringements of
the regulations issued under the Factory Acts.

The Factory Inspection Department of the Osaka Prefecture, it is stated, intends to
deal even more stringently than hitherto with all cases of infringement, "in order to commemorate
the tenth anniversary of the enforcement of the Factory Act."

INTERNATIONAL FEDERATION OF TRADE UNIONS.

"WAR AGAINST WAR."

Concentrating the Forces making for Peace.

The anniversary of the outbreak of the world war comes, alas, in the midst of new
warlike developments. New causes of war are arising, and the forces which would blot out
the memory of that terrible time is beginning to fade. Men forget quickly, and the forces which would blot out
remembrance are stronger than those which seek to keep awake in men's minds the sea
of war. Not that nations are inclined to war—that they never were, even before 1914—
but they allow themselves to be blindfolded and led by the makers of public opinion.
Hence the future is dark and dangerous, and will continue to be so unless the friends of
peace set themselves to form public opinion, and fill men's minds with the firm determination
to abolish war and all its horrors.

The working class has never ceased to wage its great fight against war and militarism,
but little has been heard lately of the middle-class peace movement. Not much is achieved
by holding a congress now and then; continuous unremitting work is essential. The
diversity and multiplicity of the middle-class peace organisations are a serious drawback
to effective co-operation. It should be possible to co-ordinate and organise continuous
and systematic work. By the amalgamation of kindred organisations, efficient bodies
could be created, and the formation of national and international peace rings might make
it possible to carry on steady and unremitting propaganda among all circles of the population,
and in dangerous crises to co-operate with the workers and thus exert a decisive
influence upon events. Although the slaughter of innocent men is worse than the financial
burdens which military preparations involve, yet the fact remains that not only is war
itself a burden to the peoples, but also the preparations for war. To put an end to this
mad state of affairs must be the untiring endeavour of all who are desirous of achieving
a happier future for humanity; and this desire must be strong enough to overcome all
political antagonism and all the many diversities of opinion and outlook which separate
men and hamper their successful co-operation.—Thursday, 6th August, 1925.

TRADE UNION PERCENTAGES OF UNEMPLOYED.

BOOT AND SHOE TRADE.—Membership of Unions reporting at end of July, 1925,
80,967. Unemployed at end of July, 1925, 4,449; percentage, 5.5. Increase in
percentage unemployed as compared with a month ago, 0.9. Increase in percentage as
compared with a year ago, 0.3.—The Ministry of Labour Gazette, August, 1925.Here's a transcription of the text from the image:

There was much unemployment and short time at
Kettering; some firms, however, were fairly well employed, and the average working
hours were reported to be about three-quarter time.
At Leeds, but employment remained generally poor,
with a large number of boot and shoe operatives unemployed. At Stafford it was very
poor, and about the same as in the previous month, but not
so good as a year earlier; some departments were on short time, others worked some over-
time during the month. A further slight seasonal improvement was reported from Bristol;
short time working was still common, especially
with the smaller firms. At Street there was a further decline, and employment was bad;
it was worse than a year ago. Fair conditions continued to prevail in the shoe and slipper
trade of the Rossendale Valley, though a good deal of short time was still being worked;
Employment was fair at Aberdeen, Kilmarnock, Maybole, and Ayr; quiet at Edin-
burgh and Glasgow; bad at Dundee and Arbroath. On the whole it was somewhat
worse in the Scottish centres than in August, 1925, or in September, 1924.

THE LABOUR PARTY CONFERENCE.

During the past month the Labour Party Conference has been held at
Liverpool. The Conference was not only very largely attended, but the
spirit of the delegates was one of optimism and determination. The over-
whelming majority of votes recorded upon policy and support of the Executive
was very significant, making it quite clear that there must be on the part of
those who from time to time are nominated for positions that they must adhere
to the Party and its declared policy. Two votes especially we would call
attention to—the one dealing with the claim of the
COMMUNISTS,

and the one dealing with the question of another prospective Labour Govern-
ment. In the first case the vote against the Communists was for referring
that it would be unwise to make a special appeal at the present moment, at a time
when we want the employers to give very favourable consideration to an advance and
extraordinary claim we are likely to make upon them. Our demands will be very, very
far-reaching, and we are anxious not to mar the good relationship already existing.

Whitley Council Meeting.

On the 14th we attended a Whitley Council Meeting, the first since January of this
year, and although there are some who have very little sympathy with Whitley Councils,
I for one, do not ignore any kind of machinery, not even the dictates of theorists who are
opposed to Trade Unions and Trade Union Officials, that institutions like the Whitley
Council can be of service to the operatives engaged in the trade. It was my good fortune
when the Memoranda for these Councils were about to be set up in 1918, to get a definite
copy of the Memoranda as sent out to future Trade Union Officials, and I am quoting at the
present moment from that Memoranda to which, on one occasion, I drew the attention of
the employers, which reads: "Functions of Industrial Councils, Clause 5. The collection
of Statistics and Information on Materials, Market Costs, Rates of Wages, and Average
Profits on Turnover." I am not going to say at the present moment that we have no
right to make enquiries into these matters, in fact I think if we were to put up a claim and
ask the Whitley Council to investigate the above, that there is nothing to prevent them
so doing. If such a request was made at an enquiry of this description, I am bound to say
that, as Chairman of the Whitley Council, I could not see my way clear to rule it out of
order.

Terms of Settlement.

On October 15th we had a meeting at Tavistock Square of the Standing Committee
responsible for the interpretation of the Terms of Settlement, when we had the Northamp-
ton case before us, and the following resolution is the result: "Having considered the
representations of the Northampton Manufacturers' Association and the Operatives'
Union, decide that nothing can be added to the decision already communicated to the
organisation in question, that their province is solely to interpret the Agreements of 1919
and 1920, which must be read in conjunction with each other, and they therefore recom-
mend that any difference that may arise in Northampton as to the operations of these
Agreements should be dealt with on their merits by the Local Board of Arbitration and
Conciliation, and in the event of an agreement not being reached, the matter should be
dealt with through the Board's machinery already existing for settling such differences."

Technical Advisory Committee.

On the same day we held a meeting of the Technical Advisory Committee for training
men in the Boot and Shoe manufacturing, when the London Board asked us to train 6
men per month in the Turn-Shoe Louis Heel Work. After listening to all the evidence
that had been produced, we came to the conclusion that it would not be unwise to teach
these men, whom it will take 18 months to learn the business sufficiently to give them an
opportunity of earning their livelihood. The main feature that guided us, was the fact that
for a number of years people from the Scandinavian countries, including Russia and Poland,
have generally emigrated into this country for this class of work, and as we have men who
are unemployed among us, we considered it our bounden duty to look at home first.

Mr. A. W. E. O'Dell, Canada.

We have had staying with us in the country Mr. A. W. E. O'Dell from Canada, and
it makes one think more and more why we are not more successful with the rendering of
the Trade Union Stamp for Boots and Shoes in this country. He is a great believer in this, as
the American people themselves are, and I hope our members will impress at every oppor-
tunity the wisdom of themselves and their families, to wear Trade Union Stamped boots.
It is our intention, at the earliest possible moment, to endeavour to persuade the Govern-
ment Department responsible, to insist upon employers of labour describing in legible
language the materials of which their boots are composed. By this means, if we want to
buy a paper boot we can have one; if we want to buy a leather boot, we ought to have
one, and we never ought to be deceived without a prosecution takes place to prevent people
imposing upon the public by wilfully misleading them.

Yours fraternally,

_signature_

General President.

34, GUILFORD STREET,
LONDON, W.C. I.

FUNERAL ALLOWANCE RECEIPTS AND BRANCH RETURN SHEETS, Etc.

The above are continually being received by the Central Office incomplete—the President of the Branch having omitted to sign. In many cases too the “Exact Occupation” of the deceased member is not stated in the place provided for the purpose on the Funeral Receipts. This information is required for statistical purposes, and Branch Secretaries are requested to see, in future, before sending to the Central Office, that all the particulars asked for are given.

THE TRADE UNION STAMP

has been granted during the month to Messrs. Walker & Dunn (No. 25) of Higham Ferrers, and Messrs. Jacques & Son Ltd. (No. 26) of Rushden, and we ask that members will insist upon all their boots bearing such Stamp.
The Repairing Stamp (No. 17) has also been granted to the Dartford Industrial Co-operative Society Ltd., 93, Kent Road, Dartford, Kent, and we hope members and friends in that area will see that their boots are repaired by this Society.

TRADE UNION BUTTONS AND BROOCHES.

A fresh supply of buttons and brooches has recently been secured and can be had upon application, price 6d. each, cash with order. Secretaries, please note, when ordering buttons or brooches, cash should ALWAYS be sent with order.

Season’s Greetings.

We sincerely wish all the Members of the Union the Season’s Greetings.

MONTHLY RETURNS.

We feel bound once again to call the serious attention of Branch Secretaries to the fact that the FIFTEENTH day of each month is the LATEST day for returns to reach the General Secretary. Last month many returns were late. Branches failed to send off their returns to reach the Central Office by the 15th of the month, and this tends to upset the organisation of the work in the General Secretary’s Office. We trust in future every Branch Secretary will endeavour to get in his returns as soon as is possible after the month (or five weeks as the case may be) ends, and NOT to wait until the latest date for them to reach the Office; also before sending them to see that they are in order and properly signed.

We remain, yours fraternally,

T. F. RICHARDS, Gen. President.     THE COUNCIL.

(5) Fair Trading Councils.

These bodies have advisory functions as to the prices chargeable for certain goods.
From these bodies representatives of the Consumers’ Council are rigorously excluded.
The “Fair Trading Councils” consist almost entirely of persons who trade in those articles,
in the prices of which they, as traders, are directly interested. Thus, the men in the
dock and the members of the jury are recruited from the same cells.

Thereupon, the Council passed the following resolution:

“The Consumers’ Council, having considered the Statement as to the functions
of the Fair Trading Councils, again registers its request to the Food Controller that
it be fully represented on the various Fair Trading Councils as the only means available
for protecting the consumers whom they directly represent.”

In spite of this resolution, which was carried unanimously, the Traders’ Councils
have still no representatives of the Consumers’ Council empowered to watch, criticise
and check any steps the Traders may take in their own interest.

(4) On many other matters the Council has been active and insistent, but their protests
have been ignored.
(a) JAM has been de-controlled and prices are free.
(b) Home-killed MEAT has been de-controlled and prices have risen. IMPORTED
MEAT has been sold to foreign countries at slaughter prices.
(c) The highly successful New Bridge Street RESTAURANT was sold without notice
to the Council, and NATIONAL KITCHENS were discontinued, against the Council’s
protests.
(d) Orders relating to NET WEIGHT AND STANDARD OF QUALITY have been
revoked.
(e) All efforts in favour of the National Control and Co-ordination of the MILK
INDUSTRY have been ignored.

(5) The Consumers’ Council is convinced that the Ministry of Food should be continued
as a permanent Department. That it should be a democratic Ministry, and that
its functions should be to protect the interest of the consumer.

The Ministry of Agriculture protects the interests of the Producers.
The Board of Trade protects the interests of the Traders.
The Ministry of Food should protect the interests of the Consumers.

There is a crying need for a real live Department dealing with the people’s food.
It should control prices. It should maintain standard of quality. It should deal with
Net Weight, Adulteration, Substitutes, and the Poisons put before the public as food
without any indication of their real contents. It should concentrate on the provision of
the necessaries of life, and should have ample powers over distribution.

(6) Are Consumers content that through their apathy Traders and Trusts should
triumph over them once again; that their representatives on the Consumers’ Council
should be ignored; and that the Ministry of Food with its power to protect the Consumers’
interests should be stamped out?

If not, now and now only is the time to make a stand.

5th November, 1920.

BRITISH GLACE KID.

Tanners Decide to make Further Reduction in Output.

A meeting of the members of the British Glacé Kid Tanners’ Association was held
on the 28th ult. at the offices of the Association, 26, St. Thomas Street, Bermondsey, S.E.
The meeting was fully representative and had been called to consider the present
position of the glacé kid industry in view of the prevailing weakness in the market.

After a full discussion the following resolution was passed :-

“In view of the fact that glacé kid has been sold at far below the cost of production,
based on replacement prices of raw skins, labour, tanning materials, &c.,
the British Glacé Kid Tanners reluctantly resolve to reduce their output, which at
present is below 50 per cent. of their pre-war production.”

It was pointed out that the sales which had recently been made at prices below cost of
production had been in many instances the outcome of forced realisations. It was not
anticipated that the present unsound position could continue, for no stocks of any volume
could be expected to be imported into this country which would compete with recent
values.—Shoe and Leather Record, November 5th, 1920.320

MARCH 26TH, 1920.

5,000 MEN REFUSED EMPLOYMENT!
OUR CA CANNY GOVERNMENT.

The Prime Minister and the Minister of War have on several occasions charged
either directly, or by inference, the Labour Party with being unkind towards the men
who placed their lives at the service of the State and who have suffered the loss of limbs
and received scars that they will carry to their graves. Surely these our Brothers, Sons
and Friends, are not likely to suffer at our hands.

But let us see what the Government has done to find employment and make this
land fit for heroes to live in. Mr. Lloyd George and Mr. Churchill are both aware that
the Technical Advisory Committee set up by the Government for the purpose of training
our heroes in the art of Handsewn Boot and Shoemaking and Repairing, entered upon their
work with zeal and interest, deciding unanimously that these men should not be exploited
by either the public or the Government, and as a precaution they should spend at least
a period of 12 months in a Technical and Practical School before being introduced into
the outside world at the Government's expense, when they should receive a further six
months in a workshop before we could call them fitting to make an effort for themselves.
We also said, and carried a resolution unanimously, and backed by our respective Trade
Unions, that the Government should provide Government Workshops for the employment
of these men in making Handsewn Boots and Shoes for the Army and the Navy, thereby
giving them continuous employment and constant wages for the Trade. We estimate that
five thousand men could be so employed by making the
Handsewn Boot worn by many who can afford to pay for comfortable boots, and according
to Sir John French, who said in 1908 that an Army moves on its stomach and its feet.
consequently good comfortable boots are absolutely essential.

We claim the Handsewn Boot to be the best for all purposes in quality, comfort and
wear. We then assert that these boots could be provided by the Government in its own
workshops at a less price than they are paying for the machine made article being produced
to-day, and three parts of the purchase price would be paid in wages, as against one-fourth
paid in the Machine made boot.

Our Chairman, Mr. Box Kingham, of a well-known Handsewn firm, is heart and soul
with us in the matter.

We have deputised the War Office, Mr. Corner, the Minister of Pensions (then Mr.
John Hodge) who expressed himself willing to do all he could as he wore Handsewn boots
himself. We deputised the Admiralty who expressed sympathy, and lastly Sir Robert
Horne, all apparently sympathetic, but nothing done, and this brings me to the most
important point. Leicester and many other towns have refused to teach any more men
because no employment can be offered them, and as an individual, I want to tell the 2,000
men already trained, and the 1,600 under training, that I simply regret exceedingly that
we have trained so many men, fully believing that the Government would act honourably
and justly to these men; we believed we had a Government who had heart big enough
to find them employment after being trained at the country's expense.

We are fully satisfied that the money spent on equipping a Factory or Workshop
would be repaid at the end of three years.

I wish, Sir, to say it is a downright shame that a single man should be taken into the
Handsewn Making or Repairing for training whilst there are already at the present time
10 per cent. of Repairers unemployed, many of them members of our Union.

I little dreamed that when I advised my sons to join up that I was supporting such a
heartless one-eyed Government who turn down any practical suggestion and seem to be
inclined with the lop-sided idea that the Government must do nothing to encourage direct
employment, not even for the noble fellows who have done so much for us.

I ask the Secretary of State for War and challenge him, that he, nor any of his predecessors has not yet tested the Handsewn Boot as against the Machine made Foot for
the Army by real wear and tear, allowing one regiment of the same number to wear Hand-
sewn Boots and another regiment in the same area to wear Machine made Boots, to take
into consideration the wear and tear and expense at the end of two or three years. We
should then know which was the real economic article to the comfort of the Service, and
the cost to the Taxpayers.

Every Bye-Election fought should be made aware of these facts.

Yours faithfully,

T. F. RICHARDS,
General President.are now hoping that such improvements will be brought about as will not only reduce the excessive mortality, but will completely master this dreadful malady which for so long has been the subject of enquiry.

VOLUNTARY SICKNESS BENEFIT SCHEME.—At one of the meetings of the above-named Council, Mr. F. J. Riley, of Desborough, brought forward a scheme to provide some additional income for those members who suffer from long continued illnesses, and who are therefore, financially in a very unfortunate position. The matter has been discussed at several meetings and ultimately a decision was reached that we should recommend the members of both organisations to adopt the same. In our opinion this is a matter which should receive your careful and favourable consideration, it being a scheme which is not only worth adopting but would considerably help to relieve the anxiety of those members who are unfortunately ill for a long time.

Mr. Riley's suggested scheme is as follows:—

Outline of a Sickness Provident Scheme which might be adopted by Voluntary Agreement in the Boot and Shoe Industry.

(1) BENEFITS restricted to operatives of 20 years of age and upwards who shall have been engaged in the industry at least three years before becoming entitled to benefit, and shall have been medically certified as having been unfit for employment for a period of six months (during this period the worker would enjoy full benefits from the N.H.I.A. and other Friendly Societies to which he may belong).

(2) When the age is reached entitling the worker to Old Age Pension benefits under this scheme to cease.

(3) Women marrying during the period of unemployment shall cease to be qualified for benefits under this scheme.

(4) CONTRIBUTIONS.—Men 3d. per month, Women 2d. per month. the employer to contribute an amount equal to the employee.

(5) A Central Joint Body of Trustees to be set up.

(6) ADMINISTRATION by district joint committees, which might be drawn from existing Boards of Arbitration.

(7) BENEFITS.—Married men 17s. 6d. per week, unmarried men 15s. per week, women 12s. per week.

(8) Estimated number of employees qualified over 20 years of age, 55,000 men and 20,000 women.

(9) CONTRIBUTIONS.—Men 3d. per month Women 2d. per month

Employers’ contribution

(10) BENEFITS.—300 Married Men 17s. 6d.
100 Unmarried Men 15s. od.
100 Women 12s. od.

Compilation of Piecework and Quantity Statements by Local Arbitration
Boards.
12. Local Boards of Arbitration will forthwith prepare new piecework
or quantity statements, or revise the existing statements on a basis to ensure
the average worker earning at least 25 per cent. over the minimum wage.
Pending the revision of the piecework or quantity statements 5 per cent.
shall be added to present prices in consideration of the reduction of hours
from 521 to 48 per week.
In the event of differences arising on a local Arbitration Board as to the
procedure to be adopted in giving effect to this clause, either side of the Board
may require the Standing Committee of the Conference to advise upon such
procedure, provided always that the piecework rates to be finally fixed shall
be determined in accordance with the rules of such Board.
The new statements shall secure for the average worker an income equal
to that which was secured to him by the piecework statements, the special
percentage or war wage of 1918, together with the war bonus as operative
on 31st December, 1918.
Local Boards of Arbitration operating in outlying districts accustomed
o manufacture goods of cheaper grades shall take this factor into consideration
when estimating the productive capacity of the average workman for the pur-
pose of compiling piecework or quantity statements.
Freedom of Contract with Operatives under 16.
13. The employers shall retain freedom of contract with all operatives
under 16 years of age.

The Italian Workers send their Thanks to the I.F.T.U.
In connection with the protest made by the workers' group at the Seventh International
Labour Conference on the question of the recognition of the mandate of the Fascist repre-
sentatives, the I.F.T.U. has received the following letter from the Italian Trade Union
Centre: "Dear Comrades. It is with very great pleasure that we have noted the proof of
true fraternal soliarity that the I.F.T.U. has just given us at Geneva. The testimony
of international solidarity in such stormy, difficult and unhappy times as those through
which the Italian working class is now passing gives us new strength and courage to con-
tinue our fight for our ideal."
Resolution of the International Federation of Trade Unions
against the Policy of Protection.
The Executive Committee of the I.F.T.U. passed the following resolution at its
meeting held on June 29th, 1925 :-
"The International Federation of Trade Unions of Amsterdam places on record that
since the war a protectionist commercial policy has been carried on to an ever-increasing
degree in all European countries, a policy which divides the peoples instead of uniting them.
Not only the newly-created post-war States, but also the old Free Trade countries are being
swept into this tendency. The International Federation of Trade Unions of Amsterdam
wishes to raise a warning voice against this policy; it herewith urges the affiliated National
Trade Union Centres to fight protectionism in their countries as being a burden to the
peoples and as tending to increase unemployment and the cost of living, and it calls upon
the centres to make an energetic stand for the general withdrawal of customs tariffs and
for the creation of an economic alliance the object of which shall be to secure the inter-
national distribution of raw materials, thereby making impossible unfair competition
between the nations arising out of sweating and dumping."
"HANDS OFF MEXICO!"
The American threat to Mexico, and the protest which it has evoked from the Mexican
and United States Labour Movements, seem to indicate that American capitalist interests
are preparing some move against the Labour Government of Mexico. Mexico, like China,
is a land blessed with largely undeveloped natural resources. It is a land literally flowing
with milk and honey; ninety per cent. of the known plants of the earth grow in its fertile
soil, its plains support herds of cattle, and horses and most important of all perhaps in
its later history-there is oil in abundance to be found. Capitalist interests are resentful
of any interference with uncontrolled exploitation, and are frankly suspicious of the
Labour Ministers of the present Government, who have already succeeded in placing
labour legislation on the Statute Book.
The capitalists seem to have carved out spheres of influence for themselves, by some
tacit and invisible agreement; Great Britain, for instance, seems to be entrusted with the
lead in the protection of capitalist interests in China, while U.S.A. dominates in Central
and South America. The fact that Great Britain is constructing a naval base at Singapore
may not be unrelated from the fact that she has not yet recognised Mexico, but trusts to
the Government of the U.S.A. to watch over her interests in that part of the world.
Although the Labour Party in Mexico is a comparatively recent growth, yet it has
succeeded in getting clauses inserted in the constitution which are in some cases more
favourable to the workers than those stipulated in the Washington Conventions. There
is a maximum 8-hour day, and 7 hours in the case of night work. Overtime must be paid
at double rates, and must not exceed three hours or continue for more than three days,
and is prohibited in the case of women and young people. There are clauses enforcing
payment of maximum wage, and requiring equal pay for equal work, irrespective of sex
or race.

LANDLORD FINED £250. 
Excess of Rent to be Refunded.
For charging excessive rents for furnished rooms, an Oldham landlord was yesterday
ordered
To pay $250 in fines,
£25 in costs, and
Refund money received for the past six months in excess of the
standard rent.
The case, brought under the Rent Restriction Act, was the second to be brought
the first having been heard some time ago in London.
Five summonses were issued against the landlord, Joseph Halsall, in respect of two
houses he owned in Cheapside. These he was said to have bought in 1921 for £340.
In the two houses described by the prosecution as "miserable hovels were 10
furnished rooms.
"Out of these," said prosecuting counsel, "which are condemned
property, defendant made £450 a year."
For one room, let, with certain furniture, to a man with four children, he was paid
145. a week.
There could not be more miserable furniture. Some of it was rotten.
Time to consider an appeal against the penalties was granted.
in 1923-where houses, or parts of houses, are let at a rent which includes payment for the
use of furniture, such rent shall not exceed by more than 25 per cent, the normal profit
[Under the Increase of Rent and Mortgage Interest (Restriction) Act of 1920-amended
which could be reasonably expected from such a letting in August, 1914. If it exceeds
the 1914 profit by more than 25 per cent., such excess is extortionate, is recoverable, and
makes the landlord liable to a fine of f100. Daily Chronicle, 25th August, 1925.
LABOUR'S NEW WEAPON.
Why Not Make it More Effective.
Mobilise! This, we are told, was the word flashed through Russia and Germany in
nation directly the issue was joined, and historians will marvel at the speed with which
1914 when war was decided upon. It was the word which rang out from every warring
war it is speed that counts, yet speed is neither automatic nor accidental. It is always the
the fighting forces of the world were lined up for the greatest of all world conflicts. In
result of forethought and preparation.
In July last we saw the unity of miners, railway workers and transport workers as
never before. We saw the General Council of the T.U.C. in action, backed by the whole
Trade Union Movement. We saw the Disputes Committee of the T.U.C. equipped with
remarkable powers, and we saw the workers ready to follow their lead.
Why ready? In the main they were ready because they had secured and perfected
a quick and reliable means of contact with each other-The Daily Herald-the recognised
organ of the whole of the Trade Union and Labour Movement. During the last fateful
fortnight of July, 1925, it was urging unity, steadiness and solidarity. It was doing more.
Day by day it revealed the step-by-step strategy of the Trade Union leaders. It kept
every worker, who so desired, informed of the essential facts. Letters, duplicated circulars,
even telegrams, are all of them either more difficult, more expensive or more indirect forms
of communication than the daily newspaper, filtering as it does into every city and hamlet
in the land. By means of the Daily Herald, that hackneyed word "Solidarity" was
transformed from rhetoric to reality.
The Daily Herald's appeal is to justice, intelligence, and that spirit of progress which
will bring in a saner and happier social order. In the main it is the rank and file trade
unionist who can decide whether that appeal shall be heard and which voice the community
shall heed-reason or reaction. The prime business of the moment is to stress the age-long
workers' struggle, and to convince trade unionists that the Daily Herald, their own paper,
gives better industrial and social news than the capitalist papers. When the worker has
that conviction and acts upon it, the building of the new social order will begin.
21st August, 1925.
E. W.

(I.) "That this Congress calls upon the Government immediately to introduce

(II.) "The right to benefit to be established if the applicant is unemployed and

international unity, and urges the incoming General Council to do everything in their
power towards securing world-wide unity of the Trade Union Movement through an
all inclusive International Federation of Trade Unions.""
RUSSIAN TRADING RELATIONS.
"That this Trades Union Congress expresses its concern and profound disappoint-
ment that the efforts of the Russian Textile Trading Delegation have failed to place
anything more than a small portion of the five million pounds orders for textile
machinery with British manufacturing firms.
"This failure is clearly due to the refusal of the British banking interests to pro-
vide credit facilities, and, in view of the vast amount of unemployment now existing,
especially in the engineering, metal and cognate trades, this Congress regards with
grave apprehension the possibility of the Russian market for machinery and other
commodities being lost to Great Britain.
"This Congress therefore urges the British Government to use its influence with
all commercial and financial interests to secure these orders for British firms, and to
extend to trade with Russia the operation of the Trade Facilities Acts in the same
manner as these Acts have been applied to other countries, believing such action to
be in the best interests of British trade, and that the national burden of unemploy-
ment can be materially alleviated by the fullest use of the economic resources of the
community; further, that the General Council be instructed to take all necessary
steps for the purpose of securing the object of this resolution."
TUBERCULAR TREATMENT.
"That the General Council be instructed to consider the practicability of estab-
lishing a sanatorium in Switzerland for the sun treatment of tuberculosis, a special
fund to be organised for the purpose and treatment to be available only to members
of subscribing societies."
LORD WEIR AND THE FAIR WAGES CLAUSE.
That in view of the attacks made by the firm headed by Lord Weir on the
rates of wages and working conditions prevailing in the building industry, in its
attempts to secure the erection of steel houses by non-building union labour, this
Congress calls upon the Trade Unions and the working-class movement generally to
use their utmost endeavours to repel these attacks; that it regards these attacks as
a very serious attempt to undermine the whole operation of the Fair Wages Clauses

THE INDUSTRIAL COURTS AСТ, 1919.
INDUSTRIAL COURT (PROCEDURE) RULES, 1920.
DATED 15TH DAY OF MARCH, 1920: MADE BY THE MINISTER OF LABOUR BY VIRTUE OF
POWERS VESTED IN HIM BY THE INDUSTRIAL COURTS ACT, 1919, AND OF ALL OTHER
POWERS ENABLING HIM IN THAT BEHALF.
1. In these Rules:-
The expression
"Act" means the Industrial Courts Act, 1919; and
The expression "Minister" means the Minister of Labour; and
The expression
"Court"
means the Industrial Court established by the Act
and includes, unless the contrary intention appears, any division thereof and any
single member of the Court to whom a matter may be referred for determination; and
The expression "President" means the President of the Industrial Court; and
The expression "Division" means any group of members of the Court con-
stituted as the President may direct to hear and determine any matter referred to
the Court.
2. The Court may sit in two or more divisions.
3. Any matter referred to the Court for settlement may at the discretion of the
President be heard and determined by a single member of the Court.
4. The Court may, at the discretion of the President, in any matter in which it appears
expedient to do so call in the aid of one or more assessors and may settle the matter wholly
or partially with the assistance of such assessor or assessors.
5. The Court may with the consent of the parties act notwithstanding any vacancy
in their number and no act, proceeding, or determination of the Court shall be called in
question or invalidated by reason of any such vacancy, provided such consent has first
been obtained.
6. The Court may correct in any award any clerical mistake or error arising from
an accidental slip or omission.
7. If any question arises as to the interpretation of any award of the Court, the
Minister or any party to the award may apply for a decision on such question and the
Court shall decide the matter after hearing the parties, or without such hearing, provided
the consent of the parties has first been obtained. The decision of the Court shall be
notified to the parties and shall be final in the same manner as the decision in an original
award.
8. Persons may appear by counsel or solicitor on proceedings before the Court with
the permission of the Court.
9. Subject to these rules the Court may regulate their own procedure as they think fit.
ro. These Rules may be cited as the Industrial Court (Procedure) Rules, 1920.
Given under the official seal of the Minister of Labour this Fifteenth day of March,
One Thousand Nine Hundred and Twenty.
SEAL.
-The Labour Gazette, April, 1920.
(Signed) D. J. SHACKLETON,
Secretary.

9.  The political atmosphere is somewhat electrical at the present time and it would seem there is a great opportunity for Labour in the near future, if matters are dealt with in a wise manner. The many questions that have become of first rate importance all tend to show the necessity of Labour being adequately represented in the House of Commons, and thereby enabling it to take its proper share in the work of framing such laws as will bring relief to the community from the many burdens now having to be carried. Our representative in the House of Commons, Mr. W. R. Smith, M.P., J.P., has taken up a number of questions and has at all times shewn a great interest in the matters that come before the House. In our judgment we ought, as a trade, to have more representation in the House, and we hope when the opportunity presents itself that advantage will be taken of the same to ensure the return of more of our colleagues to such assembly.

Guarantee under Terms of Settlement.
16.-Subject to the original provisions of resolution 9 of the Terms of
Settlement of 19th April, 1895, and the provisions of the Trust Deed of 8th
March, 1898, the monetary guarantee of the Federation shall not be amenable
in any case of alleged non-compliance by an employer with agreements in
relation to wages, hours of labour, or conditions of employment, until after
the case has been dealt with in accordance with the procedure prescribed by
rule 14 of the Local Arbitration Board for dealing with disputes, and a decision
of the Board, its Arbitrators, or Umpire, has been given; but in no case shall
the retrospective payment of wages be ordered beyond the date of claim in
writing sent either to the employer or to the Secretary of the Board, which-
ever is earlier.

HALESWORTH. We held our Annual Meeting on Friday, September
24th, and we are glad to say that we had more members than was expected
from our point of view. It would make a great deal of difference to this Branch
if the members would turn up at every meeting the same, it would save a lot of
writing and make things go smoother; we only have one meeting a month,
and we don't think it fair to the Branch officers if four members out of twenty
turn up; we should like members to bear this in mind, both for the present
and also the future. Our next meeting will be held the first Friday in November.
so will you all make a special effort to attend ? Trade is very slow and a lot
of short time has been contended with, but still there are better days in store
for us, we went through our first year with a struggle through members
being discharged from their situations.
HIGHAM AND RUSHDEN. There are signs of some improvement in
trade, some factories that were on short time are now on full, and there is a
considerable falling off during the first week of October of out-of-works; as
against this, Messrs. Green's (Leicester) Branch Factory at Finedon has closed
down temporarily; also Messrs. Brown's (Leicester) Branch Factory at Irchester
has closed down for the month.

of our President's intention of asking for a 50 per cent. advance at the next

that amount. We noticed also that the nominations of the District Committees
have gone through, and earnestly hope they will soon get to business. Our
Organising Campaign has ended favourably, considering the bad times and a

PORTSMOUTH.- We were glad to welcome to our September Branch
Meeting Mr. T. F. Richards, Mr. E. L. Poulton, and Mr. W. R. Smith, M.P.
We must record the fact that it was a matter of great regret that more of the
members did not put in an appearance. We have heard some "
suggesting the leaders of the Union left us alone, and when we are favoured
with having them all here three-quarters of the members stay away! It is
little encouragement to the Branch officers, and was not inspiring to our visitors.
Those who were present had a most enlightening time, to listen to men who
are in the forefront of the struggle and who can feel the tug of the leash, the
men who are most conscious of the needs of the workers and are endeavouring
to set the pace. We only hope the words spoken may be fresh in the memories
of our comrades for many a long day. The officers who are doing their best to
keep the flag flying under trying circumstances were greatly encouraged to hear
that small Branches do find a place in the deliberations of the Executive Council.
We regret to say unemployment still abounds in the town-4,000 men with no
work to do. This is reflected in a stagnant state of trade, and other men are
thus standing off. Various committees formulate schemes for improving things,
but the only hope is for the workers to take charge and run their own house-
hold. We are just about holding our own here; we keep pretty steady as
regards financial members, and this month we are glad to register five new
members. We are glad to see Brighton is going ahead. How about Bourne-
mouth and Southampton? Now we have started in the South do not let
us go back but keep plodding on, and building as we go; if we do not see the
perfect, others shall.
REDRUTH.- At the time of writing this report things are going along
smoothly with us and trade remains fair. We are watching with keen interest
the progress of the second ballot of the miners and hope that a way out of the
difficulty will be found without having to resort to a strike. The prospects for
the coming winter seem black enough, with the industrial outlook such as
it is, and we are of the opinion that a strike at this juncture would produce
utter stagnation, and that the deprivations of the war period would be magnified
a hundredfold. We hope the industrial unrest throughout the country will soon
cease and that employers and employed will work hand in hand and drop their
of the strike and the lock-out. Our members will be looking forward for results
good of the country,

PARLIAMENT AND THE TRADE.
Upper Leather Imports.
Sir Clement Kinloch-Cooke (Devonport) on Thursday last asked the President of the
Board of Trade if his attention had been directed to the partial cessation of upper leather
from the Continent at prices which, in many cases on account of the exchange, were below
production in the United Kingdom, caused by the importation of finished upper leathers
the cost of the raw material in this country, and what steps he proposed to take in order
that trained labour and factory plants available in this country might resume employment.
Sir Philip Lloyd-Greame (Parliamentary Secretary to the Board of Trade): My
attention has been drawn to the imports of upper leather from the Continent, and I under-
stand that some of this leather has been offered at low prices, but the volume of such
to any great extent, responsible for the unsatisfactory state of employment in the leather
imports is considerably smaller than in pre-war years, and I doubt whether they are.
trade of this country. The situation is, however, being carefully watched.
Russia's Need of Boots.
Mr. Jesson (Walthamstow, W.) asked the Prime Minister if his attention had been
called to statements being made that the present unemployment in the boot, engineering
and other industries in this country was due to over-production; whether, having regard
goods and was now in urgent need of boots for practically the whole of her population
and motor-lorries and locomotives for the re-establishment of her transport system while
to the fact that before the war Russia was an important customer for our manufactured
from Russia, there were any other reasons except mistrust of the present Russian goven-
we were in equal need of the wheat, timber and many other things we formerly imported
ment for the delay in the re-establishment of trading relations with that country
Mr. Lloyd George replied that he had seen statements attributing the present unem-
ployment in certain industries to over-production. With regard to the latter part of the
question, he had nothing to add to previous statements.
Disabled Men and the Trade.
Mr. Waterson (Kettering) asked the Minister of Labour how many ex-Service men
had been trained for the boot and shoe industry, the number of such now unemployed,
and how many were now in course of training ?
Dr. Macnamara: The number of disabled ex-Service men trained for the boot and
shoe trade is approximately 2,800. It is not possible to say how many of these are now
unemployed. The number under training at the moment is 1,732. These figures relate
almost entirely to the hand-sewn trade, as the number of men trained in the machine
trade is negligible.
Unemployment in Leeds.
Mr. Myers (Spen Valley) on Monday asked the Minister of Labour if his attention
had been directed to the state of under-employment existing in the upper leather pro
the causes of such under-employment with a view to remedying the position and to provide
ducing industries in Leeds and elsewhere, and if he will order an inquiry to be made into
fuller employment for the trained men involved ?

but to refuse payment from the Central fund in all future cases.

SCOTTISH TRADES' UNION CONGRESS.
DELEGATES' REPORT.
FELLOW MEMBERS,
The twenty-third annual meeting of the Scottish Trades Union Congress took
place in Dunfermline, on the 28th of April and subsequent days, under the presidency
of Mr. Wm. Shaw, Glasgow Trades Council. The delegates received a very hearty welcome
who trusted the result of the week's deliberations would be beneficial to labour.
from the Provost of the City, and from Mr. John Beck, Dunfermline Trades Council,
economical, which had taken place since Congress had met twelve months ago, not the
The Chairman in his address to Congress, surveyed the many incidents, political and
signed. In conclusion he stated the number of delegates was 240, representing 550,000
least of these being the many political blunders which had arisen since the Armistice was
trade unionists, the largest on record for Scotland.
After the amendments to Standing Orders had been gone through, Congress settled
down to the Agenda. Rent restriction (Amended) Act 1919: "That the restriction
of evicting tenants which is removed in July, 1920, be immediately restored," first con-
sidered and agreed to.
Unemployment Insurance: "That the Bill presently before Parliament dealing with
only," agreed to without dissent.
Unemployment Insurance, should be so altered that benefit be paid through Trade Unions
Housing: "That the Government institute an enquiry as to prices of building material,
to encourage guilds working in conjunction with local authorities," was appoved of.
"That 44 hours constitute a working week," unanimously agreed to
A very able speech by Councillor E. Shinwell in support of "the establishment of a
Co-operative Commonwealth as the only solution of the evils of the present situation,"
was adopted by Congress.
A resolution " to raise the Old Age Pensions to 20s. per week, payable at the age of
60," found no dissent.
That energetic steps be taken to bring "Industrial Insurance" under State manage-
ment, which would bring about very considerable economies, was agreed to.
The result of the vote on Prohibition versus State Purchase, by 110 to 74, caused
much jubilation among prohibitionists. Nevertheless, it is far from approaching the
needful two-thirds majority.
Two Irish delegates addressed the Congress on the political situation existing to-day
in Ireland, advocating the principle of "Self-Determination," which the majority of the
delegates supported, both delegates being thanked by Congress for their addresses.
Mr. Stuart Bunning gave Congress a very interesting address on the League of Nations.
"They had been looking forward confidently to the time when war would be no more,
but their experience since the Armistice had been discouraging. The only part that had
done constructive work was that associated with labour. Already through the labour
section they had been able to secure a considerable reduction of the hours of labour in
Japan." The speaker received the thanks of Congress for his address.
Aberdeen was selected as the next meeting place by the delegates.
The session of Congress concluded by singing "Auld Lang Syne," the Prohibitionists
joining in lustily: "Surely you'll be your pint stoup."
Yours fraternally,
MATTHIAS GAMBLE,
ALEXANDER BROWN,
Delegates.
THE ECONOMIC CONSEQUENCES OF THE PEACE.
As a result of requests that the time allowed for orders of the special cheap edition of
J. M. Keynes' book should be extended, it has been decided to fix the closing date for
June 15th. The offer is limited to members of Trade Unions, Socialist and Labour bodies,
Co-operative organisations, &c. The price for a single copy is 2s. 6d., and an extra copy
is given on every dozen ordered.
No orders will be accepted after June 15th.

FOURTH SCHEDULE.

Rules as to Payment and Recovery of Contributions Paid by Employers on
behalf of Employed Persons.

(1) A weekly contribution shall be payable for each calendar week during the whole
or any part of which an employed person has been employed by an employer.
Provided that where one weekly contribution has been paid in respect of an employed
person in any week, no further contribution shall be payable in respect of him in the same
week, and that where no remuneration has been received and no services rendered by an
employed person during any such week, the employer shall not be liable to pay any con-
tribution either on his own behalf, or on behalf of the employed person in respect of that
week.

(2) The employer shall, except as hereinafter provided, be entitled to recover from
the employed person the amount of any contributions paid by him on behalf of the em-
ployed person.

(3) Except where the employed person does not receive any wages or other pecuniary
remuneration from the employer, the amounts so recoverable shall, notwithstanding the
provisions of any Act or any contract to the contrary, be recoverable by means of deduc-
tions from the wages of the employed person or from any other remuneration due from the
employer to the employed person and not otherwise, but no such deductions may be made
from any wages or remuneration other than such as are paid in respect of the period or
part of the period in respect of which the contribution is payable, or in excess of the sum
which represents the amount of the contributions for the period (if such period is longer
than a week) in respect of which the wages or other remuneration are paid.

(4) Where a contribution paid by the employer on behalf of an employed person is
recoverable from such person, but is not recoverable by means of deduction as aforesaid,
it shall (without prejudice to any other means of recovery) be recoverable summarily as
a civil debt, but no such contribution shall be recoverable unless proceedings for the pur-
pose are instituted within three months from the date when the contribution was payable.

(5) Where the employed person is employed by more than one person in any calendar
week, the first person employing him in that week or such other employer or employers as
may be prescribed, shall be deemed to be the employer for the purposes of the provisions
of this Scheme relating to the payments of contributions and of this Schedule.

(6) Regulations made under the Act providing that in any cases or classes of cases
where employed persons work under the general control and management of some person
other than their immediate employer, such as the owner, agent, or manager of a mine or
quarry or the occupier of a factory or workshop, such person shall, for the purposes of the
provisions of the Act relating to payment of contributions and of this Schedule, be treated
as the employer and may provide for allowing him to deduct the amount of any contribu-
tions (other than employers’ contributions) which he may become liable to pay from any
sums payable by him to the immediate employer, and for enabling the immediate em-
ployer to recover from the employed person the like sums and in the like manner as if he
were liable to pay the contributions, shall have like effect as if made under this Scheme.

(7) Where the employed person is not paid wages or other money payments by his
employer or any other person, the employer shall be liable to pay the contributions pay-
able both by himself and the employed person and shall not be entitled to recover any part
thereof from the employed person.

(8) Notwithstanding any contract to the contrary, the employer shall not be en-
titled to deduct from the wages of, or otherwise to recover from the employed person,
the employer’s contribution.

(9) Any sum deducted by an employer from wages or other remuneration under this
Schedule shall be deemed to have been entrusted to him for the purpose of paying the con-
tribution in respect of which it was deducted.

(10) For the purposes of this Schedule, the expression “calendar week” means the
period from midnight on one Sunday to midnight on the following Sunday.

WITHOUT NOTICE.
Short Time Prevents Full Penalty at Wellingborough.
Police Court to-day (Friday) by the Wellingborough Upper and Legging Co. for the re-
W. Abbott, legging blocker, Wellingborough, was summoned at the Wellingborough
For the applicants, Mr. J. C. Parker said that the claim was for 70s. in lieu of notice.
In consequence of defendant's sudden decision not to work any longer for the Company,
covery of a civil debt.
the works were seriously disorganised.
Mr. Arthur Kingston, a director of the Wellingborough Upper and Legging Co., said
20th, meeting witness in the street, defendant said he was not coming to work any more,
the defendant was employed in the firm's legging-blocking department. On September
adding, "When I can work full time I will give a full week's notice." In consequence
weeks previous to the date mentioned defendant had been given full-time employment.
In order to keep the workpeople employed the firm had been manufacturing for stock.
of his action, the output of the works was restricted. Witness stated that for several
Defendant (sworn) said he had been on short time, more or less, since Easter. He
did not want to shift if there was any chance of improvement in the trade, but had a bit
of luck, and unexpectedly dropped across a job which he had to take at once or leave.
As he was not getting a proper living, he decided to accept the job.
The Chairman announced that the plaintiffs were not entitled to the full amount
claimed, since their works were not on full time. There would be an order for half the
amount of the claim, with costs.
Mr. J. C. Parker said that his clients had made the application, not so much because
of the money, but because the habit of leaving without notice was becoming rather pre-
valent. - Kettering Telegraph, October 8th, 1920.
RETAIL PRICES AND RENTS.
For food alone the
The average level of retail prices (including those of food, clothing, fuel and light,
&c., as well as rents) at 1st October, as indicated by the statistics prepared by the Ministry
of Labour, was about 164 per cent. above the level of July, 1914.
average increase was 170 per cent.-The Labour Gazette, October, 1920.
CHANGES IN RATES OF WAGES AND HOURS OF LABOUR.
In the industries for which statistics are available changes in rates of wages reported
to the Department as having been arranged to come into operation in September, resulted
in a total increase of £49,100 in the weekly wages of 219,000 workpeople. Changes in
hours of labour affected about 1,500 workpeople, whose working week was reduced by an
average of nearly three hours.The Labour Gazette, October, 1920.

VOTING

For the various delegations must take place on the December Monthly Meeting
nights and the results posted to reach the General Secretary by the 15th
December, otherwise the same will be disqualified. Voting will also be taken
upon the following question :-

Or, "Are you in favour of the present system of allowing both Piecework and
Daywork methods, regulated by quantity statements, to continue under our
Agreements with the Employers, with no limit of output or wages?"
Or, "Are you in favour of a Daywork system of working with quantity state-
ments, with a limited output and wages?"

FIRST ANNUAL CONFERENCE.

Under the new Constitution of the Union the first Annual Conference
will be held in LONDON on May 16th, 17th, and 18th, 1921, and if any Branch
has questions of procedure or principle they wish to raise, the same should
be sent along AT ONCE to the General Secretary.

UNEMPLOYMENT ACT, 1920.

Having regard to the extra demand for last month's Reports, it is evident
our suggested unemployment scheme caused some discussion. During the
month further meetings have been held to help forward our scheme, and an
actuarial estimate and opinion taken in relation to the financial side of the
question.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION ACTS.

Since our last issue a member of the LIVERPOOL Branch has secured a
lump sum of £125 (against £50 originally offered) and the solicitor's costs, in
addition to the weekly compensation.

J.H., of KETTERING, has, through the efforts of Mr. Evan Barlow, the
Union solicitor, secured the sum of £60, in addition to the weekly payments
which are due under the Workmen's Compensation Acts.

We would once again remind the members that lump sums are NOT
obtainable by law, but only by arrangement. The above successful claims
shew the advantage of the member having the backing of a large trade union
such as our own, to enable him to secure justice.

MORE UNION J.P.'s.

During the month cur Branch Secretaries at KINGSWOOD and STREET,
Mr. A. G. Randall and Mr. J. H. Hooper, have been raised to the Magisterial
Bench for their respective districts. We beg to tender our hearty congratu-
lations, and trust they may enjoy good health, and thus be enabled to carry
on the work they have undertaken on behalf of the workers.

Returns from firms employing about 47,500 operatives in the week ended 26th Septem-
ber, 1925, showed that nearly 45 per cent. of these operatives worked short time in that
week, to the extent of nearly 12 hours each, on an average.

Employment remained quiet in London, but showed a slight improvement in some
districts. At Leicester it was again very slack, with a very considerable proportion of
the operatives working short time; at Northampton there was a decline, and very few
firms were on full time. At both centres employment was somewhat worse than a year
ago. In the Higham and Rushden area there was very little change, and employment
was only quiet; at Raunds it continued good on Government contract work; but at
Wellingborough, in spite of a slight upward movement, it was still poor with most firms,

380
NO ROOM TO LIVE.
SLUM PERILJADI
Grave Disclosures in a Rich City.
It is for the national conscience and local patriotism, working side by side, to make
great practical response in the next few months to the King's words spoken twelve months
ago.
If this country is to be the country we desire to see it become, a great offensive must
must be delivered is the unhealthy, ugly, overcrowded house in the mean street,
be undertaken against disease and crime, and the first point at which the attack
It is not merely houses" that are needed. The new houses must be also "homes"
leisure, brightness and peace which we usually associate with the word "home"
Can we not aim at securing to the working classes in their homes the comfort
which we all of us know so well.
CITY OF CONTRASTS.
How the Poor Fare Amid the Wealth of Bradford.
Bradford has the advantage of not being typical. It presents you with most of the
social problems of the hour through a magnifying glass. The problems are the same in
other cities, but you might possibly overlook them there because they are not stained
with Bradford's brilliant dyes.
This is the city of the 3,000 per cent. wool profits-the city where men can't help
growing rich if they once get a foot inside the ring. It is also the city where more than
one-half of the workers live in back-to-back cottages, which number altogether more than
33,000; where 32,000 people have less than half a room apiece in which to get born, eat,
sleep, and die in which the death rate exceeds the birth rate by 5 per thousand, and the
infant mortality is among the very highest in the kingdom.
Bradford's overmastering problem, however, is the bad old property. The city dates
from the Stone Age in a double sense. Practically all the working-class houses were built
refuse to fall down, and they were built back to back to save cost. And they date also
of local stone, which has the doubtful advantage in this connection of durability; they
from the Stone one Age of early Victorian economic theory and practice.
squalid, airless, lightless "homes," packed close as biscuit boxes on a shelf, round the
The result is appalling-with a dash of the ludicrous when you contemplate these
towering factories into which pours a perfect spate of wealth.
I spent a whole morning among the back-to-backs (which form over half of working-
class Bradford, remember), and found it a sickening experience. The other day a returned
soldier who was living as a lodger in a cellar with his wife and two children applied to
Dr. Buchan, the Medical Officer of Health, for permission to move into one of these wretched
houses condemned before the war and now falling into ruins. How could the doctor
forbid it? He looked the other way, and the family moved in. He is still looking the
other way, in that and many other cases. There is no alternative. The returned soldiers
have no room to live.
I saw a number of these condemned houses into which the population is slowly oozing
back. Cleared cellars are being re-inhabited. Hopelessly insanitary hovels, vacant for
years, are getting the habit of blossoming out again into curtains and make-shift furniture.
Families will endure a lot in the way of dirt and damp if only they can secure a measure
of decent privacy. Once installed, who dare turn them out? Who can, even if he dare,
try? So Bradford-booming Bradford! is reinhabiting its ruins.
for human habitation still swarming with life-two, three, four, even six men, women and
children to the room. Frederick Street has its rows of one-room cellar dwellings: Regent
In the Bromefield area I found scores of houses condemned six years ago as unfit
Street, in the White Abbey area, its patches of decayed and gloomy hovels, standing
of their own accord: Wapping, its double tiers of back-to-back closets massed horribly
among piles of bricks, where houses, having had all their timber stolen, have fallen down
at street-ends like huge, foul beehives. All through central Bradford that urban abomina-
sanitary convenience, and one is nearly always shared by two houses. Thus civilisation
tion the privy midden jostles human dwellings. There are 13,600 houses with no other
becomes worse than savagery.

WALTER R. SMITH.

BUILDING COSTS.

MR. W. R. SMITH, M.P., AND PROFITEERING.

In the House of Commons, on Wednesday, Mr. W. R. Smith, in the debate on the
Board of Works supplementary estimates, said he was surprised to hear that so many
members were very angry because houses were being built. (No, no, and laughter.)
How any other explanation could be given for the condemnation of the Board of Works
he was at a loss to know. He did not remember when he had heard so much exaggeration
and misrepresentation as he had heard in that discussion that night. One would have
thought from some of the speeches that the Board of Works was a terrible body, going
about compelling local authorities to accept its instructions with regard to the building
of houses and that local bodies had no judgment as to what was necessary for the localities
they represented. He was anxious to hear some of the criticisms of members on the other
side who had talked so much about what the Government intended to do

For the Ex-Soldiers.

Was not the development of building schemes a public duty, in order that these men
should have proper houses to live in? Local authorities were anxious to develop such
schemes in conjunction with the Ministry of health, but some had found a difficulty in
getting them erected, whilst the estimates were of such a character as to warrant them
finding some other methods of bringing about the construction of the houses. They
knew also that there had been profiteering going on in this business, and that the economic
rent of the houses would be higher if they were built by private contract than by the
method adopted by the Board of Works. (Hear, hear.) The real ground of the objection
expressed by members was because it meant the breaking down of vested interests. (Hear,
hear.) Figures which had been submitted showed that the work done by this department
had given better service and

Better Results

than had been accomplished by building contractors, and the scheme of the Board of
Works would break down the ring which had surrounded the building of houses and had
run up the cost of construction to its present level. As long as there was an assurance
that the full charge that fell on the Department was properly credited under the scheme
activities of the Board of Works in this direction? In his own locality there was an
instance of tenders for the building of houses at £1,005, there being a clause in the contract
labour during the course of the construction of the houses. Since that estimate was given
these houses had been built for £900 each, or £105 less than the contractors' estimate,
although there had been in the meantime two advances in wages.

The applicant's Association appealed to the Umpire against the Court's disallowance
were not parties to the Agreement relating to the rate of pay for work on arterial roads
whose rate of pav as a member of their organisation was IS. 4 d. per hour. The Association
A1 Association representative subsequently attended an oral hearing of the case before

civil engineering work in the district in question. It was explained by the Ministry of
Engineering Conciliation Board. The roads would pass through districts some of which
decided to pay a uniform rate, based on grade 2 of the grading of districts by the Civil
would be grade 3 or 4 and parts in grade 1, but a uniform rate was better. The men had
their fares paid to the jobs, and for London men this was worth more than another penny

Decision." On the facts before me my decision is that the claim for benefit should be
disallowed.
"I am of opinion that the applicant fails to show that on the 7th of February last he
was unable to obtain suitable employment.
" He is a navvy and was offered employment on an arterial road at Bexley at a rate
of wages of Is. Id. per hour with payment of railway fares daily from and to London.
"I have no doubt that the employment was suitable for the applicant unless I am
bound by paragraph (c) to the Proviso to Section 7 (1) of the Act of 1920 to hold that it
is unsuitable on the ground that it was at a rate of wages lower than that generally observed
in the district by agreement between Associations of Employers and of Employees or,
failing such agreement, than that generally recognised in the district by good employers.
"The rate offered was not an agreed rate for the district in question. It was fixed by
the Ministry of Transport and the London County Council, but not by agreement with any
Association of Employees. It is a rate which by agreement is payable for work of the kind
in certain districts, but there was no agreement that that or any other rate should be
applicable to the district in question. I cannot, therefore, say that it was lower than the
rate generally observed in the district by agreement between employers and employees.
Nor can I say that the rate was lower than that generally recognised by good employers in
the district. The only other employer doing work of the kind in the district appears to be
the Urban District Council, and I am informed that they pay a lower rate.
"One firm of contractors is said to have paid a somewhat higher rate recently, but
that is not sufficient to show that a higher rate is generally recognised by good employers
in the district."
