MINISTRY OF FOOD.
Extracts from Speech in House of Commons on May 6th, 1920,
by the Minister of Food, Mr. McCurdy.
The MINISTER of FOOD (Mr. McCurdy): Last year, not only the Members of
this House, but the British people generally, were in an extremely optimistic frame of
mind as to the rate at which the wastage of war would be made good, and as to the rapidity
with which world prices, not only of food, but of all essential commodities, might be expected
to fall. At that time the Ministry of Food was engaged, and, indeed, has been engaged
ever since, in a persistent endeavour to hasten, as far as might be, the time when it would
restraints upon trade which have been endured with patriotism and cheerfulness during
be possible, without injustice to the consumer or the public, to remove all those irritating
recognised, however, what is sometimes overlooked, namely, that it was not exactly the
the stress of war. We recognised that the Ministry of Food was a war Ministry. We also
Germans against whom we were waging war, but against high prices, and the exploitation
of the difficult economic situation to the advantage either of the speculator or the profiteer.
I regret that it has not yet been found possible to declare an armistice against those enemies
of the public welfare.
Last year we were engaged for a considerable time in attempts to de-control commodi-
ties, which attempts, for a time, appeared to be fully justified by the course of food prices
throughout the world. It was, I believe, about this time last year that the Prime Minister
predicted that the then continuing fall in food prices ought presently to result in a reduction
was, in fact, verified almost to the letter. For the first six months of last year prices were
in the expenses of the ordinary household by something like 4s. a week. That prediction
present moment the contrary process has been in operation. There has been, as every
falling. About August of last year that process ceased, and from that itme up to the
of all essential commodities. Of the bona-fide attempts on the part of the Ministry of
rise, not only in food prices but in the prices
householdr knows, a steady but continued
Food to remove control where it seemed to them possible, ever since the Armistice, there
really can be no doubt in the mind of anyone who will take the trouble to follow the course
of the policy pursued by my predecessor in office. I am bound to say that some of
our attempts at de-control were followed by results which were by no means
encouraging. I need only refer to an instance or two. During last summer, urged
by all our expert advisers, we thought the time had come when we could safely
remove the control of home-killed veal; but within a week or two the price of veal,
which under control had been 1s. 8d., had risen to 4s. and 5s. in different parts of
the country. In a few days we were receiving communications from distressed
representatives of agriculture, complaining that that premature de-control had
led to such a slaughter of immature calves that, unless the policy were reversed,
there would be grave danger to the milk supplies of this country in four or five
years' time; and my predecessor found it necessary to re-impose control. We
have been criticised for first de-controlling and then re-controlling bacon. I know of no
comomodity in which the trade interests, acting most legitimately and as they were fully
entitled to do, have been more active in placing before the public their view of what should
be the policy of the ministry of Food with regard to that commodity.
Mr. G. THORNE : May I ask the right hon. gentleman whether that de-control
did not relate only to the retail trade and not to the wholesale trade ?
Mr. McCurdy: No, it was a complete de-control. It was carried into effect by
my predecessor in office, acting on the competent advice of trade interests that the result
would be freedom of competition and the restoration of normal trade conditions,
and a consequent fall in price. In the five months of decontrol the actual result
was that wholesale prices were raised from 15 to 25 per cent., with no justification
in the state of supplies or in the exchange position. Forward quotations were
higher still. Quay congestion rapidly increased and valuable stores were clogged with
landed from the ships in bad condition. The resumption of control eliminated speculation
privately imported during the months of decontrol, August and September, was actually
and at once brought about a break in hog prices and bacon prices in America. Since the
Ministry have regulated shipments, quay congestion and wastage have been eliminated, as
I know from personal observation of the great docks during the last few weeks, but I am
sorry that the resumption of control was not, of course, sufficient at once to change bad
bacon into good bacon or immediately to liquidate the accumulation of food which was
time, I see suggestions as to the wastage of bacon that has taken place. It generally refers to
causing loss and inconvenience to the people and traders of this country. From time to
it refers to supplies secured by the Ministry of Food, and, as a matter of fact, the wastage has
bacon which has been privately imported, though it is very often put forward as though

this month. Some only working three days a week, as well as those that
have been discharged, are hard pressed and want their shillings to buy the
necessities of life; also some have drifted into other occupations and cannot
afford to keep two unions going.

THE TRADES UNION CONGRESS GENERAL COUNCIL.
TRADES COUNCILS
As you will probably be aware, the General Council is attempting to co-ordinate the
gratifying success. It is hoped by this method that an essential link in our chain of organi-
Trades Councils in the country, and there is evidence that their attempts are meeting with
sation will be forged, and the branches of your Union, if affiliated to the local Trades
Council, will be kept acquainted with the general trend of national affairs of an industrial
character. Associated with local branches of other Trade Unions they will be affiliated
to a body which, if representative of all industries in the area, should prove a valuable asset.
Trades Councils have in the past laboured under two great difficulties. In the first
place there has been no effective co-ordination of their work by a suitable headquarters,
and, secondly, the local Trade Union branches have refrained from affiliating, thus im-
poverishing the local Council, both from the point of view of scope and of finance. The
first difficulty is being dealt with by the General Council; the second is a matter upon which
they desire the assistance of your Organisation.
Under the circumstances the General Council request that your Executive will use
their influence to induce all your branches to affiliate to the local Trades Council on their
full membership. The work will, under the new conditions, tend to grow more interesting
and important, and, by the mutual assistance of the various Trade Unions affiliated to
the local Council, a more effective and stable organisation may be accomplished.
I am, yours faithfully,
"WEIR" HOUSES.

PARLIAMENTARY AGENT'S REPORT.
Since my last report Parliament has reassembled and under conditions which could
only be described as truly grave. A national coal stoppage had already begun, and there
was a possibility of a wide extension of the strike if an early settlement were not arrived
at. The problem of unemployment had again assumed serious proportions, and its
harassing effects were being felt in an acute form by tens of thousands of workless citizens.
In Ireland, the land of bitterness and estrangement, the Government's policy of rigorous
coercion had been intensified, and was finding expression in what are politely called
reaisals." Not since the dark days of August, 1914, has Parliament been confronted
h lowering and menacing possibilities, and responsible and rational people were
oking to it to rise above party advantage and personal rivalries in order to lead the nation

GENERAL PRESIDENT'S REPORT.

FELLOW MEMBERS,

Terms of Settlement.—The most significant thing done this month is the division in your Executive as to the policy of the Union for drawing up the New Terms of Settlement. Whilst we can generally be relied upon in asking for more than we have yet got, we shall certainly not be bashful in making a claim based on the standard of remuneration. It will be interesting to hear the opinion as to whether they would advise the Union's Executive to change its policy. Members will be entitled to particular notice: we have always been, what is described as a Piece-work Trade from time immemorial; Rightly or wrongly it has given us no greater freedom, and up to the present with the exception of some few years during the war when piece-work was being introduced into our trade, piece-work or payment by results was the order of the day. Day Labour was often introduced with all its bickerings and complainers on the part of the people in authority, and also the men themselves who were always ready to pull each other down, bullying ordered by foremen who always grumble, and grumble if a man turns his head for an instant even to attend to Nature’s requirements. The harassing of this system followed him everywhere, and several improvements were made up to the agreement, and the Leicester Finishers’ Statements, policy and conditions were included in the 1914 Agreement.

In 1914 the New Terms of Settlement, with this idea involved, was placed before the members and accepted by 4,301 to 3,820, or a majority of 481 only. After five years experience at working, another new agreement was drawn up in 1919, when there voted for 10,227, against 5,530, or a majority of 4,697. In August of this year a further vote was taken on the recommendation of 87 delegates at Conference against 19, to abolish the Terms of Settlement, but this was defeated in their resolution by 5,861 to 2,594. Declined to depart from the position, which some of us believe has been beneficial to the Industry, and a majority of 3,267 they confirmed the policy that has carried the Union during the last 25 years up to the present. All our members will understand that Payment by Results and the liberty of the individual at work are quite incompatible, and cannot go hand in hand. No man should be compelled or forced to be dragged down to the level of the slower worker who probably suffers greatly in his health, and I, as an Official of the Union with probably as much practical experience as any other, strongly recommend our members in their own interests to stand by the policy which has stood them in good stead for the last quarter of a century.

Unemployment.—On the question of Unemployment a further meeting has been held, and we have been advised to take the advice and opinion of an Actuary, whose experience will be beneficial so far as the outcome of the scheme is concerned. In all directions both employers and employees interested in the Industry, and those all look towards the need which the Executive of the Union feel that we should do better by controlling this scheme ourselves, spending our money in our own way, covering and governing the whole of the country, giving where we possibly can advantages over the general scheme, and keeping our members away from the Labour Exchanges, where officialism is just as rigid and deliberate and lacks as much courtesy as we sometimes experience in some of our Government Offices.

Council Meeting.—On the 24th and 25th we held our usual Executive Council Meeting and made preparations for submitting the above question of Payment by Results to the members in such a manner that we hope there will be no confusion in the members’ minds when they get hold of their Ballot Paper, to vote for or against limiting each other’s incomes.

Miners’ Dispute.—On the 27th with the rest of the Executive, we attended a Special Trade Union Congress to hear the report of the Miners’ negotiations with the Government, and whilst on this point, I should like to say I am not getting anything away from the Miners when I say that the Miners had not invited the Trade Union Movement of this country to meet upon their welfare. As a matter of fact the opinions of the rest of the movement passed upon the Miners Federation. Personally, I am of the opinion that no Trade Union in this country is big enough, or strong enough, or important enough, to ignore the welfare of the rest of their Trade Union confederates who meet annually at the Trade Union Congress. I am satisfied in my own mind that this is the reason why a good many people did not join the General Federation of Trade Unions, because the G.F.T.U. would have asked to have been consulted when action was likely to be taken which may involve other unions besides the one concerned. Be this as it may, there being no deputation of Miners who are out on strike in Conference with the Government and none of them available to give explanations to the meeting, it stood adjourned until 4.30. When 4.30 arrived, again there was no deputation, but a resolution was carried calling for a meeting next morning at 9.30. When we got our papers next morning we found that the meeting


MR. WALTER SMITH AND THE EMERGENCY POWERS BILL.

Clause 1.—(Issue of Proclamations of Emergency).

(1) If at any time it appears to His Majesty that any action has been taken or is immediately threatened by any persons or body of persons of such a nature and on so extensive a scale as to be calculated, by interfering with the supply and distribution of food, fuel, light, or other necessities, or with the means of locomotion of any substantial portion of the community, to deprive His Majesty’s subjects, by proclamation (hereinafter referred to as a proclamation of emergency), declare that a state of emergency exists.

(2) Where a proclamation is made, and if Parliament is then separated, forthwith communicated to Parliament, and if Parliament is then separated within fourteen days, a proclamation issued for an extraordinary meeting of Parliament shall be issued for a meeting of Parliament within fourteen days, and Parliament shall meet and sit upon the day appointed in the proclamation, and shall continue to act in like manner as if it had stood adjourned or prorogued to the same day.

Mr. Walter Smith: I beg to move, in Sub-section (f), to leave out the words “at any time.”

There is no need in the history of this Assembly have more drastic proposals than those contained in the Measure before us called upon to consider. There ought to be the very fullest consideration given to every clause of the Bill before such proposals are placed upon the Statute Book. We are frequently reminded that all changes affecting the lives of the people from the government should be effective by constitutional action. I do not know the standard of government differ from that suggestion, nor do we complain possibly, of any further statement that has frequently come from the Government that any other methods that may be adopted to effect changes will be strongly resisted. Having taken up that position, there is very great necessity for guarding the constitutional rights of the people. I know it was stated yesterday that there is nothing unconstitutional in this Bill, but such drastic changes as are suggested ought not to be made without the fullest opportunities for debate. We are assured that at times there must be necessity for emergency legislation and that there are many precedents to guide us in this matter, but all previous emergency legislation affecting such vast and drastic changes in the method of governing the country have always been of a temporary and not of a permanent character. Generally, they have been associated with passing incidents that have warranted special measures being adopted, but whenever it has been considered necessary to make such legislation permanent, then opportunities for discussion have been given, and, if not, one might reasonably have argued, should have been given.

In Clause 1 it is suggested that powers of a very wide and sweeping character, in fact so wide and sweeping that no Member can possibly state the scope of them, how far they will reach, nor where they will end, should be taken. That proposals of this description should be brought forward in the form of emergency legislation, and should be dealt with in restricted debate, is a development which we are entitled to question, even from this country. Of course, one recognises that the Constitution of this country very largely rests upon the procedure and custom established by this House, but we are entitled to ask whether the Constitution of this country, looked at from that standpoint, offers any precedent of a nature which would justify legislation being passed in this form. It was originally intended that the Bill should be passed in one sitting, and now we are to take the Committee stage, the Report stage, and the Third Reading today. No legislation, let alone legislation of this description, so wide in its character, ought to be forced through this House without the amplest opportunity being given for every line of the Bill being closely scrutinised and examined.

What chance has any Member of this House of consulting with legal advisers as to what the scope of this Bill really is? There has been no opportunity whatever for giving yet the Government propose to make a permanent part of their legislation to be used whenever the Government of the day deems a state of emergency has arisen which justifies its provisions becoming operative. I venture to suggest that that is a very dangerous procedure. We may have a sudden election during a period of passing excitement, and a Government may be returned to power which might use the provisions of a Bill such as this very largely for political purposes against their opponents. There is, in fact, no telling to what extent the Bill may be used, and therefore I say no legislation of this description must be permitted to be passed in a permanent form without ample opportunity being afforded for the discussion of every detail and for the examination of every line and word, in order to make sure that the liberty of the subject is properly safeguarded.
